# Endocrinology

## Diabetes Mellitus Management

**Q:** A 52-year-old obese patient with fasting glucose 7.8 mmol/L and HbA1c 7.4% (57 mmol/mol) on two separate occasions. Diagnosis and initial management?

**A:** Type 2 diabetes mellitus; first-line: lifestyle modification (diet, exercise, weight loss) + metformin unless contraindicated

CDA/Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines diagnose T2DM with: fasting glucose ≥7.0 mmol/L, random glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L, 2h OGTT ≥11.1 mmol/L, or HbA1c ≥6.5% — confirmed with a second test unless symptomatic. Metformin reduces HbA1c by 1-1.5%.

**Q:** What are the HbA1c targets in diabetes management per Diabetes Canada guidelines?

**A:** Most adults: HbA1c ≤7.0%; if high hypoglycemia risk or frail elderly: ≤8.0-8.5%; if low hypoglycemia risk with shorter duration and multiple risk factors: ≤6.5%

Diabetes Canada guidelines individualize HbA1c targets. Tighter control (≤7%) reduces microvascular complications (retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy). Looser control in elderly or hypoglycemia-prone patients. UKPDS and DCCT support intensive control.

**Q:** Which class of antidiabetic agents reduces cardiovascular mortality in T2DM patients with established CVD?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, canagliflozin, dapagliflozin) and GLP-1 receptor agonists (liraglutide, semaglutide) have cardiovascular outcome benefit — SGLT2i preferred with HF or CKD; GLP-1 RA preferred with atherosclerotic CVD

Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 RA as second-line after metformin for patients with established CVD, HF, or CKD — regardless of glycemic control. EMPA-REG, CANVAS, LEADER, SUSTAIN-6 trials established these benefits.

**Q:** A 25-year-old with type 1 diabetes has nausea, abdominal pain, blood glucose 28 mmol/L, blood gases pH 7.18, bicarbonate 10 mmol/L, ketones 4+. Diagnosis and immediate management?

**A:** Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA); immediate IV regular insulin infusion (0.1 units/kg/h), IV isotonic saline, potassium replacement (before insulin if K+ <3.5), identify and treat precipitant

DKA diagnostic criteria: glucose >14 mmol/L (may be normal in euglycemic DKA), ketonemia/ketonuria, pH <7.30, bicarbonate <15 mmol/L. Fluid deficit is 3-5L. Insulin drip continued until anion gap closed. Transition to subcutaneous insulin with overlap.

**Q:** What is hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state (HHS) and how does it differ from DKA?

**A:** HHS: glucose >33 mmol/L, osmolality >320 mOsm/kg, minimal ketones, no significant acidosis (pH >7.30) — occurs in T2DM; DKA: significant ketosis and acidosis, occurs in T1DM (and T2DM)

HHS has higher mortality than DKA (5-20% vs <1%). Fluid deficit larger (8-10L). Correct volume deficit first, then reduce glucose slowly (5 mmol/L/h). Insulin infusion at lower rates. Heparin prophylaxis for DVT risk.

**Q:** A T1DM patient has recurrent hypoglycemic episodes. What is hypoglycemia unawareness and how is it managed?

**A:** Hypoglycemia unawareness: loss of autonomic warning symptoms due to recurrent hypoglycemia; manage by strict hypoglycemia avoidance (higher glucose targets for 2-3 weeks to restore awareness) and CGM

Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) and insulin pump therapy (CSII) for T1DM patients with hypoglycemia unawareness to reduce severe hypoglycemia. Relaxing glycemic targets temporarily restores counter-regulatory response.

**Q:** What screening does Diabetes Canada recommend for complications of diabetes?

**A:** Annual: HbA1c q3months until stable then q6months, annual urine ACR and eGFR, annual dilated eye exam (ophthalmology), foot exam at every visit, lipid profile and BP annually

Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend systematic monitoring for microvascular (retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy) and macrovascular complications. Eye exam within 5 years of T1DM onset; at diagnosis for T2DM. Monofilament testing for peripheral neuropathy.

**Q:** What medications should be avoided or used with caution in T2DM with CKD?

**A:** Metformin: hold if eGFR <30, reduce dose if eGFR 30-45; SGLT2 inhibitors: less effective eGFR <45 but cardioprotective; avoid certain sulfonylureas (glibenclamide — renal clearance); adjust insulin doses

CDA/Diabetes Canada guidelines provide drug-specific guidance by eGFR level. SGLT2 inhibitors (specifically canagliflozin and dapagliflozin) have nephroprotective effects at eGFR 25-60. Metformin lactic acidosis risk in renal failure.

**Q:** What is the Somogyi effect vs. the dawn phenomenon in diabetes?

**A:** Somogyi effect (disputed): rebound hyperglycemia after nocturnal hypoglycemia; Dawn phenomenon: physiologic early morning hyperglycemia from GH and cortisol surge — both cause high morning glucose

Dawn phenomenon is well-established; Somogyi effect is controversial. Differentiation: check 3 AM glucose — low (Somogyi/nocturnal hypoglycemia) vs. normal (dawn phenomenon). Treatment: dawn phenomenon → increase evening basal insulin or use CSII.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of metformin and its main contraindications?

**A:** Metformin decreases hepatic gluconeogenesis (reduces hepatic glucose production); contraindications: eGFR <30, active liver disease, heart failure requiring hospitalization, metabolic acidosis

Metformin does not cause hypoglycemia when used alone. It reduces HbA1c 1-1.5%, is weight-neutral, and has CV benefits (UKPDS). Hold metformin perioperatively and 48h after IV contrast if AKI risk. Vitamin B12 deficiency with long-term use.

**Q:** What are the benefits and adverse effects of SGLT2 inhibitors in T2DM?

**A:** Benefits: HbA1c reduction 0.5-1%, weight loss, BP reduction, SGLT2i CV and renal benefits; side effects: genital mycotic infections, UTI, DKA (euglycemic DKA — especially with fasting/surgery), volume depletion, lower limb amputations (canagliflozin)

Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend SGLT2 inhibitors for patients with T2DM and CVD, HF, or CKD. Hold 3-4 days before surgery to prevent euglycemic DKA. Genital infections more common in women.

**Q:** A T2DM patient on metformin + glyburide has a blood glucose of 2.1 mmol/L after skipping lunch. What is the immediate treatment and long-term adjustment?

**A:** Hypoglycemia: immediate treatment with 15g fast-acting carbohydrate (glucose tablets, juice); recheck in 15 min; long-term: switch from glyburide to lower-risk agent (SGLT2i, DPP4i) or reduce glyburide dose

Sulfonylureas (especially glyburide) cause prolonged hypoglycemia due to long duration of action. Elderly patients and those with irregular meals or CKD are at highest risk. DPP4 inhibitors, SGLT2i, and GLP-1 RA have lower hypoglycemia risk.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for pre-diabetes?

**A:** Impaired fasting glucose: fasting glucose 6.1-6.9 mmol/L; Impaired glucose tolerance: 2h OGTT 7.8-11.0 mmol/L; HbA1c 6.0-6.4% (42-46 mmol/mol)

Diabetes Canada recommends lifestyle intervention (weight loss ≥5-7%, 150 min/week moderate exercise) for pre-diabetes — reduces progression to T2DM by 58% (DPP trial). Metformin is an option for high-risk pre-diabetes patients.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach to diabetes management in pregnancy?

**A:** Type 1/2 DM in pregnancy: insulin is the only recommended agent (oral agents except metformin are not approved); tight glycemic targets (fasting <5.3, 1h post-meal <7.8, 2h <6.7 mmol/L)

Gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) is diagnosed by 75g OGTT at 24-28 weeks. Initial management: medical nutrition therapy; add insulin if targets not met within 2 weeks. Metformin acceptable as second-line in GDM per Diabetes Canada/SOGC.

**Q:** What is the target blood pressure in patients with diabetes and hypertension?

**A:** BP target <130/80 mmHg in diabetes per Diabetes Canada and Hypertension Canada — ACEi or ARB preferred agents for nephroprotection

Tight BP control in diabetes reduces stroke, MI, and nephropathy progression. ACCORD-BP trial: SBP target <120 vs. <140 — no CV mortality reduction but lower stroke rates with tighter control. CDA recommends individualized targets.

**Q:** A patient with T2DM on metformin has HbA1c 8.4% despite maximum metformin dose. What is the preferred second-line agent if they have established CVD?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitor (empagliflozin, canagliflozin) or GLP-1 receptor agonist (liraglutide, semaglutide) — both have proven cardiovascular mortality reduction in established CVD

Diabetes Canada 2022 guidelines prioritize SGLT2i and GLP-1 RA as second-line agents for patients with established CVD, HF, or CKD. The choice between them is guided by comorbidities: SGLT2i preferred with HF/CKD, GLP-1 RA preferred with high ASCVD risk.

**Q:** What are the microvascular complications of diabetes and how are they prevented?

**A:** Retinopathy (leading cause of blindness in adults), nephropathy (leading cause of ESKD), neuropathy (peripheral: burning/numbness, autonomic: gastroparesis, orthostatic hypotension); prevented by tight glycemic control and BP management

DCCT (T1DM) and UKPDS (T2DM) trials established that HbA1c reduction significantly reduces microvascular complications. Every 1% HbA1c reduction reduces retinopathy risk by 35-40%, nephropathy by 25-35%. ACEi/ARB nephroprotective in diabetic nephropathy.

**Q:** What is the treatment of diabetic peripheral neuropathy pain?

**A:** First-line: duloxetine (SNRI) or pregabalin/gabapentin; second-line: tricyclic antidepressants (amitriptyline), venlafaxine; topical capsaicin or lidocaine for localized pain

Tight glycemic control is the primary prevention strategy. Pregabalin (FDA-approved for DPN), duloxetine (FDA-approved), and amitriptyline (off-label but effective) are first-line options per Diabetes Canada pain guidelines. Opioids are not recommended.

**Q:** What are the screening criteria for type 2 diabetes in Canada?

**A:** Screen adults ≥40 years every 3 years with fasting glucose or HbA1c; screen at any age with risk factors: obesity (BMI ≥30), first-degree family history, high-risk ethnicity (South Asian, African, Indigenous, Hispanic), prior GDM, prediabetes, CVD, medications causing hyperglycemia

Diabetes Canada 2022 guidelines recommend risk-stratified screening. CANRISK questionnaire can identify high-risk individuals. High-risk ethnic groups have higher T2DM prevalence and lower BMI thresholds.

**Q:** What is the sick day management protocol for T1DM patients?

**A:** Never stop insulin during illness; increase blood glucose monitoring (q2-4h), check ketones, maintain hydration, adjust insulin dose; insulin requirements typically INCREASE during illness

Illness, stress, and infection cause insulin resistance requiring more insulin. DKA commonly triggered by missed insulin doses or insufficient sick-day management. Diabetes Canada sick day rules: check glucose and ketones q4h, maintain at least basal insulin, seek care if vomiting prevents oral intake or ketones are rising.

**Q:** What are DKA diagnostic criteria?

**A:** Glucose>14, pH<7.30, bicarb<18, AG>12, positive ketones

Manage: IV fluids, insulin 0.1U/kg/hr, K replacement when<5.3. Switch subQ when AG closes and eating.

**Q:** How does HHS differ from DKA?

**A:** HHS: glucose>33.3, osm>320, minimal ketosis, pH>7.30. More dehydration (8-12L), higher mortality

More aggressive fluids. Lower insulin dose. Correct Na for glucose: add 1.6 per 5.6mmol/L above normal.

**Q:** What is CDA approach to T2DM glycemic management?

**A:** A1C<=7%. Metformin+lifestyle. Add SGLT2i or GLP-1RA if CVD/HF/CKD. Then other agents.

SGLT2i preferred for HF/CKD. GLP-1RA for ASCVD. Insulin if A1C>10% or symptomatic.

**Q:** What are the key differences between DKA and HHS?

**A:** DKA: Type 1 (or 2), glucose 14-30, pH <7.3, bicarb <18, AG >12, ketones positive, mental status variable. HHS: Type 2, glucose >33.3, pH >7.3, bicarb >18, minimal ketones, Osm >320, profound dehydration, altered mental status. HHS mortality higher (5-20% vs 1-5%).

DKA results from absolute insulin deficiency → lipolysis → ketogenesis. HHS results from relative insulin deficiency sufficient to prevent ketosis but not hyperglycemia → severe osmotic diuresis → dehydration. Both: precipitated by infection, non-adherence, new-onset DM. Some patients have overlap (ketosis-prone T2DM).

**Q:** What is the DKA management protocol?

**A:** 1) IV NS 1-1.5 L/h first hour. 2) Insulin: regular insulin 0.1 U/kg bolus then 0.1 U/kg/h infusion (or 0.14 U/kg/h no bolus). 3) Potassium: if K <3.3 hold insulin, replace K first; if 3.3-5.3 add 20-40 mEq/L to fluids; if >5.3 monitor q2h. 4) When glucose <14: switch to D5+½NS, reduce insulin to 0.02-0.05 U/kg/h.

Resolution criteria: glucose <11.1, pH >7.3, bicarb >18, AG ≤12. Overlap SC basal insulin 2h before stopping IV insulin to prevent rebound DKA. Bicarbonate ONLY if pH <6.9 (100 mmol NaHCO3). Monitor glucose q1h, lytes q2-4h. Cerebral edema risk in children — avoid aggressive fluid resuscitation.

**Q:** What are the Diabetes Canada A1C targets and when should they be individualized?

**A:** Most adults with T2DM: A1C ≤7.0%. Functional elderly or those at risk of hypoglycemia: ≤8.0-8.5%. Type 1 or newly diagnosed young T2DM without CV disease: consider ≤6.5% if safely achievable. Pregnancy: ≤6.5% preconception, ≤6.1% in pregnancy.

Diabetes Canada 2024 guidelines emphasize individualized targets. UKPDS showed microvascular benefit of intensive control in T2DM. ACCORD showed increased mortality with A1C <6.0% target in high-CV-risk patients — hence relaxed targets for complex/elderly patients. A1C unreliable in conditions affecting RBC lifespan (hemolysis, transfusion, CKD).

**Q:** What is the Diabetes Canada pharmacotherapy algorithm for T2DM?

**A:** Step 1: Metformin + lifestyle (unless A1C >1.5% above target → start dual). If CV disease: add SGLT2i or GLP-1 RA regardless of A1C. If CKD: add SGLT2i. If heart failure: add SGLT2i. Step 2: add second agent based on clinical profile. Insulin if A1C remains above target.

Diabetes Canada 2024 prioritizes cardiorenal-protective agents (SGLT2i, GLP-1 RA) for patients with established CVD, CKD, or HF — independent of A1C. GLP-1 RA preferred if weight loss desired (semaglutide, liraglutide). DPP-4i: weight-neutral, no CV benefit. TZDs: weight gain, edema, fractures. SUs: hypoglycemia risk, weight gain.

**Q:** What are the mechanisms and side effects of SGLT2 inhibitors?

**A:** Mechanism: block sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 in proximal tubule → glycosuria → lowers glucose, weight (2-3 kg), BP (3-5 mmHg), reduces intraglomerular pressure. Side effects: genital mycotic infections, UTI, euglycemic DKA, volume depletion, Fournier gangrene (rare), amputations (canagliflozin).

SGLT2i (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin) have proven CV benefit (EMPA-REG, CANVAS) and renal protection (CREDENCE, DAPA-CKD). Euglycemic DKA: rare but dangerous, triggered by illness/surgery — hold perioperatively 3 days before. Approved for HFrEF and CKD regardless of diabetes status.

**Q:** What insulin regimens are used in T2DM and T1DM?

**A:** T2DM: start basal insulin (glargine/detemir/degludec) at 10 U or 0.1-0.2 U/kg, titrate by 2-4 U q3-4 days to fasting target. Add prandial (rapid-acting) if needed. T1DM: basal-bolus (MDI) or insulin pump (CSII). Total daily dose ~0.5-1.0 U/kg; 50% basal, 50% bolus divided across meals.

Diabetes Canada recommends insulin initiation when A1C >1.5% above target or symptomatic hyperglycemia. Basal insulin analogues (glargine, degludec) preferred over NPH (less hypoglycemia, more predictable). Insulin degludec has longest duration (>42h) and lowest hypoglycemia. ICR (insulin-to-carb ratio) and ISF (correction factor) used for bolus dosing in T1DM.

**Q:** What are the microvascular complications of diabetes and their screening protocols?

**A:** Retinopathy: dilated eye exam at diagnosis T2DM (5 yrs T1DM), then annually. Nephropathy: ACR + eGFR annually from diagnosis T2DM (5 yrs T1DM). Neuropathy: 10g monofilament + 128Hz tuning fork annually. Foot exam annually.

Diabetes Canada screening recommendations: retinopathy screening can be extended to q2 years if consistently normal. Albuminuria first detected → confirm with repeat in 3 months. ACEi/ARBs for confirmed albuminuria (ACR >2 men, >2.8 women) regardless of BP. SGLT2i added for cardiorenal protection. Tight glycemic + BP control prevents/slows all microvascular complications.

**Q:** What is the management of hypoglycemia and what defines severe hypoglycemia?

**A:** Mild-moderate (conscious): glucose <4.0 mmol/L → 15g fast-acting carbs (3-4 glucose tabs, 150 mL juice), recheck in 15 min, repeat if still <4.0, then snack. Severe (altered consciousness, unable to self-treat): glucagon 1 mg IM/SC or nasal glucagon 3 mg, or IV dextrose 50% (D50W) 25-50 mL.

Diabetes Canada defines hypoglycemia by blood glucose <4.0 mmol/L. Severe hypoglycemia = requires assistance. Recurrent severe hypoglycemia: reassess targets (relax A1C), review medications (reduce/stop SUs or insulin), consider hypoglycemia unawareness (raise targets to >5.0 for 2-3 weeks to restore awareness). Glucagon now available OTC as nasal spray.

**Q:** What is the cardiovascular risk management approach in diabetes per Diabetes Canada?

**A:** All T2DM >40 or with microvascular disease: statin therapy (moderate-high intensity). BP target <130/80. ACEi/ARB if albuminuria, CVD, or age ≥55 with additional CV risk factor. ASA: secondary prevention only (not primary). SGLT2i or GLP-1 RA if established CVD.

Diabetes is a CV risk equivalent. 10-year CV risk assessment using Framingham Risk Score modified for diabetes. LDL target <2.0 mmol/L or >50% reduction. PCSK9 inhibitors if statin-intolerant or not at target. Smoking cessation is the single most impactful intervention. Exercise: 150 min/week moderate-vigorous aerobic + resistance training 2-3x/week.

**Q:** What is gestational diabetes (GDM) screening and management?

**A:** Screen all at 24-28 weeks with 50g GCT (threshold ≥7.8 → 75g OGTT) or direct 75g OGTT. Diagnostic: FPG ≥5.3 or 1h ≥10.6 or 2h ≥9.0 (one abnormal value). Management: diet + exercise first → insulin if targets not met (FBG <5.3, 1h post-meal <7.8, 2h post-meal <6.7).

Diabetes Canada uses IADPSG-based criteria. GDM increases risk of macrosomia, shoulder dystocia, preeclampsia, neonatal hypoglycemia, and future T2DM in mother (50% lifetime risk). Metformin is alternative to insulin (crosses placenta but increasingly accepted). Glyburide no longer recommended. Screen with 75g OGTT at 6 weeks postpartum.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for diabetes mellitus?

**A:** FPG ≥7.0 mmol/L (fasting ≥8h), OR 2h PG ≥11.1 on 75g OGTT, OR A1C ≥6.5% (standardized lab), OR random PG ≥11.1 with symptoms. Two abnormal tests required if asymptomatic (can be same sample, two different tests). Single test sufficient if symptomatic + random ≥11.1.

Diabetes Canada 2024 diagnostic criteria. A1C 6.0-6.4% = prediabetes (high risk). A1C unreliable in: hemoglobinopathies, iron deficiency, CKD, hemolytic anemia, pregnancy. Screen high-risk individuals annually (age ≥40, first-degree relative, high-risk ethnic group, GDM history, impaired glucose tolerance, metabolic syndrome).

**Q:** What are the GLP-1 receptor agonists and their clinical benefits?

**A:** Agents: liraglutide, semaglutide (SC or oral), dulaglutide, exenatide. Benefits: A1C reduction 1.0-1.8%, weight loss 3-6 kg (semaglutide up to 15%), CV benefit (LEADER, SUSTAIN-6, REWIND), renal protection (FLOW trial — semaglutide). Side effects: GI (nausea, vomiting), pancreatitis risk (theoretical).

GLP-1 RAs are incretin mimetics that stimulate glucose-dependent insulin secretion, suppress glucagon, slow gastric emptying, and promote satiety. Semaglutide 2.4 mg (Wegovy) approved for obesity. Contraindicated in personal/family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma or MEN2 (thyroid C-cell tumors in rodent studies). Tirzepatide (dual GIP/GLP-1) offers even greater A1C and weight reduction.

**Q:** What is the perioperative diabetes management approach?

**A:** Hold metformin day of surgery (restart when eating, normal renal function). Hold SGLT2i 3 days before (euglycemic DKA risk). Continue basal insulin at 80% dose night before/morning of. Hold bolus insulin and oral agents day of surgery. Use IV insulin infusion if glucose >10-12 mmol/L. Target glucose 5-10 mmol/L perioperatively.

Diabetes Canada recommends tight but safe glucose control perioperatively to reduce wound infections and complications. Reduce basal insulin to 60-80% to prevent hypoglycemia while fasting. Monitor glucose q1-2h. Resume home regimen when eating normally. DKA can occur perioperatively in T1DM — never completely hold insulin.

**Q:** What is the diabetic foot assessment and management approach?

**A:** Annual screening: 10g monofilament (4 sites per foot), 128Hz tuning fork, visual inspection, pedal pulses. High risk: calluses, deformity (Charcot), peripheral neuropathy, PAD, prior ulcer/amputation. Ulcer management: offloading (total contact cast), debridement, infection control, revascularization if ischemic.

Wagner classification grades ulcers 0-5. Diabetic foot infections: mild (superficial, <2cm cellulitis) → oral antibiotics. Moderate (deeper, >2cm cellulitis) → IV antibiotics. Severe (systemic toxicity, osteomyelitis) → IV antibiotics + surgical debridement. MRI best for osteomyelitis diagnosis. Probe-to-bone test positive has high PPV for osteomyelitis.

**Q:** What is the sick day management protocol for diabetes?

**A:** Never stop insulin completely (T1DM: maintain basal at minimum). Monitor BG q2-4h + ketones. Increase fluids (sugar-free if BG >14, sugary if BG <14). Hold metformin, SGLT2i, ACEi/ARBs, diuretics, NSAIDs if dehydrated/vomiting ('sick day drugs'). Seek medical attention if persistent vomiting, BG >20 with ketones, or unable to maintain hydration.

Diabetes Canada publishes sick day management guides for patients. SGLT2i carry euglycemic DKA risk during illness — hold early. T1DM patients should have ketone testing supplies at home. Supplemental rapid-acting insulin doses based on correction factor if BG elevated. Prevention of DKA is key — education is critical.

**Q:** What is the screening schedule for diabetic complications?

**A:** Eyes: annual dilated exam from diagnosis (T2DM) or 5 years (T1DM). Feet: every visit. Kidneys: annual ACR + eGFR. Neuropathy: annual monofilament/vibration. CV: lipids, BP every visit

CDA guidelines. Early referral: proliferative retinopathy, eGFR<30, foot ulcer/Charcot. A1C q3 months if not at target, q6 months if stable. Dental exam annually.

**Q:** What are the insulin initiation and titration strategies for T2DM?

**A:** Basal insulin (glargine/detemir): start 10U or 0.2U/kg at bedtime. Titrate by 2U q3 days to fasting glucose 4-7mmol/L. Add prandial if A1C still above target: rapid-acting before largest meal

CDA: basal-bolus or basal-plus regimens. Hypoglycemia prevention: glucose monitoring, patient education, avoid SU with basal insulin. Weight gain expected ~2-4kg. GLP-1RA can be combined with basal insulin.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of type 1 vs type 2 diabetes?

**A:** T1DM: autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells (anti-GAD, anti-IA2) causing absolute insulin deficiency. T2DM: insulin resistance + relative insulin deficiency from progressive beta-cell dysfunction

T1DM: immune-mediated, often HLA-DR3/DR4 associated. T2DM: obesity/visceral fat drives insulin resistance via adipokines, free fatty acids. Beta-cell failure is progressive — eventually need insulin.

**Q:** How does pregestational diabetes management differ in pregnancy?

**A:** Target A1C <6.5% preconception, strict glycemic targets (fasting <5.3, 1h PP <7.8, 2h PP <6.7). Insulin is preferred. Stop ACEi/ARB/statins. Start folic acid 5mg. Retinal screening each trimester

Pregestational DM has higher risk than GDM: congenital anomalies (NTD, cardiac) if poor periconception control. Insulin needs increase 2-3x by third trimester. Hypoglycemia risk increases in first trimester.

**Q:** What is the approach to hypoglycemia unawareness?

**A:** Loss of adrenergic warning symptoms (tremor, sweating, palpitations) due to recurrent hypoglycemia. Management: raise glucose targets for 2-3 weeks to restore awareness, CGM, glucagon kit, patient education

Caused by defective counter-regulatory response. Risk factors: long-standing T1DM, tight control, autonomic neuropathy. Impaired awareness hypoglycemia affects 25% of T1DM patients. CGM with alarms reduces severe episodes.

**Q:** Outline the diabetic foot ulcer assessment algorithm.

**A:** 1) Probe-to-bone test (positive = high probability osteomyelitis). 2) Classify with Wagner or UT system. 3) Vascular assessment (ABI, toe pressures). 4) Offloading (total contact cast for plantar ulcers). 5) Debridement. 6) Antibiotics if infected (mild=oral, moderate/severe=IV per Diabetes Canada). 7) MRI if osteomyelitis suspected.

Diabetes Canada recommends annual comprehensive foot exams with monofilament and pedal pulses. Probe-to-bone has ~90% specificity for osteomyelitis. Total contact casting is gold standard for offloading neuropathic ulcers. Multidisciplinary foot care teams reduce amputation by 50%.

**Q:** What is hypoglycemia unawareness and how is it managed?

**A:** Loss of autonomic warning symptoms at glucose <4.0 mmol/L due to repeated hypoglycemia shifting the glycemic threshold downward. Management: strict hypoglycemia avoidance for 2-3 weeks, relaxed A1C targets (7.5-8.0%), CGM with alarms, glucagon kit prescription.

Affects ~25% of type 1 patients, increasing severe hypoglycemia risk 6-fold. Diabetes Canada recommends Clarke or Gold questionnaires to screen. CGM with low-glucose suspend technology significantly reduces events. Recurrent severe hypoglycemia is an indication for islet cell transplantation in Canada.

**Q:** How does management of pregestational diabetes differ from gestational diabetes (GDM)?

**A:** Pregestational: optimize A1C <6.5% pre-conception, insulin is preferred (metformin acceptable in type 2), folic acid 5 mg, retinal/renal screening each trimester, tight targets (fasting 4.0-5.3, 1h post-meal <7.8). GDM: diagnosed 24-28 weeks by OGTT, lifestyle first, insulin if targets not met in 2 weeks.

Pregestational diabetes carries higher congenital anomaly risk (cardiac, NTDs) if poorly controlled in the first trimester. Both require postpartum glucose testing: 75g OGTT at 6 weeks for GDM. SOGC and Diabetes Canada guidelines align on these recommendations.

**Q:** What are the indications and key metrics of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM)?

**A:** Indications: all type 1 diabetes, type 2 on intensive insulin, hypoglycemia unawareness, pregnancy with pregestational diabetes. Key metrics: time-in-range 3.9-10.0 (goal >70%), time below range (<4% below 3.9), glucose management indicator (GMI).

Diabetes Canada 2024 guidelines recommend CGM for all type 1 patients. Flash glucose monitoring (FreeStyle Libre) is widely available with variable provincial coverage. CGM reduces A1C by 0.3-0.5% and significantly reduces hypoglycemia frequency.

**Q:** What are key complications specific to insulin pump therapy?

**A:** DKA risk from pump failure/site occlusion (no basal depot, DKA can develop in 4-6 hours), infusion site infections, lipohypertrophy. Must rotate sites every 2-3 days, always carry backup insulin pens, and have sick-day management plans.

Insulin pumps deliver only rapid-acting insulin, so any interruption causes rapid hyperglycemia. Diabetes Canada recommends ketone testing access for all pump users. Hybrid closed-loop systems automate basal adjustments and reduce hypoglycemia but require user engagement for bolusing.

**Q:** What is the A1C target for most adults with type 2 diabetes per Diabetes Canada, and when should it differ?

**A:** Target <=7.0% for most. Consider <=6.5% if achievable without significant hypoglycemia (reduces CKD/retinopathy risk). Consider 7.1-8.5% in elderly, limited life expectancy, frailty, or recurrent severe hypoglycemia.

Diabetes Canada emphasizes individualized targets. The ACCORD trial showed increased mortality with aggressive targets (<6.0%) in high-risk patients. SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 RAs are preferred second-line for cardiovascular and renal benefit regardless of A1C.

**Q:** A type 2 diabetic with established CVD has A1C 8.2% on metformin. What agent to add next?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitor (empagliflozin, canagliflozin) or GLP-1 RA (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide) with proven CV benefit, irrespective of A1C. SGLT2i preferred if HF/CKD predominates; GLP-1 RA if ASCVD predominates.

Diabetes Canada 2024 guidelines prioritize cardiorenal benefit over A1C when selecting second-line therapy. EMPA-REG, CANVAS, LEADER, SUSTAIN-6 trials demonstrated CV outcome benefits. These agents can be combined for additive benefit.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for DKA vs HHS?

**A:** DKA: glucose >14 mmol/L, pH <7.3, bicarbonate <18, anion gap >12, positive ketones. HHS: glucose >33.3 mmol/L, osmolality >320, pH >7.3, minimal ketones, profound dehydration. DKA more common in type 1; HHS in elderly type 2.

DKA results from absolute insulin deficiency causing ketogenesis. HHS from relative deficiency preventing ketosis but not hyperglycemia. HHS mortality is higher (10-20% vs 1-5%). Both require IV fluids, insulin, and potassium monitoring. Diabetes Canada recommends standardized order sets.

**Q:** What is euglycemic DKA and what are its causes?

**A:** DKA with blood glucose <14 mmol/L. Most commonly from SGLT2 inhibitor use. Also seen with reduced carbohydrate intake, pregnancy, partial insulin treatment, or alcohol. Presents with metabolic acidosis and ketonemia despite near-normal glucose.

SGLT2 inhibitors cause glucosuria, lowering glucose while ketogenesis continues. Health Canada has issued safety warnings. Hold SGLT2i 3 days before elective surgery per Diabetes Canada. Always check ketones and blood gas in an ill diabetic on SGLT2i.

**Q:** What screening is recommended for microvascular complications at type 2 diabetes diagnosis?

**A:** At diagnosis: dilated eye exam, uACR + eGFR, monofilament + tuning fork foot exam. Then: eyes every 1-2 years, uACR/eGFR annually, foot exam annually. Type 1: screening starts 5 years post-diagnosis (post-pubertal).

Diabetes Canada recommends immediate screening at type 2 diagnosis since the disease may have been present years prior. ACEi/ARB is indicated when uACR >20 mg/mmol to slow nephropathy. Early detection of retinopathy enables timely laser/anti-VEGF treatment.

**Q:** What metabolic effects are associated with beta-adrenergic receptor activation?

**A:** Liver: gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis. Fat cells: lipolysis. Pancreatic beta cells: increased insulin secretion

Beta-adrenergic stimulation mobilizes energy substrates (glucose and free fatty acids) during stress, supporting the fight-or-flight response.

**Q:** What is physiological (functional) antagonism?

**A:** Drugs that produce opposite physiological effects through different receptors (e.g., insulin decreases glucose vs. glucagon raises glucose)

Both drugs act through different receptors but produce opposing physiological responses, providing a form of physiological antagonism.

**Q:** Give a clinical example of physiological/functional antagonism

**A:** Insulin decreases blood glucose via insulin receptors vs. glucagon raises blood glucose via glucagon receptors - opposite physiological effects

These drugs work through different mechanisms to produce opposing effects, useful when one system is pathologically overactive.

**Q:** What is the insulin infusion rate for DKA management?

**A:** 0.05-0.1 U/kg/h continuous IV infusion

Low-dose insulin infusion at 0.05-0.1 U/kg/h is the standard approach for DKA management, achieving adequate ketogenesis suppression while minimizing hypoglycemia risk.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of hypoglycemia in the emergency setting?

**A:** Excessive insulin use in the setting of poor PO intake.

Most hypoglycemia presenting to ED is iatrogenic from insulin or sulfonylureas.

**Q:** What is unique about hypoglycemia due to long-acting insulin or sulfonylureas?

**A:** Watch for prolonged hypoglycemia due to long half-life; may require admission for monitoring.

These agents can cause prolonged hypoglycemia requiring extended observation.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of hypoglycemia in outpatients?

**A:** Excessive insulin use in setting of poor PO intake

Iatrogenic insulin overdose with inadequate carbohydrate intake is the most frequent cause.

**Q:** What rare causes of hypoglycemia should be considered?

**A:** Insulinomas, hypopituitarism, adrenal insufficiency, liver failure, medication side effects

These endocrine and hepatic disorders can cause hypoglycemia through impaired gluconeogenesis or excess insulin.

**Q:** What are common causes of hypoglycemia to investigate after treatment?

**A:** Common causes include: exogenous insulin, alcohol, or sulfonylureas. Also consider insulinomas, hypopituitarism, adrenal insufficiency.

Identifying the underlying cause prevents recurrent episodes.

**Q:** What is the starting dose of basal insulin for T2DM according to Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines?

**A:** 10U at bedtime (lower for lean patients <50kg)

Starting at a low dose reduces risk of hypoglycemia. Titrate by 1U nightly until fasting BG reaches 4-7 mmol/L target.

**Q:** What is the target fasting blood glucose for basal insulin titration in T2DM?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

This is the fasting BG target used to titrate basal insulin doses in T2DM management per Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines.

**Q:** Calculate the correction factor for a patient with total daily insulin dose of 50 units

**A:** CF = 100/50 = 2 mmol/L per unit of insulin

Correction Factor = 100/TDD. This represents the expected decrease in blood glucose per unit of insulin administered.

**Q:** What is the typical total daily insulin (TDI) requirement for basal-bolus regimen in T2DM?

**A:** 0.3-0.5 U/kg

For a 100kg patient, this equals 30-50U total daily dose, with 40% as basal and 60% as prandial bolus.

**Q:** In a basal-bolus regimen, what percentage of TDI is allocated to basal insulin vs bolus insulin?

**A:** 40% basal, 60% bolus (or 40% basal, 20% each meal)

Basal insulin is 40% of TDI, while bolus (prandial) insulin is 60% of TDI, typically divided equally before each meal.

**Q:** What are the target postprandial blood glucose levels for bolus insulin adjustment?

**A:** 5-10 mmol/L

Postprandial BG should be 5-10 mmol/L. Preprandial BG for subsequent meals should be 4-7 mmol/L.

**Q:** What is the typical insulin requirement for most T2DM patients to achieve target BG?

**A:** 40-50 units/day

Most T2DM patients will need 40-50 U/day to achieve glycemic targets, though there is no maximum dose.

**Q:** What should be done if fasting hypoglycemia occurs with basal insulin?

**A:** Reduce the dose of bedtime basal insulin

Fasting hypoglycemia indicates the basal dose is too high and should be reduced. Daytime hypoglycemia may require reducing oral agents, especially secretagogues.

**Q:** Which oral antihyperglycemic agents are typically continued when adding bolus insulin?

**A:** Metformin is continued; secretagogues are typically stopped

Metformin is continued as it does not cause hypoglycemia. Insulin secretagogues (e.g., sulfonylureas) are typically discontinued when prandial insulin is added.

**Q:** What type of insulin is used in insulin pump therapy (CSII)?

**A:** Rapid-acting insulin analogues (aspart, glulisine, or lispro)

CSII uses continuous subcutaneous infusion of rapid-acting insulin, with boluses programmed for meals based on carb:insulin ratios.

**Q:** What is the major risk of insulin pump therapy if disconnected or malfunctioning?

**A:** Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)

If the pump is disconnected or malfunctions, there is no basal insulin delivery, leading to rapid insulin deficiency and DKA risk.

**Q:** Distinguish DKA pathophysiology from HHS in terms of insulin deficiency

**A:** DKA: absolute insulin deficiency; HHS: partial/relative insulin deficiency

DKA occurs with complete or near-complete insulin deficiency. HHS has enough insulin to inhibit lipolysis and prevent ketosis but insufficient glucose utilization.

**Q:** Why does DKA have metabolic acidosis while HHS typically does not?

**A:** DKA has absolute insulin deficiency → unchecked lipolysis → increased FFA → ketone production → metabolic acidosis. HHS has enough insulin to inhibit lipolysis → no significant ketosis.

The presence of ketones distinguishes DKA from HHS. Even with high glucose, partial insulin in HHS prevents ketone formation.

**Q:** When should insulin be held in DKA treatment?

**A:** When serum K+ <3.3 mmol/L

If K+ is <3.3 mmol/L, give 40 mEq/L K+ replacement and hold insulin until potassium is ≥3.3 mmol/L to prevent life-threatening hypokalemia.

**Q:** Why is urine ketones usually positive in DKA but negative in HHS?

**A:** DKA has absolute insulin deficiency → unchecked lipolysis → ketone production. HHS has relative insulin deficiency → enough insulin to inhibit lipolysis and ketone production.

The presence or absence of ketones is a key differentiator between DKA and HHS.

**Q:** Which type of diabetes is more commonly associated with DKA?

**A:** Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

DKA typically occurs in T1DM due to absolute insulin deficiency. It can occur in T2DM but is less common.

**Q:** Which type of diabetes is more commonly associated with HHS?

**A:** Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

HHS typically occurs in T2DM, often in elderly patients with partial insulin deficiency.

**Q:** What is the insulin titration for basal-bolus: for a 70kg patient starting at 0.5 U/kg TDI?

**A:** TDI = 35U: Basal = 14U at bedtime, Bolus = 7U with each meal

70kg × 0.5 = 35U TDI. Basal 40% = 14U, Bolus 60% = 21U (7U per meal).

**Q:** What is the formula for calculating the correction factor (CF) in insulin dosing?

**A:** CF = 100/Total Daily Dose (TDD) = change in blood glucose per unit of insulin

The correction factor represents how much 1 unit of insulin will lower blood glucose. For example, if TDD is 50U, CF = 2 mmol/L reduction per unit.

**Q:** What is the target fasting blood glucose for patients with T2DM on insulin therapy?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines recommend fasting BG target of 4-7 mmol/L for most patients with T2DM on insulin therapy.

**Q:** In a basal-bolus insulin regimen, what percentage of total daily insulin (TDI) should be basal vs bolus?

**A:** 40% basal, 60% bolus (distributed as 20% with each of 3 meals)

For basal-bolus: TDI = 0.3-0.5 U/kg, with 40% as basal insulin and 60% as prandial bolus insulin divided before each meal.

**Q:** What type of diabetes is Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) typically associated with?

**A:** Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

DKA usually occurs in T1DM due to absolute insulin deficiency, though it can occur in T2DM under stress conditions.

**Q:** What type of diabetes is Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) typically associated with?

**A:** Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

HHS occurs predominantly in T2DM due to partial insulin deficiency that prevents ketosis but allows extreme hyperglycemia.

**Q:** Why does total body potassium deplete in DKA despite normal or elevated serum K+?

**A:** Insulin deficiency and plasma osmolality shift K+ from ICF to ECF

Lack of insulin and high plasma osmolality cause potassium to shift from intracellular to extracellular compartment, masking total body depletion.

**Q:** What is the insulin pump (CSII) delivery mechanism?

**A:** Continuous subcutaneous infusion of rapid-acting insulin analogue (aspart, glulisine, or lispro) via external battery-operated device

Insulin pumps deliver continuous basal insulin through a small subcutaneous catheter, with patient-programmed boluses at meals based on carbohydrate ratios.

**Q:** What is the major risk of insulin pump therapy?

**A:** DKA if pump is inadvertently disconnected or malfunctions

Since pumps only use rapid-acting insulin, any interruption in delivery can quickly lead to insulin deficiency and DKA.

**Q:** What is the postprandial blood glucose target for patients on insulin therapy?

**A:** 5-10 mmol/L

Postprandial BG target is 5-10 mmol/L, while preprandial BG for subsequent meals should be 4-7 mmol/L.

**Q:** In HHS, why is potassium depletion less severe than in DKA?

**A:** HHS patients typically have less profound insulin deficiency and less acidosis

The partial insulin deficiency in HHS causes less potassium shift from ICF and less osmotic diuresis compared to the absolute insulin deficiency in DKA.

**Q:** What should be done regarding oral antihyperglycemic agents when basal insulin is started?

**A:** Continue metformin, consider stopping secretagogues

Metformin is typically continued with insulin therapy, but secretagogues (sulfonylureas) should be stopped to reduce hypoglycemia risk.

**Q:** When are insulin secretagogues typically stopped in T2DM management?

**A:** When bolus (prandial) insulin is added

Insulin secretagogues are stopped when bolus insulin is added to avoid redundant therapy and reduce hypoglycemia risk.

**Q:** What is the blood glucose threshold for calling physician in insulin correction dosing?

**A:** BG <4 mmol/L

When BG is <4 mmol/L, the physician should be called and the patient should receive 15g of rapid-acting carbohydrates, rechecking in 15 minutes.

**Q:** In correction factor dosing, what is the insulin dose if BG is between 8 and (8+CF)?

**A:** Give 1 additional unit

Correction factor dosing: BG 8 to (8+CF) = 1 unit; (8+CF) to (8+2CF) = 2 units; (8+2CF) to (8+3CF) = 3 units.

**Q:** What is the normal response in an insulin tolerance test?

**A:** Hypoglycemia induced by insulin should stimulate increased GH and cortisol secretion

The insulin tolerance test evaluates the entire hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis and GH axis. Hypoglycemia is a physiologic stressor that should trigger ACTH and GH release.

**Q:** What is the starting dose and titration approach for basal insulin in T2DM according to Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines?

**A:** Start at 10U at bedtime; increase by 1U every night until fasting BG reaches target of 4-7 mmol/L

Most patients need 40-50 units/day to achieve target. If fasting hypoglycemia occurs, reduce bedtime basal dose.

**Q:** For a basal-bolus insulin regimen, how is the total daily insulin (TDI) divided?

**A:** TDI = 0.3-0.5 U/kg; 40% basal (bedtime), 60% bolus (20% before each meal)

Basal insulin controls fasting glucose while bolus insulin covers postprandial glucose levels.

**Q:** What is the correction factor formula for insulin dosing?

**A:** CF = 100/Total Daily Dose (TDD) = change in blood glucose per unit of insulin

Example: if TDD = 50U, CF = 100/50 = 2 mmol/L, meaning 1 unit of insulin lowers BG by 2 mmol/L.

**Q:** What insulin correction dosing should be given when BG is between 8 and (8 + CF) mmol/L?

**A:** Give one additional unit of insulin

This is part of the insulin correction scale: 8 to (8+CF) = 1 unit, (8+CF) to (8+2CF) = 2 units, etc.

**Q:** How does the pathophysiology of DKA differ from HHS?

**A:** DKA: complete insulin deficiency with ketogenesis (occurs in T1DM); HHS: partial/relative insulin deficiency prevents ketosis (occurs in T2DM)

A small amount of insulin in HHS prevents lipolysis and ketone production while still allowing some glucose utilization.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for DKA regarding potassium replacement?

**A:** If serum K+ <3.3 mmol/L, give 40 mEq/L K+ replacement and hold insulin until [K+] ≥3.3 mmol/L

Insulin drives K+ into cells, potentially causing life-threatening hypokalemia if K+ is not replaced first.

**Q:** What is the typical insulin requirement (TDI) for most T2DM patients to achieve target?

**A:** 40-50 U/day (no maximum dose)

This applies to basal, basal-bolus, and premixed insulin regimens.

**Q:** Which oral antihyperglycemic is typically continued when adding bolus insulin?

**A:** Metformin

Insulin secretagogues (sulfonylureas) are typically stopped when bolus insulin is added to avoid hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What causes low HDL secondary to endocrine disorders?

**A:** Obesity/metabolic syndrome and diabetes mellitus

Low HDL is part of the metabolic syndrome phenotype and improves with weight loss and glycemic control.

**Q:** What is the starting dose for basal insulin in T2DM according to Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines?

**A:** 10 units at bedtime (lower for lean patients <50 kg)

Starting dose is 10U at bedtime, titrated by 1U every night until fasting BG reaches 4-7 mmol/L target.

**Q:** What is the target fasting blood glucose level for insulin titration in T2DM?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

Target fasting BG is 4-7 mmol/L. Dose is increased by 1U nightly until target is achieved.

**Q:** How is the correction factor (CF) calculated for insulin dosing?

**A:** CF = 100/Total Daily Dose (TDD)

Correction factor represents the change in blood glucose (mmol/L) per unit of insulin administered.

**Q:** What is the typical total daily insulin (TDI) requirement for basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** 0.3-0.5 U/kg

TDI of 0.3-0.5 U/kg is split: 40% basal insulin, 60% as prandial bolus divided before each meal.

**Q:** In a basal-bolus regimen, what percentage of TDI is allocated to basal vs prandial insulin?

**A:** 40% basal, 60% prandial (bolus)

Basal insulin is 40% of TDI to maintain fasting BG, while bolus (prandial) is 60% distributed before meals.

**Q:** What insulin is used in insulin pump therapy (CSII)?

**A:** Rapid-acting insulin analogues (aspart, glulisine, or lispro)

CSII uses external battery-operated devices delivering continuous basal rapid-acting insulin through subcutaneous catheter.

**Q:** What is a major risk of insulin pump therapy?

**A:** Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) if pump is disconnected or malfunctions

Risk of DKA exists if the pump is inadvertently disconnected or malfunctions, interrupting insulin delivery.

**Q:** Which type of diabetes is DKA typically associated with?

**A:** Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

DKA usually occurs in T1DM due to absolute insulin deficiency, though it can occur in T2DM with stress.

**Q:** Which type of diabetes is HHS typically associated with?

**A:** Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

HHS occurs in T2DM, though it can occasionally occur in T1DM. Characterized by hyperglycemia without ketosis.

**Q:** Why does ketosis typically not develop in HHS?

**A:** Presence of a small amount of insulin inhibits lipolysis

Partial or relative insulin deficiency in HHS prevents ketogenesis by inhibiting lipolysis, unlike absolute insulin deficiency in DKA.

**Q:** What is the K+ replacement threshold in DKA treatment?

**A:** Hold insulin and give 40 mEq/L K+ if serum K+ <3.3 mmol/L

If serum potassium is <3.3 mmol/L, give potassium replacement and hold insulin until K+ is ≥3.3 mmol/L.

**Q:** What is the target postprandial blood glucose level for bolus insulin adjustment?

**A:** 5-10 mmol/L

Bolus insulin is adjusted to achieve postprandial BG levels of 5-10 mmol/L, while preprandial BG for subsequent meals should be 4-7 mmol/L.

**Q:** When using correction factor dosing, what is the insulin dose for blood glucose between 8 and (8+CF)?

**A:** Give 1 additional unit

For BG between 8 to (8+CF), give one additional unit of rapid-acting insulin on top of the meal bolus.

**Q:** What diabetes medication is typically continued when starting basal insulin?

**A:** Metformin

Metformin is typically continued as it improves insulin sensitivity and has low hypoglycemia risk, while secretagogues may be stopped.

**Q:** What is the typical maximum dose of basal insulin needed to achieve target?

**A:** 40-50 units/day (no maximum dose)

Most patients need 40-50 U/day to achieve target, but there is no maximum dose - doses can be much higher if needed.

**Q:** What is the main difference in insulin therapy between DKA and HHS?

**A:** DKA requires more aggressive insulin therapy to resolve ketoacidosis; HHS has less severe K+ depletion

In DKA, insulin is critical to resolve acidosis (not just hyperglycemia), while HHS patients have less severe potassium depletion.

**Q:** What is the initial test for evaluating suspected hypopituitarism with low 8am cortisol?

**A:** Cosyntropin stimulation test; if equivocal, proceed to insulin tolerance test

The insulin tolerance test checks both cortisol and GH reserve by inducing hypoglycemia to stimulate hormone release.

**Q:** What does the insulin tolerance test evaluate?

**A:** The pituitary's ability to release GH and cortisol in response to hypoglycemia

Insulin-induced hypoglycemia should stimulate increased GH and cortisol. A normal response indicates intact pituitary function.

**Q:** What is the target fasting blood glucose range for insulin titration?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

This is the target range for fasting BG when titrating basal insulin in T2DM patients.

**Q:** How is Total Daily Insulin (TDI) calculated in basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** 0.3-0.5 U/kg

TDI is calculated as 0.3-0.5 units per kilogram of body weight, with 40% as basal and 60% as bolus.

**Q:** In a basal-bolus regimen, what percentage of TDI is allocated to basal insulin?

**A:** 40% of TDI

Basal insulin comprises 40% of total daily insulin in a standard basal-bolus regimen.

**Q:** In a basal-bolus regimen, what percentage of TDI is allocated to bolus (prandial) insulin?

**A:** 60% of TDI (20% per meal)

Bolus insulin is 60% of TDI, typically divided as 10% of TDI (or 10U per meal from 50U example) before each meal.

**Q:** What is the correction factor (CF) formula for insulin?

**A:** CF = 100/Total Daily Dose (TDD)

The correction factor represents the change in blood glucose (mmol/L) per unit of insulin administered.

**Q:** For blood glucose 8-10 mmol/L with CF=2, how many additional units of insulin are given?

**A:** 1 additional unit

BG between 8 and (8+CF) = give 1 additional unit. With CF=2, range is 8-10 mmol/L.

**Q:** For blood glucose 10-12 mmol/L with CF=2, how many additional units of insulin are given?

**A:** 2 additional units

BG between (8+CF) and (8+2CF) = give 2 additional units. With CF=2, range is 10-12 mmol/L.

**Q:** When should insulin secretagogues be stopped in basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** When bolus (prandial) insulin is added

Insulin secretagogues are typically stopped when bolus insulin is initiated, while metformin is continued.

**Q:** What is the major risk if insulin pump is disconnected or malfunctions?

**A:** Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)

Risk of DKA exists if pump is inadvertently disconnected or malfunctions due to lack of basal insulin delivery.

**Q:** DKA typically occurs in which type of diabetes?

**A:** Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

DKA usually occurs in T1DM due to absolute insulin deficiency, but can also occur in T2DM under stress.

**Q:** HHS typically occurs in which type of diabetes?

**A:** Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State occurs predominantly in T2DM, though can rarely occur in T1DM.

**Q:** What is the primary pathophysiological mechanism of DKA?

**A:** Insulin deficiency with increased counterregulatory hormones

Lack of insulin + elevated glucagon, cortisol, catecholamines, and GH leads to hyperglycemia, lipolysis, and ketone production.

**Q:** Why is ketosis present in DKA but not HHS?

**A:** HHS has partial/relative insulin deficiency that inhibits lipolysis

The small amount of insulin present in HHS prevents ketogenesis by inhibiting lipolysis, unlike the absolute insulin deficiency in DKA.

**Q:** Why is K+ typically normal or elevated in DKA despite total body depletion?

**A:** Shift from ICF to ECF due to lack of insulin and increased plasma osmolality

Insulin deficiency and hyperosmolality cause K+ to shift from intracellular to extracellular space, masking total body depletion.

**Q:** What is the test of choice for confirming central adrenal insufficiency?

**A:** Insulin tolerance test - insulin-induced hypoglycemia should stimulate increased GH and cortisol

If 8am cortisol is low/borderline, the insulin tolerance test assesses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis response to stress.

**Q:** What is the gold standard test for evaluating ACTH and GH reserve in suspected hypopituitarism?

**A:** Insulin tolerance test (ITT)

Insulin-induced hypoglycemia stimulates ACTH and GH release. A normal response is an increase in both cortisol and GH. Contraindicated in patients with seizures or heart disease.

**Q:** What is the fasting blood glucose target for patients on basal insulin therapy?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

This target is used to guide titration of basal insulin doses in T2DM management.

**Q:** How is Total Daily Insulin (TDI) calculated for a basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** 0.3-0.5 U/kg of body weight

For a 100kg patient, TDI would be 30-50 units total, distributed as 40% basal and 60% bolus (prandial).

**Q:** In a basal-bolus insulin regimen, what percentage of TDI is given as basal vs bolus insulin?

**A:** 40% basal, 60% bolus (prandial)

Basal insulin provides background coverage while bolus insulin covers meals. For a 100kg patient with TDI of 50U: 20U basal, 30U bolus (10U per meal).

**Q:** What is the correction factor formula for insulin?

**A:** CF = 100 / Total Daily Dose (TDD)

The correction factor represents the change in blood glucose (mmol/L) per unit of insulin. For example, if TDD is 50U, CF = 2 mmol/L per unit.

**Q:** What are the postprandial and preprandial blood glucose targets for bolus insulin adjustment?

**A:** Postprandial: 5-10 mmol/L; Preprandial for subsequent meals: 4-7 mmol/L

Bolus insulin is titrated to achieve these targets, separate from fasting glucose targets for basal insulin.

**Q:** What type of diabetes is Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) most commonly associated with?

**A:** Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

DKA occurs due to absolute insulin deficiency. It can also occur in T2DM under stress, but T1DM is the classic association.

**Q:** What type of diabetes is Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) most commonly associated with?

**A:** Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

HHS occurs in T2DM due to partial insulin deficiency sufficient to prevent ketosis but inadequate to control hyperglycemia.

**Q:** What is the key pathophysiological difference between DKA and HHS?

**A:** DKA has absolute insulin deficiency leading to ketosis; HHS has partial/relative insulin deficiency that prevents ketosis

The small amount of insulin in HHS inhibits lipolysis and ketone production, preventing metabolic acidosis despite extreme hyperglycemia.

**Q:** Why may serum potassium be normal or elevated in DKA despite total body potassium depletion?

**A:** Insulin deficiency and hyperosmolality cause potassium shift from ICF to ECF

Total body K+ is depleted due to osmotic diuresis, but serum levels appear normal or high due to extracellular shift.

**Q:** What is the potassium replacement threshold in DKA treatment?

**A:** If serum K+ <3.3 mmol/L, give 40 mEq/L K+ and hold insulin until K+ ≥3.3 mmol/L

Insulin drives K+ intracellularly, so hypokalemia must be corrected before starting insulin to prevent life-threatening arrhythmias.

**Q:** What are the advantages of insulin pump therapy (CSII)?

**A:** Improved quality of life and flexibility

CSII uses a battery-operated device delivering continuous basal rapid-acting insulin with mealtime boluses programmed by the patient.

**Q:** In insulin titration, if fasting hypoglycemia occurs, what adjustment should be made?

**A:** Reduce the dose of bedtime basal insulin

Fasting hypoglycemia suggests overnight basal insulin is too high. Daytime hypoglycemia may require reducing oral antihyperglycemic agents, especially secretagogues.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach when adding bolus insulin to a basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** Stop insulin secretagogues; continue metformin

Secretagogues (sulfonylureas) increase hypoglycemia risk when combined with bolus insulin. Metformin can be continued as it does not cause hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What are the physiological effects of cortisol?

**A:** Cortisol stimulates hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis), increases insulin resistance, increases protein catabolism, stimulates leukocytosis and lymphopenia, increases cardiac output, vascular tone, and Na+ retention, increases PTH release and urine calcium excretion

Cortisol also supports blood pressure, vasomotor tone, behavioral regulation, and immunosuppression.

**Q:** List the stimulatory effects of glucocorticoids

**A:** Stimulate hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis), increase insulin resistance in peripheral tissues, increase protein catabolism, stimulate leukocytosis and lymphopenia, increase cardiac output, vascular tone, Na+ retention, increase PTH release and urine calcium excretion

Glucocorticoids have widespread metabolic effects that increase available energy substrates.

**Q:** What are the effects of cortisol on glucose metabolism?

**A:** Stimulates hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis) and increases insulin resistance in peripheral tissues

**Q:** What effect does cortisol have on glucose metabolism?

**A:** Stimulates hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis) and increases insulin resistance in peripheral tissues

**Q:** What are the stimulatory effects of glucocorticoids?

**A:** Stimulate hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis), increase insulin resistance, increase protein catabolism, stimulate leukocytosis and lymphopenia, increase cardiac output, vascular tone, Na+ retention, increase PTH release and urine calcium excretion

Glucocorticoids also stimulate bone resorption and inhibit bone formation.

**Q:** What was the primary outcome of the 4T Trial comparing insulin regimens in T2DM?

**A:** Three-year HbA1c levels

The 4T Trial compared thrice-daily prandial, twice-daily biphasic, and once-daily basal insulin regimens in 708 T2DM patients over 3 years.

**Q:** What was a major adverse effect of intensive insulin therapy in the DCCT?

**A:** Increased severe hypoglycemia

Intensive therapy was associated with an increase in the occurrence of severe hypoglycemia compared to conventional therapy.

**Q:** In UKPDS 34, how did metformin compare to sulfonylureas/insulin in overweight T2DM?

**A:** Metformin was superior for diabetes-related endpoints, all-cause mortality, and stroke

Metformin showed risk reductions of 32% for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.002), 42% for diabetes-related death (P=0.017), and 36% for all-cause mortality (P=0.011).

**Q:** Why is metformin considered first-line therapy for overweight T2DM per UKPDS 34?

**A:** Reduces diabetes-related endpoints with less weight gain and hypoglycemia than sulfonylureas/insulin

Metformin decreases risk of diabetes-related endpoints in overweight patients with less weight gain and fewer hypoglycemic episodes compared to sulfonylureas or insulin.

**Q:** Which insulin regimen had the highest withdrawal rate in the 4T Trial?

**A:** Prandial insulin (11.7%)

There was a significant difference in withdrawal rates: 5.1% biphasic, 11.7% prandial, and 8.5% basal regimens (P=0.04).

**Q:** What percentage of patients reached HbA1c <7.0% in the biphasic arm vs other arms in 4T Trial?

**A:** Smaller proportion in the biphasic arm

A smaller proportion of patients reached HbA1c <6.5% or <7.0% in the biphasic arm compared to prandial and basal arms.

**Q:** In UKPDS 34, what was the HbA1c difference between metformin and conventional therapy?

**A:** 7.4% vs 8.0% (0.6% lower with metformin)

Patients allocated to metformin had reduced HbA1c of 7.4% compared to 8.0% in the conventional therapy group.

**Q:** In UKPDS 34, how did metformin compare to sulfonylureas/insulin for intensive glucose control in overweight T2DM patients?

**A:** Metformin was superior to sulfonylureas or insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (p=0.0034), all-cause mortality (p=0.021), and stroke (p=0.032)

UKPDS 34 showed metformin reduced diabetes-related endpoints by 32%, diabetes-related death by 42%, and all-cause mortality by 36% in overweight T2DM patients, with less weight gain and hypoglycemia than sulfonylureas/insulin.

**Q:** Which insulin regimen in the 4T Trial had the highest rate of patient withdrawal?

**A:** Prandial insulin aspart (11.7% withdrawal rate)

The 4T Trial showed significant differences in withdrawal rates: 5.1% biphasic, 11.7% prandial, and 8.5% basal regimens (p=0.04).

**Q:** According to UKPDS 34, what were the HbA1c levels achieved with metformin vs conventional therapy?

**A:** Metformin: 7.4%; Conventional: 8.0%

Patients randomized to metformin achieved lower HbA1c (7.4% vs 8.0%) along with reduced risk of diabetes-related complications.

**Q:** In the 4T Trial comparing insulin regimens in T2DM, which insulin regimen provided the best glycemic control over 3 years?

**A:** Basal insulin detemir - it had better HbA1c control, fewer hypoglycemic events, and less weight gain compared to prandial and biphasic regimens

The 4T Trial (NEJM 2009) randomized 708 T2DM patients to thrice-daily prandial, twice-daily biphasic, or once-daily basal insulin. Basal had the least weight gain and fewest severe hypoglycemic events.

**Q:** In UKPDS 34, how did metformin compare to sulfonylurea or insulin for intensive glucose control in overweight T2DM?

**A:** Metformin was superior - it reduced risk of any diabetes-related endpoint by 32% (P=0.002), diabetes-related death by 42% (P=0.017), and all-cause mortality by 36% (P=0.011), with less weight gain and hypoglycemia

UKPDS 34 (Lancet 1998) showed metformin is preferred first-line intensive therapy for overweight T2DM patients, with better outcomes than sulfonylureas or insulin.

**Q:** What was unique about the 4T Trial's patient population regarding prior therapy?

**A:** Patients were on maximal metformin and sulfonylurea therapy, not on insulin or thiazolidinedione therapy before randomization

The 4T Trial enrolled 708 T2DM patients failing oral therapy to compare different insulin intensification strategies.

**Q:** In the DCCT, what were the two treatment arms compared?

**A:** Intensive therapy: 3+ daily insulin injections or insulin pump with BG monitoring QID and strict targets; Conventional therapy: 1-2 insulin injections daily with daily BG monitoring

The DCCT (NEJM 1993) investigated whether maintaining normal blood glucose reduces complications in T1DM.

**Q:** Why is metformin considered first-line therapy for overweight T2DM according to UKPDS 34?

**A:** It decreases risk of diabetes-related endpoints with less weight gain and hypoglycemia than sulfonylureas or insulin

Metformin was superior to sulfonylureas or insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.0034), all-cause mortality (P=0.021), and stroke (P=0.032).

**Q:** What was the HbA1c target in the intensive glucose control arms of UKPDS?

**A:** FPG <6 mmol/L

Both UKPDS 33 and UKPDS 34 used FPG <6 mmol/L as the target for intensive glucose control, compared to conventional target of <15 mmol/L without symptoms.

**Q:** In overweight T2DM patients, how does metformin compare to sulfonylureas/insulin for diabetes-related endpoints?

**A:** Metformin was superior - 32% risk reduction for any diabetes-related endpoint, 42% for diabetes-related death, 36% for all-cause mortality

UKPDS 34 showed metformin in overweight T2DM patients had better outcomes than sulfonylureas/insulin with less weight gain and hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What did UKPDS 33 find about intensive glucose control in newly diagnosed T2DM?

**A:** Intensive therapy with sulfonylurea or insulin reduced risk of any diabetes-related endpoint by 12% (P=0.029) and improved HbA1c, but did not significantly reduce diabetes-related death (10%, P=0.34) or all-cause mortality (6%, P=0.44).

UKPDS 33 randomized 3867 newly diagnosed T2DM patients to intensive treatment (target FPG <6 mmol/L) vs. conventional treatment (target FPG <15 mmol/L). Benefits were primarily microvascular, not macrovascular.

**Q:** How did metformin compare to sulfonylureas and insulin in overweight T2DM patients (UKPDS 34)?

**A:** Metformin was superior to sulfonylureas and insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.0034), all-cause mortality (P=0.021), and stroke (P=0.032), with less weight gain and hypoglycemia.

UKPDS 34 randomized 753 overweight T2DM patients to metformin vs. conventional therapy. Metformin reduced HbA1c (7.4% vs 8.0%), diabetes-related endpoints by 32% (P=0.002), diabetes-related death by 42% (P=0.017), and all-cause mortality by 36% (P=0.011).

**Q:** What was the primary outcome measure in the 4T trial?

**A:** Three-year HbA1c levels

The 4T Trial studied three insulin regimens over 3 years in 708 T2DM patients, with HbA1c as the primary outcome measure comparing prandial, biphasic, and basal insulin regimens.

**Q:** What was unique about the UKPDS 34 findings regarding different intensive therapy regimens?

**A:** Among intensive glucose control regimens, metformin was superior to sulfonylureas or insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint, all-cause mortality, and stroke

UKPDS 34 found that metformin provided better outcomes than sulfonylurea or insulin-based intensive therapy in overweight T2DM patients, with less weight gain and hypoglycemia.

**Q:** In the 4T trial, which insulin regimen had the most serious adverse effects?

**A:** Biphasic insulin (insulin aspart) had the most serious adverse effects

The 4T trial found that the biphasic insulin arm had the most serious adverse effects, while the basal arm had the fewest severe hypoglycemic events per patient year.

**Q:** What proportion of patients reached HbA1c <6.5% or <7.0% in the biphasic arm of the 4T trial?

**A:** A smaller proportion of patients reached HbA1c <6.5% or <7.0% in the biphasic arm compared to other arms

The 4T trial found that while there were no significant differences in median HbA1c between all three arms from years 1-3, the biphasic arm had a smaller proportion of patients achieving target HbA1c levels.

**Q:** What was unique about metformin in UKPDS 34 compared to sulfonylureas/insulin?

**A:** Metformin reduced risk of diabetes-related endpoints with less weight gain and hypoglycemia

UKPDS 34 showed metformin was superior to sulfonylureas or insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint, all-cause mortality, and stroke in overweight T2DM patients.

**Q:** Why is metformin preferred over sulfonylureas or insulin for intensive glucose control in overweight T2DM?

**A:** Metformin reduced diabetes-related endpoints by 32% (P=0.002), diabetes-related death by 42% (P=0.017), and all-cause mortality by 36% (P=0.011), with less weight gain and hypoglycemia than sulfonylureas/insulin.

UKPDS 34 (Lancet 1998) showed metformin was superior to sulfonylureas or insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.0034), all-cause mortality (P=0.021), and stroke (P=0.032) in overweight T2DM patients.

**Q:** In the 4T Trial, which insulin regimen had the highest rate of patient withdrawal?

**A:** Prandial insulin aspart had the highest withdrawal rate at 11.7%, compared to 8.5% for basal and 5.1% for biphasic (P=0.4).

The 4T Trial showed significant differences in withdrawal rates (P=0.04), with prandial regimen having the highest dropout rate, possibly due to the burden of thrice-daily injections.

**Q:** Define Diabetes Mellitus according to Toronto Notes 2025

**A:** A metabolic disorder characterized by the presence of hyperglycemia due to defective insulin secretion, defective insulin action, or both

DM is fundamentally a disorder of glucose metabolism due to inadequate insulin action or secretion.

**Q:** What are the major complications of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction

Diabetic retinopathy is the leading cause of blindness, and diabetic nephropathy is a major cause of renal failure.

**Q:** What is the recommended screening for T2DM in average-risk adults?

**A:** FBG or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 years old every 3 years

Standard screening recommendation is to test adults age 40+ every 3 years using fasting blood glucose or HbA1c.

**Q:** What is the advantage of HbA1C over fasting blood glucose for diabetes diagnosis?

**A:** Better predictor of CVD than FBG or 2-hour OGTT; reflects long-term glucose; convenient (any time of day); slow day-to-day variability

HbA1c provides a better measure of chronic glucose exposure and cardiovascular risk prediction.

**Q:** What is the optimal HbA1C target for diabetes management?

**A:** Usually <7%

Optimal glycemic control is generally defined as HbA1c below 7% to reduce complications.

**Q:** What is the TIDM insulin requirement estimate?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

Type 1 diabetes typically requires approximately 0.5-0.7 units of insulin per kg body weight daily.

**Q:** What is the T2DM insulin requirement estimate?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

Type 2 diabetes typically requires lower insulin doses (approximately 0.3 units/kg/day) due to residual insulin secretion.

**Q:** What non-pharmacological interventions can reduce HbA1C?

**A:** Diet and exercise can reduce HbA1C by 1-2%

Lifestyle modifications including diet and physical activity have significant impact on glycemic control.

**Q:** What is the primary conclusion of the 4T Trial regarding insulin regimens in T2DM?

**A:** Basal insulin (detemir) provides the best glycemic control over 3 years with better HbA1c, fewer hypoglycemic events, and less weight gain compared to prandial and biphasic insulin

The 4T Trial compared three insulin regimens in 708 T2DM patients on maximal metformin and sulfonylurea therapy over 3 years

**Q:** What was the main adverse effect of intensive insulin therapy in the DCCT?

**A:** Severe hypoglycemia

While intensive therapy significantly reduced microvascular complications in T1DM, it was associated with an increased occurrence of severe hypoglycemic events

**Q:** Why is metformin recommended as first-line therapy for overweight T2DM patients?

**A:** It decreases risk of diabetes-related endpoints with less weight gain and fewer hypoglycemic episodes compared to sulfonylureas or insulin

UKPDS 34 showed metformin was superior to sulfonylureas/insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.0034), all-cause mortality (P=0.021), and stroke (P=0.032)

**Q:** What was the difference in hypoglycemia rates between intensive and conventional therapy in UKPDS?

**A:** Intensive therapy (sulfonylurea or insulin) induced more hypoglycemic episodes than conventional therapy

UKPDS 33 and 34 both demonstrated that intensive glucose control with sulfonylureas or insulin increased hypoglycemia risk compared to conventional treatment

**Q:** What is the approximate prevalence of diabetes mellitus in Canadians?

**A:** Up to 10%

Diabetes is a major health concern in Canada, affecting approximately 10% of the population, with rising incidence due to obesity and sedentary lifestyle.

**Q:** What are the complications of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Blindness (leading cause of new-onset), renal dysfunction, CAD, CVD, PVD

Diabetes causes both microvascular (retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy) and macrovascular (CAD, CVD, PVD) complications.

**Q:** List 5 risk factors for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus

**A:** First-degree relative with DM, age ≥40yr, obesity (especially abdominal), HTN, hyperlipidemia, CAD, vascular disease, prior gestational diabetes, macrosomic baby >4kg, PCOS, history of IGT or IFG, member of high-risk population (Indigenous, Hispanic, Asian, African descent), certain medications (glucocorticoids, atypical antipsychotics, HAART)

T2DM has multiple modifiable and non-modifiable risk factors; screening should be individualized based on risk profile.

**Q:** List 3 risk factors for Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus

**A:** Personal or family history of autoimmune disease

T1DM is an autoimmune condition characterized by destruction of pancreatic beta cells, often associated with other autoimmune disorders.

**Q:** What is the screening test and frequency for T2DM in average-risk adults ≥40?

**A:** FBG or HbA1C every 3 years

Screening should begin at age 40 for average-risk adults using FBG or HbA1C, with more frequent testing for higher-risk individuals.

**Q:** Calculate the estimated total daily insulin requirement for a 70kg patient with T1DM

**A:** Approximately 35.5 units/day (0.5 × 70kg)

The starting estimate for total daily insulin is 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day for T1DM, divided between basal and bolus insulin.

**Q:** Calculate the estimated total daily insulin requirement for a 80kg patient with T2DM

**A:** Approximately 24 units/day (0.3 × 80kg)

T2DM patients typically start at 0.3 units/kg/day as they often have residual insulin secretion.

**Q:** What dietary modifications can reduce HbA1C?

**A:** Can reduce HbA1C by 1-2%

Medical nutrition therapy is foundational in diabetes management and can significantly improve glycemic control.

**Q:** What is the prevalence of diabetes mellitus in Canada?

**A:** Up to 10% of Canadians

Diabetes is a major health concern affecting approximately 10% of the Canadian population.

**Q:** List the risk factors for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus

**A:** First-degree relative with DM, age ≥40yr, obesity (especially abdominal), HTN, hyperlipidemia, CAD, vascular disease, prior gestational diabetes/macrosomic baby >4kg, PCOS, history of IGT or IFG, presence of DM complications, PCOS, acanthosis nigricans, psychiatric disorders, HIV, medications (glucocorticoids, atypical antipsychotics, HAART), high-risk ethnic background

T2DM has multiple modifiable and non-modifiable risk factors.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus?

**A:** Personal or family history of autoimmune disease

T1DM is an autoimmune condition with strong association with other autoimmune diseases.

**Q:** What is the recommended screening for T2DM?

**A:** FBG or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 q3yr, or at high-risk using CANRISK calculator; more frequent/earlier testing if >1 risk factor present

Screening recommendations allow for risk-stratified testing in high-risk individuals.

**Q:** What is the daily insulin requirement calculation for T1DM?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM patients typically require 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day of total insulin.

**Q:** What is the daily insulin requirement calculation for T2DM?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

T2DM patients typically require less insulin than T1DM patients.

**Q:** What dietary interventions can reduce HbA1C?

**A:** Can reduce HbA1C by 1-2%

Dietary modification is a cornerstone of diabetes management.

**Q:** What investigations are done for initial diabetes assessment?

**A:** FBG, HbA1C, fasting lipids, Cr, urine ACR, baseline ECG

Baseline investigations establish the patient's metabolic status and guide treatment.

**Q:** Define Diabetes Mellitus

**A:** Metabolic disorder characterized by hyperglycemia due to defective insulin secretion, defective insulin action, or both

Diabetes mellitus is a group of metabolic diseases characterized by chronic hyperglycemia.

**Q:** What is the recommended screening test for T2DM?

**A:** FBG (fasting blood glucose) or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 q3yr, or at high-risk using CANRISK calculator

Screening should begin earlier and be more frequent with presence of multiple risk factors.

**Q:** How much insulin is typically required for T1DM?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM patients require insulin replacement; starting dose is approximately 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day.

**Q:** How much insulin is typically required for T2DM?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

T2DM patients typically require lower insulin doses compared to T1DM due to residual insulin secretion.

**Q:** How often should HbA1C be monitored in diabetes?

**A:** Every 3 months

Regular HbA1C monitoring assesses long-term glycemic control and treatment efficacy.

**Q:** What percentage of Canadians are affected by diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Up to 10%

Diabetes is a major health concern affecting up to 10% of Canadians. The incidence of T2DM is rising due to obesity, sedentary lifestyle, and aging population.
