**A:** FBG (Fasting Blood Glucose) or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 q3yr, or earlier if high-risk using CANRISK calculator

Screening for T2DM should begin at age 40 every 3 years, or earlier and more frequently with additional risk factors.

**Q:** What is the total daily insulin requirement for Type 1 Diabetes?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

Total insulin requirement for T1DM is approximately 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day (0.507 units/kg/d as per Toronto Notes).

**Q:** What is the total daily insulin requirement for Type 2 Diabetes?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

Total insulin requirement for T2DM is approximately 0.3 units/kg/day.

**Q:** How much can dietary changes reduce HbA1C in diabetes?

**A:** 1-2%

Dietary modifications can reduce HbA1C by 1-2% in diabetic patients. Moderate weight loss (5%) also improves glycemic control.

**Q:** What is the leading cause of new-onset blindness in Canadian adults?

**A:** Diabetes Mellitus

Diabetes is the leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction in Canadian adults.

**Q:** Why is metformin considered first-line therapy for overweight patients with T2DM?

**A:** Metformin reduces HbA1c (7.4% vs 8.0%) and decreases risk of diabetes-related endpoints (32%, P=0.002), diabetes-related death (42%, P=0.017), and all-cause mortality (36%, P=0.011) with less weight gain and hypoglycemia than sulfonylureas or insulin.

UKPDS 34 (n=753 overweight patients) showed metformin was superior to sulfonylureas/insulin for any diabetes-related endpoint (P=0.0034), all-cause mortality (P=0.021), and stroke (P=0.032).

**Q:** In the DCCT, what was the major downside of intensive insulin therapy in T1DM?

**A:** Intensive therapy was associated with a significant increase in severe hypoglycemia episodes.

While intensive treatment reduced microvascular complications, the trade-off was more frequent severe hypoglycemic events requiring assistance.

**Q:** What proportion of T2DM patients in the 4T Trial reached HbA1c targets <6.5% or <7.0%?

**A:** A smaller proportion of patients in the biphasic insulin arm reached HbA1c <6.5% or <7.0% compared to the other arms.

The 4T Trial compared three insulin regimens over 3 years and found the biphasic arm had the lowest proportion achieving glycemic targets.

**Q:** Which intensive glucose control regimen was superior to sulfonylureas and insulin for stroke prevention in overweight T2DM?

**A:** Metformin was superior to sulfonylureas and insulin for stroke prevention (P=0.032) in overweight patients with T2DM.

UKPDS 34 showed metformin had multiple advantages over sulfonylureas/insulin including reduced all-cause mortality (P=0.021) and stroke (P=0.032).

**Q:** What is the screening test for T2DM in adults ≥40 years?

**A:** FBG or HbA1c every 3 years

Screening is recommended for everyone ≥40 q3 years, or earlier/more frequently with risk factors using the CANRISK calculator.

**Q:** What is the daily insulin requirement estimate for T1DM?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM typically requires 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day, while T2DM requires ~0.3 units/kg/day.

**Q:** What percentage HbA1c reduction can diet alone achieve?

**A:** 1-2%

Dietary modification can reduce HbA1c by 1-2%, and moderate weight loss (5%) improves glycemic control.

**Q:** How often should HbA1c be monitored in diabetics?

**A:** Every 3 months (q3 months)

HbA1c should be monitored every 3 months to assess long-term glycemic control.

**Q:** What are high-risk populations for Type 2 Diabetes?

**A:** Indigenous peoples, African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, or South Asian descent

Certain ethnic groups have increased risk of T2DM and should be screened earlier and more frequently.

All pregnant individuals should be screened for gestational diabetes between 24-28 weeks gestation.

Everyone age 40 or older should be screened for diabetes every 3 years using fasting blood glucose or HbA1C.

**A:** Between 24-28 weeks gestation

All pregnant individuals should be screened for gestational diabetes between 24-28 weeks gestation.

**A:** First-degree relative with DM, age ≥40yr, obesity, HTN, hyperlipidemia, CAD, vascular disease, prior gestational diabetes, macrosomic baby >4kg, PCOS, IGT/IFG, certain ethnicities (Indigenous, African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, South Asian)

T2DM is associated with metabolic syndrome, genetic predisposition, and high-risk ethnic backgrounds.

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM patients typically require 0.5-0.7 units of insulin per kg of body weight daily.

**A:** FBG or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 q3yr, or at high-risk using CANRISK calculator; more frequent/earlier testing if >1 risk factor present

Early screening allows for prevention and early intervention of diabetes complications.

**Q:** What is the initial insulin requirement calculation for T1DM?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM patients require insulin replacement, typically starting at 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day.

**Q:** What is the initial insulin requirement calculation for T2DM?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

T2DM patients typically start with lower insulin doses than T1DM due to residual insulin secretion.

**Q:** What should be included in the initial diabetes assessment?

**A:** History (symptoms of hyperglycemia, ketoacidosis, hypoglycemia), past medical history, family history, risk factors, medications, sexual function, lifestyle, physical exam (height, weight, BMI, BP, WC, fundoscopy, thyroid, cardiovascular, neurological), investigations (FBG, HbA1C, fasting lipids, Cr, urine ACR, ECG)

Comprehensive initial assessment establishes baseline and guides management.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for diabetes patients?

**A:** HbA1C q2-4mo (q3mo if at goal), fasting lipid profile annually, random urine ACR and Cr annually, foot exam (10g monofilament), retinopathy screening annually, BP at each visit, influenza/pneumococcal vaccination

Regular monitoring helps prevent and detect diabetes complications early.

**Q:** What are major complications of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction; Canadian adults with DM are twice as likely to die prematurely

Diabetes causes significant microvascular (retinopathy, nephropathy) and macrovascular (CVD) complications.

**Q:** What are risk factors for Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus?

**A:** Personal or family history of autoimmune disease

T1DM is an autoimmune condition with strong association with other autoimmune disorders and family history.

**Q:** What are risk factors for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus?

**A:** First-degree relative with DM, age ≥40yr, obesity (especially abdominal), HTN, hyperlipidemia, CAD, vascular disease, prior gestational diabetes, macrosomic baby >4kg, PCOS, IGT or IFG, high-risk ethnicity, certain medications (glucocorticoids, atypical antipsychotics, HAART)

T2DM has multiple modifiable and non-modifiable risk factors including metabolic syndrome components and genetic predisposition.

**Q:** What is the initial insulin dose calculation for Type 1 DM?

**A:** 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day

T1DM patients typically require approximately 0.5-0.7 units of insulin per kg body weight daily.

**Q:** What is the initial insulin dose calculation for Type 2 DM?

**A:** 0.3 units/kg/day

T2DM patients typically start with lower insulin doses (~0.3 units/kg/day) due to residual insulin sensitivity.

**Q:** What are the components of diabetes monitoring assessment?

**A:** Initial: FBG, HbA1c, fasting lipids, Cr, urine ACR, baseline ECG. Ongoing: HbA1c q3mo, fasting lipids annually, random urine ACR and Cr annually

Regular monitoring helps assess glycemic control and detect complications early.

**Q:** What screening tests are recommended for T2DM?

**A:** FBG (fasting blood glucose) or HbA1C in everyone ≥40 q3yr, or at high-risk using CANRISK calculator

Screening guidelines recommend testing adults ≥40 years every 3 years, with earlier/more frequent testing for high-risk individuals.

**Q:** What are causes of secondary achalasia?

**A:** Scleroderma and diabetes mellitus (DM)

Secondary achalasia (pseudoachalasia) can result from systemic diseases like scleroderma affecting esophageal smooth muscle function.

**Q:** What are the causes of esophageal motor disorders?

**A:** Idiopathic, achalasia, scleroderma, diabetes mellitus, and distal esophageal spasm (DES)

These disorders present with dysphagia to solids, liquids, or both, sometimes with chest pain. Manometry is required for definitive diagnosis.

**Q:** What are the patient-specific risk factors for surgical site infection?

**A:** Age, diabetes mellitus, steroids, immunosuppression, smoking, obesity, burns, malnutrition, other infections, traumatic wounds, radiation, and chemotherapy.

These patient factors impair wound healing and increase susceptibility to infection through various mechanisms including impaired immunity and tissue perfusion.

**Q:** GLP-1 receptor agonists (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide) have demonstrated what cardiovascular benefit beyond glycemic control in T2DM patients?

**A:** Reduced major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE: CV death, non-fatal MI, non-fatal stroke) in high-risk T2DM patients

Multiple trials (LEADER, SUSTAIN-6, REWIND, HARMONY-OUTCOMES) consistently showed GLP-1 receptor agonists reduce MACE in T2DM patients at high CV risk. Canadian Diabetes Association guidelines recommend GLP-1 RAs with proven CV benefit for T2DM patients with established CVD.

**Q:** CANVAS trial found canagliflozin (SGLT2 inhibitor) reduced cardiovascular events in T2DM but also increased risk of what serious adverse event?

**A:** Lower limb amputation (particularly at the toe/forefoot level)

CANVAS (NEJM 2017) showed canagliflozin reduced MACE but doubled amputation risk (6.3 vs. 3.4 per 1000 patient-years). This risk appears to be a class effect but is most pronounced with canagliflozin. Canadian guidelines recommend caution in patients with peripheral artery disease or prior amputations.

**Q:** EMPA-REG OUTCOME demonstrated empagliflozin's benefit in T2DM with high CV risk. What was the notable finding regarding hospitalization for heart failure?

**A:** Empagliflozin significantly reduced hospitalization for heart failure and CV death, but did not significantly reduce MI or stroke rates

EMPA-REG OUTCOME (NEJM 2015) showed empagliflozin reduced HF hospitalization and CV death by ~35%, with a specific HF benefit that appeared early in the trial. Genital infections were more common with empagliflozin. This established SGLT2 inhibitors as cardioprotective beyond glucose lowering.

**Q:** A patient with T2DM at high CV risk is asking about oral semaglutide (GLP-1 agonist) for CV protection. According to PIONEER 6, what can you tell them?

**A:** Oral semaglutide's CV risk profile was non-inferior to placebo — it demonstrated CV safety but was not shown to be superior in the PIONEER 6 trial

PIONEER 6 (NEJM 2019) was primarily a safety trial demonstrating that oral semaglutide was non-inferior to placebo for MACE in T2DM patients at high CV risk. However, subcutaneous semaglutide (SUSTAIN-6) showed superior CV outcomes, supporting injectable semaglutide for CV risk reduction.

**Q:** What skin manifestations are associated with diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Acanthosis nigricans, necrobiosis lipoidica diabeticorum, granuloma annulare, eruptive xanthomas, diabetic bullae, infections (boils, candidiasis, tinea), pruritus, diabetic foot.

Necrobiosis lipoidica diabeticorum presents as waxy yellow-orange plaques with telangiectasias on the pretibial areas. Acanthosis nigricans reflects insulin resistance. These skin findings may precede formal DM diagnosis.

**Q:** What is acanthosis nigricans and what conditions is it associated with?

**A:** Velvety hyperpigmentation of flexural skin (most commonly peri-nuchal). Associated with insulin resistance, DM, obesity, other endocrine disorders, and malignancy (especially GI adenocarcinoma).

Acanthosis nigricans is a cutaneous marker of insulin resistance. Malignant acanthosis nigricans is typically more extensive and rapidly progressive, often associated with gastric adenocarcinoma.

**Q:** What is the triad of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA)?

**A:** Hyperglycemia, ketosis (ketonemia/ketonuria), and metabolic acidosis — caused by severe insulin deficiency with counter-regulatory hormone excess.

DKA most commonly affects young patients with T1DM, though it can be the first presentation of undiagnosed T2DM. SGLT2 inhibitors can cause euglycemic DKA (normal blood glucose). Precipitating factors include infection, cardiac or mesenteric ischemia, MI, intoxication, and insulin omission.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of DKA from early to late?

**A:** Early: polyuria, polydipsia, malaise, nocturia, weight loss, abdominal pain. Late GI: anorexia, nausea, vomiting, worsening abdominal pain. Neurological: fatigue, drowsiness, stupor, coma. Respiratory: Kussmaul's respiration (deep, laboured), dyspnea (compensatory for acidosis), fruity ketotic breath.

Kussmaul breathing is a deep, sighing respiratory pattern that represents respiratory compensation for metabolic acidosis — the body attempts to blow off CO2 to increase pH. Fruity breath results from acetone (a ketone) exhalation. Altered consciousness correlates with severity of acidosis and osmolality.

**Q:** What investigations are required in DKA?

**A:** Blood work: CBC, electrolytes, Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, PO4³⁻, Cr, BUN, glucose, ketones, osmolality, AST/ALT/ALP, troponin. Urine: glucose and ketones. VBG (pH, bicarbonate, pCO2). ECG (electrolyte disturbances → dysrhythmia; MI rarely precipitant).

Potassium is particularly critical: DKA causes total body potassium depletion (cellular K+ shifts to extracellular space due to acidosis), but serum K+ may be normal or elevated initially. As insulin is given, K+ shifts intracellularly and hypokalemia can develop rapidly — continuous cardiac monitoring is required.

**Q:** How is pseudohyponatremia corrected in DKA?

**A:** Add 3 mmol/L of sodium for every 10 mmol/L of glucose above 5.5 mmol/L. This corrects for the dilutional effect of hyperglycemia on measured serum sodium.

Hyperglycemia draws water from cells into the extracellular space, diluting plasma sodium. The corrected sodium reflects the true sodium once glucose is normalized. Using uncorrected sodium overestimates hyponatremia severity and can lead to under-treatment of sodium imbalance.

**Q:** What are the key principles of initial fluid resuscitation in DKA?

**A:** Bolus of normal saline (NS), then high-rate NS infusion. Beware of overhydration and cerebral edema (especially in pediatric patients). Replace KCl 20 mEq/L only if patient is voiding, has adequate renal function, and initial K+ <5.5 mmol/L. Use cardiac monitoring if potassium is normal or low.

Fluid resuscitation is the first priority in DKA to restore intravascular volume and correct hyperosmolality. Aggressive IV fluid replacement also dilutes glucose and ketones. Pediatric patients are at higher risk of cerebral edema with rapid fluid administration — a common cause of DKA mortality in children.

**Q:** What is Whipple's triad for hypoglycemia?

**A:** 1) Low plasma glucose, 2) Symptoms suggestive of hypoglycemia, 3) Prompt resolution of symptoms when glucose is administered.

Whipple's triad is the classic diagnostic criterion for hypoglycemia. All three components must be present to confirm the diagnosis. The most common cause in the ED is excessive insulin use in the setting of poor oral intake.

**Q:** A hypoglycemic patient cannot take oral glucose and has no IV access. What is the next step?

**A:** Glucagon 1-2 mg IM, repeat in 10-20 minutes.

When neither oral glucose nor IV access is available, IM glucagon is the treatment of choice. Glucagon stimulates hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis to raise blood glucose. If IV access is later obtained, D50W 50 mL IV push is the preferred treatment.

**Q:** Why must thiamine be given before dextrose in a suspected alcoholic with hypoglycemia?

**A:** To prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy. Glucose metabolism without thiamine (vitamin B1) in thiamine-deficient patients precipitates acute Wernicke's. Thiamine 300 mg IM should be given first.

Patients with alcohol use disorder are at high risk for thiamine deficiency. Administering dextrose without thiamine drives pyruvate into a pathway that depletes remaining thiamine stores, precipitating Wernicke's encephalopathy (confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia).

**Q:** When should insulin NOT be given in DKA management?

**A:** When potassium is <3.3 mmol/L. Insulin drives K+ into cells and will worsen hypokalemia, risking fatal dysrhythmias.

Insulin therapy is critical in DKA to inhibit ketogenesis and gluconeogenesis, but administering it in the setting of hypokalemia can cause life-threatening cardiac arrhythmias. Potassium must be repleted to ≥3.3 mmol/L before starting insulin.

**Q:** At what blood glucose level should dextrose be added to IV fluids in DKA management?

**A:** When blood glucose drops below 14 mmol/L. Dextrose is added to maintain euglycemia while insulin continues to suppress ketogenesis.

Insulin must continue until ketoacidosis resolves (pH >7.3, bicarbonate >18 mEq/L, anion gap normalized), even after glucose normalizes. Adding dextrose to IVF at glucose <14 mmol/L prevents hypoglycemia while allowing continued insulin infusion.

**Q:** How does Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) differ from DKA in typical presentation?

**A:** HHS: older T2DM patients, glucose ≥33.3 mmol/L, fewer GI symptoms, more neurological deficits (mental disturbances, coma, seizures), symptoms evolving over days to weeks, larger fluid losses. Expected pH >7.3 and bicarbonate >18 mEq/L (no significant ketoacidosis).

HHS results from relative insulin deficiency causing extreme hyperglycemia and osmotic diuresis without significant ketosis. The neurological manifestations predominate due to hyperosmolarity. DKA typically has metabolic acidosis with ketosis and more GI symptoms.

**Q:** What drugs have been associated with precipitating HHS?

**A:** Diuretics, glucocorticoids, lithium, and atypical antipsychotics. HHS can also follow cardiac surgery.

Unlike DKA which has classic precipitants (infection, missed insulin), HHS has additional drug-related precipitants. Atypical antipsychotics cause insulin resistance and impair insulin secretion. This is important to recognize in patients on these medications who present with altered mental status.

**Q:** What is the role of HDL in lipid transport?

**A:** HDL transports cholesterol from peripheral tissues to the liver (reverse cholesterol transport) and acts as a reservoir for apolipoproteins.

HDL-mediated reverse cholesterol transport is cardioprotective. High HDL levels are associated with decreased CVD and mortality. LCAT (lecithin-cholesterol acyltransferase) esterifies cholesterol in HDL. HDL also receives phospholipids and gives cholesterol esters in exchange with IDL.

**Q:** What enzyme is required to activate lipoprotein lipase (LPL), and what is its significance?

**A:** ApoC-II is required to activate lipoprotein lipase. Deficiency of LPL or ApoC-II causes hereditary chylomicronemia with severe hypertriglyceridemia.

LPL degrades TG in chylomicrons and VLDL at capillary surfaces of adipose tissue and muscle. ApoC-II is an obligate cofactor. Both LPL deficiency and ApoC-II deficiency lead to familial chylomicronemia syndrome with markedly elevated TG and risk of pancreatitis.

**Q:** What is Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH), and what are its key clinical features?

**A:** FH is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by defects in LDLR (most common), PCSK9, or ApoB, leading to markedly elevated LDL cholesterol. Clinical features include tendinous xanthomata (Achilles, patellar, extensor tendons of hand), arcus cornealis, and xanthelasmata.

FH heterozygotes have 50% risk of MI in men by age 30. FH homozygotes manifest CAD and vascular disease early in childhood (can be fatal before age 20). Risk calculators (Framingham, SCORE) do not apply to FH patients — all adults with FH should be considered 'high-risk.'

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for Familial Hypercholesterolemia?

**A:** 1st line: maximally tolerated statin. 2nd line: add ezetimibe and/or PCSK9 inhibitor. 3rd line (homozygotes): portacaval shunt, LDL apheresis, or potential liver transplant.

FH homozygotes have extremely high LDL levels and often require LDL apheresis due to lack of functional LDL receptors in the liver. PCSK9 inhibitors work by blocking PCSK9-mediated LDLR degradation, increasing hepatic LDL clearance. Drug-resistant cases should be referred to a lipid specialist.

**Q:** What are the key features of Familial Combined Hyperlipidemia?

**A:** Elevated ApoB, elevated TG, and elevated LDL (multiple phenotypes). Caused by increased hepatic production of ApoB-100-containing lipoproteins. Associated with premature coronary heart disease, xanthelasma, and obesity.

Familial combined hyperlipidemia (FCHL) is the most common primary dyslipidemia causing premature CAD. Unlike FH, it can present with elevated LDL, elevated TG, or both. Treatment: statin 1st; add ezetimibe/PCSK9 inhibitor if LDL not controlled; fibrate if TG >10 mmol/L.

**Q:** What is Tangier Disease and what are its characteristic clinical features?

**A:** Tangier Disease is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by ABCA1 gene mutations, resulting in very low HDL cholesterol. Characteristic features include enlarged orange-coloured tonsils, neuropathy, splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, corneal clouding, mild hypertriglyceridemia, and premature atherosclerosis.

ABCA1 is essential for HDL-mediated cholesterol efflux from macrophages. Without it, cholesterol accumulates in tissues (hence the orange tonsils from cholesterol-laden macrophages). Treatment focuses on lifestyle modifications and managing coexisting lipid abnormalities to reduce atherosclerosis risk.

**Q:** What are the secondary causes of hypertriglyceridemia?

**A:** Endocrine: obesity/metabolic syndrome, DM. Renal: nephrotic syndrome, CKD. Drugs: corticosteroids, estrogen, hydrochlorothiazide, retinoic acid, beta-blockers (without ISA), antiretrovirals, atypical antipsychotics, OCPs. Lifestyle: alcohol, high carbohydrate/high fat diet. Other: pregnancy.

Secondary causes of dyslipidemia are much more common than primary genetic causes. Identifying and treating the underlying cause is essential. Insulin resistance (obesity, DM) promotes excess VLDL production by the liver, leading to hypertriglyceridemia.

**Q:** What are the secondary causes of hypercholesterolemia?

**A:** Endocrine: hypothyroidism. Renal: nephrotic syndrome, CKD. Immunologic: monoclonal gammopathy. Hepatic: cholestatic liver disease (e.g. primary biliary cirrhosis). Nutritional: anorexia nervosa. Drugs: cyclosporin, carbamazepine, steroids. Lifestyle: smoking, obesity.

Hypothyroidism causes elevated LDL due to decreased LDL receptor activity and reduced bile acid synthesis. Nephrotic syndrome causes elevated LDL due to increased hepatic lipoprotein synthesis in response to hypoalbuminemia. Treating the underlying condition is the first step.

**Q:** What is the LDL-lowering effect of statins, ezetimibe, and PCSK9 inhibitors?

**A:** Statins lower LDL by ~30-40%. Ezetimibe lowers LDL by ~18%. PCSK9 inhibitors lower LDL by ~50%. Each 1.0 mmol/L decrease in LDL corresponds to approximately 20-25% relative risk reduction in cardiovascular disease. Doubling the statin dose only adds ~6% further LDL reduction.

The rule of 6: each doubling of statin dose provides approximately a 6% incremental reduction in LDL beyond the initial response. This is why combining agents (ezetimibe, PCSK9 inhibitors) is preferred over simply escalating statin dose for patients who need greater LDL reduction.

**Q:** According to CCS 2021 guidelines, when should lipid-lowering therapy be intensified with ezetimibe and/or PCSK9 inhibitors?

**A:** In patients with established ASCVD who have LDL-C ≥1.8 mmol/L (or non-HDL cholesterol ≥2.4 mmol/L or ApoB >0.7 g/L) despite maximally tolerated statin therapy.

Patients with clinical atherosclerosis, AAA, LDL-C ≥5.0 mmol/L, and most with diabetes or CKD should be started on statin therapy. For ASCVD patients not at LDL target on max statin, add ezetimibe first, then PCSK9 inhibitor if still above target.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for pre-diabetes (impaired glucose tolerance/impaired fasting glucose) per Diabetes Canada 2018?

**A:** IFG: fasting plasma glucose 6.1–6.9 mmol/L. IGT: 2h 75g OGTT 7.8–11.0 mmol/L. A1C: 6.0–6.4%.

Pre-diabetes carries significant cardiovascular risk. Approximately 15% per year progress to diabetes; 50-80% revert to normal glucose tolerance. Lifestyle modifications reduce progression by 58%; metformin reduces it by 31%. Weight loss of 5-10% of body weight can improve glucose tolerance.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for diabetes mellitus per Diabetes Canada 2018?

**A:** Any ONE of: FPG ≥7.0 mmol/L (fasting = no caloric intake ≥8h); A1C ≥6.5% (adults only; not for T1DM, children, adolescents, or pregnant women); 2h PG in 75g OGTT ≥11.1 mmol/L; Random PG ≥11.1 mmol/L. In the ABSENCE of hyperglycemia symptoms, a confirmatory test on another day is required.

If the patient has hyperglycemia symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia, polyphagia, weight loss, blurry vision), a single abnormal test is sufficient. A1C is not used to diagnose T1DM, in children/adolescents, or in pregnant women, as factors like hemolysis or hemoglobinopathies can affect A1C accuracy.

**Q:** What are the main differences between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus?

**A:** T1DM: onset usually <30 yr, 5-10% of DM, autoimmune/idiopathic beta-cell destruction, absolute insulin deficiency, HLA DR3/DR4 association, islet cell antibodies in 60-85%, normal to thin body habitus, treated with insulin. T2DM: onset usually >40 yr, 90% of DM, multifactorial insulin resistance + secretion impairment, treated with lifestyle + non-insulin agents ± insulin.

T1DM monozygotic twin concordance is 30-40%; T2DM is 70-90%, reflecting greater heritability. The most common autoantibody in T1DM is against glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD). T2DM involves the 'ominous octet': impaired insulin secretion, enhanced glucagon, increased hepatic glucose production, decreased incretin effect, increased lipolysis, decreased muscle glucose uptake, increased renal reabsorption, neurotransmitter dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the causes of diabetes mellitus due to diseases of the exocrine pancreas?

**A:** Pancreatitis, pancreatectomy, neoplasia, cystic fibrosis, and hemochromatosis ('bronze diabetes').

These represent Type 3c diabetes (pancreatogenic/exocrine pancreas disease-related). Hemochromatosis causes iron deposition in the pancreas (and liver, heart, gonads, skin), leading to 'bronze diabetes.' Cystic fibrosis-related diabetes (CFRD) is increasingly common as CF patients live longer.

**Q:** Which endocrinopathies can cause secondary diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Acromegaly (excess GH causes insulin resistance), Cushing's syndrome (cortisol causes insulin resistance), glucagonoma (excess glucagon promotes gluconeogenesis), pheochromocytoma (catecholamines inhibit insulin secretion), and hyperthyroidism (accelerated glucose metabolism and insulin resistance).

Counter-regulatory hormones (GH, cortisol, glucagon, catecholamines, thyroid hormone) all antagonize insulin action. Treatment of the underlying endocrinopathy may improve glycemic control. Recognition is important as these conditions may be curable (e.g., surgical resection of an adrenal adenoma in Cushing's).

**Q:** What infections are associated with causing diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Congenital rubella, cytomegalovirus (CMV), and coxsackievirus are associated with causing diabetes mellitus (Type 1 pathogenesis).

Viral infections can trigger autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells in genetically susceptible individuals. Molecular mimicry (viral antigens resembling islet cell proteins) is one proposed mechanism. These are categorized under the 'Other Specific Causes' of DM in the etiologic classification.

**Q:** What is the honeymoon period in Type 1 Diabetes?

**A:** After initial T1DM presentation, a honeymoon period often occurs where glycemic control can be achieved with little or no insulin as residual beta cells are still able to produce insulin. Once these cells are destroyed, complete insulin deficiency results.

The honeymoon period (partial remission phase) typically lasts weeks to months. During this time, insulin requirements are very low. It is important to continue some insulin therapy during this phase to preserve remaining beta cells and avoid ketoacidosis. Eventually, progressive autoimmune destruction leads to absolute insulin deficiency.

**Q:** What is the A1C target for most adults with T1DM or T2DM, and when might a higher or lower target be appropriate?

**A:** Target A1C <7.0% for most patients. Lower target <6.5% may be set to further reduce nephropathy/retinopathy risk if no significant hypoglycemia. Higher targets 7.1-8.5% are appropriate for limited life expectancy, greater functional dependency, or history of recurrent severe hypoglycemia.

A1C reflects average glucose over ~3 months and should be measured every 3-6 months. Targets must be personalized. More intensive control (A1C <6.0%) may cause harm (ACCORD trial). A1C can be falsely elevated in iron/B12 deficiency, alcoholism, CKD; falsely lowered in chronic liver disease, reticulocytosis, ASA/vitamin C use.

**Q:** What are the time-in-range targets for glucose monitoring in diabetes?

**A:** Time in range (glucose 3.9-10 mmol/L): >70%. Time above range: <25%. Time below range (hypoglycemia): <4%.

Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) provides time-in-range metrics that supplement A1C. Time-in-range has been associated with reduced risk of microvascular complications. CGM devices can be recommended for some people with diabetes to optimize self-management.

**Q:** What are the nutritional recommendations for patients with diabetes?

**A:** 45-60% carbohydrates, 15-20% protein, <35% fats as a percentage of total daily energy. <7% saturated fats and <10% polyunsaturated fats. Replace high-glycemic-index with low-glycemic-index carbohydrates. Increase fibre. Limit sodium, alcohol, caffeine. Nutritional therapy can reduce A1C by 1-2%.

In T1DM, carbohydrate counting is used to determine prandial insulin dosing. In T2DM, weight reduction is the primary dietary goal to improve insulin sensitivity and glycemic control. Personalized dietary patterns based on values and preferences are encouraged by Diabetes Canada guidelines.

**Q:** What are the exercise recommendations for T2DM?

**A:** At least 150 minutes per week of moderate-vigorous aerobic exercise and at least 2 sessions per week of resistance exercise.

Regular physical exercise improves insulin sensitivity, lowers lipid concentrations, and helps control blood pressure. Losing 5-10% of initial body weight can improve insulin sensitivity, glycemic control, hypertension, and dyslipidemia. Smoking cessation and psychosocial support are also important components of T2DM management.

**Q:** When should pharmacologic therapy be initiated in T2DM?

**A:** Initiate non-insulin antihyperglycemic therapy within 3 months if lifestyle management does not achieve adequate glycemic control. If initial A1C is more than 1.5% above the patient's target, start pharmacologic therapy immediately and consider combination therapy. If presenting in metabolic decompensation, start insulin + metformin.

The 'treat to target' approach requires adjusting therapy in a timely fashion to achieve target A1C within 3-6 months of diagnosis. Metformin remains the preferred initial agent due to high efficacy, low hypoglycemia risk, weight-neutral or weight-lowering effect, low cost, and cardiovascular safety.

**Q:** What are the key properties of metformin as initial therapy for T2DM?

**A:** Metformin: high A1C-lowering efficacy, low hypoglycemia risk, weight neutral/loss effect, low cost. Main side effect: lactic acidosis (rare). Should be held when patient is ill and unable to maintain fluid intake or has acute decline in renal function (part of 'SAD MANS' medications to hold when sick).

SAD MANS = Sulfonylureas, ACEIs, Diuretics/direct renin inhibitors, Metformin, ARBs, NSAIDs, SGLT2i — medications to hold on sick days to prevent acute kidney injury, electrolyte disturbances, and lactic acidosis. Metformin is contraindicated or should be dose-adjusted in significant renal impairment.

**Q:** What is the 'SAD MANS' mnemonic for sick day medication management in diabetes?

**A:** SAD MANS = Sulfonylureas, ACEIs, Diuretics and direct renin inhibitors, Metformin, ARBs, NSAIDs, SGLT2i. These should be held when a diabetic patient is ill, unable to maintain fluid intake, or has acute renal function decline.

Sick day management is critical in diabetes. Holding these medications prevents acute kidney injury (NSAIDs, ACEIs, ARBs, diuretics), lactic acidosis (metformin), hypoglycemia (sulfonylureas), euglycemic DKA (SGLT2i), and electrolyte disturbances. Blood glucose should be monitored more frequently when ill.

**Q:** What are the 8 I's that precipitate diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA)?

**A:** The 8 I's precipitating DKA: Infection, Ischemia or Infarction, Iatrogenic (glucocorticoids), Intoxication, Insulin missed, Inflammation (pancreatitis, cholecystitis), Intraoperative/perioperative stress, Infant (pregnancy-related).

DKA occurs primarily in T1DM but can also occur in T2DM (especially with SGLT2i). Identifying and treating the precipitating cause is essential alongside insulin and fluid resuscitation. The most common precipitant is infection. Missed insulin is preventable through patient education.

**Q:** What are the second-line add-on therapy options for T2DM, and what factors guide selection?

**A:** Options include: SGLT2 inhibitors, GLP-1 receptor agonists, DPP-4 inhibitors, sulfonylureas, insulin (usually basal), thiazolidinediones, and other agents. Selection is guided by: CV risk, kidney disease, heart failure, GFR, glucose-lowering effectiveness, hypoglycemia risk, and effect on body weight.

SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists have demonstrated cardiovascular and renal protective benefits beyond glucose lowering in high-risk patients. Sulfonylureas have the highest efficacy but carry hypoglycemia risk and weight gain. Thiazolidinediones cause edema, heart failure risk, and fractures. Cost also varies significantly between agents.

**Q:** What is the onset, peak, and duration of rapid-acting insulin analogues (e.g., aspart, lispro, glulisine)?

**A:** Onset: 10-15 minutes. Peak: 1-1.5 hours. Duration: 3.5-4.75 hours. Insulin faster aspart (Fiasp) has faster onset of ~4 minutes.

Rapid-acting analogues are given just before or at mealtime (prandial/bolus insulin). They mimic the normal physiological insulin spike after meals. Their faster onset compared to regular insulin (30 min onset, 2-3h peak, 6.5h duration) allows more flexible meal timing.

**Q:** What are the long-acting basal insulin analogues and their durations?

**A:** Insulin detemir (Levemir): 16-24 hours. Insulin glargine 100 units/mL (Lantus/Basaglar): 24 hours. Insulin glargine 300 units/mL (Toujeo): >30 hours. Insulin degludec (Tresiba): ~42 hours.

Long-acting insulins have no pronounced peak, providing stable background insulin coverage. Glargine 300 and degludec have lower hypoglycemia risk due to flatter profiles. Detemir may require twice-daily dosing. These basal insulins are used in both T1DM (with bolus) and T2DM (with oral agents initially).

**Q:** What is the basal-bolus insulin regimen for T1DM?

**A:** Estimated total insulin requirement: 0.5-0.7 units/kg/day. 40% given as basal insulin at bedtime. 20% given as bolus insulin before each of breakfast, lunch, and dinner (i.e., 60% total as bolus, split equally between 3 meals).

Basal-bolus (multiple daily injections, MDI) is the standard regimen for T1DM. Alternatively, a continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII/insulin pump) can be used. When paired with continuous glucose monitoring, automated insulin delivery ('artificial pancreas') significantly improves time-in-range.

**Q:** How do you titrate insulin for hyperglycemia readings throughout the day?

**A:** High AM sugar → increase bedtime basal insulin. High lunch sugar → increase AM rapid/regular insulin. High supper sugar → increase lunch rapid/regular insulin OR increase AM basal insulin. High bedtime sugar → increase supper rapid/regular insulin.

Pattern management of blood glucose requires systematic identification of which insulin dose is responsible for which time period. AM glucose reflects overnight basal control; post-meal readings reflect bolus coverage. This logical approach helps avoid overcorrecting with incorrect insulin type or timing.

**Q:** What genetic syndromes are associated with development of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Down's syndrome (trisomy 21), Klinefelter's syndrome (47,XXY), and Turner's syndrome (45,XO) are all associated with increased risk of diabetes mellitus.

These chromosomal abnormalities are categorized under 'genetic syndromes associated with DM.' Down syndrome is associated with both T1DM and T2DM. Klinefelter syndrome is associated with hypogonadism and metabolic syndrome increasing T2DM risk. Turner syndrome is associated with autoimmune T1DM risk.

**Q:** What are the glycemic targets for patients with diabetes as per Diabetes Canada guidelines?

**A:** Fasting/preprandial plasma glucose: 4-7 mmol/L. 2h post-prandial glucose: 5-10 mmol/L (5-8 mmol/L if A1C not at target and can be safely achieved). LDL-C: <2.0 mmol/L or 50% reduction. Blood pressure: <130/80 mmHg.

These comprehensive targets reflect the multifactorial nature of diabetes management — not just glucose but also lipid and BP control are critical. The combination of glycemic control, BP management, and statin therapy has additive benefits in reducing microvascular and macrovascular complications.

**Q:** What are the autoantibodies associated with Type 1 Diabetes?

**A:** Islet cell antibodies present in up to 60-85% of T1DM patients. Most common is anti-glutamic acid decarboxylase (anti-GAD). Up to 60% have antibodies against insulin. Pancreatic islet autoantibodies (Stages 1-2) can be detected as a subclinical prodrome in first- and second-degree relatives.

Screening first-degree relatives for islet autoantibodies can identify pre-symptomatic T1DM (Stage 1: two or more autoantibodies, normoglycemia; Stage 2: dysglycemia; Stage 3: symptomatic). LADA (latent autoimmune diabetes in adults) is classified under T1DM and presents with slow autoimmune beta-cell destruction.

**Q:** What is icosapent ethyl (Vascepa) and in what clinical context is it used in Canada?

**A:** Icosapent ethyl is a purified EPA (eicosapentaenoic acid) omega-3 fatty acid used to decrease cardiovascular event risk in patients with ASCVD or with diabetes plus ≥1 CVD risk factor who have TG 1.5-5.6 mmol/L even on maximally tolerated statin therapy (per CCS 2021 guidelines).

The REDUCE-IT trial demonstrated significant CV event reduction with icosapent ethyl. A substudy showed it had minimal effect on Lp(a), homocysteine, oxidized LDL, interleukin-6, and hs-CRP, while the mineral oil comparator increased these biomarkers — suggesting the mineral oil may have attenuated the true benefit seen in REDUCE-IT.

**Q:** What is Maturity-Onset Diabetes of the Young (MODY)?

**A:** MODY (also known as monogenic diabetes) is a type of diabetes caused by genetic defects in beta-cell function (category III of DM etiologic classification). It presents typically in young people, is autosomal dominant, and mimics either T1DM or T2DM depending on the subtype.

MODY is often misdiagnosed as T1DM or T2DM. Key features: strong family history (autosomal dominant), onset usually <25 years, non-obese, absent islet autoantibodies. Different MODY subtypes have different genes affected (e.g., MODY2: GCK mutations; MODY3: HNF1A). Genetic testing is required for definitive diagnosis.

**Q:** What was the primary conclusion of the 4T Trial (ATTrial) regarding insulin regimens in T2DM?

**A:** The basal insulin (detemir) regimen provided the best glycemic control over 3 years, with fewer severe hypoglycemic events and less weight gain compared to prandial or biphasic insulin regimens.

The 4T Trial compared thrice-daily prandial aspart, twice-daily biphasic aspart, and once-daily basal detemir over 3 years. Despite no significant difference in median HbA1c between arms, basal insulin had the fewest hypoglycemic events and least weight gain, making it the preferred starting insulin regimen.

**Q:** What did the ACCORD Blood Pressure Trial conclude about intensive BP control (target <120 mmHg) in T2DM patients with CV risk factors?

**A:** Intensive BP lowering to <120 mmHg (vs. 140 mmHg) in T2DM patients with CV risk factors did NOT reduce major CV events (composite nonfatal MI, nonfatal stroke, CV death), but did significantly reduce stroke rates. It increased adverse events including bradycardia/arrhythmia and hyperkalemia.

The ACCORD BP trial (n=4733) found no significant difference in the composite primary outcome with intensive vs. standard BP control. However, stroke rates were significantly reduced (0.32%/yr vs. 0.53%/yr). Intensive therapy required more medications (mean 3.4 vs. 2.1) and caused more serious adverse events.

**Q:** What did the ACCORD Lipid Trial conclude about adding fibrate therapy to statin therapy in T2DM?

**A:** Adding fibrate therapy to statin therapy in T2DM did NOT reduce major cardiovascular events (nonfatal MI, nonfatal stroke, or CV death).

The ACCORD Lipid Trial (n=5518, mean follow-up 4.7 years) found no significant differences in the primary composite outcome, all MI, all stroke, or all-cause mortality between statin + fibrate vs. statin alone. This does not support routine combination therapy for CV risk reduction in T2DM.

**Q:** What were the key findings of the DCCT trial regarding intensive insulin therapy in T1DM?

**A:** Intensive therapy (≥3 daily injections or insulin pump with QID BG monitoring) significantly reduced retinopathy development/progression, microalbuminuria, albuminuria, and clinical neuropathy compared to conventional therapy (1-2 injections/day). It increased severe hypoglycemia.

The DCCT trial (n=1441 T1DM patients) demonstrated that tight glycemic control reduces microvascular complications. This established the evidence base for intensive insulin therapy in T1DM. The tradeoff is increased risk of severe hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What did the EDIC study (post-DCCT follow-up) show about the long-term cardiovascular benefits of intensive therapy in T1DM?

**A:** Over 17-year follow-up, intensive therapy reduced the risk of any cardiovascular event by 42% and nonfatal MI, stroke, or CV death by 57% compared to conventional therapy.

The EDIC follow-up of DCCT patients demonstrated 'metabolic memory' — the CV protective effects of intensive glycemic control during the DCCT persisted long after glycemic differences were equalized. This supports early intensive glucose control in T1DM for both micro- and macrovascular benefit.

**Q:** What did UKPDS 33 demonstrate about intensive glucose control with sulfonylurea or insulin in newly diagnosed T2DM?

**A:** Intensive glucose control (target FPG <6 mmol/L) reduced microvascular complications by 25% and any diabetes-related endpoint by 12%, but did NOT significantly reduce macrovascular events or all-cause mortality. It increased hypoglycemia and weight gain.

UKPDS 33 (n=3867 newly diagnosed T2DM) established that intensive glycemic control primarily benefits microvascular outcomes. The lack of macrovascular benefit with sulfonylurea/insulin (unlike metformin in UKPDS 34) guided subsequent treatment algorithms.

**Q:** What did UKPDS 34 show about metformin as first-line therapy in overweight T2DM patients?

**A:** Metformin reduced any diabetes-related endpoint by 32%, diabetes-related death by 42%, and all-cause mortality by 36% in overweight T2DM. It outperformed sulfonylureas and insulin for diabetes-related endpoints, all-cause mortality, and stroke, with less weight gain and hypoglycemia.

UKPDS 34 established metformin as the preferred first-line agent in overweight T2DM, as it provided both microvascular AND macrovascular benefits — unlike sulfonylurea/insulin in UKPDS 33. This remains foundational to Canadian Diabetes guidelines recommending metformin as first-line therapy.

**Q:** What is the key concept of 'legacy effect' or 'metabolic memory' demonstrated by the UKPDS Extension (10-year follow-up)?

**A:** Despite early loss of between-group HbA1c differences after the trial ended, sustained reductions in microvascular risk and emergent risk reductions for MI and all-cause mortality persisted in both the sulfonylurea/insulin and metformin groups over 10 years of post-trial follow-up.

The UKPDS Extension showed that early intensive glucose control produces durable benefits even after glycemic equalization — the 'legacy effect.' The metformin group showed 33% reduction in MI and 27% reduction in all-cause mortality at 10 years. This supports early glycemic optimization.

**Q:** What did the Steno-2 study demonstrate about multifactorial intervention in T2DM with microalbuminuria?

**A:** Intensified, target-driven multifactorial therapy (combination of medications and focused behavior modification) significantly reduced all-cause mortality, cardiovascular mortality, cardiovascular events, progression to end-stage renal disease, and rates of retinal photocoagulation compared to conventional treatment.

Steno-2 (n=160 T2DM patients with microalbuminuria) demonstrated that addressing multiple risk factors simultaneously (BP, lipids, glucose, lifestyle) yields greater benefits than treating each factor in isolation. This supports comprehensive risk factor management in high-risk T2DM.

**Q:** What did the FIELD study show about fenofibrate in T2DM patients?

**A:** Fenofibrate in T2DM patients not on statin therapy significantly reduced non-fatal MI (HR 0.76) and total cardiovascular events (HR 0.89), but did NOT significantly reduce coronary events or CHD mortality. Overall, it did not significantly reduce the primary coronary event endpoint.

FIELD (n=9,795 T2DM patients) showed modest benefit of fenofibrate for total CV events but failed the primary coronary endpoint. Combined with the neutral ACCORD Lipid result, this does not support routine fibrate use for primary CV risk reduction in T2DM, especially when added to statin therapy.

**Q:** What additional cardiovascular and renal outcomes trials are relevant for GLP-1 agonists in T2DM (list key trials)?

**A:** Key GLP-1 agonist CV outcome trials: REWIND (dulaglutide, Lancet 2019), SUSTAIN-6 (semaglutide, NEJM 2016), PIONEER 6 (oral semaglutide, NEJM 2019), HARMONY-OUTCOMES (albiglutide, Lancet 2018), AMPLITUDE-O (efpeglenatide, NEJM 2021). SELECT trial (semaglutide in obesity without diabetes, NEJM 2023) showed CV benefit even without T2DM.

Multiple GLP-1 receptor agonists have demonstrated cardiovascular benefits beyond glucose lowering in T2DM. The SELECT trial established that semaglutide reduces major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) in obese patients without T2DM, expanding the indication for weight management with CV benefit.

**Q:** What is the Canadian screening recommendation for Type 2 diabetes in adults without risk factors?

**A:** Screen all adults ≥40 years with FBG or HbA1C every 3 years. Use the CANRISK calculator to identify high-risk individuals who may need earlier/more frequent testing.

Diabetes Canada recommends population-level screening starting at age 40 every 3 years. More frequent/earlier testing is required if ≥1 risk factor is present. The CANRISK calculator assesses individual risk.

**Q:** What are the key risk factors for Type 2 diabetes in Canada?

**A:** First-degree relative with DM; age ≥40; obesity (especially abdominal); HTN, hyperlipidemia, CAD, vascular disease; prior gestational DM or macrosomic baby (>4 kg); PCOS; history of IGT or IFG; acanthosis nigricans; medications (glucocorticoids, atypical antipsychotics, HAART); high-risk populations (Indigenous, Hispanic, Asian, African descent).

Indigenous Canadians have particularly high rates of T2DM — 1 in 5 Indigenous people has diabetes. Risk factors reflect both metabolic predispositions and social determinants of health.

**Q:** What are the advantages and disadvantages of HbA1C as a screening/monitoring tool for diabetes?

**A:** Advantages: convenient (any time of day), single sample, predicts microvascular complications, better CVD predictor than FBG, low day-to-day variability, reflects long-term glucose. Disadvantages: costly, misleading in hemoglobinopathies/iron deficiency/hemolytic anemia/severe hepatic or renal disease, altered by ethnicity and aging, NOT for diagnosis in children/adolescents, pregnant women, CF, or suspected T1DM.

HbA1C reflects average blood glucose over ~3 months (RBC lifespan). Its accuracy is compromised in conditions affecting RBC turnover or structure. Standardized, validated assays are required.

**Q:** What are the SMART goals for diabetes management in Canada?

**A:** A1C: <7% (optimal glycemic control); BP: <130/80 mmHg; Cholesterol: LDL-C <2 mmol/L if treating; Drugs: consider ACEi/ARB, Statin, and ASA; Exercise/Eating; Smoking Cessation.

The SMART mnemonic captures the multifactorial targets in diabetes management. Achieving multiple targets simultaneously reduces microvascular and macrovascular complications more effectively than glycemic control alone.

**Q:** What is the initial pharmacologic approach for a newly diagnosed T2DM patient with HbA1C >1.5% above target?

**A:** Start metformin PLUS a second agent (rather than metformin alone). If symptomatic hyperglycemia or metabolic decompensation is present, start insulin + metformin.

Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend more aggressive initial therapy when HbA1C is substantially above target. Dual therapy from the start speeds attainment of glycemic goals and reduces time-to-target.

**Q:** Which T2DM pharmacologic agents have demonstrated cardiorenal benefits and when should they be prioritized?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin) and GLP-1 receptor agonists (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide) have cardiorenal benefits. Prioritize SGLT2i for patients with atherosclerotic CVD, CKD, or heart failure (especially with reduced ejection fraction). GLP-1 RA preferred if ASCVD with MACE risk or weight loss needed.

Per 2020 Diabetes Canada CPG, SGLT2i are the preferred add-on for HF and CKD. GLP-1 RAs are preferred for ASCVD with prominent MACE risk. Both classes reduce CV mortality beyond glucose lowering.

**Q:** When should an ACEi/ARB be used in a diabetic patient?

**A:** Use ACEi or ARB in DM patients with: clinical macrovascular disease; age ≥55; OR age <55 with microvascular complications.

ACEi/ARBs reduce progression of diabetic nephropathy and provide cardiovascular protection. They are recommended regardless of blood pressure status when the above criteria are met.

**Q:** When should a statin be prescribed in a diabetic patient?

**A:** Prescribe statin if: clinical macrovascular disease; age ≥40; OR age <40 with diabetes duration >15 yr and age >30 yr, microvascular complications, or other CV risk factors.

Statins reduce cardiovascular events in diabetic patients. The age-based thresholds reflect increasing absolute CV risk with advancing age and disease duration.

**Q:** What are the non-pharmacologic management strategies for T2DM?

**A:** Diet: reduce HbA1C by 1-2%, moderate weight loss (5%) improves glycemic control, see registered dietician, decrease saturated + trans fats to <10% calories, avoid simple sugars, low glycemic index foods. Physical activity: ≥150 min aerobic exercise + 2 resistance training sessions/week; encourage 30-45 min moderate exercise 4-7 days/week.

Lifestyle interventions are the cornerstone of T2DM management. Even modest weight loss of 5% meaningfully improves glycemic control and cardiovascular risk. Exercise increases insulin sensitivity and lowers BP.

**Q:** What is the definition of T2DM remission and what weight loss is required?

**A:** T2DM remission: achieving prediabetes or normal HbA1C levels without antihyperglycemic medications for a minimum of 3 months. Sustained weight loss of ≥15 kg from initial body weight is associated with the greatest probability of remission.

Remission is most achievable in early T2DM in overweight/obese patients with motivation for weight loss. It is not appropriate for patients with significant eating disorders, mental health conditions, atherosclerotic CVD, CHF, or CKD.

**Q:** List the oral pharmacologic agents used for T2DM.

**A:** Biguanides (metformin); Thiazolidinediones (pioglitazone, rosiglitazone); α-glucosidase inhibitors (acarbose); Non-sulfonylurea insulin secretagogues (repaglinide); Sulfonylureas (glyburide, glimepiride, gliclazide); DPP-4 inhibitors (sitagliptin, linagliptin); SGLT2 inhibitors (canagliflozin, dapagliflozin, empagliflozin).

Each class has different mechanisms, side effect profiles, and cardiorenal implications. Metformin remains first-line. SGLT2i and GLP-1 RAs have added cardiorenal benefits beyond glucose lowering.

**Q:** What is the recommended physical activity target for type 2 diabetic patients?

**A:** At least 150 minutes of aerobic exercise per week plus 2 sessions of resistance training per week. Encourage 30-45 minutes of moderate exercise 4-7 days/week. Promotes cardiovascular fitness, increases insulin sensitivity, lowers BP, and improves lipid profile.

Exercise is a cornerstone of T2DM management with effects on multiple risk factors. Patients using insulin may need to adjust diet, insulin regimen, injection sites, and blood glucose monitoring around exercise.

## Thyroid Disorders

**Q:** A 35-year-old woman presents with weight loss, palpitations, heat intolerance, tremor, and exophthalmos. TSH is undetectable, free T4 is elevated. Diagnosis and management?

**A:** Graves disease (most common cause of hyperthyroidism); treat with antithyroid drugs (methimazole first-line), radioactive iodine ablation, or thyroidectomy

CTS thyroid guidelines recommend methimazole (preferred) over PTU for Graves hyperthyroidism (except in first trimester pregnancy and thyroid storm). Beta-blockers (propranolol) provide symptomatic relief. Ophthalmopathy managed separately with steroids/surgery.

**Q:** What are the signs and symptoms of hypothyroidism?

**A:** Fatigue, cold intolerance, weight gain, constipation, dry skin, hair loss, bradycardia, delayed relaxation of deep tendon reflexes, goiter, myxedema in severe cases

Most common cause in Canada: Hashimoto thyroiditis (autoimmune). Diagnosis: elevated TSH, low free T4. Treatment: levothyroxine (T4) — start low in elderly/cardiac disease (25 mcg), titrate to TSH 0.5-2.5 mIU/L.

**Q:** What is subclinical hypothyroidism and when should it be treated?

**A:** Elevated TSH (4.5-10 mIU/L) with normal free T4 and no symptoms; treat when: TSH >10, symptoms present, pregnancy planned or ongoing, strong family history or positive TPO antibodies

CTS guidelines recommend treating subclinical hypothyroidism when TSH >10 mIU/L or with symptoms. In asymptomatic patients with TSH 5-10, treat if TPO antibodies positive (higher progression risk). Annual monitoring if not treated.

**Q:** A 60-year-old with known thyroid disease presents with confusion, hypothermia, bradycardia, and hypoventilation. Labs show TSH >100, T4 undetectable. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Myxedema coma (severe hypothyroidism); treat with IV levothyroxine (300-500 mcg loading, then 50-100 mcg daily), IV T3 in critical illness, stress-dose steroids (hydrocortisone), supportive care

Myxedema coma has ~30-60% mortality. Precipitants: infection, cold exposure, surgery, medications (amiodarone, lithium). IV steroids given because of possible coexisting secondary adrenal insufficiency. Slow external rewarming.

**Q:** What is thyroid storm and how is it managed?

**A:** Thyroid storm: life-threatening exaggerated hyperthyroidism (Burch-Wartofsky score ≥45); treat with PTU (blocks T4 to T3 conversion) + potassium iodide (1h after PTU), beta-blockers, IV corticosteroids, ICU care

Precipitants of thyroid storm: surgery, infection, radioiodine, trauma. PTU is preferred over methimazole in thyroid storm due to additional inhibition of peripheral T4→T3 conversion. Lugol iodine given 1h after PTU to prevent thyroid hormone synthesis from iodine.

**Q:** What is the approach to a thyroid nodule found on imaging?

**A:** Ultrasound evaluation → TIRADS classification; FNA (fine needle aspiration) if ≥1 cm with suspicious features or ≥1.5-2 cm regardless of features

CTS/ACR-TIRADS guidelines recommend biopsy based on nodule size and ultrasound features: hypoechoic, irregular margins, taller-than-wide, calcifications, extrathyroidal extension. Bethesda cytology classification guides management.

**Q:** What are the Bethesda categories for thyroid FNA and their malignancy risk?

**A:** I: non-diagnostic (repeat); II: benign (1-3%); III: atypia of undetermined significance (5-15%); IV: follicular neoplasm (15-30%); V: suspicious for malignancy (60-75%); VI: malignant (97-99%)

Bethesda III typically requires repeat FNA or molecular testing. Bethesda IV — lobectomy or molecular testing. Bethesda V/VI — thyroidectomy. Papillary thyroid carcinoma is most common thyroid cancer and has excellent prognosis.

**Q:** What is Hashimoto thyroiditis and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Autoimmune thyroiditis (most common cause of hypothyroidism in developed countries); elevated anti-TPO antibodies, elevated TSH, possibly goiter; treat with levothyroxine when hypothyroid

Hashimoto thyroiditis has female predominance (7:1). Lymphocytic infiltration and germinal center formation on histology. Most patients progress to hypothyroidism over years. Associated with other autoimmune diseases (T1DM, vitiligo, pernicious anemia, SLE).

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman is 10 weeks pregnant with TSH 0.1 mIU/L and free T4 normal. What is the likely diagnosis?

**A:** Gestational hyperthyroidism — transient, caused by hCG stimulating TSH receptors; usually self-limiting and does not require antithyroid drugs

hCG shares alpha-subunit with TSH and cross-stimulates the TSH receptor. Gestational hyperthyroidism is most common in the first trimester, peaks with hCG. Hyperemesis gravidarum is associated. If free T4 elevated and symptomatic, consider methimazole (after 10 weeks — PTU in first trimester due to methimazole embryopathy).

**Q:** What is the role of TSH in monitoring thyroid replacement therapy?

**A:** TSH is the most sensitive marker for adequacy of thyroid hormone replacement; target TSH 0.5-2.5 mIU/L for most patients; recheck 6-8 weeks after dose change

CTS guidelines recommend TSH as primary monitoring test for levothyroxine therapy. Avoid over-replacement (low TSH) which causes atrial fibrillation and bone loss. Increased levothyroxine requirements in pregnancy (increase dose by 25-30% immediately upon pregnancy confirmation).

**Q:** What are the most common types of thyroid cancer and their prognosis?

**A:** Papillary (80%, excellent prognosis, 10-year survival >95%); Follicular (10%, good prognosis); Medullary (5%, MEN2-associated, intermediate prognosis, calcitonin marker); Anaplastic (<1%, extremely aggressive, median survival <6 months)

Papillary thyroid carcinoma spreads via lymphatics. BRAF mutation common. Treatment: thyroidectomy ± radioiodine. Medullary thyroid carcinoma: screen for RET mutation and MEN2. Anaplastic carcinoma: no effective treatment, palliative care.

**Q:** How does amiodarone affect thyroid function?

**A:** Amiodarone contains 37% iodine and causes thyrotoxicosis (type 1: excess iodine in pre-existing thyroid disease; type 2: destructive thyroiditis) or hypothyroidism (most common — block of T4→T3 conversion)

Amiodarone-induced thyroid disease occurs in 15-20% of patients. TSH should be checked every 6 months. Type 1 thyrotoxicosis: antithyroid drugs; type 2: corticosteroids; mixed: combination treatment. Hypothyroidism: levothyroxine; may continue amiodarone.

**Q:** What is de Quervain (subacute granulomatous) thyroiditis?

**A:** Self-limited thyroiditis following viral illness; painful thyroid, initial hyperthyroidism then hypothyroidism then recovery; ESR elevated, thyroglobulin antibodies negative, radioiodine uptake low

De Quervain thyroiditis phases: hyperthyroid (2-8 weeks) → hypothyroid → recovery (~months). NSAIDs for pain (aspirin of choice). Prednisone for severe cases. Beta-blockers for symptomatic hyperthyroidism. Low RAI uptake distinguishes from Graves.

**Q:** What is postpartum thyroiditis?

**A:** Autoimmune thyroiditis within 1 year postpartum: hyperthyroid phase (1-4 months) followed by hypothyroid phase (4-8 months) then recovery; elevated TPO antibodies

Postpartum thyroiditis occurs in 5-9% of women. Distinguished from Graves: no eye disease, RAI uptake low. Hypothyroid phase may require levothyroxine if symptomatic. 20-30% develop permanent hypothyroidism. Recurs in subsequent pregnancies.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic approach to hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Step 1: TSH (suppressed); Step 2: free T4 and free T3 (elevated); Step 3: etiology — TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb/TSIG) for Graves, thyroid scan and RAI uptake, ultrasound for nodules

Elevated RAI uptake: Graves disease, toxic multinodular goiter, toxic adenoma; Low RAI uptake: thyroiditis (subacute, postpartum, amiodarone type 2), exogenous thyroid hormone, iodine excess. TRAb positive in 90-95% of Graves.

**Q:** What is diagnostic workup for thyroid nodule?

**A:** TSH first. Low TSH: scan (hot=benign). Normal/high TSH: US -> FNA if >=1cm with suspicious features

Bethesda: I-VI. Molecular testing for indeterminate III/IV. Suspicious: hypoechoic, irregular, calcifications.

**Q:** What is thyroid storm management?

**A:** PTU first (blocks synthesis+conversion), beta-blocker, iodine 1h after PTU, corticosteroids, cooling

Burch-Wartofsky >=45. Mortality 20-30%. Never give iodine before thionamide.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic approach to a thyroid nodule?

**A:** TSH first. If low TSH → thyroid scan (hot nodule = likely benign). If normal/high TSH → US. FNA if ≥1 cm with suspicious US features (microcalcifications, irregular margins, taller-than-wide, hypoechoic). Use TI-RADS.

5-10% of nodules are malignant. Bethesda FNA cytology: I (non-diagnostic), II (benign), III (AUS), IV (follicular neoplasm→lobectomy), V (suspicious→surgery), VI (malignant→surgery). Molecular testing (Afirma, ThyroSeq) for Bethesda III/IV to avoid diagnostic surgery.

**Q:** What is thyroid storm and how is it managed?

**A:** Life-threatening thyrotoxicosis: fever >40°C, tachycardia/AFib, AMS, GI symptoms, HF. Burch-Wartofsky >45. Treatment ORDER: 1) propranolol, 2) PTU (blocks synthesis+T4→T3), 3) iodine 1h AFTER PTU, 4) hydrocortisone 100mg q8h, 5) supportive.

Iodine 1h after PTU prevents using iodine as substrate. PTU preferred over methimazole in storm (blocks peripheral T4→T3). Precipitants: infection, surgery, DKA, contrast. ICU required. Cholestyramine may reduce thyroid hormone enterohepatic recirculation.

**Q:** What differentiates Graves from toxic multinodular goiter?

**A:** Graves: autoimmune (TRAb+), diffuse goiter, ophthalmopathy, pretibial myxedema, young women, diffuse uptake on scan. TMNG: autonomous nodules, elderly, nodular goiter, NO ophthalmopathy, patchy uptake. Toxic adenoma: single hot nodule.

TRAb 97% sensitive/99% specific for Graves. Graves ophthalmopathy: proptosis, lid retraction, diplopia — treat with selenium, steroids, teprotumumab. First-line Graves treatment in Canada: methimazole (PTU in 1st trimester pregnancy only).

**Q:** What is the management of Graves hyperthyroidism?

**A:** 1) Methimazole 15-30 mg/day (first-line Canada), taper 12-18 months; 30-50% remission. 2) RAI: definitive, causes hypothyroidism. 3) Total thyroidectomy: if large goiter, suspicious nodule, severe ophthalmopathy. ATD major risk: agranulocytosis (0.3%).

Methimazole preferred over PTU (less hepatotoxicity) EXCEPT 1st trimester pregnancy (methimazole teratogenic). RAI contraindicated in pregnancy and moderate-severe ophthalmopathy. If fever/sore throat on ATDs → stop and check WBC immediately.

**Q:** What are causes of hypothyroidism and levothyroxine dosing?

**A:** Primary: Hashimoto (#1 in Canada, anti-TPO+), post-RAI, post-surgical, drugs (amiodarone, lithium). Central: pituitary disease. LT4: 1.6 μg/kg/day young healthy; start 25-50 μg if elderly/cardiac, titrate q4-6 weeks. Check TSH 6 weeks after dose change.

Subclinical hypothyroidism: treat if TSH >10, symptomatic, pregnant, or anti-TPO+ with TSH 5-10. Take LT4 fasting 30-60 min before food. Pregnancy: increase dose 30-50%. Interference: calcium, iron, PPI reduce absorption — separate by 4h.

**Q:** What is myxedema coma and its management?

**A:** Severe hypothyroidism: hypothermia, AMS, bradycardia, hypoventilation, hyponatremia, hypoglycemia. Treatment: IV LT4 200-400 μg load then 50-100 μg/day + IV hydrocortisone 100 mg q8h (before/with thyroid hormone) + supportive.

30-60% mortality. Give steroids before thyroid hormone to prevent adrenal crisis. Precipitants: cold, infection, sedatives. Avoid active rewarming (vasodilation→shock). IV T3 may be added in severe cases. ICU admission mandatory.

**Q:** How does amiodarone affect the thyroid?

**A:** 37% iodine by weight. AIH (hypothyroid): treat with LT4, can continue amiodarone. AIT1 (thyrotoxicosis, underlying thyroid disease): methimazole. AIT2 (destructive thyroiditis): steroids. Mixed: both.

Check TFTs before starting and q6 months. AIT1 vs AIT2: Doppler US (AIT1=increased, AIT2=decreased vascularity). Amiodarone has 100-day half-life. If life-threatening AIT unresponsive to treatment: thyroidectomy.

**Q:** What is thyroid cancer approach by type?

**A:** Papillary (80%): excellent prognosis, LN mets, total thyroidectomy±RAI. Follicular (10%): hematogenous spread, total thyroidectomy+RAI. Medullary (5%): C-cells, calcitonin, screen MEN2/RET. Anaplastic (<5%): aggressive, poor prognosis, palliative.

Papillary: >95% 10-year survival. Post-op: TSH suppression, thyroglobulin monitoring, neck US. Medullary: no RAI uptake. RET mutation→prophylactic thyroidectomy in MEN2 families.

**Q:** What is subacute (de Quervain) thyroiditis?

**A:** Viral, painful tender thyroid, elevated ESR, low RAI uptake. Phases: hyperthyroid→hypothyroid→euthyroid (self-limited over months). Treatment: NSAIDs, steroids if severe. Postpartum thyroiditis: painless, similar phases, anti-TPO+.

All destructive thyroiditis: low RAI uptake during thyrotoxic phase (vs Graves=high). Subacute: follows URI, jaw/ear pain, tender gland. Beta-blockers for symptomatic thyrotoxicosis. Most recover fully.

**Q:** How is thyroid function managed in pregnancy?

**A:** TSH targets: 1st trimester <2.5 or trimester-specific range. Overt hypothyroidism: increase LT4 30-50%. PTU in 1st trimester, methimazole 2nd-3rd. RAI absolutely contraindicated. Check TRAb in 3rd trimester if Graves history.

hCG has TSH-like activity→physiologic TSH suppression in 1st trimester. Uncontrolled hypothyroidism impairs fetal neurodevelopment. TRAb crosses placenta→neonatal thyrotoxicosis risk.

**Q:** What is TRAb significance in Graves disease?

**A:** Pathognomonic for Graves (97% sens, 99% spec). Uses: confirm diagnosis, predict relapse after ATDs (persistent TRAb=higher relapse), assess neonatal thyrotoxicosis risk in pregnant Graves patients (TRAb crosses placenta).

Check TRAb at ATD discontinuation: negative predicts 70% remission. Elevated in 3rd trimester→monitor neonate for thyrotoxicosis. Also called thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin (TSI).

**Q:** What drugs interfere with thyroid function tests?

**A:** Biotin: falsely low TSH, high T4 (stop 2 days before). Estrogen/OCPs: increase TBG→high total T4, normal TSH. Glucocorticoids/dopamine: suppress TSH. Phenytoin: lower total T4. Heparin: falsely high free T4.

Sick euthyroid (non-thyroidal illness): low T3, low/normal T4, low/normal TSH — do not treat, reassess after recovery. Metformin may modestly lower TSH. Amiodarone and lithium affect thyroid directly.

**Q:** What is subclinical hyperthyroidism management?

**A:** TSH low, free T4/T3 normal. Repeat in 3-6 months. If persistent + TSH <0.1: thyroid scan and consider treatment. Treat if: TSH <0.1, age >65, osteoporosis, CV risk/AFib.

TSH 0.1-0.4: observe. TSH <0.1: higher AFib risk (2-3x), fractures. In elderly, subclinical hyperthyroidism increases mortality. Treatment based on etiology (ATDs, RAI, or surgery).

**Q:** What are thyroidectomy complications?

**A:** Immediate: hemorrhage/hematoma (airway emergency→open at bedside). Early: hypocalcemia from hypoparathyroidism (most common, 20-30% transient), unilateral RLN injury (hoarseness). Late: permanent hypoparathyroidism (1-2%), hypothyroidism. Bilateral RLN injury: stridor, cord paralysis→intubation.

Monitor Ca q6-12h post-op x 24h. Symptomatic hypocalcemia (tingling, Chvostek/Trousseau): IV calcium gluconate, then oral Ca+calcitriol. Check vocal cords pre- and post-op.

**Q:** How are incidental thyroid nodules on imaging managed?

**A:** Found on CT/MRI/PET: assess with dedicated thyroid US + TSH. Apply TI-RADS for FNA. Not all need FNA. Focal FDG-PET uptake: 30-50% malignancy→FNA. Diffuse FDG uptake: thyroiditis (benign).

Thyroid incidentalomas on CT very common (~16-18%). Size alone should not determine FNA — US features matter. Follow up incidental findings with dedicated thyroid US.

**Q:** What conditions and drugs cause QTc prolongation?

**A:** Genetic long QT syndrome (channelopathies), antiarrhythmics (Class I, III), antipsychotics (haloperidol, ziprasidone), antidepressants (citalopram), antibiotics (macrolides), electrolyte abnormalities (hypocalcemia, hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia), hypothyroidism, hypothermia, cardiomyopathy

Prolonged QTc increases risk of Torsades de Pointes, a potentially lethal polymorphic VT.

**Q:** What causes shortened QTc interval and what is the associated risk?

**A:** Hypercalcemia, digoxin, hyperthyroidism. Risk: VFib (very rare)

Short QT syndrome is associated with increased sudden cardiac death risk due to shortened refractory period.

**Q:** What factors increase myocardial oxygen demand?

**A:** Increased heart rate (hyperthyroidism, exercise, anemia, pregnancy), increased contractility (hyperthyroidism), and increased wall stress (myocardial hypertrophy, aortic stenosis)

Myocardial oxygen demand is determined by heart rate, contractility, and wall stress (governed by the Law of Laplace).

**Q:** What conditions cause HIGH-OUTPUT heart failure?

**A:** Anemia, thiamine deficiency (beriberi), hyperthyroidism, arteriovenous fistula or left-to-right shunting, Paget's disease, renal disease, hepatic disease

High-output states increase metabolic demand, which can decompensate patients with marginal cardiac reserve or exacerbate existing heart failure.

**Q:** What is high-output heart failure and what conditions cause it?

**A:** Heart failure caused by demand for increased CO; DDx includes anemia, thiamine deficiency (beriberi), hyperthyroidism, A-V fistula or L-R shunting, Paget's disease, renal disease, hepatic disease

High-output HF occurs when normal cardiac output is insufficient to meet metabolic demands, often unmasking underlying cardiac pathology or occurring with systemic hypermetabolic states.

**Q:** What are the non-infectious causes of pericarditis mentioned in Toronto Notes?

**A:** Post-MI (acute or Dressler's syndrome), post-cardiac surgery/trauma, uremia, hypothyroidism, malignancy (Hodgkin's, breast, lung, renal, melanoma, lymphoma), autoimmune diseases (SLE, polyarteritis, RA, scleroderma), drugs (hydralazine), radiation, sarcoidosis

Pericarditis has numerous non-infectious etiologies requiring different management approaches, from immunosuppression for autoimmune causes to chemotherapy for malignant pericarditis.

**Q:** What are the side effects of amiodarone?

**A:** Photosensitivity, pulmonary toxicity, hepatotoxicity, thyroid disease (hypo/hyperthyroidism), increased INR

Amiodarone has numerous extracardiac toxicities requiring monitoring of pulmonary function, liver enzymes, and thyroid function.

**Q:** What are the major precipitants of Telogen Effluvium? (SEND mnemonic)

**A:** Stress (biological/psychological), Endocrine (hypothyroidism), Nutritional (iron/protein deficiency), Drugs (acitretin, heparin, lithium, IFN, beta-blockers, valproic acid, SSRIs)

Any physiological stress can shift anagen hairs prematurely into telogen. The 'SEND' mnemonic covers the most common causes.

**Q:** What are precipitants of telogen effluvium (SEND mnemonic)?

**A:** Stress (biological/psychological), Endocrine (hypothyroidism), Nutritional (iron/protein deficiency), Drugs (acitretin, heparin, lithium, IFN, beta-blockers, valproic acid, SSRIs)

Telogen effluvium can be triggered by various systemic insults that force hair follicles prematurely into the resting phase.

**Q:** What are causes of non-scarring alopecia?

**A:** Autoimmune (alopecia areata), endocrine (hypothyroidism, androgens), micronutrient deficiencies, toxins (heavy metals, chemotherapy, vitamin A excess), trauma (traction/tight ponytail/braiding), other (syphilis, severe illness, childbirth)

Unlike scarring alopecia, these conditions preserve the hair follicle stem cells, allowing potential for regrowth.

**Q:** What are common causes of non-scarring alopecia?

**A:** Alopecia areata (autoimmune), androgenetic alopecia, telogen effluvium, anagen effluvium, hypothyroidism, nutritional deficiencies, traction, syphilis

Non-scarring alopecias preserve the hair follicle stem cells, making spontaneous or treatment-induced regrowth possible.

**Q:** What is the Breslow thickness threshold for T4 melanoma staging?

**A:** >4.0 mm

T4 melanomas have the worst prognosis with approximately 10% 5-year survival, indicating very deep invasion.

**Q:** What are common causes of hyponatremia?

**A:** Hypovolemic (GI, renal, skin, blood loss), euvolemic (SIADH, adrenal insufficiency, hypothyroid), hypervolemic (CHF, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome).

Determining volume status is crucial for guiding treatment.

**Q:** What are the categories and causes of hyponatremia?

**A:** Hypovolemic (GI, renal, skin, blood loss), Euvolemic (SIADH, adrenal insufficiency, hypothyroid), Hypervolemic (CHF, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome)

Hyponatremia is classified by volume status which guides underlying cause and treatment.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for a first psychiatric presentation?

**A:** Blood glucose, urine and serum toxicology, pregnancy test, electrolytes, TSH, AST/ALT, bilirubin, serum Cr, BUN, osmolality, CT head if neurological etiology suspected

These tests rule out organic causes of psychiatric symptoms including metabolic disorders, infection, intoxication, and structural brain lesions.

**Q:** What are the causes of hyponatremia?

**A:** Causes include: hypovolemic (GI, renal, skin, blood loss), euvolemic (SIADH, stress, adrenal insufficiency, hypothyroid), and hypervolemic (CHF, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome).

Classifying hyponatremia by volume status guides appropriate treatment.

**Q:** What are causes of secondary hypercholesterolemia?

**A:** Hypothyroidism, nephrotic syndrome, CKD, cholestatic liver disease, anorexia nervosa, drugs (cyclosporine, steroids)

These conditions affect lipid metabolism and can cause elevated LDL cholesterol levels.

**Q:** What causes secondary hypercholesterolemia?

**A:** Hypothyroidism, nephrotic syndrome, CKD, cholestatic liver disease (e.g., primary biliary cirrhosis), anorexia nervosa, drugs (cyclosporine, carbamazepine, steroids), monoclonal gammopathy

Secondary causes must be excluded and treated when managing dyslipidemias.

**Q:** What are the investigations for first-presentation psychosis?

**A:** Blood glucose, urine and serum toxicology, pregnancy test, electrolytes, TSH, AST/ALT, bilirubin, serum Cr, BUN, osmolality, medication levels; CT head if neurological etiology suspected; LP if indicated

First-presentation psychosis requires ruling out organic causes through comprehensive testing including metabolic panel, thyroid function, toxicology, and neuroimaging as indicated.

**Q:** What endocrine condition causes hypercholesterolemia with small dense LDL?

**A:** Hypothyroidism

Hypothyroidism reduces LDL receptor activity and can cause elevated total cholesterol with small dense LDL particles.

**Q:** What is the order of hormone loss in hypopituitarism following pituitary infarction (e.g., Sheehan's syndrome)?

**A:** GH → LH/FSH → TSH → ACTH → PRL (anterior pituitary, in that sequence)

The anterior pituitary has different vascular supply zones. GH-secreting cells are most vulnerable to ischemia, followed by gonadotrophs, thyrotrophs, corticotrophs, and finally lactotrophs.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of central hypothyroidism (TSH deficiency)?

**A:** Tiredness, cold intolerance, constipation, weight gain

Central hypothyroidism results from TSH deficiency. It is usually milder than primary hypothyroidism because TSH is low rather than elevated, and some residual thyroid hormone production may persist.

**Q:** What is pituitary apoplexy and how does it present?

**A:** Acute hemorrhage or infarction of a pituitary tumor. Presents with sudden severe headache, altered level of consciousness, and acute deficiency of all pituitary hormones (GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL).

Pituitary apoplexy is a surgical emergency. The expanding hemorrhage causes sudden increased intracranial pressure and compression of surrounding structures.

**Q:** What is the first-line test for evaluating thyroid function?

**A:** TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone)

TSH is the most sensitive test because most thyroid disorders are primary. If TSH is normal, primary thyroid dysfunction is ruled out. Secondary thyroid disorders require both TSH and free T4.

**Q:** What percentage of thyroid hormone secreted is T4?

**A:** More than 90%

The thyroid secretes predominantly T4 (thyroxine). T3 (triiodothyronine) is the more metabolically active form, but most circulating T3 comes from peripheral conversion of T4.

**Q:** How does the potency of T3 compare to T4?

**A:** T3 is approximately 4 times more biologically potent than T4

T3 has a higher affinity for thyroid hormone receptors and produces more rapid metabolic effects. This is why T4 is considered a prohormone.

**Q:** What is the Wolff-Chaikoff effect?

**A:** An autoregulatory mechanism where excess iodine temporarily inhibits thyroid hormone synthesis

This is an intrathyroid negative feedback mechanism. High iodine concentrations reduce thyroid sensitivity to TSH and decrease hormone production. It is the basis for using iodine in hyperthyroidism treatment.

**Q:** What is the Jod-Basedow effect?

**A:** Hyperthyroidism induced by excess iodine in patients with underlying thyroid autonomy

In patients with Graves' disease or autonomous thyroid nodules, iodine supplementation can trigger hyperthyroidism because the thyroid can produce excess hormone without normal feedback inhibition.
