**Q:** How do thyroid hormones increase basal metabolic rate?

**A:** By increasing Na+/K+ ATPase activity, O2 consumption, respiration, heat generation, and cardiovascular activity

 thyroid hormones uncouple oxidative phosphorylation in mitochondria, increasing heat production (non-shivering thermogenesis) and driving overall metabolic rate upward.

**Q:** What are the thyroid function test patterns in primary vs secondary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Primary: low TSH with elevated T4/T3. Secondary: inappropriately normal or elevated TSH with elevated T4/T3

Primary hyperthyroidism is due to thyroid gland dysfunction (negative feedback suppresses TSH). Secondary hyperthyroidism is due to pituitary TSH-secreting adenoma (TSH not suppressed despite high thyroid hormones).

**Q:** What are the thyroid function test patterns in primary vs secondary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Primary: elevated TSH (most sensitive) with low T4/T3. Secondary: low or inappropriately normal TSH with low T4/T3

Primary hypothyroidism: thyroid gland failure → lack of negative feedback → high TSH. Secondary hypothyroidism: pituitary/hypothalamic disease → low TSH → low thyroid hormones.

**Q:** What screening tests are used to evaluate pituitary function?

**A:** 8am cortisol, PRL, TSH, Free T4, LH, FSH, Estradiol or Testosterone, GH, IGF-1, Na, Osmolality

These baseline tests evaluate all anterior pituitary hormone axes. The 8am cortisol is crucial because cortisol follows a diurnal rhythm.

**Q:** What are common causes of secondary hypercholesterolemia?

**A:** Hypothyroidism, nephrotic syndrome, CKD, cholestatic liver disease, monoclonal gammopathy, drugs (cyclosporine, carbamazepine, steroids)

Secondary causes should be ruled out before diagnosing primary dyslipidemia.

**Q:** In what order are pituitary hormones typically lost in hypopituitarism?

**A:** GH, LH/FSH, TSH, ACTH, then PRL (in that sequence)

The mnemonic 'Go Look For The Adenoma Please' helps recall: GH, LH/FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL.

**Q:** What are the steps of thyroid hormone synthesis?

**A:** Iodide trapping (NIS), oxidation (TPO), iodination of thyroglobulin (organification), coupling of MIT/DIT, proteolysis and release of T4/T3

Thyroid peroxidase enzyme catalyzes the oxidation, organification, and coupling reactions in the follicular colloid.

**Q:** How does T3 potency compare to T4?

**A:** T3 is approximately 4 times more potent than T4

Despite being more potent, T3 is present in smaller quantities and has a shorter half-life compared to T4.

**Q:** What percentage of circulating T4 and T3 are free (biologically active)?

**A:** Free T4 is 0.02% and free T3 is 0.3%

The majority of thyroid hormones are bound to TBG, albumin, and transthyretin, which renders them biologically inactive.

**Q:** Why is TSH the best test for assessing thyroid function?

**A:** Most functional thyroid disorders are primary, and TSH is the most sensitive indicator of primary thyroid dysfunction

If TSH is normal, a primary thyroid disorder is ruled out, though secondary disorders require free T4 testing as well.

**Q:** What is the TSH pattern in primary vs secondary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Primary: low TSH (negative feedback from high T4/T3); Secondary: inappropriately normal or increased TSH

Secondary hyperthyroidism originates from pituitary or hypothalamic disease, so TSH fails to suppress appropriately.

**Q:** What is the TSH pattern in primary vs secondary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Primary: increased TSH (most sensitive test); Secondary: low or inappropriately normal TSH

Primary hypothyroidism shows high TSH due to loss of negative feedback, while secondary shows pituitary failure.

**Q:** What are the actions of thyroid hormones on metabolism?

**A:** Increase basal metabolic rate via Na+/K+ ATPase activity, O2 consumption, heat generation, and cardiovascular activity

T3 increases sensitivity to catecholamines and potentiates the actions of GH, glucagon, and cortisol.

**Q:** What are clinical features of central hypothyroidism (TSH deficiency)?

**A:** Tiredness, cold intolerance, constipation, weight gain

Symptoms are similar to primary hypothyroidism but typically milder; TSH will be low or inappropriately normal.

**Q:** What is the function of the sodium iodide cotransporter (NIS) in thyroid cells?

**A:** Actively transports iodide from blood into thyroid follicular cells

This is the first step in thyroid hormone synthesis and is the target for radioactive iodine therapy.

**Q:** What is the order of hormone loss in Sheehan's syndrome?

**A:** GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL (in that order)

Sheehan's syndrome is pituitary infarction due to postpartum hemorrhage. Hormones are lost in a predictable sequence starting with GH.

**Q:** What investigation is the best test for assessing thyroid function and why?

**A:** TSH (Thyroid Stimulating Hormone)

TSH is the most sensitive test because most functional thyroid disorders are primary. If TSH is normal, a functional disorder is unlikely (unless secondary disorder is suspected).

**Q:** What is the relative potency of T3 compared to T4?

**A:** T3 is approximately 4 times more potent than T4

Despite being more potent, T3 has a shorter half-life and is present in smaller quantities in the blood.

**Q:** What percentage of T4 is converted to T3 or reverse T3 in the periphery?

**A:** 85%

Most peripheral T4 is converted to either active T3 or inactive reverse T3 by deiodinase enzymes.

**Q:** What is the normal TSH pattern in primary vs secondary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Primary: low TSH (due to negative feedback); Secondary: increased or inappropriately normal TSH

In primary hyperthyroidism, the thyroid itself is overproducing hormone, suppressing TSH. In secondary (pituitary-driven), TSH is inappropriately normal or elevated.

**Q:** What is the normal TSH pattern in primary vs secondary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Primary: increased TSH (most sensitive test); Secondary: low or inappropriately normal TSH

In primary hypothyroidism, the thyroid cannot produce hormone, leading to elevated TSH. In secondary hypothyroidism, the pituitary cannot produce TSH.

**Q:** What is the normal sequence of pituitary hormone loss in hypopituitarism (mnemonic)?

**A:** Go Look For The Adenoma Please (GH, LH/FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL)

This mnemonic helps remember the usual order of hormone deficiency, with GH and gonadotropins typically affected first.

**Q:** What are the three main steps in thyroid hormone synthesis?

**A:** Iodide trapping (NIS), iodination of thyroglobulin (organification), and proteolysis with release of T4/T3

Thyroid hormones are synthesized on thyroglobulin within the follicular colloid, then released into the bloodstream.

**Q:** What is the role of thyroid peroxidase enzyme in thyroid hormone synthesis?

**A:** Catalyzes iodination of tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin and coupling of MIT/DIT to form T3 and T4

TPO is the key enzyme in thyroid hormone synthesis and is the target of anti-TPO antibodies in autoimmune thyroid disease.

**Q:** Why are thyroid hormones required for normal fetal growth?

**A:** They stimulate GH release in synergism with cortisol, essential for CNS development and growth

Maternal thyroid hormones cross the placenta and are crucial for fetal brain development, especially in the first trimester.

**Q:** What is the treatment sequence for combined adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism due to pituitary disease?

**A:** Treat adrenal insufficiency first with glucocorticoids, then treat hypothyroidism

Giving thyroid hormone before glucocorticoids can precipitate adrenal crisis by increasing metabolic rate without cortisol coverage.

**Q:** What endocrine conditions cause secondary dyslipidemia?

**A:** Hypothyroidism (causes small dense LDL), obesity/metabolic syndrome, diabetes mellitus

Endocrine disorders significantly affect lipid metabolism and are common causes of secondary dyslipidemia.

**Q:** What is the order of hormone loss following pituitary infarction (e.g., Sheehan's syndrome)?

**A:** GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL (in that order, with posterior pituitary hormones ADH and oxytocin affected last)

The anterior pituitary loses hormones in a sequential pattern following infarction, with GH and gonadotropins affected first.

**Q:** What is the best initial test for assessing thyroid function and why?

**A:** TSH - it is the most sensitive test for primary thyroid disorders and normal TSH rules out functional thyroid disease

TSH provides a sensitive indicator of thyroid status because most thyroid disorders are primary (thyroid-origin).

**Q:** What percentage of thyroid hormone secreted is T4 vs T3?

**A:** More than 90% is T4; T3 is secreted in smaller quantities

The thyroid primarily secretes T4 (levothyroxine), which is converted to the more active T3 peripherally.

**Q:** What is the hormonally active fraction of thyroid hormones?

**A:** Free T4 (0.02%) and free T3 (0.3%)

Only the unbound (free) fractions are biologically active; the majority is bound to TBG, albumin, and transthyretin.

**Q:** What is the relative potency and half-life of T3 vs T4?

**A:** T3 is approximately 4x more potent than T4 but has a shorter half-life

T3 is the active form that binds nuclear receptors, while T4 serves as a prohormone with longer duration of action.

**Q:** Where does most of the plasma T3 pool come from?

**A:** Peripheral conversion of T4 by deiodinase enzymes

Approximately 85% of T4 is converted to T3 or reverse T3 in the periphery, making most circulating T3 derived from T4.

**Q:** In primary vs secondary hypothyroidism, what are the TSH findings?

**A:** Primary: increased TSH; Secondary: low or inappropriately normal TSH

Primary hypothyroidism shows elevated TSH due to loss of negative feedback. Secondary hypothyroidism has pituitary dysfunction with inadequate TSH.

**Q:** In primary vs secondary hyperthyroidism, what are the TSH findings?

**A:** Primary: low TSH (due to negative feedback); Secondary: increased or inappropriately normal TSH

Primary hyperthyroidism suppresses TSH via negative feedback. Secondary hyperthyroidism has inappropriate TSH secretion from pituitary.

**Q:** What is the treatment order for combined adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism from hypopituitarism?

**A:** Treat glucocorticoids first, then thyroid hormone

Giving thyroid hormone before corticosteroids can precipitate adrenal crisis by increasing metabolic clearance of cortisol.

**Q:** In pituitary adenoma, which hormone deficiencies should be recognized urgently?

**A:** Adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism - these should be treated with glucocorticoids first, then thyroid hormone

Concurrent adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism require glucocorticoid replacement before thyroid hormone to prevent adrenal crisis from increased metabolic demand.

**Q:** What investigations are used to assess pituitary function?

**A:** 8am cortisol, PRL, TSH, Free T4, LH, FSH, Estradiol/Testosterone, GH, IGF-1, Na+, Osmolality

This panel assesses all anterior and posterior pituitary hormones. 8am cortisol is crucial as cortisol follows a diurnal rhythm.

**Q:** What is the biologically active fraction of thyroid hormones?

**A:** Free T4 (0.02%) and free T3 (0.3%)

Most thyroid hormones are bound to TBG, albumin, and transthyretin. Only the free fractions are hormonally active.

**Q:** How much more potent is T3 compared to T4?

**A:** Approximately 4 times more potent

T3 has greater receptor affinity and faster onset of action, but has a shorter half-life compared to T4.

**Q:** Why is TSH the best initial test for assessing thyroid function?

**A:** Most functional thyroid disorders are primary - if TSH is normal, a functional disorder is ruled out

TSH is extremely sensitive to thyroid hormone levels due to negative feedback. It detects primary thyroid dysfunction before T4/T3 abnormalities become apparent.

**Q:** What TSH pattern would you see in primary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Low TSH due to negative feedback from elevated T4 and T3

Primary hyperthyroidism (thyroid gland overproduction) causes high thyroid hormone levels that suppress pituitary TSH secretion.

**Q:** What TSH pattern would you see in primary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Increased TSH (most sensitive test) due to less negative feedback from T4 and T3

Primary hypothyroidism causes low thyroid hormone levels, removing negative feedback and resulting in elevated TSH from the pituitary.

**Q:** What TSH pattern would you see in secondary (pituitary) hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Increased TSH (or inappropriately normal) with elevated T4 and T3

Secondary hyperthyroidism is caused by a pituitary adenoma secreting TSH, causing high TSH with correspondingly elevated thyroid hormones.

**Q:** What TSH pattern would you see in secondary (pituitary) hypothyroidism?

**A:** Low or inappropriately normal TSH with low T4

Secondary hypothyroidism results from pituitary TSH deficiency. The TSH is low rather than elevated, distinguishing it from primary hypothyroidism.

**Q:** What is the order of pituitary hormone loss in hypopituitarism?

**A:** GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL (in that sequence typically)

The classic sequence is 'Go Look For The Adenoma Please' - GH, LH/FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL. However, any pattern is possible depending on cause.

**Q:** What is the triple bolus test used to assess?

**A:** Combined test of GH, LH/FSH, and TSH reserve

This rarely-performed test uses insulin, GnRH, and TRH to stimulate pituitary hormone release and assess anterior pituitary function.

**Q:** What is the initial screening test for suspected hypopituitarism?

**A:** 8am cortisol, PRL, TSH, Free T4, LH, FSH, Estradiol or Testosterone, GH, IGF-1

Multiple hormone levels are checked simultaneously to assess both anterior and posterior pituitary function. 8am cortisol is used because cortisol follows a diurnal rhythm.

**Q:** What percentage of thyroid hormone secreted by the thyroid is T4?

**A:** More than 90%

T4 (thyroxine) is the major secretory product, while T3 (triiodothyronine) is more biologically potent but secreted in smaller amounts.

**Q:** What is the free (biologically active) fraction of T4 and T3?

**A:** Free T4: 0.02%, Free T3: 0.3%

The majority of thyroid hormones are bound to TBG, albumin, and transthyretin. Only the free fraction is metabolically active.

**Q:** How does T3 compare to T4 in potency and half-life?

**A:** T3 is approximately 4x more potent than T4 but has a shorter half-life

T4 serves as a prohormone and is converted to T3 in peripheral tissues by deiodinase enzymes. Most plasma T3 comes from peripheral conversion.

**Q:** What happens to T4 conversion in peripheral tissues in hypothyroidism?

**A:** Increased conversion of T4 to T3 (active) and decreased conversion to reverse T3 (inactive)

This is a compensatory mechanism - in hypothyroidism, more T4 is converted to the active T3 form to maintain tissue thyroid hormone activity despite low T4 levels.

**Q:** What are the metabolic effects of thyroid hormones?

**A:** Increase basal metabolic rate through increased Na+/K+ ATPase activity, O2 consumption, heat generation, and cardiovascular activity

Thyroid hormones increase resting energy expenditure, heart rate and contractility, and respiratory rate - explaining symptoms of hyperthyroidism.

**Q:** What is the expected TSH pattern in primary vs secondary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Primary: low TSH (negative feedback); Secondary: increased or inappropriately normal TSH with elevated T4/T3

Secondary hyperthyroidism is rare and due to a pituitary tumor secreting TSH, causing inappropriate TSH levels that should be suppressed but aren't.

**Q:** What is the most sensitive test for primary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Elevated TSH

TSH rises early in primary hypothyroidism as the pituitary attempts to compensate for low thyroid hormone levels, making it more sensitive than T4 measurements.

**Q:** What is the role of thyroglobulin in thyroid hormone synthesis?

**A:** It is the protein scaffold on which thyroid hormones are synthesized via iodination of tyrosine residues

Thyroglobulin is secreted into the follicular colloid and serves as the substrate for hormone synthesis. After iodination and coupling, it is proteolytically cleaved to release T4 and T3.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic for remembering anterior pituitary hormones?

**A:** Go Look For The Adenoma Please (GH, LH/FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL)

This helpful mnemonic organizes the anterior pituitary hormones in the typical order they are lost in hypopituitarism.

**Q:** What is the definition of a thyroid nodule?

**A:** A discrete lesion that can be distinguished sonographically from the rest of the thyroid parenchyma

Thyroid nodules have a 19-67% prevalence based on incidentally found nodules on ultrasound.

**Q:** What are the etiologies of thyroid nodules?

**A:** Benign tumors (e.g., follicular adenoma), thyroid malignancy, hyperplastic area in multinodular goiter, or cyst (true cyst or area of cystic degeneration)

The majority of thyroid nodules are benign; only a small percentage represent malignancy.

**Q:** How does the approach to thyroid nodules differ based on TSH level?

**A:** Low TSH suggests autonomous functioning (hot nodule); normal/elevated TSH suggests non-functioning (cold nodule). Hot nodules are treated for hyperthyroidism; cold nodules may require US-guided biopsy if suspicious.

A hot nodule produces excess thyroid hormone, suppressing TSH, while a cold nodule does not function actively.

**Q:** What is the prevalence of incidentally found thyroid nodules on ultrasound?

**A:** 19-67%

A large proportion of thyroid nodules are discovered incidentally during ultrasound imaging for other reasons.

**Q:** List the etiologies of thyroid nodules

**A:** Benign tumors (e.g., follicular adenoma), thyroid malignancy, hyperplastic area in multinodular goiter, cyst (true cyst or area of cystic degeneration)

The majority of thyroid nodules are benign, but malignancy must be ruled out through appropriate investigation.

**Q:** What are the main etiologies of thyroid nodules?

**A:** Benign tumors (follicular adenoma), thyroid malignancy, hyperplastic area in multinodular goiter, or cyst (true cyst or area of cystic degeneration)

The vast majority of thyroid nodules are benign, but malignancy must be ruled out.

**Q:** How is a hot nodule on thyroid scan managed?

**A:** If TSH is suppressed and the nodule is hot, treat hyperthyroidism; if TSH normal/elevated, treat thyroiditis based on US characteristics

Hot nodules are almost never malignant but require management of associated hyperthyroidism.

**Q:** What is the order in which pituitary hormones are lost in pituitary apoplexy?

**A:** GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL (anterior pituitary), then ADH and oxytocin (posterior pituitary)

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for concurrent adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism in hypopituitarism?

**A:** Treat adrenal insufficiency first, then add thyroid hormone

**Q:** Where is most plasma T3 derived from?

**A:** Peripheral conversion of T4

**Q:** What triggers the negative feedback mechanism for TSH?

**A:** T3 acts on the anterior pituitary to inhibit TSH and on the hypothalamus to inhibit TRH

**Q:** What is the best test for assessing thyroid function and why?

**A:** TSH, because most functional thyroid disorders are primary

**Q:** What is the TSH pattern in primary hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Low TSH due to negative feedback from elevated T4 and T3

**Q:** What is the TSH pattern in primary hypothyroidism?

**A:** Increased TSH (most sensitive test)

**Q:** What is the TSH pattern in secondary (central) hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Increased or inappropriately normal TSH with elevated T4 and T3

**Q:** What is the initial test for suspected hypopituitarism?

**A:** 8am cortisol, PRL, TSH, Free T4, LH, FSH, Estradiol or Testosterone, GH, IGF-1, Na, Osmolality

**Q:** What are the common etiologies of thyroid nodules?

**A:** Benign tumors (follicular adenoma), thyroid malignancy, hyperplastic area in multinodular goiter, and cysts (true cysts or areas of cystic degeneration)

The majority of thyroid nodules are benign, but malignancy must be ruled out through appropriate investigation.

**Q:** What is the approach to a hot nodule on thyroid scan?

**A:** If the nodule is hot (functioning) and TSH is suppressed, treat hyperthyroidism; if TSH is normal/elevated, treat thyroiditis

Hot nodules are typically benign and functioning, while cold nodules require biopsy if suspicious.

**Q:** How is a hot nodule on thyroid scan typically managed?

**A:** Hot nodules are managed by treating hyperthyroidism, as they are usually functioning adenomas producing excess thyroid hormone

**Q:** What is the PTH level in primary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** PTH is high or inappropriately normal

Primary hyperparathyroidism causes hypersecretion of PTH from parathyroid adenoma (81%) or hyperplasia (15%).

**Q:** What is tertiary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** Increased PTH after prolonged secondary hyperparathyroidism (usually due to renal failure) - autonomous PTH secretion develops

Long-term stimulation of parathyroid glands in secondary hyperparathyroidism leads to nodular hyperplasia and autonomous secretion.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of primary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** Solitary adenoma (81%), followed by hyperplasia (15%) and carcinoma (4%).

Primary hyperparathyroidism is a common cause of outpatient hypercalcemia and is usually due to a single benign adenoma.

**Q:** What are the causes of tertiary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** Prolonged secondary hyperparathyroidism (usually due to renal failure) leading to autonomous PTH hypersecretion even after the trigger is removed.

Tertiary hyperparathyroidism develops after long-standing secondary hyperparathyroidism, typically in patients on dialysis.

**Q:** What medical conditions are associated with gynecomastia?

**A:** Hypogonadism, cirrhosis, CKD, hyperthyroidism, testicular/germ cell tumors, HIV treatment (lipodystrophy)

These conditions alter hormone metabolism or cause hormonal imbalances.

**Q:** What medical conditions cause gynecomastia?

**A:** Male hypogonadism, cirrhosis, CKD, hyperthyroidism, testicular/germ-cell neoplasms, Klinefelter syndrome

These conditions disrupt hormonal balance between estrogens and androgens.

**Q:** What is the difference between a hot nodule and a cold nodule on thyroid scan?

**A:** Hot nodules are hyperfunctioning (take up more radioactive iodine) and are usually benign; cold nodules are hypofunctioning and have higher malignancy risk

Hot nodules are associated with low TSH and are treated for hyperthyroidism; cold nodules require ultrasound-guided biopsy if suspicious.

**Q:** In the approach to thyroid nodules, what does a 'hot nodule' indicate?

**A:** A hot nodule suggests hyperfunctioning thyroid tissue that typically appears on radionuclide scan as areas of increased uptake

Hot nodules are usually benign and may be associated with hyperthyroidism; management includes treating hyperthyroidism.

**Q:** In the approach to thyroid nodules, what does a 'cold nodule' indicate?

**A:** A cold nodule shows decreased uptake on radionuclide scan and requires further evaluation with ultrasound-guided biopsy

Cold nodules have a higher risk of malignancy compared to hot nodules and warrant further investigation.

**Q:** How are thyroid nodules with hot nodules on scan managed?

**A:** Hot nodules are treated by addressing the underlying hyperthyroidism; they are typically suppressive rather than requiring biopsy

Hot nodules concentrate radioactive iodine and are usually функционирующие, indicating excess hormone production.

**Q:** How are cold nodules on thyroid scan managed?

**A:** Cold nodules require ultrasound-guided biopsy if they have suspicious sonographic characteristics

Cold nodules do not concentrate radioactive iodine and have a higher risk of malignancy compared to hot nodules.

**Q:** What are causes of hypoparathyroidism?

**A:** Surgical removal, autoimmune destruction, congenital absence, magnesium deficiency (functional)

Lack of PTH leads to inadequate bone resorption, reduced renal calcium reabsorption, and decreased vitamin D activation.

**Q:** What is the relationship between magnesium and calcium?

**A:** Severe magnesium deficiency causes functional hypoparathyroidism (impaired PTH secretion) and end-organ resistance to PTH

Magnesium is required for PTH release and action. Correction of magnesium is required before calcium can be normalized.

**Q:** What investigations should be considered for unexplained sensorineural hearing loss?

**A:** Blood glucose, CBC, TSH, syphilis testing, and MRI to exclude vestibular schwannoma

These tests help identify potentially reversible causes and exclude tumors.

**Q:** List 6 etiologies of secondary headaches

**A:** Drug-related (withdrawal, medication overuse, side effects), Infectious (meningitis, encephalitis, abscess), Vascular (aneurysm, stroke, SAH, HTN, temporal arteritis), Endocrine (thyroid disease, pheochromocytoma), Neoplastic (tumour), Trauma (TMJ, c-spine injury, subdural hematoma)

Secondary headaches require urgent evaluation as they may indicate serious or life-threatening conditions.

**Q:** What is the management for hearing impairment?

**A:** Counsel about noise control/hearing protection, treat sudden unilateral sensorineural hearing loss urgently with steroids, consider blood glucose/CBC/TSH/syphilis testing for unexplained sensorineural loss, MRI for progressive asymmetric loss (exclude vestibular schwannoma), refer to ENT for unknown etiology or sudden hearing loss, hearing aids/assistive devices/cochlear implants

Management depends on etiology and may include amplification devices which significantly improve quality of life.

**Q:** What is the management of sudden sensorineural hearing loss?

**A:** Urgent treatment with steroids; consider glucose, CBC, TSH, syphilis testing; MRI to exclude vestibular schwannoma

Sudden onset requires urgent ENT referral for potential steroid treatment and workup.

**Q:** What is the management for sudden unilateral sensorineural hearing loss?

**A:** Urgent treatment with steroids; consider blood glucose, CBC, TSH, syphilis testing; refer to ENT for MRI to exclude vestibular schwannoma

Sudden sensorineural hearing loss is an ENT emergency requiring prompt evaluation and treatment to preserve hearing.

**Q:** What is the management of hearing impairment?

**A:** Counsel about noise control and hearing protection, treat sudden unilateral sensorineural hearing loss urgently with steroids, consider blood glucose/CBC/TSH/syphilis testing for unexplained sensorineural loss, consider MRI for progressive asymmetric sensorineural loss to exclude vestibular schwannoma, refer to ENT specialist, amplification (hearing aids), assistive listening devices, cochlear implants

Management includes prevention, treatment of reversible causes, and rehabilitation.

**Q:** List the conditions strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, and chronic inflammatory conditions (e.g., IBD)

These conditions can cause bone loss through various mechanisms including hormonal imbalances, malabsorption, or chronic inflammation.

**Q:** List 5 conditions strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, chronic inflammatory conditions (IBD)

These conditions affect bone metabolism through various mechanisms including hormone imbalances, nutrient malabsorption, and chronic inflammation.

**Q:** List 10 conditions strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition/malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, IBD

These conditions are strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis and should prompt earlier BMD testing.

**Q:** List 8 conditions strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, and chronic inflammatory conditions (IBD)

These conditions are known to cause secondary osteoporosis through various mechanisms affecting bone metabolism.

**Q:** Which conditions are strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, and chronic inflammatory conditions (IBD)

These conditions are recognized by Canadian guidelines as causes of secondary osteoporosis requiring specific management.

**Q:** What are the primary risk factors strongly associated with osteoporosis?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease

These conditions directly affect bone metabolism and are strongly associated with the development of osteoporosis.

**Q:** What are causes of secondary osteoporosis?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism, Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition/malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, IBD, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta

These conditions increase bone loss through various mechanisms and should be considered when evaluating patients with osteoporosis, especially those with typical risk factors.

**Q:** What is the management approach to hearing loss?

**A:** Counsel about noise control and hearing protection, treat sudden unilateral sensorineural hearing loss urgently with steroids, consider blood glucose, CBC, TSH, syphilis testing for unexplained sensorineural loss, consider MRI for progressive asymmetric loss to exclude vestibular schwannoma, refer to ENT for unknown etiology or sudden hearing loss, and consider hearing aids, assistive devices, or cochlear implants

Management depends on etiology. Sudden sensorineural hearing loss is a medical emergency requiring prompt ENT referral.

**Q:** What are the conditions strongly associated with secondary osteoporosis?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, and chronic inflammatory conditions (e.g., IBD)

These conditions can cause bone loss through various mechanisms including hormonal imbalances, malabsorption, or chronic inflammation.

**Q:** What are the secondary causes of osteoporosis strongly associated with the condition?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism, TIDM, osteogenesis imperfecta, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hypogonadism or premature menopause (<45 yr), Cushing's disease, chronic malnutrition or malabsorption, chronic liver disease, COPD, and chronic inflammatory conditions (e.g., IBD)

These conditions either affect bone remodeling, hormone levels, or nutrient absorption necessary for bone health.

**Q:** What is prucalopride and how does it work?

**A:** A 5-HT4 inhibitor prokinetic agent used for constipation

Prucalopride stimulates gut motility by acting as a serotonin receptor agonist. Used for chronic constipation.

**Q:** What metabolic causes of constipation should be considered?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, uremia

Metabolic disorders can affect gut motility through various mechanisms including autonomic neuropathy (DM) and electrolyte disturbances.

**Q:** What is prucalopride and what is its mechanism?

**A:** Prokinetic agent, 5-HT4 inhibitor, used for chronic constipation

Prucalopride stimulates intestinal motility through serotonin receptor agonism.

**Q:** What are the metabolic causes of constipation?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, and uremia.

Metabolic derangements affect gut motility through various mechanisms including autonomic dysfunction (DM) and electrolyte disturbances.

**Q:** What are prokinetic agents used for constipation and give an example?

**A:** Prucalopride - a 5-HT4 inhibitor that enhances gut motility. Used when other treatments fail.

Prucalopride stimulates 5-HT4 receptors in the GI tract, increasing peristalsis and reducing transit time. It is used for chronic idiopathic constipation.

**Q:** What metabolic causes can lead to constipation?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, and uremia

Metabolic disorders affect gut motility through various mechanisms including autonomic dysfunction, slowed metabolism, and electrolyte imbalances.

**Q:** List metabolic causes of constipation

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, uremia

Metabolic disturbances affect gut motility and can cause constipation.

**Q:** What prokinetic agent is used for constipation?

**A:** Prucalopride, a 5-HT4 inhibitor

Prucalopride stimulates colonic motility to improve transit.

**Q:** Name 3 metabolic causes of constipation

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, uremia

Metabolic disturbances affect gut motility and should be considered in the evaluation of constipation.

**Q:** What are the common metabolic causes of constipation?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, and uremia

Metabolic abnormalities affect bowel motility. Hypothyroidism and hypercalcemia are particularly well-known causes of slowed transit.

**Q:** What is prucalopride and what is its mechanism of action?

**A:** Prucalopride is a prokinetic agent and 5-HT4 inhibitor used for constipation

Prucalopride stimulates colonic motility by acting as a selective serotonin 5-HT4 receptor agonist.

**Q:** What are prokinetic agents used for constipation?

**A:** Prucalopride - a 5-HT4 inhibitor

Prucalopride stimulates gut motility by activating 5-HT4 receptors.

**Q:** What metabolic causes of constipation are mentioned?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, uremia

Metabolic abnormalities affect gut motility and can cause or contribute to constipation.

**Q:** What is the role of TSH in constipation investigation?

**A:** Consider TSH to rule out hypothyroidism as metabolic cause of constipation

Hypothyroidism is a treatable metabolic cause of constipation and should be considered in the workup.

**Q:** What are the common causes of constipation?

**A:** Most common: functional/idiopathic. Medication side effects (narcotics, antidepressants) most common organic cause. Also: DM, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, Parkinson disease, MS, scleroderma, anal fissures.

A careful medication review is essential as drug-induced constipation is very common and often reversible.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for patients with PBC?

**A:** Liver enzymes and LFTs q3-6mo, TSH annually, BMD q2-4yr (pending baseline)

Regular monitoring of liver function, thyroid function (autoimmune association), and bone density (high osteoporosis risk) is essential.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for PBC patients?

**A:** Liver enzymes and LFTs q3-6 months, TSH annually, BMD q2-4 years (pending baseline)

Regular monitoring tracks disease progression and treatment response. Thyroid disease and osteoporosis are common associations requiring surveillance.

**Q:** Which autoimmune conditions are associated with PBC?

**A:** Sjogren's syndrome, scleroderma, CREST syndrome (calcinosis, Raynaud's, esophageal dysfunction, sclerodactyly, telangiectasia), RA, thyroiditis

PBC has strong autoimmune associations, particularly in middle-aged women

**Q:** When is preoperative antibiotic prophylaxis NOT required?

**A:** Low-risk or clean surgery such as elective thyroidectomy, cholecystectomy, hemorrhoidectomy, fistulotomy, and sphincterotomy for fissure.

These procedures have very low SSI rates and prophylactic antibiotics provide minimal benefit while adding risk of antibiotic complications.

**Q:** What prokinetic and secretagogue agents are used for constipation?

**A:** Prokinetic: prucalopride (5-HT4 inhibitor). Secretagogues: linaclotide, plecanatide (increase water secretion into intestinal lumen). NHE3 inhibitor: tenapanor.

These agents are used when simpler laxatives fail; they target specific mechanisms of constipation.

**Q:** List causes of hypoventilation leading to hypercapnia

**A:** Restrictive: chest wall disorders (e.g., severe kyphoscoliosis); Central: brainstem stroke, hypothyroidism, metabolic alkalosis, drugs (opiates, benzodiazepines); Neuromuscular: myasthenia gravis, Guillain-Barré, phrenic nerve injury, muscular dystrophy, polymyositis

Hypoventilation means low minute ventilation - either the brain fails to drive breathing or the muscles cannot respond to the drive.

**Q:** What extraglandular manifestations can occur in Sjogren's syndrome?

**A:** Fatigue, low-grade fever, autoimmune thyroid dysfunction, arthralgias/arthritis, subclinical ILD, xerotrachea with dry cough, renal disease/glomerulonephritis, palpable purpura, vasculitis, peripheral neuropathy

Systemic involvement occurs in ~20% of patients, affecting multiple organ systems.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for pseudogout (CPPD)?

**A:** Old age, advanced osteoarthritis, neuropathic joints, hyperparathyroidism, hypothyroidism, hypomagnesemia, hypophosphatasia, hemochromatosis

CPPD is associated with metabolic disorders and typically affects elderly patients.

**Q:** What skin findings are associated with hypothyroidism?

**A:** Cool, dry, scaly, thickened, hyperpigmented skin; coarse dry hair; brittle nails; non-pitting myxedema; alopecia (including loss of lateral 1/3 of eyebrows — 'Queen Anne's sign').

Hypothyroid skin changes result from mucopolysaccharide deposition in the dermis (myxedema). TSH is the key screening test. Loss of the lateral eyebrows is a classic but non-specific sign.

**Q:** What skin findings are specific to hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Moist warm skin, pretibial myxedema (in Graves'), acropachy, onycholysis, toxic alopecia, hyperpigmentation, seborrhea, acne.

Pretibial myxedema and acropachy (clubbing-like periosteal new bone formation) are pathognomonic of Graves' disease. Onycholysis in hyperthyroidism is called Plummer's nails.

**Q:** What is a thyroid nodule and what is its prevalence on ultrasound?

**A:** A discrete lesion distinguishable sonographically from the rest of the thyroid parenchyma. Prevalence is 19–67% based on incidentally found nodules on ultrasound.

Thyroid nodules are common incidental findings. The TIRADS scoring system (Thyroid Imaging Reporting and Data System) uses ultrasound characteristics to stratify which nodules require biopsy, avoiding unnecessary procedures for benign or very small low-risk nodules.

**Q:** What is the TIRADS scoring system used for in the evaluation of thyroid nodules?

**A:** TIRADS is a scoring system based on ultrasound characteristics used to determine which thyroid nodules require biopsy. Benign or very small low-risk nodules do not need ongoing surveillance; small suspicious nodules require up to 5 years of surveillance; larger suspicious nodules require biopsy.

TIRADS stratifies thyroid nodule management by sonographic risk features (composition, echogenicity, shape, margins, echogenic foci). This systematic approach avoids overinvestigation while identifying malignant nodules early.

**Q:** In the evaluation of a thyroid nodule with LOW TSH, what is the next step?

**A:** Perform a radionuclide thyroid scan. A 'hot' nodule (autonomous) is treated as hyperthyroidism. A 'cold' nodule proceeds to ultrasound-guided biopsy based on US characteristics.

Low TSH suggests a hyperfunctioning nodule. Hot nodules on scintigraphy are almost never malignant. Cold nodules carry a higher malignancy risk and require further evaluation with ultrasound and biopsy.

## Adrenal Disorders & Cushing's

**Q:** A 45-year-old has hypertension, central obesity, moon face, buffalo hump, wide purple striae, and hyperglycemia. What is the diagnosis and screening test?

**A:** Cushing syndrome; screen with: 24h urine free cortisol, late-night salivary cortisol (x2), or 1mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test (failure to suppress cortisol to <50 nmol/L)

Endogenous Cushing syndrome most commonly from pituitary ACTH-secreting adenoma (Cushing disease, 65%). Distinguish ACTH-dependent (pituitary or ectopic ACTH) from independent (adrenal adenoma) with plasma ACTH. CRH stimulation and high-dose dexamethasone test to differentiate.

**Q:** What is the approach to primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease)?

**A:** Primary AI: low cortisol + elevated ACTH + hyponatremia + hyperkalemia + hyperpigmentation; diagnose with cosyntropin stimulation test (peak cortisol <500 nmol/L); treat with hydrocortisone + fludrocortisone

Addison disease is most commonly autoimmune in developed countries. Adrenal crisis precipitated by illness/surgery/stress requires stress dosing. Patients must carry medical alert bracelet. Emergency management: IV hydrocortisone 100mg stat, IV fluids.

**Q:** A patient on prednisone 20mg/day for 6 months is having elective surgery. How should perioperative steroids be managed?

**A:** Stress-dose corticosteroids: hydrocortisone 50-100mg IV q8h on day of surgery and taper to usual dose over 1-3 days depending on surgical stress (major surgery: 3-5 days)

HPA axis suppression occurs after ≥4 weeks of ≥20mg/day prednisone equivalent. Adrenal crisis with perioperative hypotension is potentially fatal. Any dose of exogenous steroid for >3 weeks requires stress dosing for major procedures. Minor procedures require modest increases.

**Q:** What is a pheochromocytoma and what is the pre-operative management?

**A:** Catecholamine-secreting adrenal tumor causing hypertensive crises; diagnose with plasma free metanephrines; pre-operative management: alpha-blockade (phenoxybenzamine) ≥2 weeks, then beta-blockade, high-sodium diet

Phenoxybenzamine (non-selective irreversible alpha-blocker) is standard pre-op preparation; alpha-1 selective blockers (doxazosin) also used. Never start beta-blocker before alpha-blocker — may cause hypertensive crisis (unopposed alpha stimulation). IV saline to expand volume pre-operatively.

**Q:** How is secondary adrenal insufficiency differentiated from primary?

**A:** Secondary (pituitary): low ACTH, low cortisol, NO hyperkalemia or hyperpigmentation (aldosterone axis intact); Primary (adrenal): high ACTH, low cortisol, hyperkalemia, hyperpigmentation

Secondary AI: pituitary causes (ACTH deficiency), usually from long-term exogenous steroids causing HPA suppression, or pituitary tumor/surgery. No mineralocorticoid deficiency (aldosterone regulated by RAS). Treatment: hydrocortisone alone (no fludrocortisone needed).

**Q:** What is congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH) and what is the most common enzyme deficiency?

**A:** CAH: autosomal recessive disorder of adrenal steroidogenesis; most common: 21-hydroxylase deficiency (90%): cortisol and aldosterone deficiency, excess androgens — virilization and salt-wasting in classic form

21-hydroxylase deficiency: low cortisol → ACTH excess → adrenal hyperplasia → androgen excess. Classic salt-wasting: ambiguous genitalia in females, adrenal crisis in neonates. Treatment: glucocorticoid replacement suppresses ACTH and androgen excess. Newborn screening in Canada.

**Q:** What are the indications for adrenalectomy for an adrenal incidentaloma?

**A:** Adrenalectomy for: functioning tumors (cortisol-secreting, pheochromocytoma, aldosteronoma), malignant features (>4cm, heterogeneous, irregular, >10 HU on unenhanced CT), rapid growth (>1cm/year)

Adrenal incidentalomas found in ~5% of abdominal CT scans. All incidentalomas require biochemical screening: 1mg DST, plasma metanephrines, aldosterone/renin ratio (if hypertensive). Non-functioning adenomas <4cm: repeat CT in 6-12 months, then annually for 3 years.

**Q:** What is Cushing syndrome diagnostic approach?

**A:** Screen: 24h urine cortisol, 1mg dex suppression, late-night salivary cortisol. Two positive tests needed

Then ACTH: low=adrenal. High=pituitary vs ectopic. High-dose dex + IPSS differentiates.

**Q:** What is the Cushing syndrome diagnostic workup?

**A:** Screen (2 of 3 positive): 24h urine free cortisol, late-night salivary cortisol ×2, 1mg overnight DST (cortisol >50 = positive). Then ACTH: high→pituitary MRI±IPSS; low→adrenal CT.

Cushing disease = pituitary ACTH adenoma (70%). Ectopic ACTH (15%): SCLC, carcinoid. Adrenal (15%): adenoma/carcinoma. High-dose DST suppresses pituitary not ectopic. IPSS differentiates pituitary from ectopic.

**Q:** What is primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison) and its diagnosis?

**A:** Adrenal cortex destruction→cortisol+aldosterone+androgen deficiency. Features: fatigue, hypotension, hyperpigmentation, salt craving, hyperK, hypoNa. Diagnosis: cosyntropin stim test (250μg IV)→cortisol <500 at 30-60 min confirms.

Most common cause in Canada: autoimmune (anti-21-hydroxylase Ab). Secondary AI: NO hyperpigmentation, NO hyperK (aldosterone intact). Treatment: hydrocortisone 15-25 mg/day + fludrocortisone 0.05-0.2 mg daily.

**Q:** What is adrenal crisis management?

**A:** Acute cortisol deficiency: refractory hypotension/shock, AMS, fever, abdominal pain, hyperK, hypoNa, hypoglycemia. Treatment: IV hydrocortisone 100 mg bolus then 50-100 mg q8h + aggressive IV NS. Do NOT delay for diagnostics.

Precipitants: stress in undiagnosed/under-replaced AI, steroid withdrawal, bilateral adrenal hemorrhage (Waterhouse-Friderichsen). Dexamethasone 4 mg IV if diagnosis uncertain (doesn't interfere with cosyntropin test).

**Q:** How is pheochromocytoma diagnosed and managed perioperatively?

**A:** Screen: plasma free metanephrines (most sensitive). Localize: CT/MRI abdomen. Pre-op: alpha blockade first (phenoxybenzamine/doxazosin) ×10-14 days, THEN beta blockade. Never beta-block first (unopposed alpha→crisis). Genetic testing for all (~40% hereditary).

Rule of 10s outdated. Associated with MEN2, VHL, SDH mutations. Intraoperative: sodium nitroprusside or phentolamine for hypertensive crisis. Post-op: monitor for hypotension (loss of catecholamine drive) and hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What is primary hyperaldosteronism screening and confirmation?

**A:** Screen (ARR) if: resistant HTN, hypoK, adrenal incidentaloma. Confirm: salt loading test or fludrocortisone suppression. Differentiate: CT + adrenal vein sampling. Unilateral adenoma→adrenalectomy. Bilateral hyperplasia→spironolactone/eplerenone.

Conn syndrome = 5-10% of hypertension. Stop interfering drugs before ARR (spironolactone 6 weeks). Adrenalectomy cures HTN in ~50% of unilateral cases.

**Q:** What is the approach to adrenal incidentaloma?

**A:** 4-5% of CT scans. Assess: 1) Function: plasma metanephrines (pheo), 1mg DST (Cushing), ARR if hypertensive. 2) Malignancy: >4cm, HU >10, irregular→surgery. HU <10 on non-contrast CT = benign adenoma (~100%).

Metastases common in known extra-adrenal cancer — biopsy only if changes management (rule out pheo first!). Follow-up: repeat imaging 6-12 months, repeat functional testing annually ×4 years.

**Q:** What is CAH and its most common form?

**A:** 21-hydroxylase deficiency (>90%): AR. Classic: salt-wasting crisis in neonates + female virilization. Non-classic: hirsutism/oligomenorrhea in women. Diagnosis: elevated 17-OHP. Treatment: hydrocortisone + fludrocortisone (salt-wasting). Newborn screening in all Canadian provinces.

Cortisol deficiency→ACTH excess→adrenal hyperplasia→androgen excess. Stress dosing needed during illness. Non-classic: low-dose dexamethasone or OCP for hirsutism.

**Q:** What are sick day/stress dose steroid rules?

**A:** Minor illness: double/triple oral hydrocortisone ×2-3 days. Moderate stress: 50 mg IM/IV pre-procedure. Major surgery: 100 mg IV q8h, taper post-op. Vomiting: IM hydrocortisone 100 mg + ER.

All AI patients need MedicAlert + emergency IM hydrocortisone kit. Family trained for injection. Also applies to chronic steroid users (≥5mg prednisone ×3+ weeks). Failure to stress dose→adrenal crisis.

**Q:** What is secondary adrenal insufficiency from exogenous steroids?

**A:** Most common cause of AI. HPA suppression after ≥3 weeks prednisone ≥5 mg/day. Taper 10-20% q1-2 weeks. At physiologic dose, check morning cortisol or cosyntropin test. NO hyperpigmentation, NO hyperK (aldosterone intact).

Even inhaled/topical steroids in high doses can suppress HPA, especially in children. Recovery takes weeks-months. Stress dosing needed until HPA axis recovery confirmed.

**Q:** What are MEN syndromes?

**A:** MEN1 (3Ps): Parathyroid + Pituitary + Pancreatic NET (menin gene). MEN2A: MTC + Pheo + Hyperparathyroidism (RET). MEN2B: MTC + Pheo + Mucosal neuromas + Marfanoid (RET, no HPT).

RET mutation carriers: prophylactic thyroidectomy (timing by mutation risk). Always screen for pheo before any surgery. MEN1: screen for gastrinoma, insulinoma, prolactinoma.

**Q:** What is the workup for an adrenal incidentaloma?

**A:** All: 1mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test (Cushing), plasma metanephrines (pheo), aldosterone/renin ratio if hypertensive (Conn). If >4cm or suspicious features: surgical referral

Prevalence 4-5% on CT. Most are non-functioning adenomas. Imaging features of concern: >4cm, irregular margins, >10 HU on unenhanced CT, interval growth. Follow with repeat imaging at 6-12 months if <4cm.

**Q:** An adrenal incidentaloma is found on CT. What is the workup algorithm?

**A:** All adrenal incidentalomas require two assessments: 1) FUNCTIONAL workup (rule out hormone excess): Pheochromocytoma: 24h urine metanephrines or plasma free metanephrines (MUST rule out first — hypertensive crisis risk); Cushing syndrome: 1 mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test (cortisol <50 nmol/L is normal); Primary aldosteronism (if hypertensive): aldosterone-to-renin ratio; Sex hormones: DHEA-S if virilization signs. 2) MALIGNANCY assessment: Size: <4 cm with benign features (homogeneous, lipid-rich, <10 HU on unenhanced CT) — likely adenoma; >4 cm, irregular, >20 HU, heterogeneous — concerning for malignancy; >6 cm — consider surgical excision. Follow-up: repeat imaging at 6-12 months for stable incidentalomas; repeat functional testing annually for 5 years.

Adrenal incidentalomas are found in ~5% of abdominal CT scans. The most important step is ruling out pheochromocytoma (even in normotensive patients) because undiagnosed pheo can cause fatal hypertensive crisis during surgery or anesthesia. Canadian Endocrine Society guidelines follow European Society of Endocrinology recommendations for workup and follow-up protocols.

**Q:** What is the approach to a patient with suspected pheochromocytoma?

**A:** Classic triad: episodic headache, diaphoresis, palpitations/tachycardia (+ hypertension — often paroxysmal). The '10% rule': 10% bilateral, 10% extra-adrenal (paraganglioma), 10% malignant, 10% familial (MEN2A/2B, VHL, NF1, SDH mutations — actually 30-40% are hereditary). Diagnosis: plasma free metanephrines (most sensitive screening test — >99% sensitivity) or 24h urine metanephrines/catecholamines. Imaging: CT or MRI abdomen/pelvis after biochemical confirmation. Management: alpha-blockade FIRST (phenoxybenzamine or doxazosin for 10-14 days), THEN beta-blockade (propranolol — NEVER beta-block first → unopposed alpha stimulation → hypertensive crisis), volume expansion with salt/fluid loading, then surgical resection. Genetic testing recommended for all patients.

The rule 'alpha before beta' is critical and commonly tested — beta-blockade alone removes beta-2 vasodilation, leaving unopposed alpha vasoconstriction causing severe hypertension. Phenoxybenzamine is an irreversible alpha-blocker preferred preoperatively. Canadian Endocrine Society recommends genetic testing for all pheochromocytoma patients given the high rate of hereditary syndromes.

**Q:** How is primary aldosteronism (Conn syndrome) diagnosed as part of the adrenal incidentaloma workup?

**A:** Suspect if: hypertension (especially resistant), hypokalemia (but most patients are normokalemic), adrenal incidentaloma. Screening: aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR) — elevated aldosterone with suppressed renin (ARR >100 pmol/L per ng/mL/h, or equivalent cutoffs depending on assay). Prepare: hold interfering medications (spironolactone 6 weeks, ACEi/ARB/beta-blockers 2 weeks if possible; use alpha-blockers/verapamil for BP control during testing). Confirmatory testing: oral sodium loading test, IV saline infusion test, or fludrocortisone suppression test (aldosterone fails to suppress). Subtype differentiation: adrenal CT + adrenal vein sampling (AVS) to lateralize — unilateral adenoma (Conn): laparoscopic adrenalectomy; bilateral hyperplasia (more common): mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist (spironolactone or eplerenone).

Primary aldosteronism is the most common cause of secondary hypertension (~5-10% of hypertensive patients). Adrenal vein sampling is essential before surgery because CT alone misclassifies ~40% of cases. Canadian Hypertension Education Program (CHEP/Hypertension Canada) guidelines recommend screening for primary aldosteronism in resistant hypertension.

**Q:** What is the workup for Cushing syndrome as part of adrenal incidentaloma evaluation?

**A:** Screening tests (need 2+ positive for diagnosis): 1) 1 mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test (DST): give 1 mg dexa at 11 PM, measure 8 AM cortisol — normal suppression <50 nmol/L (1.8 μg/dL); 2) 24h urine free cortisol (×2 collections — elevated >3× upper limit suggests Cushing); 3) Late-night salivary cortisol (×2 — loss of diurnal variation). Localization after confirming Cushing syndrome: ACTH level — ACTH-independent (low ACTH): adrenal source (adenoma, carcinoma); ACTH-dependent (normal-high ACTH): pituitary (Cushing disease, 70%) or ectopic (lung small cell, carcinoid). High-dose DST and inferior petrosal sinus sampling (IPSS) differentiate pituitary from ectopic. For adrenal incidentaloma: subclinical Cushing (autonomous cortisol secretion without classic features) is common — may warrant surgery if associated comorbidities (diabetes, hypertension, osteoporosis).

The 1 mg overnight DST is the preferred screening test for adrenal incidentalomas. False positives occur with depression, alcoholism, obesity, OCP use, and medications that increase dexamethasone metabolism (phenytoin, rifampin). Canadian Endocrine Society guidelines recommend monitoring adrenal incidentalomas for subclinical cortisol excess annually.

**Q:** Phenoxybenzamine is used in pheochromocytoma pre-operatively. What type of receptor antagonism does it produce and why is it particularly useful?

**A:** Phenoxybenzamine is an irreversible non-competitive (allosteric) antagonist at adrenergic receptors. It forms a covalent bond, providing sustained blockade that cannot be overcome by surges in catecholamine release during pheochromocytoma manipulation.

Non-competitive antagonists bind to an allosteric site distinct from the active site, reducing maximal agonist effect and lowering the Emax without shifting the EC50. The irreversible covalent bond makes phenoxybenzamine ideal for sustained alpha-blockade in pheochromocytoma.

**Q:** What skin manifestations are seen in Addison's disease (primary adrenal insufficiency)?

**A:** Generalized hyperpigmentation or limited to skin folds, buccal mucosa, and scars due to excess ACTH and MSH stimulation.

In primary adrenal insufficiency, low cortisol leads to elevated ACTH, which cross-reacts with MSH receptors causing hyperpigmentation. The buccal mucosa and scars are classic sites. Secondary adrenal insufficiency does NOT cause hyperpigmentation.

**Q:** What skin manifestation is associated with Cushing's syndrome?

**A:** Moon facies, purple (violaceous) striae, acne, hirsutism, hyperpigmentation, atrophic skin with telangiectasias.

Purple striae (>1 cm wide) in young patients are a classic feature of Cushing's syndrome. The skin atrophy is due to excess glucocorticoids inhibiting fibroblast activity. Screening is with 24-hour urinary free cortisol or overnight dexamethasone suppression test.

**Q:** What are the major endocrine organs and their key hormones?

**A:** Hypothalamus: CRH, GnRH, TRH, GHRH, ADH*, Oxytocin*. Anterior pituitary: GH, PRL, TSH, LH, FSH, ACTH. Posterior pituitary: ADH, Oxytocin. Thyroid: T3, T4. Parathyroid: PTH. Adrenal cortex: aldosterone, cortisol, androgens. Adrenal medulla: catecholamines. Pancreas: insulin, glucagon, somatostatin. Ovaries: estrogen, progesterone. Testes: testosterone. (*ADH and oxytocin are produced in the hypothalamus but stored in the posterior pituitary.)

The hypothalamic-pituitary axis is the master regulator of endocrine function. Releasing hormones from the hypothalamus stimulate or inhibit anterior pituitary hormone secretion, which in turn regulates peripheral gland hormone production (classic negative feedback loops). ADH and oxytocin are synthesized in hypothalamic nuclei and transported axonally to the posterior pituitary for release.

**Q:** What is ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) and what are its key features?

**A:** ACTH is a polypeptide cleaved from the prohormone POMC, secreted in a pulsatile fashion from the anterior pituitary with diurnal variability (peak 0200–0400 h; trough 1800–2400 h). It stimulates adrenal cortex growth and release of glucocorticoids, adrenal androgens, and (to a very limited extent) mineralocorticoids.

ACTH secretion is regulated by CRH and AVP from the hypothalamus. Importantly, ACTH can directly bind MSH receptors on melanocytes, enhancing melanogenesis — explaining hyperpigmentation in primary adrenal insufficiency where ACTH is very high.

**Q:** What are the three zones of the adrenal cortex and what hormones does each produce?

**A:** Zona glomerulosa → mineralocorticoids (aldosterone); Zona fasciculata → glucocorticoids (cortisol); Zona reticularis → sex steroids (DHEA, androstenedione).

Mnemonic: 'GFR' (glomerulosa, fasciculata, reticularis) — Salt, Sugar, Sex. The zona glomerulosa is regulated by RAAS; the fasciculata and reticularis are regulated by ACTH.

**Q:** How is aldosterone regulated and what are its physiological effects?

**A:** Aldosterone is regulated by the RAAS and hyperkalemia. It acts on the distal tubule Na+/K+ ATPase to cause Na+ (and Cl−) retention and K+ (and H+) excretion, expanding extracellular fluid volume.

Negative feedback to the JGA occurs via long loop (aldosterone + volume expansion) and short loop (angiotensin II + peripheral vasoconstriction). Dopamine and prostaglandins also modulate renin release.

**Q:** What are the stimulatory and inhibitory physiological effects of glucocorticoids (cortisol)?

**A:** Stimulatory: hepatic gluconeogenesis, insulin resistance in peripheral tissues, protein catabolism, leukocytosis/lymphopenia, increased cardiac output/vascular tone/Na+ retention, increased PTH release and urine calcium excretion. Inhibitory: bone formation (stimulates resorption), fibroblasts/collagen, inflammation/cell-mediated immunity, GH axis*, reproductive axis*, vitamin D3/calcium uptake. (*Only with excess cortisol.)

These effects explain the clinical manifestations of Cushing's syndrome (hyperglycemia, osteoporosis, immunosuppression, HTN, central obesity) and are relevant to understanding steroid side effects in clinical practice.

**Q:** What are the principal adrenal androgens and what is their main physiological role?

**A:** Principal adrenal androgens: DHEA, androstenedione, and 11-hydroxyandrostenedione. They are regulated by ACTH and are primarily responsible for adrenarche (growth of axillary and pubic hair). Their proportion of total androgens (adrenal:gonadal) increases with age.

Adrenal androgens are weak compared to gonadal testosterone but play a significant role in women's androgenic physiology. Elevated adrenal androgens are seen in CAH, adrenal tumours, and Cushing's disease.

**Q:** How is the Cosyntropin (ACTH analogue) Stimulation Test performed and interpreted?

**A:** Administer 250 mcg cosyntropin IV/IM; measure plasma cortisol before and at 30 and 60 min after. A physiologic response is stimulated plasma cortisol >500 nmol/L (>18 mcg/dL) at 30 or 60 min. An inappropriate response (peak cortisol <500 nmol/L) indicates adrenal insufficiency.

This test assesses glucocorticoid reserve. It is used to diagnose primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease). The threshold may vary with newer assays; confirm with local lab. It does NOT reliably diagnose recent-onset secondary AI.

**Q:** How is the low-dose overnight dexamethasone (DXM) suppression test performed and interpreted?

**A:** Give 1 mg DXM orally between 11 pm and midnight; measure plasma cortisol the next morning at 8–9 am. Physiologic response (normal): cortisol <50 nmol/L (<1.8 mcg/dL). Failure to suppress (cortisol ≥50 nmol/L) is a positive screen for Cushing's syndrome.

False positives occur with obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications inducing DXM metabolism (e.g., rifampin, phenytoin, barbiturates), or oral contraceptives. A positive screen must be confirmed with additional tests.

**Q:** What are three screening tests for Cushing's syndrome and what constitutes a positive result?

**A:** 1) 24-hour urine free cortisol (≥2 tests) — elevated value; 2) Low-dose overnight DXM suppression test — failure to suppress cortisol below 50 nmol/L; 3) Late-night salivary cortisol (≥2 tests) — cortisol >145 ng/dL (4 nmol/L) at midnight (lacks normal diurnal nadir).

At least one of these tests must be performed; confirm with one of the remaining. Late-night salivary cortisol is convenient and non-invasive. Reasons for false positives include pregnancy, depression, alcoholism, morbid obesity, poorly controlled DM, and intense chronic exercise.

**Q:** What is Primary Aldosteronism (PA) and what are its clinical features?

**A:** PA is excess aldosterone production from an intra-adrenal cause. Clinical features: hypertension, hypokalemia (+ mild hypernatremia), metabolic alkalosis, fatigue, weakness, paresthesia, headache. Severe hypokalemia (<2.5 mEq/L) may cause tetany or intermittent paralysis. Increased cardiovascular risk (LV hypertrophy, AF, stroke, MI) and elevated risk of metabolic syndrome/T2DM.

PA is the most common cause of secondary hypertension. Unlike secondary hyperaldosteronism, PA features elevated aldosterone with suppressed renin (high aldosterone:renin ratio). Secondary hyperaldosteronism shows elevated aldosterone AND elevated renin.

**Q:** When should plasma aldosterone:renin ratio be investigated for primary aldosteronism (PA)?

**A:** Investigate in patients with: (1) HTN and hypokalemia; (2) drug-resistant HTN; (3) HTN and a first-degree relative with PA; (4) HTN and family history of stroke in a first-degree relative <40 yr; (5) HTN and adrenal incidentaloma.

PA is underdiagnosed. A positive screen (elevated aldosterone:renin ratio with high aldosterone) requires confirmatory testing demonstrating inappropriate aldosterone secretion with ECF volume expansion. Adrenal vein sampling may be needed to lateralize disease.

**Q:** How does the aldosterone:renin ratio differ between primary and secondary hyperaldosteronism?

**A:** Primary hyperaldosteronism: elevated aldosterone:renin ratio (high aldosterone, LOW/suppressed renin). Secondary hyperaldosteronism: normal aldosterone:renin ratio (high aldosterone, HIGH renin — both elevated in response to RAAS activation).

This distinction is critical. In PA, autonomous aldosterone secretion suppresses renin via negative feedback. In secondary hyperaldosteronism (e.g., renal artery stenosis, heart failure), RAAS is overactivated, so both aldosterone and renin are elevated.

**Q:** What is the confirmatory test for primary aldosteronism and how is it interpreted?

**A:** IV saline loading test (confirmatory): infuse 500 mL/h of NS for 4 hours, then measure plasma aldosterone. Plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L confirms PA; <140 pmol/L is normal (indeterminate: 140–277 pmol/L). Oral salt loading with 24-hour urine aldosterone can also be used.

ECF volume expansion normally suppresses aldosterone. Failure to suppress confirms autonomous aldosterone secretion. Multiple medications (e.g., spironolactone, eplerenone, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics) can interfere with screening/confirmatory tests and may need to be held prior to testing.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for primary aldosteronism?

**A:** 1) Aldosterone-producing adenoma (Conn's): surgical excision (adrenalectomy) — curative. 2) Medical management: aldosterone antagonists — spironolactone or eplerenone (first-line); triamterene or amiloride (act on sodium channels). 3) Secondary hyperaldosteronism: treat underlying cause.

Spironolactone is the preferred medical treatment; eplerenone has fewer anti-androgenic side effects. Bilateral adrenal hyperplasia is typically managed medically rather than surgically. Adrenal vein sampling is required to confirm lateralization before surgery.

**Q:** What is Cushing's syndrome and what are its major causes by ACTH-dependency?

**A:** Cushing's syndrome is a metabolic disorder caused by chronic glucocorticoid excess. ACTH-dependent (85%): ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma — Cushing's disease (80% of ACTH-dependent); ectopic ACTH-secreting tumour (e.g., small cell lung carcinoma). ACTH-independent (15%): primary adrenocortical tumours (adenoma/carcinoma); bilateral adrenal nodular hyperplasia; iatrogenic (most common overall — exogenous glucocorticoids).

Iatrogenic Cushing's is likely more common than endogenous disease. Distinguishing ACTH-dependent from ACTH-independent causes requires measuring ACTH levels and imaging. Cushing's disease specifically refers to pituitary ACTH hypersecretion.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of Cushing's syndrome?

**A:** Symptoms: weakness, insomnia, mood disorders, impaired cognition, easy bruising, oligomenorrhea/amenorrhea, hirsutism, acne (ACTH-dependent). Signs: central obesity, moon face, supraclavicular and dorsal fat pads (buffalo hump), facial plethora, proximal muscle wasting, purple abdominal striae, skin atrophy, acanthosis nigricans, HTN, hyperglycemia, osteoporosis/pathologic fractures, hyperpigmentation (if ACTH-dependent), hyperandrogenism (if ACTH-dependent).

Purple striae (vs. pink/white stretch marks in obesity) are characteristic. Hyperpigmentation is a feature of high ACTH states (Cushing's disease, ectopic ACTH) due to ACTH binding MSH receptors — it is NOT seen in ACTH-independent causes.

**Q:** What is the treatment of Cushing's syndrome based on etiology?

**A:** Adrenal adenoma: unilateral adrenalectomy (curative) + postoperative glucocorticoid supplementation (taper slowly). Adrenal carcinoma: adrenalectomy if localized; adjunctive mitotane for high recurrence risk; mitotane + chemo for metastatic disease. Pituitary (Cushing's disease): transsphenoidal resection + postoperative glucocorticoid supplementation; if failed/contraindicated: ketoconazole, mitotane, pasireotide, cabergoline. Ectopic ACTH: surgical resection if possible; chemo/radiation for primary; medical: mitotane or ketoconazole.

All surgical patients need perioperative glucocorticoid supplementation because the suppressed contralateral adrenal requires time to recover. Medical management (ketoconazole) inhibits cortisol synthesis and is used while awaiting surgery or when surgery is not possible.

**Q:** A patient has drug-resistant HTN and a low potassium. Investigations reveal elevated plasma aldosterone with suppressed renin. What is the likely diagnosis and most appropriate next step?

**A:** Likely diagnosis: primary aldosteronism (PA). Next step: confirmatory testing — IV saline loading test (500 mL/h NS for 4 hours; plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L confirms PA) and CT adrenal glands; then adrenal vein sampling if CT is non-diagnostic to lateralize aldosterone excess before surgical planning.

An elevated aldosterone:renin ratio with high absolute aldosterone in the context of hypertension warrants confirmation. CT helps identify an adenoma (Conn's) vs. bilateral hyperplasia. Adrenal vein sampling is the gold standard for lateralization and determines surgical candidacy.

**Q:** What is hyperandrogenism and what are its main etiologies in women?

**A:** Hyperandrogenism is excessive secretion of androgens (DHEA, DHEA-sulfate, testosterone). Main etiologies in women: Ovarian (most common) — PCOS, ovarian hyperthecosis, theca cell tumours; Adrenal — CAH (late-onset), adrenal tumours (adenoma/carcinoma); Pituitary — Cushing's disease (high ACTH), hyperprolactinemia; Medications — anabolic steroids, ACTH, androgens, progestational agents.

PCOS is the most common cause of hyperandrogenism in women of reproductive age. It is important to differentiate true hirsutism (androgen-dependent hair) from hypertrichosis (drug-induced, non-androgenic) and distinguish from virilization, which suggests a more severe/tumoral cause.

**Q:** What is the Ferriman-Gallwey scoring system and how is it used to assess hirsutism?

**A:** A standardized scoring system to quantify severity of androgen-dependent (male-pattern) terminal body hair in women. Score >8 is abnormal for White/Black women; >9 for Mediterranean/Hispanic/Middle-Eastern women; >2 for Asian women. Scores 8–15: mild; 16–25: moderate; >25: severe hirsutism. Scores should be interpreted considering cosmetic hair removal practices.

Ethnic-specific thresholds reflect the variation in baseline hair density. This system specifically assesses androgen-sensitive areas (face, back, chest, upper abdomen, linea alba). Drug-induced hypertrichosis (phenytoin, cyclosporine, minoxidil, diazoxide) is NOT true hirsutism — it is non-androgenic generalized hair growth.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated in a woman presenting with hirsutism?

**A:** First-line: testosterone (total and free), DHEA-S (measures adrenal androgen production), LH/FSH (LH:FSH >2.5 suggests PCOS). 17-OH progesterone (elevated in CAH due to 21-hydroxylase deficiency — check on day 3 of menstrual cycle with a progesterone level). If virilization present: CT/MRI of adrenals and ovaries to identify tumours. If PCOS suspected: fasting blood glucose and lipids.

17-OH progesterone is the key test for non-classic (late-onset) CAH. DHEA-S elevation suggests adrenal source. Very high testosterone (>5–7 nmol/L) or rapid onset virilization warrants urgent tumour workup. Virilization (clitoromegaly, voice deepening, frontal balding) suggests significant androgen excess.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for hyperandrogenism (hirsutism)?

**A:** 1) Discontinue causative medications (DHEA, valproate, danazol). 2) Antiandrogens: spironolactone (first-line medical therapy). 3) Oral contraceptives (increase SHBG, bind androgens > estrogens; reduce ovarian androgen production). 4) Surgical resection if tumour identified. 5) Glucocorticoid + mineralocorticoid if CAH confirmed. 6) Treat specific causative disorders (Cushing's, tumours). 7) Cosmetic therapy (laser, electrolysis).

In PCOS, combined OCP + spironolactone is often used. OCP reduces LH-driven androgen production and increases SHBG. Spironolactone blocks androgen receptors and inhibits 5-alpha reductase. Metformin helps with insulin resistance in PCOS but has modest effects on hirsutism.

**Q:** What are the causes of primary adrenocortical insufficiency (Addison's disease)?

**A:** Autoimmune (70–90%): isolated Addison's disease or polyglandular autoimmune syndromes types I and II. Infections: TB (7–20%, most common in developing world), fungal (histoplasmosis, paracoccidioidomycosis), HIV, CMV, syphilis. Infiltrative: metastatic cancer (lung > stomach > esophagus > colon > breast), lymphoma, sarcoidosis, amyloidosis, hemochromatosis. Vascular: bilateral adrenal hemorrhage (heparin/warfarin risk), sepsis (meningococcal, Pseudomonas), Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome (children), thrombosis/infarction. Drugs: ketoconazole, etomidate, megestrol acetate (inhibit cortisol); rifampin, phenytoin, barbiturates (increase cortisol metabolism).

Autoimmune is the most common cause in developed countries. Bilateral adrenal hemorrhage in the context of anticoagulation or meningococcemia (Waterhouse-Friderichsen) is a medical emergency. Adrenoleukodystrophy is a rare genetic cause presenting in young males.

**Q:** How do primary and secondary adrenal insufficiency differ in clinical features and electrolytes?

**A:** Primary AI (Addison's): hyperpigmentation (high ACTH stimulates MSH receptors), hyperkalemia, hyponatremia, metabolic acidosis. Associated with primary hypothyroidism, T1DM, vitiligo. Diagnosed with Cosyntropin stimulation test + high morning ACTH + high renin. Secondary AI: pale skin (low ACTH), normokalemia, hyponatremia, NO metabolic acidosis, NO mineralocorticoid deficiency. Associated with central hypogonadism/hypothyroidism, GH deficiency, DI. Diagnosed with cosyntropin stimulation test + low/inappropriately normal morning ACTH.

In primary AI, mineralocorticoid (aldosterone) is also deficient → hyperkalemia, metabolic acidosis, salt craving, and hypotension. In secondary AI, aldosterone secretion is relatively preserved (still responsive to RAAS) → no hyperkalemia or metabolic acidosis.

**Q:** How is an acute adrenal crisis managed?

**A:** 1) IV NS 1L within the first hour, then continuous IV NS guided by requirements; add D5W if hypoglycemic. 2) Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV stat, then 50 mg IV q6h. 3) Identify and correct precipitating factors (infection, surgery, trauma, medication non-compliance).

Adrenal crisis is life-threatening and must be treated empirically if suspected — do not delay treatment to obtain cortisol levels. Precipitating factors must be identified and treated. After stabilization, transition to oral hydrocortisone and investigate for the underlying cause of AI.

**Q:** What is the maintenance treatment for chronic primary adrenal insufficiency?

**A:** Glucocorticoid: hydrocortisone 15–25 mg/day PO (or cortisone acetate 20–35 mg/day) in 2–3 divided doses, with the highest dose in the morning (mimics diurnal cortisol peak). Alternatively, prednisolone 3–5 mg once daily or BID for improved compliance. Mineralocorticoid: fludrocortisone (Florinef) 0.05–0.2 mg PO once daily (required in primary AI only). Stress dosing: increase steroids 2–3 fold during moderate-severe illness; hydrocortisone 150–300 mg/day IV divided q8h for major stress (surgery, trauma). Medical alert bracelet and emergency injectable hydrocortisone/dexamethasone.

Fludrocortisone is essential in primary AI to replace aldosterone (prevents hyperkalemia, volume depletion). It is NOT needed in secondary AI. Sick day rules (doubling/tripling dose) and emergency injection kits are critical patient education elements for safety.

**Q:** What is pheochromocytoma and what is the classic clinical triad?

**A:** Pheochromocytoma is a catecholamine-secreting tumour derived from chromaffin cells of the adrenal gland. Classic triad (not found in most patients): episodic 'pounding' headache, palpitations/tachycardia, and diaphoresis. 50% have paroxysmal HTN; others have sustained HTN.

Pheo is a rare cause of HTN (<0.2% of all hypertensives). Symptoms may be triggered by stress, exertion, anesthesia, abdominal pressure, or tyramine-containing foods (aged cheese, cured meats). Other signs include orthostatic hypotension, papilledema, hyperglycemia, and dilated cardiomyopathy.

**Q:** What genetic syndromes are associated with pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma?

**A:** 30–40% of cases are linked to germline mutations. Key syndromes (all autosomal dominant): MEN2A/2B (RET mutation, 50% frequency) — most common hereditary association; von Hippel-Lindau (VHL, 10–20%); Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1, 0.1–5.7%). Other mutations: SDHx, SDHAF2. All patients are currently offered genetic testing.

Pheochromocytomas and paragangliomas have the greatest genetic inheritance among neuroendocrine tumours. Hereditary forms present earlier and are more likely bilateral. Probability of germline disease increases with young age, multifocal disease, or paraganglioma. RET genetic screening is standard of care in MEN2.

**Q:** How is pheochromocytoma diagnosed biochemically?

**A:** First-line: urine metanephrines (increased catecholamine metabolites — metanephrines and catecholamines). Plasma metanephrines (most sensitive, if available). Cut-off values depend on assay used. Imaging: CT abdomen; if negative, whole-body CT + MIBG scintigraphy, Octreoscan, or MRI.

Plasma free metanephrines have higher sensitivity than urine metanephrines but lower specificity. MIBG (meta-iodo-benzylguanidine) scintigraphy is highly specific for functional chromaffin tissue and is used to locate extra-adrenal or metastatic disease. Urinary VMA and HVA are older tests, now less used.

**Q:** What is the preoperative preparation for surgical resection of pheochromocytoma?

**A:** 1) Alpha-blockade FIRST (10–21 days preoperatively): doxazosin or phenoxybenzamine (most commonly used); IV phentolamine perioperatively if needed. 2) Beta-blockade ONLY after adequate alpha-blockade (if needed for HR control — adding beta-blocker before alpha-blocker risks severe hypertension). 3) Metyrosine (catecholamine synthesis inhibitor) + alpha-blocker. 4) Volume restoration with vigorous salt-loading and IV fluids. 5) Monitor for hypoglycemia postoperatively. 6) Rescreen urine 1–3 months postoperatively.

Alpha-blockade first is critical — unopposed alpha receptors (with beta-blockade alone) cause paradoxical severe vasoconstriction and hypertensive crisis. Preoperative volume loading counteracts the vasoconstriction and prepares for the hypotension that follows catecholamine surge removal at surgery.

**Q:** What is MEN1 and what are its classic three components (3 Ps)?

**A:** MEN1 (chromosome 11, autosomal dominant) — the 3 Ps: Pituitary adenomas (30–40%) — most commonly prolactinomas; also GH, ACTH-secreting, non-functional. Parathyroid (>95%) — primary hyperparathyroidism from hyperplasia or adenomas (most common manifestation). Pancreas/entero-pancreatic endocrine (30–80%) — gastrinoma (most common functional tumour), insulinoma, VIPoma, glucagonoma, carcinoid.

Parathyroid disease is the most common and earliest manifestation of MEN1. Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison syndrome) presents with refractory peptic ulcers. Insulinoma causes hypoglycemia. MEN1 mutation (menin gene on chromosome 11) should be screened in all patients with clinical MEN1 and their first-degree relatives.

**Q:** What are the components and clinical manifestations of MEN2A (Sipple's syndrome)?
