**A:** MEN2A (chromosome 10, RET mutation, autosomal dominant) — triad: 1) Medullary thyroid cancer (MTC, ~90%) — neck mass/thyroid nodule, non-tender anterior lymph nodes. 2) Pheochromocytoma (40–50%) — HTN, palpitations, headache, sweating. 3) Primary hyperparathyroidism (20–30%) — symptoms of hypercalcemia. Some cases also have cutaneous lichen amyloidosis (scaly skin rash).

MTC arises from parafollicular C-cells and secretes calcitonin. It is the most common and lethal component if untreated. Pheochromocytoma must be ruled out BEFORE thyroidectomy to avoid perioperative hypertensive crisis. Prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended in RET mutation carriers.

**Q:** What distinguishes MEN2B (MEN3) from MEN2A?

**A:** MEN2B has: MTC (90%, more aggressive and earlier onset than MEN2A), pheochromocytoma (>50%), mucosal neuromas and intestinal ganglioneuromas (100% — chronic constipation, megacolon), Marfanoid habitus (without aortic abnormalities). MEN2B does NOT have hyperparathyroidism. Both are caused by RET mutations (chromosome 10).

MEN2B is the most aggressive form of MEN2. The Marfanoid habitus and mucosal neuromas (lips, tongue, eyelid margins) are pathognomonic. MTC in MEN2B presents earlier and is more aggressive — these patients require prophylactic thyroidectomy in infancy.

**Q:** What laboratory investigations confirm gastrinoma in MEN1?

**A:** Significantly elevated serum gastrin with a low gastric pH. If gastrin is <10x ULN, a secretin stimulation test may be required (secretin paradoxically increases gastrin in gastrinoma, unlike other causes of hypergastrinemia).

Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison syndrome) causes refractory peptic ulcers, esophagitis, and secretory diarrhea due to excess gastrin. The secretin stimulation test distinguishes gastrinoma (gastrin rises ≥200 pg/mL) from G-cell hyperplasia and other causes. PPI therapy is essential for symptom control.

**Q:** What laboratory tests confirm insulinoma in MEN1?

**A:** Hypoglycemia with inappropriately high insulin AND C-peptide levels for the level of glucose (C-peptide confirms endogenous insulin secretion, ruling out exogenous insulin use).

Insulinoma is the most common functional pancreatic neuroendocrine tumour in MEN1. The 72-hour supervised fast is the gold standard diagnostic test, with measurement of glucose, insulin, C-peptide, and proinsulin at the time of hypoglycemia. C-peptide distinguishes insulinoma from factitious hypoglycemia (exogenous insulin suppresses C-peptide).

**Q:** What is the management strategy for MEN2 regarding medullary thyroid cancer?

**A:** Prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended in individuals with documented pathogenic RET mutation and increased risk of aggressive MTC. If incident MTC, thyroidectomy after diagnosis. Genetic screening for RET mutations (chromosome 10) is the standard of care for all individuals with a family history of MEN2. Rule out pheochromocytoma AND hyperparathyroidism before thyroidectomy. Thyroid hormone supplementation following total thyroidectomy.

The timing of prophylactic thyroidectomy in RET mutation carriers depends on the specific mutation (risk stratification A–D). Calcitonin levels are used to monitor for MTC recurrence. Pheochromocytoma must be excluded and treated first to avoid hypertensive crisis during thyroidectomy.

**Q:** A patient with a family history of MEN2 is found to have a RET mutation. What is the recommended management?

**A:** Prophylactic thyroidectomy (timing based on specific RET mutation risk category). Before thyroidectomy: rule out pheochromocytoma (24-hour urine/plasma metanephrines) and hyperparathyroidism (serum Ca2+ and PTH). Monitor calcitonin levels for MTC surveillance. Genetic counseling offered to first-degree relatives. Thyroid hormone supplementation following total thyroidectomy.

RET mutation carriers face near-certainty of developing MTC, which is lethal if untreated. Prophylactic thyroidectomy is the only cure. The sequence is critical: pheochromocytoma must be managed first (alpha-blockade + surgery) to prevent hypertensive crisis during thyroid surgery. Annual biochemical surveillance is lifelong.

**Q:** What is glucagonoma and how does it present?

**A:** Glucagonoma is a glucagon-secreting pancreatic islet cell tumour seen in MEN1. Presents with: elevated glucagon levels, necrolytic migratory erythema (characteristic rash), anorexia, anemia, secretory diarrhea, glossitis. Managed with somatostatin analogue (e.g., octreotide) for symptom control, and surgery.

Necrolytic migratory erythema (NME) is the pathognomonic rash of glucagonoma — erythematous, pruritic, blistering lesion affecting the lower extremities and perineum. Hyperglycemia (due to glucagon's counter-regulatory effect on insulin) and weight loss are also common. Somatostatin analogues suppress glucagon secretion.

**Q:** What drugs can cause or worsen adrenal insufficiency and through what mechanism?

**A:** Drugs that inhibit cortisol synthesis: ketoconazole (antifungal), etomidate (anesthetic induction agent), megestrol acetate (progestogen). Drugs that increase cortisol metabolism (reduce effectiveness of replacement): rifampin, phenytoin, barbiturates. Exogenous glucocorticoids: cause secondary AI via HPA axis suppression upon withdrawal.

Etomidate is notable as a single dose can suppress adrenal function for 24–48 hours — relevant in ICU patients. Ketoconazole inhibits steroidogenic enzymes (17-alpha hydroxylase, 11-beta hydroxylase) and is also used therapeutically to reduce cortisol in Cushing's syndrome. Abrupt steroid withdrawal after prolonged use is a common cause of secondary AI.

**Q:** What is the Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome?

**A:** Bilateral adrenal hemorrhage and acute adrenal crisis occurring as a complication of severe bacterial sepsis, classically Neisseria meningitidis (meningococcemia) in children, but also Pseudomonas and other organisms. Presents with rapidly progressive hemorrhagic purpura, cardiovascular collapse, and adrenal failure. A medical emergency requiring immediate IV hydrocortisone and antibiotics.

Bilateral adrenal hemorrhage can also occur in adults on anticoagulation (heparin, warfarin) or with coagulopathy. The adrenal glands are highly vascular and vulnerable to hemorrhagic infarction. Diagnosis may require CT adrenals. Treatment is empiric stress-dose hydrocortisone + management of the underlying infection.

**Q:** What are the triggers for symptoms/paroxysms in pheochromocytoma?

**A:** Symptoms may be triggered by: physical or emotional stress, exertion, anesthesia induction, abdominal pressure (e.g., palpation, urination in bladder paraganglioma), tyramine-containing foods (aged/strong cheese, cured meats), certain medications (e.g., metoclopramide, glucagon, tricyclic antidepressants).

Tyramine-containing foods trigger catecholamine release from the tumour. This is relevant in patients on MAO inhibitors where the 'cheese effect' is amplified. Awareness of anesthetic triggers is critical — alpha-blockade must be in place before any surgery or anesthesia in a known or suspected pheochromocytoma.

**Q:** A 45-year-old woman presents with HTN, episodic headaches, palpitations, and sweating. Plasma metanephrines are markedly elevated. CT abdomen shows a 4 cm right adrenal mass. What is the correct sequence of management?

**A:** 1) Alpha-blockade first: start doxazosin or phenoxybenzamine for 10–21 days preoperatively. 2) Add beta-blocker ONLY after adequate alpha-blockade (for HR control if needed). 3) Vigorous salt-loading and IV fluid hydration for volume restoration. 4) Surgical excision of the adrenal mass (adrenalectomy). 5) Monitor for hypoglycemia postoperatively. 6) Rescreen urine/plasma metanephrines 1–3 months postoperatively. 7) Offer genetic testing (germline mutations).

Starting a beta-blocker before alpha-blockade is dangerous — it removes beta-mediated vasodilation while leaving alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, causing severe hypertensive crisis. IV phentolamine (short-acting alpha-blocker) is available perioperatively for acute BP surges during tumour manipulation.

**Q:** What is secondary adrenocortical insufficiency and what are its common causes?

**A:** Secondary AI is inadequate pituitary ACTH secretion leading to reduced cortisol production (but relatively preserved aldosterone). Most common cause: exogenous glucocorticoid use with abrupt withdrawal (suppresses HPA axis). Other causes: pituitary tumours, pituitary surgery/radiation, Sheehan's syndrome (postpartum pituitary necrosis), head trauma, infiltrative diseases (sarcoidosis, hemochromatosis), lymphocytic hypophysitis.

In secondary AI, aldosterone is mostly preserved because it is primarily regulated by the RAAS (not ACTH) — therefore no hyperkalemia, salt craving, or metabolic acidosis. Patients may have other signs of hypopituitarism (secondary hypogonadism, hypothyroidism, GH deficiency). The insulin tolerance test is the gold standard for diagnosing secondary AI when there is uncertainty.

## Osteoporosis & Metabolic Bone Disease

**Q:** When should bone mineral density (BMD) testing with DXA be done per Osteoporosis Canada?

**A:** Women ≥65 or postmenopausal women <65 with risk factors; men ≥70 or men <70 with risk factors; all patients on long-term steroids (≥3 months at prednisone ≥7.5mg/day)

Osteoporosis Canada guidelines recommend DXA for high-risk individuals. T-score: -1.0 to -2.5 = osteopenia; ≤-2.5 = osteoporosis. FRAX tool estimates 10-year fracture probability to guide treatment decisions.

**Q:** What is the pharmacological treatment for osteoporosis in postmenopausal women?

**A:** First-line: bisphosphonates (alendronate weekly or zoledronic acid annual infusion); alternatives: denosumab, raloxifene; anabolic: teriparatide or romosozumab for severe osteoporosis

Osteoporosis Canada guidelines recommend treatment for T-score ≤-2.5 or fragility fracture. Bisphosphonates reduce vertebral fractures by 40-70%, hip fractures by 40-50%. Denosumab reduces vertebral fractures by 68%. Calcium 1000-1200 mg/day + vitamin D 800-2000 IU/day for all.

**Q:** A 68-year-old woman on alendronate for 7 years has hip pain. Imaging shows cortical thickening of the femoral shaft with a transverse fracture line. Diagnosis?

**A:** Atypical femoral fracture (AFF) — bisphosphonate complication; hold bisphosphonate, orthopaedic evaluation, consider anabolic therapy (teriparatide) which may enhance healing

AFFs are rare complications of long-term bisphosphonate use (>3-5 years). Prodromal thigh/groin pain may precede fracture. Radiograph shows transverse fracture with cortical thickening. Both femurs should be evaluated. Drug holiday from bisphosphonate after 5 years if risk-benefit appropriate.

**Q:** What is osteonecrosis of the jaw (ONJ) and when does it occur with osteoporosis treatment?

**A:** ONJ: exposed bone in maxillofacial region >8 weeks without healing in patients on antiresorptive therapy; risk highest with IV bisphosphonates/denosumab at cancer doses; rare at osteoporosis doses

Dental assessment before starting antiresorptive therapy is recommended. ONJ risk with oral bisphosphonates for osteoporosis: ~0.001-0.01%. Cancer patients on IV zoledronic acid monthly: much higher risk. Management: conservative oral hygiene, antimicrobials, avoid elective dental surgery.

**Q:** What is hyperparathyroidism and how does it cause bone disease?

**A:** Primary HPT: parathyroid adenoma (80%) secretes excess PTH → bone resorption (cortical bone especially) → hypercalcemia, hypophosphatemia, hypercalciuria — treat with parathyroidectomy

Osteitis fibrosa cystica is the bone disease of severe primary HPT (now rare). Presentation: usually asymptomatic hypercalcemia on routine labs. Symptoms: bones (fractures), moans, stones, psychic overtones. DXA for fracture risk assessment. Parathyroidectomy curative.

**Q:** What is the FRAX tool and how is it used in osteoporosis management?

**A:** FRAX: WHO fracture risk assessment tool estimating 10-year probability of major osteoporotic fracture and hip fracture; incorporates age, sex, BMI, BMD, risk factors — guides treatment decisions

Osteoporosis Canada guidelines recommend FRAX to determine treatment thresholds: treat if 10-year major osteoporotic fracture risk ≥20% or hip fracture risk ≥3%. FRAX can be calculated with or without BMD.

**Q:** A 70-year-old man on prednisone 15mg/day for polymyalgia rheumatica. What bone protection measures should be initiated?

**A:** Calcium (1200 mg/day) + vitamin D (800-2000 IU/day) + bisphosphonate (alendronate or zoledronic acid) started at initiation of steroid therapy for ≥3 months

Glucocorticoid-induced osteoporosis (GIOP) is the most common secondary cause of osteoporosis. Steroids reduce osteoblast function and increase osteoclast activity. Osteoporosis Canada recommends preventive treatment for patients starting ≥7.5mg prednisone/day for ≥3 months.

**Q:** What is Paget disease of bone and what treatment is recommended?

**A:** Paget: excessive and disorganized bone remodeling causing enlarged, deformed bones (skull, spine, pelvis, femur); markedly elevated ALP; treat symptomatic disease with bisphosphonates (zoledronic acid)

Paget disease presents with bone pain, deformity, increased hat size, hearing loss, high-output heart failure (rare). Diagnosis: markedly elevated ALP, bone scan showing increased uptake in affected areas. Zoledronic acid provides prolonged remission.

**Q:** What is vitamin D deficiency and how is it treated?

**A:** Vitamin D deficiency (25-OH vitamin D <50 nmol/L): treat with vitamin D3 (cholecalciferol) 1000-2000 IU/day or weekly high-dose loading; recheck in 3 months

Vitamin D deficiency is highly prevalent in Canada due to limited sun exposure. Risk groups: elderly, institutionalized, dark skin, malabsorption, low dietary intake. Osteoporosis Canada recommends vitamin D 800-2000 IU/day for adults at risk for osteoporosis.

**Q:** A 55-year-old woman has a fragility fracture of her wrist after a minor fall. DXA T-score: -2.7. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Osteoporosis with fragility fracture; initiate pharmacotherapy (bisphosphonate first-line) regardless of T-score when fragility fracture has occurred, plus calcium + vitamin D

Fragility fractures are defined as fractures from a fall from standing height or less. Any fragility fracture at major sites (hip, spine, wrist) qualifies as osteoporosis regardless of BMD. Hip fractures have 20-30% mortality within 1 year.

**Q:** When is osteoporosis pharmacotherapy indicated?

**A:** Prior fragility fracture, T-score<=-2.5, FRAX major>=20% or hip>=3%

First-line: bisphosphonates. Holiday after 5y oral/3y IV. Ensure Ca 1200mg + vitD 800-2000IU.

**Q:** What is FRAX and how is it used in Canada?

**A:** 10-year fracture probability calculator. Inputs: age, sex, BMI, prior fracture, parent hip fx, steroids, RA, secondary OP, alcohol, smoking, BMD. Osteoporosis Canada: treat if major OP fracture ≥20% or hip ≥3%.

CAROC also used in Canada (BMD+age+sex+fracture+steroids→low/moderate/high). Moderate risk (10-20%): use FRAX for additional factors. BMD alone insufficient for treatment decisions.

**Q:** What are bisphosphonate options and duration guidelines?

**A:** Oral: alendronate 70 mg/wk, risedronate 35 mg/wk. IV: zoledronic acid 5 mg/yr. Reassess after 3-5yr (oral) or 3yr (IV). Drug holiday if low risk. Continue if T-score ≤-2.5 or recent fracture.

Take oral fasting, upright, with water, 30-60 min before food. Rare risks: ONJ (dental exam first), atypical femur fractures (thigh pain→X-ray). Contraindicated if eGFR <30-35.

**Q:** What is denosumab and the rebound concern?

**A:** Anti-RANKL monoclonal Ab, 60 mg SC q6 months. No renal adjustment needed. CRITICAL: stopping causes rapid rebound bone loss + vertebral fractures. Must transition to bisphosphonate before/at discontinuation.

Preferred when eGFR <30. Rebound fractures 7-12 months after last dose. Osteoporosis Canada: give zoledronic acid 6 months after last denosumab. Monitor calcium (hypocalcemia risk).

**Q:** Who should be screened for osteoporosis with DXA in Canada?

**A:** Women ≥65, men ≥70. Younger if: fragility fracture, steroids ≥3 months, parental hip fx, smoking, alcohol, RA, hypogonadism, early menopause (<45). T-score ≤-2.5 = osteoporosis. Hip/vertebral fragility fracture = osteoporosis regardless of T-score.

DXA: lumbar spine + hip, use lowest T-score. VFA can detect asymptomatic vertebral fractures. Repeat DXA q1-3 years. Osteopenia: T-score -1.0 to -2.5.

**Q:** What is glucocorticoid-induced osteoporosis prevention?

**A:** Prednisone ≥7.5 mg ≥3 months: Ca 1200mg + Vit D 800-2000 IU for ALL. Bisphosphonate/denosumab if FRAX ≥10-20% (lower threshold), or any dose if ≥70 or prior fracture. Teriparatide for severe GIOP.

Most common secondary OP. Bone loss fastest in first 3-6 months. GCs decrease osteoblasts, increase osteoclasts. FRAX underestimates GIOP risk — use lower treatment thresholds.

**Q:** What is teriparatide and when is it used?

**A:** Recombinant PTH(1-34), 20 μg SC daily ×24 months max. Anabolic (stimulates osteoblasts). For: severe OP (T-score <-3.5 or multiple fractures), GIOP, failed anti-resorptives. Must follow with bisphosphonate/denosumab.

Better BMD gains than bisphosphonates at spine. Contraindicated: Paget, bone mets, elevated ALP, prior radiation, children. Romosozumab (anti-sclerostin) is newer dual-mechanism anabolic.

**Q:** What non-pharmacologic measures prevent osteoporotic fractures?

**A:** Weight-bearing + resistance exercise, fall prevention (home safety, vision, medication review, balance/tai chi), Ca 1200 mg/day (diet-first), Vit D 800-2000 IU/day (target 25-OH-D >75 nmol/L), smoking cessation, limit alcohol.

Tai chi reduces falls 20-40%. Medication review: minimize benzos, anticholinergics, sedatives. Hip protectors may reduce fractures in institutionalized elderly.

**Q:** How is vertebral compression fracture managed?

**A:** Conservative: analgesics, early mobilization, physio, bracing. Vertebroplasty/kyphoplasty if refractory pain >4-6 weeks. Start OP pharmacotherapy immediately. Assess secondary causes.

Only 1/3 of vertebral fractures symptomatic. Strongest predictor of future fractures (5x risk). Height loss >2cm→VFA or lateral spine X-ray.

**Q:** What secondary OP causes should be evaluated?

**A:** Endocrine: hyperPTH, hyperthyroidism, Cushing, hypogonadism. GI: celiac, IBD. Renal: CKD. Hematologic: myeloma. Drugs: GCs, aromatase inhibitors, PPIs, phenytoin, heparin. Workup: CBC, Ca, PO4, ALP, Cr, 25-OH-D, TSH, SPEP.

SPEP important in elderly to exclude myeloma. Correct secondary causes alongside pharmacotherapy. Additional tests per suspicion: celiac serology, PTH, testosterone.

**Q:** What is romosozumab?

**A:** Anti-sclerostin monoclonal Ab: increases bone formation AND decreases resorption. 210 mg SC monthly ×12 months, then anti-resorptive. For very high fracture risk postmenopausal women. Black box: increased CV events→contraindicated if MI/stroke in past year.

ARCH trial: romosozumab then alendronate reduced fractures 48% vs alendronate alone. Sequential therapy paradigm: anabolic first, then anti-resorptive for severe OP. Osteoporosis Canada recommends for imminent fracture risk.

**Q:** What are the normal serum calcium values and how is calcium distributed in the blood?

**A:** Normal total serum Ca2+: 2.2–2.6 mmol/L. Normal ionized/free Ca2+: 1.15–1.31 mmol/L. Distribution: 40% protein-bound (mostly albumin), 45% ionized (biologically active), 15% complexed with PO4^3- and citrate.

Only ionized calcium is biologically active. When albumin is low, total calcium is low but ionized calcium may be normal (corrected calcium = measured total Ca + 0.02 × (40 − albumin in g/L)). Ionized calcium measurement is more accurate in critically ill patients with abnormal albumin.

**Q:** What are the major regulators of calcium homeostasis and their sources?

**A:** PTH: parathyroid glands (stimulated by low Ca2+, high PO4, low calcitriol; inhibited by high Ca2+, high calcitriol, FGF23, chronic low Mg2+) → net effect: ↑Ca2+. Calcitriol (1,25-(OH)2D3): synthesized from dietary cholecalciferol/ergocalciferol (UV → cholecalciferol → liver → calcidiol → kidney → calcitriol; stimulated by low PO4, high PTH, low Ca2+) → net effect: ↑Ca2+, ↑PO4. Calcitonin: thyroid C-cells (stimulated by high Ca2+ and pentagastrin; inhibited by low Ca2+) → net effect: ↓Ca2+ (only in pharmacologic doses). FGF23: → ↓Ca2+ and ↓PO4.

PTH and calcitriol act on three target organs: GI tract (absorption), bone (resorption), and kidney (reabsorption/excretion). PTH increases calcitriol production by stimulating 1-alpha-hydroxylase in the kidney. Calcitonin plays a minimal role in normal calcium homeostasis but is clinically used in hypercalcemia treatment.

**Q:** What is primary hyperparathyroidism (PH) and what are the indications for surgery?

**A:** PH is increased PTH secretion most commonly due to parathyroid adenoma; less often parathyroid carcinoma or multigland hyperplasia. Most common cause of hypercalcemia in healthy outpatients. Surgery is recommended if any of: serum Ca2+ >0.25 mmol/L above ULN; skeletal involvement (fracture on VFA, T-score ≤-2.5, vertebral fracture); renal involvement (eGFR or CrCl <60 mL/min, nephrocalcinosis/nephrolithiasis); hypercalciuria (>250 mg/day women; >300 mg/day men); age <50 yr.

Asymptomatic primary hyperparathyroidism is common. Medical surveillance with annual serum calcium, creatinine, and DEXA is appropriate for those who do not meet surgical criteria. Secondary hyperparathyroidism is elevated PTH in response to low Ca2+ (chronic renal failure, osteomalacia). Tertiary hyperparathyroidism is irreversible autonomous PTH secretion after long-standing secondary disease.

**Q:** What distinguishes primary, secondary, and tertiary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism: autonomous overproduction of PTH (usually parathyroid adenoma) → high PTH, high Ca2+. Secondary hyperparathyroidism: elevated PTH in response to low Ca2+ — most commonly in renal failure/osteomalacia → high PTH, low or normal Ca2+. Tertiary hyperparathyroidism: irreversible monoclonal outgrowth of parathyroid glands after long-standing, inadequately treated secondary hyperparathyroidism (typically on dialysis) → autonomous high PTH even if Ca2+ corrected.

Secondary hyperparathyroidism is treated by addressing the underlying cause (e.g., vitamin D supplementation, phosphate binders, calcitriol in CKD, cinacalcet). When PTH secretion becomes autonomous and unresponsive despite normalization of calcium (tertiary), parathyroidectomy may be required.

**Q:** What is the role of vitamin D in calcium homeostasis and how is it synthesized?

**A:** Vitamin D (calcitriol/1,25-(OH)2D3) increases intestinal calcium and phosphate absorption, stimulates bone resorption, and reduces renal calcium excretion → net increase in serum Ca2+ and PO4. Synthesis: UV light on skin → cholecalciferol (D3); liver → 25-hydroxylation → calcidiol (25-OH D3); kidney (1-alpha-hydroxylase) → calcitriol (1,25-(OH)2D3 — active form). Synthesis stimulated by: low PO4, high PTH, low Ca2+. Inhibited by: high PO4, low PTH, FGF23, calcitriol (negative feedback).

Deficiency of 1-alpha-hydroxylase (e.g., chronic kidney disease) leads to secondary hyperparathyroidism and renal osteodystrophy — this is why activated vitamin D (calcitriol/alfacalcidol) is used in CKD, not standard cholecalciferol. Checking 25-OH vitamin D (calcidiol) is the appropriate test to assess body stores.

## General

**Q:** What is the typical blood glucose range in DKA?

**A:** 14-55 mmol/L

DKA typically presents with glucose levels between 14-55 mmol/L, though euglycemic DKA can occur in pregnancy or with SGLT2 inhibitors.

**Q:** What is the typical blood glucose range in HHS?

**A:** 44.4-133.2 mmol/L

HHS is characterized by extremely high glucose levels (often >44 mmol/L), much higher than DKA, due to more severe dehydration.

**Q:** What is the anion gap in DKA typically?

**A:** Elevated (usually >20 mEq/L)

DKA presents with high anion gap metabolic acidosis due to accumulation of ketone bodies (beta-hydroxybutyrate and acetoacetate).

**Q:** What is the most important endpoint for monitoring DKA resolution?

**A:** Anion gap (not blood glucose)

The anion gap is the most important marker for monitoring resolution of metabolic acidosis in DKA, not glucose levels.

**Q:** Why is mental obtundation/coma more common in HHS than DKA?

**A:** Higher serum osmolality in HHS causes fluid shift from neurons → cerebral dysfunction

HHS has much higher osmolality (often >320 mOsm/kg) due to extreme hyperglycemia and severe dehydration, causing neurological symptoms.

**Q:** At what blood glucose level should D5W or D10W be added to IV fluids in DKA treatment?

**A:** When BG reaches 14.0 mmol/L

Once BG reaches 14.0 mmol/L, add dextrose to maintain BG of 12-14 mmol/L to prevent hypoglycemia while continuing to treat acidosis.

**Q:** What type of respiratory pattern is characteristic of DKA?

**A:** Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid breathing)

Kussmaul respirations represent the body's compensation for metabolic acidosis by blowing off CO2. Breath may have acetone odor.

**Q:** At what blood glucose level should you switch from NS to D5W in DKA treatment?

**A:** When BG reaches 13.9 mmol/L (250 mg/dL)

Once BG reaches 14.0 mmol/L, add D5W or D10W to maintain BG of 12-14 mmol/L to prevent hypoglycemia while continuing to resolve acidosis.

**Q:** What is the most important endpoint for monitoring resolution of DKA?

**A:** Anion Gap (AG)

The anion gap is the most important endpoint for monitoring resolution of metabolic acidosis in DKA, not blood glucose or serum ketone level.

**Q:** What type of respirations is characteristic of DKA?

**A:** Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid breathing)

Kussmaul respirations represent the body's compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis in DKA, blowing off CO2 to reduce acidemia.

**Q:** Why is Kussmaul respirations typically ABSENT in HHS?

**A:** Because metabolic acidosis is absent in pure HHS

HHS is characterized by hyperglycemia and hyperosmolality without significant ketosis or acidosis, so Kussmaul respirations are not present unless there is a separate precipitant causing acidosis.

**Q:** What is the ABG finding in DKA?

**A:** Anion gap metabolic acidosis with possible secondary respiratory alkalosis

DKA demonstrates high anion gap metabolic acidosis due to ketoacids. The body may compensate with respiratory alkalosis (hyperventilation).

**Q:** What is the ABG finding in HHS (unless precipitant causes acidosis)?

**A:** Metabolic acidosis typically absent

Pure HHS does not have metabolic acidosis because the small amount of insulin prevents ketosis. Acidosis only occurs if a separate precipitant (e.g., lactic acidosis from MI) is present.

**Q:** When treating HHS, if corrected serum sodium is high or normal, what fluid should be used?

**A:** 0.45% NaCl (hypotonic saline)

If corrected serum Na+ is high or normal, switch from NS to 0.45% NaCl at 4 mL/kg/h to correct hypernatremia.

**Q:** What is the insidious onset feature of HHS?

**A:** Preceded by weakness, polyuria, polydipsia over days to weeks

HHS has a gradual, insidious onset over days to weeks, unlike the more acute presentation of DKA.

**Q:** What is the mental status change in HHS due to?

**A:** High serum osmolality causing fluid shift from neurons to ECF

Extreme hyperosmolality causes water to shift from brain neurons to extracellular fluid, leading to lethargy, confusion, and coma.

**Q:** What is the urine finding in DKA vs HHS?

**A:** DKA: positive for glucose and ketones; HHS: positive for glucose, negative for ketones (unless starvation ketosis)

The presence of ketones in urine helps differentiate DKA (ketonuria present) from HHS (ketonuria absent or minimal).

**Q:** Why is there more severe dehydration in HHS compared to DKA?

**A:** More gradual onset and longer duration of metabolic decompensation plus impaired fluid intake common in bedridden patients

HHS develops over days to weeks with patients often being too ill to drink adequately, leading to profound dehydration.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (LH/FSH deficiency)?

**A:** Oligo/amenorrhea, infertility, decreased facial/body hair and muscle mass in men, erectile dysfunction, delayed puberty

Deficiency of LH and FSH leads to low sex steroid production from the gonads, causing symptoms of hypogonadism and reproductive dysfunction.

**Q:** What is the target preprandial (before subsequent meal) blood glucose level?

**A:** 4-7 mmol/L

Postprandial target is 5-10 mmol/L, while fasting and preprandial targets are 4-7 mmol/L.

**Q:** What is the typical blood glucose range in DKA vs HHS?

**A:** DKA: 14-55 mmol/L; HHS: 44.4-133.2 mmol/L

HHS has much higher glucose levels due to more gradual onset and more severe dehydration.

**Q:** What respiratory pattern is seen in DKA and why?

**A:** Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid breathing) due to metabolic acidosis stimulating respiratory center

Kussmaul respirations represent the body's compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis by blowing off CO2.

**Q:** How do you monitor resolution of DKA?

**A:** Anion gap (AG) is the most important endpoint - monitor until acidosis resolves

AG normalizes as ketone production stops, even though hyperglycemia may persist longer.

**Q:** What IV fluid switch occurs when serum BG reaches 13.9 mmol/L in DKA/HHS treatment?

**A:** Switch from NS to D5W (or D10W) to maintain BG of 12-14 mmol/L

This prevents hypoglycemia while continuing insulin therapy to resolve acidosis.

**Q:** How does corrected serum sodium guide fluid choice in HHS?

**A:** If corrected Na+ is high or normal: switch to 0.45% NaCl. If low: maintain NS

This prevents rapid osmolality shifts that could cause cerebral edema.

**Q:** What is the typical blood glucose level in HHS?

**A:** 44.4-133.2 mmol/L

HHS is characterized by extremely high glucose levels, typically in the range of 44.4-133.2 mmol/L.

**Q:** What is the hallmark clinical feature distinguishing HHS from DKA?

**A:** Severe hyperosmolality with mental obtundation or coma, without significant ketosis

HHS is characterized by extreme hyperosmolality (often >320 mOsm/kg) causing neurological symptoms, without the ketoacidosis seen in DKA.

**Q:** When treating DKA, at what blood glucose level should you add dextrose to IV fluids?

**A:** When BG reaches 13.9 mmol/L (250 mg/dL)

Once BG reaches 14.0 mmol/L, add D5W or D10W to maintain BG of 12-14 mmol/L while continuing insulin to resolve acidosis.

**Q:** What type of respiratory pattern is seen in DKA?

**A:** Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid breathing)

Kussmaul respirations represent the body's compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis in DKA.

**Q:** What breath odor is characteristic of DKA?

**A:** Acetone-odoured breath (fruity odor)

The fruity/acetone odor on breath is due to exhaled ketone bodies (acetoacetate) in DKA.

**Q:** What are the classic triad of DKA symptoms?

**A:** Polyuria, polydipsia, and weight loss

Classic hyperglycemia symptoms include polyuria (osmotic diuresis), polydipsia (thirst), and weight loss.

**Q:** What is the typical BG range in DKA?

**A:** 14-55 mmol/L

DKA typically presents with blood glucose levels between 14-55 mmol/L, though it can be higher.

**Q:** What is the urine ketone finding in HHS?

**A:** Negative for ketones (unless starvation ketosis)

Urine is negative for ketones in HHS, distinguishing it from DKA. Ketosis is usually absent due to residual insulin effect.

**Q:** Why is dehydration more severe in HHS compared to DKA?

**A:** More gradual onset and longer duration of metabolic decompensation plus impaired fluid intake common in bedridden patients

HHS has an insidious onset over days to weeks with polyuria and inadequate fluid intake, leading to more profound dehydration than DKA.

**Q:** What type of acidosis is typically absent in HHS unless caused by underlying precipitant?

**A:** Metabolic acidosis

Metabolic acidosis is typically absent in HHS unless there is an underlying precipitant like lactic acidosis from MI.

**Q:** What is the onset of HHS symptoms?

**A:** Insidious (gradual), preceded by weakness, polyuria, and polydipsia over days to weeks

HHS has an insidious onset over several days to weeks, unlike the more acute presentation of DKA.

**Q:** What hormones are produced by the posterior pituitary?

**A:** ADH (antidiuretic hormone/vasopressin) and oxytocin

Unlike anterior pituitary hormones which are synthesized in the pituitary itself, these are produced in the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior pituitary.

**Q:** What is the imaging modality of choice for evaluating pituitary pathology?

**A:** MRI of the sella

MRI provides excellent soft tissue detail and is superior to CT for visualizing pituitary microadenomas, macroadenomas, and surrounding structures like the optic chiasm.

**Q:** What is ACTH and where is it secreted from?

**A:** ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) is a polypeptide cleaved from POMC, secreted in a pulsatile fashion from the anterior pituitary

ACTH has diurnal variability with peak secretion at 0200-0400h and trough at 1800-2400h.

**Q:** What regulates ACTH secretion?

**A:** ACTH secretion is regulated by CRH (corticotropin-releasing hormone) and AVP (arginine vasopressin) from the hypothalamus

These hypothalamic factors stimulate pituitary ACTH release in response to stress and circadian rhythm.

**Q:** What is the principal precursor for all steroid hormones?

**A:** Cholesterol is the precursor for all steroid hormones

Cholesterol undergoes enzymatic conversions to produce mineralocorticoids, glucocorticoids, and sex steroids.

**Q:** What are the principal adrenal androgens?

**A:** DHEA (dehydroepiandrosterone), androstenedione, and 11-hydroxyandrostenedione

These androgens are primarily responsible for adrenarche (growth of axillary and pubic hair) and are regulated by ACTH.

**Q:** How is Primary Aldosteronism (PA) screened and confirmed?

**A:** Screening: elevated aldosterone:renin ratio in presence of high aldosterone. Confirmation: lack of aldosterone suppression with ECFV expansion using normal saline infusion

Confirmatory test: IV infusion of 500 mL/h NS for 4 hours; plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L is consistent with PA, <140 pmol/L is normal.

**Q:** What is the difference between Primary Aldosteronism and Secondary Aldosteronism?

**A:** Primary Aldosteronism (PA) is excess aldosterone production from an intra-adrenal cause; Secondary Aldosteronism (SA) is aldosterone production in response to excess RAAS (extra-adrenal cause)

PA involves adrenal pathology (adenoma, hyperplasia), while SA is a physiologic response to hypovolemia or renal issues.

**Q:** What is Conn's syndrome?

**A:** Conn's syndrome is primary aldosteronism caused by an aldosterone-producing adrenal adenoma

Characterized by hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis.

**Q:** What is the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) cascade?

**A:** Renin (from kidney) converts angiotensinogen to angiotensin I → ACE (lung/kidney) converts angiotensin I to angiotensin II → angiotensin II stimulates aldosterone release → renal Na+ retention, K+ excretion, arteriolar vasoconstriction, and ADH release

The system is activated by decreased volume, decreased arterial pressure, or decreased Na delivery to macula densa.

**Q:** What are the components of the HPA axis?

**A:** Hypothalamus (produces CRH) → Pituitary (releases ACTH) → Adrenal cortex (releases cortisol)

The HPA axis has negative feedback: cortisol inhibits CRH and ACTH release.

**Q:** What is the diurnal variation of cortisol?

**A:** Cortisol peaks at 0200-0400h and is at its trough between 1800-2400h

This diurnal rhythm is disrupted in Cushing's syndrome, where midnight cortisol is inappropriately elevated.

**Q:** What is ACTH and what regulates its secretion?

**A:** ACTH is a polypeptide cleaved from POMC, secreted pulsatilely with diurnal variation, regulated by CRH and AVP

ACTH secretion peaks between 0200-0400h and troughs between 1800-2400h, following a circadian rhythm.

**Q:** What is the diurnal variation pattern of ACTH secretion?

**A:** Peak: 0200-0400h; Trough: 1800-2400h

ACTH follows a circadian rhythm with highest levels in early morning and lowest in late evening.

**Q:** What is the Cosyntropin (ACTH) Stimulation Test used to diagnose?

**A:** Hormone deficiencies, specifically glucocorticoid reserve

Administer 250 μg cosyntropin IV/IM, measure plasma cortisol before and at 30 and 60 min. A physiologic response is stimulated cortisol >500 nmol/L at 30 or 60 min.

**Q:** What is the Low-Dose Overnight DXM Suppression Test used to diagnose?

**A:** Cushing's syndrome (excess cortisol production)

Administer 1 mg oral DXM between 11 pm and midnight, measure plasma cortisol next day between 8-9 am. A physiologic response is plasma cortisol <50 nmol/L (<1.8 μg/dL).

**Q:** What is a normal result for the DXM Suppression Test?

**A:** Plasma cortisol <50 nmol/L (<1.8 μg/dL)

Failure to suppress plasma cortisol indicates inappropriate response and suggests Cushing's syndrome.

**Q:** What does elevated 24-hour urine free cortisol indicate?

**A:** Overproduction of cortisol

This is a test for confirming excess cortisol production in the workup of Cushing's syndrome.

**Q:** What is Primary Aldosteronism (PA)?

**A:** Excess aldosterone production from an intra-adrenal cause

Previously called hyperaldosteronism, PA is distinct from Secondary Aldosteronism which is aldosterone production in response to excess RAAS.

**Q:** What is Secondary Aldosteronism (SA)?

**A:** Aldosterone production in response to excess RAAS (extra-adrenal cause)

SA occurs when renin production is elevated, leading to increased angiotensin II and aldosterone secretion.

**Q:** What is the screening test for Primary Aldosteronism?

**A:** Elevated aldosterone:renin ratio in the presence of high aldosterone

There is variability in interpretation depending on assays used, and confirmation requires lack of aldosterone suppression with ECFV expansion.

**Q:** What is the diurnal variation of ACTH secretion?

**A:** Peak ACTH secretion occurs at 0200-0400h and trough at 1800-2400h

ACTH is secreted in a pulsatile fashion with diurnal variability, regulating cortisol production.

**Q:** What is the Cosyntropin Stimulation Test used to diagnose?

**A:** Adrenal insufficiency (glucocorticoid deficiency)

250 mcg cosyntropin IV/IM is administered; plasma cortisol is measured before and at 30 and 60 minutes. A peak cortisol >500 nmol/L indicates normal adrenal reserve.

**Q:** What is the low-dose overnight DXM suppression test used for?

**A:** Screening test for Cushing's syndrome (cortisol hypersecretion)

1 mg dexamethasone is given at 11pm-midnight, and plasma cortisol is measured at 8-9am the next day. Normal response is cortisol <50 nmol/L.

**Q:** How is primary aldosteronism confirmed?

**A:** Lack of aldosterone suppression with ECFV expansion using saline infusion

500 mL/h NS infusion for 4 hours; plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L is consistent with PA, <140 pmol/L is normal.

**Q:** What enzyme is deficient in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

**A:** undefined

21-hydroxylase deficiency causes impaired conversion of 17-OH-progesterone to 11-deoxycortisol, leading to cortisol deficiency and adrenal androgen excess

**Q:** What is the effect of 11-hydroxylase deficiency?

**A:** undefined

Impaired conversion of 11-deoxycortisol to cortisol, causing cortisol deficiency and accumulation of androgen precursors

**Q:** What stimulates renin release?

**A:** Decreased volume, decreased arterial pressure, decreased Na delivery to macula densa, sympathetic stimulation, prostaglandins

Renin is released in response to decreased renal perfusion and sodium delivery.

**Q:** What is the final product of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system?

**A:** Aldosterone release leading to renal Na+ retention, K+ excretion, arteriolar vasoconstriction, and promotion of ADH release

ACE converts angiotensin I to angiotensin II, which stimulates aldosterone release.

**Q:** What is the primary function of aldosterone?

**A:** Regulates extracellular fluid volume (ECFV) through Na+ and Cl- retention and K+ and H+ excretion in the distal tubule

Aldosterone stimulates Na+/K+ ATPase in the distal tubule and is regulated by RAAS and hyperkalemia.

**Q:** What is the normal response in the saline infusion test for primary aldosteronism?

**A:** Plasma aldosterone <140 pmol/L after 4 hours of NS infusion (500 mL/h)

In primary aldosteronism, aldosterone fails to suppress and remains >277 pmol/L despite volume expansion.

**Q:** What is the difference between primary and secondary aldosteronism?

**A:** Primary aldosteronism (PA) is excess aldosterone production from an intra-adrenal cause; secondary aldosteronism (SA) is aldosterone production in response to excess RAAS (extra-adrenal cause)

PA is also called hyperaldosteronism and involves adrenal pathology.

**Q:** What activates renin release?

**A:** Decreased volume, decreased arterial pressure, decreased Na delivery to macula densa, sympathetic stimulation, prostaglandins

Renin release is inhibited by dopamine, volume expansion, and increased arterial pressure.

**Q:** What are the effects of angiotensin II?

**A:** Stimulates aldosterone release, causes arteriolar vasoconstriction, promotes ADH release, provides negative feedback to inhibit JGA

Angiotensin II is the active form converted by ACE from angiotensin I.

**Q:** What is the precursor molecule for all steroid hormones?

**A:** Cholesterol

Steroidogenesis begins with cholesterol converted to pregnenolone by cholesterol hydroxylase (side-chain cleavage enzyme).

**Q:** What is Cosyntropin and what does the Cosyntropin Stimulation Test diagnose?

**A:** Cosyntropin is an ACTH analog used in the stimulation test to diagnose glucocorticoid deficiency (adrenal insufficiency)

**Q:** What is the low-dose overnight DXM suppression test used to screen for?

**A:** Cushing syndrome (hypercortisolism)

**Q:** What is the physiologic response in a normal low-dose overnight DXM suppression test?

**A:** Plasma cortisol <50 nmol/L (<1.8 μg/dL)

**Q:** What are the three principal adrenal androgens?

**A:** DHEA, androstenedione, and 11-hydroxyandrostenedione

**Q:** What are the physiologic effects of cortisol on bone?

**A:** Cortisol inhibits bone formation and stimulates bone resorption

**Q:** What effect does cortisol have on the immune system?

**A:** Cortisol suppresses inflammation and impairs cell-mediated immunity

**Q:** What is the effect of cortisol on the reproductive axis?

**A:** Cortisol inhibits the reproductive axis

**Q:** What screening test is used for Primary Aldosteronism?

**A:** Elevated aldosterone:renin ratio in the presence of high aldosterone

**Q:** What is the confirmatory test for Primary Aldosteronism using saline infusion?

**A:** IV infusion of 500 mL/h of normal saline for 4 hours; plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L is consistent with PA, <140 pmol/L is normal

**Q:** What effect does cortisol have on protein metabolism?

**A:** Increases protein catabolism

**Q:** What are the effects of cortisol on cardiovascular system?

**A:** Increases cardiac output and vascular tone, promotes Na+ retention

**Q:** What effect does cortisol have on white blood cells?

**A:** Stimulates leukocytosis and lymphopenia

**Q:** What is the ACTH stimulation test used to diagnose?

**A:** Adrenal insufficiency (glucocorticoid deficiency)

**Q:** What enzymes are involved in steroid synthesis in the adrenal cortex?

**A:** Cholesterol hydroxylase, 3-β-dehydrogenase, 17α-hydroxylase, 21-hydroxylase, 11β-hydroxylase, 18-hydroxylase, 18-oxidase

**Q:** What is the difference between zona glomerulosa and zona fasciculata in terms of regulation?

**A:** Zona glomerulosa is regulated by RAAS (angiotensin II and K+), while zona fasciculata is regulated by ACTH

**Q:** What are the physiologic effects of glucocorticoids on bone?

**A:** undefined

**Q:** What effect does cortisol have on bone?

**A:** undefined

**Q:** What is the effect of calcitriol on calcium and phosphate?

**A:** Increases intestinal calcium and phosphate absorption, increases bone resorption, increases renal calcium reabsorption, decreases PTH secretion

Active vitamin D (calcitriol) raises calcium through multiple pathways but also increases phosphate - its production is stimulated by PTH

**Q:** Why should you ask about fish allergies before prescribing calcitonin?

**A:** Calcitonin is derived from salmon and can cause allergic reactions in patients with fish allergies

Salmon calcitonin contains proteins that may be allergenic - always screen for fish allergy before administration

**Q:** When is testosterone therapy NOT suggested according to guidelines?

**A:** Men >65 years old, men with T2DM and low testosterone, men planning fertility in the near term

Testosterone suppresses spermatogenesis and is generally not recommended for older men or those seeking fertility.

**Q:** What hereditary disorders cause male infertility?

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

Cystic fibrosis causes congenital absence of the vas deferens, preventing sperm transport.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of testosterone deficiency in the first trimester in utero?

**A:** Incomplete virilization of external genitalia (ambiguous genitalia) and incomplete development of Wolffian ducts (male pseudohermaphroditism)

Testosterone is needed between weeks 8-12 for male external genitalia development.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of testosterone deficiency prepuberty?

**A:** Incomplete pubertal maturation: high-pitched voice, sparse pubic and axillary hair, absence of facial hair, eunuchoidal body habitus, poor muscle development, reduced peak bone mass

Without testosterone at puberty, male secondary sexual characteristics fail to develop.

**Q:** What is the treatment for defects in androgen action?

**A:** Hormone replacement or supplementation, psychological support, gonadectomy for cryptorchidism and streak gonads (due to increased testicular cancer risk)

Treatment is individualized based on the specific defect and patient goals (fertility vs. gender assignment).

**Q:** What are the common routes for testosterone replacement therapy?

**A:** Intramuscular (IM) or subcutaneous (SC) injection, transdermal testosterone, or gel

Multiple delivery methods exist to accommodate patient preferences and optimize testosterone levels.

**Q:** What are hereditary causes of male infertility?

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

Genetic conditions affect sperm motility or reproductive tract development.

**Q:** What surgical procedures can cause male infertility?

**A:** TURP, radical prostatectomy, or orchiectomy

Surgical removal or damage to reproductive organs can impair fertility.

**Q:** What is 5-alpha-reductase deficiency?

**A:** A deficiency of the enzyme that converts testosterone to DHT, leading to incomplete virilization of external genitalia

Without DHT, the external genitalia fail to fully masculinize during fetal development.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of testosterone deficiency in prepuberty?

**A:** Incomplete pubertal maturation (high pitch voice, sparse pubic/axillary hair, absence of facial hair), eunuchoidal body habitus, poor muscle development, reduced peak bone mass

Testosterone drives pubertal changes and bone/muscle development during adolescence.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of testosterone deficiency in postpuberty?

**A:** Decrease in energy, mood, and libido; fine wrinkles around mouth and eyes; decrease in pubic/axillary hair, hematocrit, muscle mass, strength, and BMD

Testosterone maintains secondary sexual characteristics and musculoskeletal health in adults.

**Q:** What red flags should be ruled out in a male breast mass?

**A:** Unilateral, eccentric, hard or fixed mass, skin dimpling or retraction, bloody nipple discharge or crusting

These findings suggest breast carcinoma rather than benign gynecomastia.

**Q:** What is the purpose of the Cosyntropin (ACTH) Stimulation Test?

**A:** To diagnose glucocorticoid (cortisol) deficiency

250 mcg cosyntropin is administered IV/IM, and plasma cortisol is measured before and at 30 and 60 minutes.

**Q:** What is the purpose of the Dexamethasone Suppression Test?

**A:** To diagnose cortisol hypersecretion (Cushing's syndrome)

Dexamethasone suppresses pituitary ACTH; plasma cortisol should be lowered if HPA axis is normal.

**Q:** What are the components of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS)?

**A:** Renin (kidney) converts angiotensinogen to angiotensin I; ACE (lung and kidney) converts angiotensin I to angiotensin II; angiotensin II causes arteriolar vasoconstriction, promotes ADH release, and stimulates aldosterone release

Aldosterone then promotes renal Na+ retention and K+ excretion.

**Q:** What is the normal response in saline infusion test for primary aldosteronism?

**A:** Plasma aldosterone <140 pmol/L is normal; >277 pmol/L is consistent with primary aldosteronism

The test involves IV infusion of 500 mL/h normal saline for 4 hours with measurement of plasma aldosterone.

**Q:** What is the precursor molecule for steroid synthesis in the adrenal gland?

**A:** Cholesterol converted to pregnenolone by cholesterol hydroxylase (P450scc)

This is the rate-limiting first step in all steroid hormone biosynthesis.

**Q:** What is the 24-hour urine free cortisol test used to diagnose?

**A:** Cushing's syndrome - shows overproduction of cortisol

Elevated 24-hour urine free cortisol confirms endogenous cortisol excess.

**Q:** Name four causes of male infertility related to hereditary disorders

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia), cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

Genetic conditions can affect sperm motility or reproductive tract development, causing infertility.

**Q:** What are the effects of testosterone deficiency during the first trimester in utero?

**A:** Incomplete virilization of external genitalia (ambiguous genitalia) and incomplete development of Wolffian ducts (male pseudohermaphroditism)

Testosterone is essential for male genital development between weeks 8-12 of gestation.

**Q:** What are the effects of testosterone deficiency prepubertally?

**A:** Incomplete pubertal maturation (high-pitched voice, sparse pubic and axillary hair, no facial hair), eunuchoidal body habitus, poor muscle development, reduced peak bone mass

Testosterone drives puberty; deficiency leads to eunuchoid proportions due to delayed epiphyseal closure.

**Q:** Which cancers are commonly associated with paraneoplastic syndromes?

**A:** Lung, breast, ovaries, and lymphatic system

These tumors commonly produce ectopic hormones or trigger autoimmune responses.

**Q:** When is testosterone therapy NOT suggested despite low testosterone levels?

**A:** Men >65 years old, men with T2DM and low testosterone, men planning fertility in the near term.

Evidence for benefit in these populations is lacking, and testosterone therapy impairs fertility.

**Q:** Name 4 hereditary disorders causing male infertility.

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens).

These genetic conditions affect sperm motility or reproductive tract development.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for testosterone deficiency?

**A:** Hormone replacement or supplementation, psychological support, gonadectomy for cryptorchidism and streak gonads (due to increased testicular cancer risk).

Treatment aims to replace testosterone and address complications including malignancy risk.

**Q:** What was a significant adverse effect of intensive BP therapy in the ACCORD Trial?

**A:** Increased serious adverse events (3.3% vs 1.27%, P<0.001)

The intensive treatment arm had significantly more serious adverse events, including bradycardia/arrhythmia (0.5% vs 0.13%) and hyperkalemia (0.4% vs 0.04%).

**Q:** Does adding fibrate to statin therapy reduce CV events in T2DM?

**A:** No, no significant reduction in major CV events

The ACCORD Lipid Trial found no significant differences in primary outcome (nonfatal MI, stroke, or CV death) between statin + fibrate vs statin alone.

**Q:** What percentage risk reduction for any diabetes-related endpoint did intensive glucose control achieve in UKPDS 33?

**A:** 12% reduction

Patients allocated to intensive treatment had their risk reduced by 12% for any diabetes-related endpoint (95% CI 1-21, P=0.029).

**Q:** What was the mean follow-up duration in the ACCORD Trial?

**A:** 4.7 years

The ACCORD Trial was an RCT with 4.7 years of mean follow-up studying intensive vs standard therapy in T2DM patients with CV risk factors.

**Q:** How many medications on average were required at 1 year for intensive BP therapy in ACCORD?

**A:** 3.4 medications

Mean number of medications at 1 year for intensive therapy was 3.4 (95% CI 3.4-3.5) vs 2.1 (95% CI 2.1-2.2) for standard therapy.

**Q:** What was the population size in the DCCT/EDIC trial?

**A:** 1441 patients with T1DM

The DCCT trial randomly assigned 1441 patients with T1DM to intensive or conventional therapy for a mean of 6.5 years, with 93% followed until 2005.

**Q:** In UKPDS 33, what were the target FPG levels for intensive vs conventional therapy?

**A:** Intensive: FPG <6 mmol/L; Conventional: FPG <15 mmol/L without symptoms

Intensive treatment targeted FPG <6 mmol/L while conventional treatment aimed for FPG <15 mmol/L without hyperglycemic symptoms.

**Q:** Name 3 hereditary disorders that cause male infertility

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

These genetic conditions affect ciliary function or reproductive tract development.

**Q:** What surgeries can cause male infertility?

**A:** TURP, radical prostatectomy, and orchiectomy

These procedures can damage sperm production, transport, or ejaculation.

**Q:** What are the red flags suggesting breast cancer in a male breast mass?

**A:** Unilateral, eccentric, hard or fixed mass, skin dimpling or retraction, and nipple discharge (especially bloody) or crusting

These features warrant investigation to rule out malignancy.

**Q:** What is ACTH and what are its key features?

**A:** Adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) is a polypeptide cleaved from POMC, secreted pulsatilely from the anterior pituitary with diurnal variation (peak 0200-0400h, trough 1800-2400h)

ACTH stimulates adrenal cortex growth and glucocorticoid release; it can bind to MSH receptors enhancing melanogenesis.

**Q:** What is the normal response of plasma aldosterone to saline infusion (NS) in primary aldosteronism testing?

**A:** Plasma aldosterone >277 pmol/L is consistent with primary aldosteronism, while <140 pmol/L is normal

Failure to suppress aldosterone with volume expansion confirms autonomous aldosterone production.

**Q:** What are the steps in the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis?

**A:** Renin (kidney) converts angiotensinogen to angiotensin I → ACE (lung/kidney) converts to angiotensin II → stimulates aldosterone release → renal Na+ retention, K+ excretion, arteriolar vasoconstriction, ADH release

This axis is critical for blood pressure, volume, and electrolyte homeostasis.

**Q:** What stimulates renin release from the juxtaglomerular apparatus?

**A:** Decreased volume, decreased arterial pressure, decreased Na delivery to macula densa, and sympathetic stimulation

These factors signal the kidney to increase renin production to restore blood pressure and volume.

**Q:** What enzyme converts testosterone to dihydrotestosterone?

**A:** 5-alpha-reductase converts testosterone to dihydrotestosterone (DHT)

DHT is a more potent androgen responsible for male pattern hair growth and prostate development.

**Q:** What is the source of estradiol in females?

**A:** Estradiol is produced from testosterone via aromatase enzyme activity

In females, aromatase is primarily active in ovarian granulosa cells and adipose tissue.

**Q:** What is the 24-hour urine free cortisol test used to evaluate?

**A:** It evaluates for overproduction of cortisol in Cushing's syndrome

Elevated 24-hour urine free cortisol indicates increased endogenous cortisol production.

**Q:** What is the diurnal variation pattern of ACTH and cortisol?

**A:** Peak ACTH and cortisol secretion occurs at 0200-0400h, with lowest levels at 1800-2400h

This circadian rhythm is regulated by the suprachiasmatic nucleus and is disrupted in Cushing's syndrome.

**Q:** What was the conclusion of the ACCORD Lipid trial regarding adding fibrate to statin therapy in T2DM?

**A:** Adding fibrate to statin therapy does not reduce major CV event risk in T2DM patients

This double-blinded RCT with 5518 T2DM patients over 4.7 years found no significant differences in primary outcome (major CV events) between statin + fibrate vs statin alone.

**Q:** What hereditary disorders cause male infertility due to ciliary dysfunction?

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia)

Defective cilia affect sperm motility, causing infertility and also causing bronchiectasis and sinusitis.

**Q:** Why does cystic fibrosis cause male infertility?

**A:** Absence of the vas deferens

CFTR mutations cause congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens (CBAVD), obstructing sperm transport.

**Q:** What fetal abnormalities occur with testosterone deficiency in the first trimester?

**A:** Incomplete virilization of external genitalia (ambiguous genitalia) and incomplete development of Wolffian ducts

Testosterone is essential for male internal and external genital development during the first trimester.

**Q:** What are the features of prepubertal testosterone deficiency?

**A:** High-pitched voice, sparse pubic and axillary hair, absence of facial hair, eunuchoidal body habitus, poor muscle development, reduced peak bone mass

Without testosterone, pubertal maturation does not occur, leading to eunuchoid proportions from continued long bone growth.

**Q:** What are the features of postpubertal testosterone deficiency?

**A:** Decreased energy, mood, and libido; fine wrinkles around mouth and eyes; decreased pubic/axillary hair, hematocrit, muscle mass, strength, and BMD

Testosterone deficiency after puberty causes regression of secondary sexual characteristics and reduced anabolic effects.

**Q:** Which cancers commonly cause paraneoplastic syndromes?

**A:** Lung, breast, ovaries, or lymphatic system

These tumors commonly produce ectopic hormones or trigger immune cross-reactions.

**Q:** List hereditary disorders that cause male infertility

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

Kartagener syndrome causes immotile sperm. CFTR mutations cause congenital absence of the vas deferens.

**Q:** List surgical causes of male infertility

**A:** TURP, radical prostatectomy, and orchiectomy

These procedures can damage nerves, remove reproductive structures, or impair sperm transport.

**Q:** What cancers are commonly associated with paraneoplastic syndromes?

**A:** Lung, breast, ovaries, and lymphatic system

These cancers frequently produce ectopic hormones or trigger immune cross-reactions.

**Q:** What causes ectopic ACTH secretion leading to Cushing's syndrome?

**A:** Small cell lung cancer (most common), pancreatic cancer, and other neuroendocrine tumors

These tumors produce ACTH or ACTH-like peptides causing cortisol excess.

**Q:** What hereditary disorders can cause male infertility?

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia) and cystic fibrosis (absence of vas deferens)

These genetic conditions affect sperm motility or reproductive tract development.

**Q:** What clinical features occur with testosterone deficiency in the first trimester in utero?

**A:** Incomplete virilization of external genitalia (ambiguous genitalia) and incomplete development of Wolffian ducts

Androgens are critical for male genital development during early pregnancy.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of prepubertal testosterone deficiency?

**A:** Incomplete pubertal maturation (high pitch voice, sparse pubic/axillary hair, absent facial hair), eunuchoidal body habitus, poor muscle development, and reduced peak bone mass

Testosterone is essential for pubertal development and bone/muscle maturation.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of postpubertal testosterone deficiency?

**A:** Decrease in energy, mood, and libido; fine wrinkles around mouth and eyes; decrease in pubic/axillary hair, hematocrit, muscle mass, strength, and BMD

Testosterone maintains secondary sexual characteristics and lean body mass in adults.

**Q:** What is the treatment for testosterone deficiency due to defects in androgen action?

**A:** Hormone replacement or supplementation, psychological support, and gonadectomy for cryptorchidism and streak gonads (due to increased cancer risk)

Treatment addresses both hormone deficiency and cancer prevention in dysgenetic gonads.

**Q:** When is testosterone therapy NOT suggested?

**A:** Men >65 years, men with T2DM with low testosterone, and men planning fertility in the near term.

Evidence for benefit in older men is unclear, and testosterone suppresses spermatogenesis, causing infertility.

**Q:** What percentage of Canadians are affected by diabetes?

**A:** Up to 10%

Diabetes is a major health concern affecting approximately 10% of the Canadian population.

**Q:** How does mortality risk compare for Canadian adults with vs without diabetes?

**A:** Adults with DM are twice as likely to die prematurely

Canadian adults with diabetes have significantly increased mortality risk compared to non-diabetic adults.

**Q:** Which ethnic groups have increased risk of T2DM?

**A:** African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, or South Asian descent

These ethnic groups have higher genetic predisposition to type 2 diabetes mellitus.

**Q:** What tool is recommended for assessing T2DM risk?

**A:** CANRISK calculator

CANRISK is a validated Canadian tool for assessing type 2 diabetes risk based on risk factors.

**Q:** What is the LDL-C target for diabetics on treatment?

**A:** <2 mmol/L

Lipid management in diabetes aims for LDL cholesterol below 2 mmol/L to reduce CV risk.

**Q:** What percentage of calories should come from combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids?

**A:** <10% of calories

Diabetics should limit saturated and trans fats to less than 10% of total caloric intake.

**Q:** What is the CANRISK calculator used for?

**A:** To identify high-risk individuals for T2DM screening

CANRISK is a validated Canadian tool that calculates diabetes risk based on modifiable and non-modifiable risk factors.

**Q:** What screening should be done annually for diabetes patients?

**A:** Retinopathy screening, fasting lipid profile, random urine ACR and Cr, foot exam with 10g monofilament

Annual comprehensive screening is essential for early detection and prevention of diabetes complications.

**Q:** How often should glucose monitoring be done in diabetes patients?

**A:** Regular glucose monitoring as directed; frequency depends on treatment regimen

Self-monitoring of blood glucose helps patients and providers adjust therapy and prevent hypo/hyperglycemia.

**Q:** How often should home glucose monitors be calibrated?

**A:** Bimonthly (bi-weekly)

Regular calibration ensures accuracy of self-monitored glucose readings, which are critical for treatment decisions.

**Q:** How often should ECG be repeated for high-risk diabetes patients?

**A:** Every 2 years

Regular cardiac screening is important due to the increased cardiovascular risk in diabetes patients.

**Q:** What are the complications and comorbidities associated with diabetes?

**A:** Leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction; Canadian adults with DM are twice as likely to die prematurely

Diabetes has significant microvascular and macrovascular complications.

**Q:** What does a foot exam for sensation involve in diabetes monitoring?

**A:** 10g monofilament test

The 10g monofilament test is used to assess sensation in the feet of diabetic patients to identify those at risk for foot ulcers.

**Q:** What percentage of calories should come from combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids for diabetics?

**A:** Less than 10%

Diabetics should decrease combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids to less than 10% of total calories.

**Q:** How often should retinopathy screening occur in diabetic patients?

**A:** Annually

Annual retinopathy screening is recommended for diabetic patients to prevent vision loss.

**Q:** What is the baseline ECG recommendation for diabetic patients?

**A:** Baseline ECG with repeat testing every 2 years for those at high-risk

A baseline ECG is recommended for diabetic patients, with repeat testing every 2 years for those at high cardiovascular risk.

**Q:** How does diabetes affect cardiovascular outcomes in Canadian adults?

**A:** Canadian adults with diabetes are twice as likely to die prematurely compared to persons without diabetes

Diabetic adults have significantly increased cardiovascular mortality, being twice as likely to die prematurely.

**Q:** Which high-risk ethnic populations have increased T2DM risk?

**A:** African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, or South Asian descent

People of African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, or South Asian descent have increased risk of developing type 2 diabetes.

**Q:** What is the role of a registered dietician in diabetes management?

**A:** All diabetics should see a registered dietician for nutrition counselling

Medical nutrition therapy from a registered dietician is a cornerstone of diabetes management.

**Q:** What are the major long-term complications of diabetes?

**A:** New-onset blindness and renal dysfunction

Diabetes is the leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction in adults.

**Q:** What is the target LDL cholesterol for diabetics on treatment?

**A:** LDL-C <2 mmol/L

LDL-C should be <2 mmol/L when treating cardiovascular risk in diabetics.

**Q:** How often should retinopathy screening be arranged for diabetics?

**A:** Annually

Annual retinopathy screening should be arranged to prevent vision loss from diabetic eye disease.

**Q:** When should T1DM patients have their first ophthalmology consult?

**A:** Within 5 years of diagnosis

T1DM patients should have an ophthalmology consult within 5 years of diagnosis for retinopathy screening.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency for ECG testing in high-risk diabetics?

**A:** Every 2 years

Baseline ECG should be done, with repeat testing every 2 years for those at high cardiovascular risk.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency for lipid profile testing in diabetics?

**A:** Annually

Fasting lipid profile should be checked annually in diabetics to assess cardiovascular risk.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency for urine ACR and Cr testing in diabetics?

**A:** Annually

Annual random urine ACR and creatinine should be monitored to detect diabetic nephropathy.

**Q:** What is the SMART goals framework for diabetes management?

**A:** Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound

SMART goals help diabetics set concrete objectives for glycemic control, blood pressure, and lifestyle modifications.

**Q:** What is a key dietary recommendation for diabetics regarding saturated fats?

**A:** Decrease combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids to <10% of calories

Diabetics should limit combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids to less than 10% of total caloric intake.

**Q:** What is the target LDL cholesterol for diabetic patients on lipid-lowering therapy?

**A:** <2 mmol/L

LDL-C target of <2 mmol/L is recommended for cardiovascular risk reduction in diabetes.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency of diabetes monitoring visits?

**A:** Every 2-4 months for ongoing management

Regular follow-up every 2-4 months allows for treatment adjustment and complication monitoring.

**Q:** How often should ECG be repeated for high-risk diabetic patients?

**A:** Every 2 years

High-risk diabetic patients should have ECG monitoring every 2 years for cardiovascular assessment.

**Q:** What percentage of total calories should come from combined saturated fats and trans fatty acids?

**A:** <10%

Limiting saturated and trans fats to less than 10% of total caloric intake is recommended for cardiovascular health.

**Q:** What ethnic groups have increased risk of T2DM?

**A:** African, Arab, Asian, Hispanic, or South Asian descent

Certain ethnic populations have higher predilection for developing type 2 diabetes.

**Q:** What are the chronic macrovascular complications of diabetes?

**A:** CAD (coronary artery disease), CVD (cerebrovascular disease), PVD (peripheral vascular disease)

Macrovascular complications are the leading cause of mortality in diabetic patients.

**Q:** What are the major complications of diabetes that Canadians face?

**A:** Leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction

Diabetes is the leading cause of new-onset blindness (retinopathy) and renal dysfunction (nephropathy) in Canadians.

**Q:** What is the mortality risk for Canadian adults with diabetes?

**A:** Twice as likely to die prematurely compared to persons without diabetes

Canadian adults with diabetes have twice the risk of premature death compared to those without diabetes.

**Q:** What is the LDL-C target in diabetic patients?

**A:** <2 mmol/L if treating

LDL cholesterol should be less than 2 mmol/L in diabetic patients receiving lipid-lowering therapy.

**Q:** What is the annual screening recommendation for diabetic retinopathy?

**A:** Annual retinopathy screening

Annual retinopathy screening should be arranged for all diabetic patients to detect and prevent vision loss.

**Q:** How often should fasting lipid profile be checked in diabetes?

**A:** Annually

Fasting lipid profile should be checked annually in diabetic patients to assess cardiovascular risk.

**Q:** What is the target LDL cholesterol for diabetic patients?

**A:** LDL-C <2 mmol/L

Statin therapy is recommended to achieve LDL-C <2 mmol/L in diabetic patients for CVD risk reduction.

**Q:** How often should fasting lipid profile be checked in diabetic patients?

**A:** Annually

Annual lipid profiling is recommended to assess cardiovascular risk in diabetic patients.

**Q:** What is used to test foot sensation in diabetic patients?

**A:** 10g monofilament

The 10g monofilament test assesses protective sensation and identifies patients at risk for foot ulcers.

**Q:** How often should diabetic retinopathy screening occur?

**A:** Annually

Annual retinopathy screening is recommended to detect early diabetic eye disease.

**Q:** What are the long-term complications of diabetes?

**A:** Blindness (retinopathy), renal dysfunction (nephropathy), CAD, CVD, PVD

Diabetes leads to microvascular and macrovascular complications affecting multiple organ systems.

**Q:** Diabetic adults in Canada are how much more likely to die prematurely compared to non-diabetics?

**A:** Twice as likely

Diabetes significantly increases mortality risk, with diabetic adults having double the risk of premature death.

**Q:** Why is diabetes a major health concern in Canada?

**A:** Leading cause of new-onset blindness and renal dysfunction; Canadian adults with DM are twice as likely to die prematurely

DM causes significant microvascular and macrovascular complications leading to morbidity and mortality.

**Q:** When should Type 1 DM patients have their first ophthalmology exam?

**A:** Within 5 years of diagnosis

Early retinopathy screening is essential as diabetic eye disease can develop silently.

**Q:** What is IGT and IFG?

**A:** Impaired Glucose Tolerance and Impaired Fasting Glucose - prediabetic states

IGT and IFG represent intermediate metabolic states indicating prediabetes.

**Q:** What are the rising causes of T2DM incidence?

**A:** Increasing obesity, sedentary lifestyle, and aging of the population

Type 2 diabetes incidence is increasing due to modifiable lifestyle factors (obesity, inactivity) and demographic changes (aging population).

**Q:** What annual investigations are recommended for diabetes?

**A:** Annual fasting lipid profile, random urine ACR and Cr, annual retinopathy screening

Annual screening for dyslipidemia, nephropathy (urine albumin:creatinine ratio), and diabetic retinopathy is essential for complication prevention.

**Q:** When should ophthalmology consultation be arranged for T1DM patients?

**A:** Within 5 years of diagnosis

T1DM patients should have their first ophthalmology consultation within 5 years of diagnosis to screen for diabetic retinopathy.

**Q:** What pituitary dysfunction occurs in hemochromatosis?

**A:** Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (impotence, decreased libido, amenorrhea)

Iron deposition in the anterior pituitary causes secondary hypogonadism with low LH/FSH and low testosterone/estrogen levels.

## Endocrinology

**Q:** How do you distinguish central diabetes insipidus from nephrogenic diabetes insipidus?

**A:** Both present with large volumes of dilute urine (>3 L/day) and polydipsia. Fluid deprivation test differentiates DI from psychogenic polydipsia (urine remains dilute in DI). Desmopressin (DDAVP) challenge distinguishes the types: central DI concentrates urine with DDAVP; nephrogenic DI does not respond to DDAVP.

Central DI is caused by insufficient ADH (pituitary surgery, tumours, idiopathic). Nephrogenic DI is caused by renal resistance to ADH (lithium, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia). Treatment: central = desmopressin; nephrogenic = solute restriction + thiazide diuretics.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of hypercalcemia in a healthy outpatient, and when is surgery indicated?

**A:** Primary hyperparathyroidism (most commonly due to a solitary parathyroid adenoma). Surgery is recommended for symptomatic patients or asymptomatic patients meeting any of: serum Ca >0.25 mmol/L above upper limit, skeletal involvement (fracture, BMD T-score <-2.5), renal involvement (eGFR <60, nephrolithiasis, hypercalciuria), or age <50.

Primary hyperparathyroidism presents with elevated PTH and calcium. In contrast, malignancy is the most common cause of hypercalcemia in hospitalized patients. Asymptomatic patients not meeting surgical criteria can be monitored with annual serum calcium, eGFR, and BMD every 1-2 years.

**Q:** What is myxedema coma, and what is the emergency treatment?

**A:** Myxedema coma is severe decompensated hypothyroidism with hallmark features of decreased mental status and hypothermia, plus hyponatremia, hypotension, hypoglycemia, and bradycardia. Emergency treatment: ICU admission, hydrocortisone 100 mg q8h (for possible concurrent adrenal insufficiency), L-thyroxine 0.2-0.5 mg IV loading dose then 0.1 mg IV daily, passive rewarming, IV dextrose, vasopressors.

Corticosteroids must be given before or with thyroid hormone replacement because treating hypothyroidism increases cortisol metabolism, which can precipitate adrenal crisis in patients with concurrent adrenal insufficiency.

**Q:** What is the sick euthyroid syndrome (non-thyroidal illness), and should thyroid hormone replacement be given?

**A:** Sick euthyroid syndrome is altered thyroid hormone metabolism during severe illness, trauma, or starvation. Labs show initially low free T3, then low TSH, and in severe illness, low free T4. TSH may transiently rise during recovery. Thyroid hormone replacement has NOT been shown to be beneficial; treat the underlying disease.

This syndrome may be protective by reducing tissue catabolism during illness. It is important to distinguish from true hypothyroidism. Thyroid function tests normalize once the patient recovers, often with a transient TSH elevation.

**Q:** For Type 1 diabetes, what is the estimated total daily insulin requirement, and how is it distributed in a basal-bolus regimen?

**A:** Estimated total insulin requirement is 0.5-0.7 U/kg/day. In a basal-bolus (MDI) regimen: 40% given as basal insulin at bedtime, and the remaining 60% divided equally (20% each) as bolus insulin before breakfast, lunch, and dinner.

Basal insulin (glargine, detemir, degludec) provides a steady baseline, while rapid-acting bolus insulin (aspart, lispro, glulisine) covers mealtime glucose spikes. Continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (insulin pump) is an alternative that may be paired with continuous glucose monitoring.

**Q:** A 52-year-old obese man with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes has an HbA1c of 8.5% (target <7%). According to Canadian guidelines, what is the first-line pharmacotherapy and what additional agent class should be considered if he has established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease?

**A:** First-line: metformin. If ASCVD is present, add a GLP-1 receptor agonist (e.g., liraglutide, semaglutide) or SGLT2 inhibitor (e.g., empagliflozin) for cardiorenal benefit.

Metformin remains the cornerstone of T2DM management. Canadian guidelines recommend adding agents with demonstrated cardiorenal benefits (GLP-1 RA or SGLT2i) in patients with ASCVD, CKD, or heart failure, independent of glycemic control.

**Q:** What are the SMART goals mnemonic components for optimal diabetes management?

**A:** S - Smoking cessation, M - Medication (ACEi/ARB, statin, ASA), A - A1C <7%, R - Risk factor modification (BP <130/80), T - Targets (LDL <2 mmol/L if treating).

The SMART mnemonic helps organize the comprehensive approach to diabetes care beyond glycemic control, including cardiovascular risk factor management, which is essential since CVD is the leading cause of death in diabetic patients.

**Q:** A 68-year-old woman had a wrist fracture after a fall from standing height. She has a BMI of 19 and a family history of hip fracture. What screening tool should be used and what is the recommended pharmacotherapy threshold?

**A:** Use FRAX (preferred) or CAROC tool with BMD to calculate 10-year fracture risk. Pharmacotherapy is suggested if 10-year fracture risk >20% OR T-score <-2.5 with age >70.

Key osteoporosis risk factors include prior fragility fracture after age 40, glucocorticoid use, parental hip fracture, BMI <20, smoking, and alcohol >3 drinks/day. All patients need vitamin D (800-2000 IU/day if >50) and calcium (1200 mg/day).

**Q:** What are the indications for SGLT2 inhibitors in diabetic patients beyond glycemic control?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitors (canagliflozin, dapagliflozin, empagliflozin) are indicated for cardiorenal benefit in patients with: ASCVD, heart failure (especially HFrEF), and chronic kidney disease, independent of glycemic targets.

SGLT2 inhibitors reduce hospitalization for heart failure and slow CKD progression. They should be initiated if eGFR is adequate (varies by agent). Key side effects include genital mycotic infections and euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis.

**Q:** What is the approach to acute hypercalcemia/hypercalcemic crisis?
