**A:** 1) IV NS 4-6L/24h (FLUIDS first). 2) Calcitonin 4IU/kg SC q12h (rapid onset 4-6h, works only 48h). 3) Bisphosphonate IV (treatment of choice — zoledronic acid 4mg or pamidronate 60-90mg; effect in 24-48h). 4) Loop diuretic only if hypervolemic.

Hypercalcemia is defined as corrected Ca >2.6mmol/L. Severe (>3.5mmol/L) requires urgent treatment. The two most common causes are primary hyperparathyroidism (outpatient) and malignancy (inpatient). Corrected Ca = measured Ca + 0.02 × (40 - albumin).

**Q:** What are the symptoms of hypercalcemia using the classic mnemonic?

**A:** 'Bones, stones, groans, and psychiatric overtones' — Bones (bone pain, fractures), Stones (nephrolithiasis, renal failure), Groans (constipation, nausea, vomiting, PUD, pancreatitis), Psychiatric overtones (depression, confusion, psychosis).

Symptoms depend on absolute Ca value and rate of rise. Additional features include shortened QT interval, polyuria (nephrogenic DI), hypotonia, and weakness. Hypercalcemic crisis (>4mmol/L) presents with oliguria and mental status changes progressing to coma.

**Q:** What are Chvostek's sign and Trousseau's sign, and what condition do they indicate?

**A:** Chvostek's sign: tapping CN VII produces facial muscle contraction. Trousseau's sign: blood pressure cuff inflation above systolic for 3 minutes produces carpal spasm. Both indicate hypocalcemia.

Tetany is the hallmark of hypocalcemia. It ranges from mild (perioral numbness, paresthesia) to severe (carpopedal spasm, laryngospasm, seizures). Must treat concurrent hypomagnesemia or calcium will not normalize.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of hypocalcemia following thyroidectomy?

**A:** Iatrogenic hypoparathyroidism from inadvertent damage to or removal of parathyroid glands. This is permanent in <3% of surgeries.

Treatment of hypoparathyroidism-induced hypocalcemia requires 1,25 vitamin D (calcitriol) for GI absorption of calcium since PTH is needed for renal 1-alpha-hydroxylation. The goal is to raise Ca to low-normal (2.0-2.1 mmol/L) to maximize residual PTH stimulation.

**Q:** What is the definition of osteoporosis by BMD T-score?

**A:** T-score ≤ -2.5. Osteopenia (low bone mass): T-score between -1.0 and -2.5.

BMD is measured at hip and lumbar spine by DEXA. 10-year fracture risk guides treatment: <15% unlikely to benefit from pharmacotherapy; 15-19.9% suggest starting therapy; >20% recommend therapy. First-line treatments include bisphosphonates and denosumab.

**Q:** What are the first-line pharmacologic treatments for osteoporosis?

**A:** Bisphosphonates (alendronate PO, risedronate PO, zoledronic acid IV) and denosumab (RANKL inhibitor) — all Grade A evidence for prevention of hip, nonvertebral, and vertebral fractures.

Denosumab should not be abruptly stopped due to risk of multiple vertebral fractures from increased bone turnover on discontinuation. Teriparatide (PTH analogue) and romosozumab (anti-sclerostin) are used for 12-24 months followed by anti-resorptive therapy.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and classic radiologic findings of Paget's disease of bone?

**A:** Usually asymptomatic (incidental elevated ALP with normal LFTs). Features: severe bone pain, skeletal deformities (bowed tibias, kyphosis), frequent fractures. Radiology: osteolytic lesions, cortical thickening, pseudofractures. 'Cotton wool' appearance of skull.

Paget's affects 3% of population, 10% of those >80. It involves increased osteoclastic activity followed by osteoblastic response producing structurally abnormal bone. Complications include osteosarcoma (1-3%), cranial nerve compression, and high-output CHF.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Any one of: FPG ≥7.0 mmol/L, A1c ≥6.5%, 2h PG in 75g OGTT ≥11.1 mmol/L, or random PG ≥11.1 mmol/L with symptoms. Without symptoms, a confirmatory test on another day is required.

Pre-diabetes criteria: IFG 6.1-6.9, IGT 7.8-11.0 on OGTT, or A1c 6.0-6.4%. A1c is not used for diagnosis of suspected T1DM, children, adolescents, or pregnant women. Lifestyle modifications decrease progression to DM by 58%, metformin by 31%.

**Q:** Compare Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes mellitus by onset, pathophysiology, and acute complications.

**A:** T1DM: usually <30yr, autoimmune β-cell destruction, absolute insulin deficiency, DKA. T2DM: usually >40yr, insulin resistance + impaired secretion, accounts for 90% of DM, HHS (hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state).

T1DM is associated with HLA-DR3/DR4 and has 30-40% monozygotic twin concordance. T2DM has greater heritability (70-90% twin concordance) and is polygenic. Risk factors for T2DM include obesity, family history, ethnicity, and metabolic syndrome components.

**Q:** What is the A1c target for most patients with diabetes, and when might higher targets be acceptable?

**A:** Target A1c <7.0% for most T1DM and T2DM patients. Higher targets (7.1-8.5%) may be considered for limited life expectancy, greater functional dependency, or history of recurrent severe hypoglycemia. A1c <6.0% may cause harm (ACCORD trial).

A1c measures glycemic control over 3 months and should be measured every 3-6 months. More intensive targets (<6.5%) may reduce nephropathy and retinopathy if achieved without significant hypoglycemia. Time in range (3.9-10 mmol/L) should be >70%.

**Q:** What is the first-line medication for T2DM and what are its key advantages?

**A:** Metformin. Advantages: A1c reduction ~1.5%, weight neutral/loss, negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy, low cost, cardiovascular benefit (UKPDS 34 showed reduced all-cause mortality).

Metformin sensitizes peripheral tissues to insulin and decreases hepatic glucose production via AMPK activation. Contraindicated in severe renal dysfunction (eGFR <15), moderate-severe liver dysfunction, and cardiac dysfunction. Main side effects are GI upset and rare lactic acidosis.

**Q:** What are the key differences between DKA and HHS?

**A:** DKA: T1DM, absolute insulin deficiency, BG 14-55, metabolic acidosis with ketosis, Kussmaul respirations. HHS: T2DM, relative insulin deficiency, BG 44-133, hyperosmolality without significant ketosis, more severe dehydration, altered LOC.

In DKA, the anion gap is the most important endpoint for monitoring resolution. In HHS, small amounts of insulin prevent ketosis by inhibiting lipolysis. HHS has more severe dehydration due to gradual onset and impaired fluid intake. Both require aggressive IV fluid resuscitation.

**Q:** What is the SAD MANS mnemonic for sick day management in diabetes?

**A:** Hold the following medications when ill and unable to maintain fluid intake: Sulfonylureas, ACEIs, Diuretics/direct renin inhibitors, Metformin, ARBs, NSAIDs, SGLT2 inhibitors.

These medications should be held during acute illness with poor fluid intake or acute renal function decline to prevent complications such as hypoglycemia, AKI, and lactic acidosis. Patients should monitor blood glucose more frequently during illness.

**Q:** What SGLT2 inhibitor benefits extend beyond glycemic control?

**A:** Cardiovascular mortality reduction, CV outcomes benefit in patients with known CVD, renal protection, and weight loss. Rare side effects include euglycemic DKA, UTIs, and genital infections.

SGLT2 inhibitors (canagliflozin, dapagliflozin, empagliflozin) work by inhibiting glucose reabsorption in the proximal renal tubule. They provide A1c reduction of 0.7-1.0% with negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy. Contraindicated in severe renal impairment (eGFR <15).

**Q:** What are the 8 I's precipitating DKA?

**A:** Infection, Ischemia or Infarction, Iatrogenic (glucocorticoids), Intoxication, Insulin missed, Inflammation (pancreatitis, cholecystitis), Intraoperative/perioperative stress, Initial presentation (new T1DM diagnosis).

DKA management: ABCs first, then IV NS (500mL/h x4h, then 250mL/h), insulin infusion (typically 0.1 U/kg/h), potassium replacement (watch for hypokalemia as insulin drives K intracellular), and monitoring of anion gap for resolution.

**Q:** What are the statin-indicated conditions in diabetes regardless of LDL level?

**A:** Age >40, microvascular complications or diabetes >15 years duration AND age >30, or warrants therapy based on CCS Guidelines for Dyslipidemia.

In diabetes management, LDL target is <2.0mmol/L or 50% reduction. Blood pressure target is <130/80. ACEIs/ARBs are indicated regardless of BP in: clinical CVD, age >55 with additional CV risk factor, or microvascular complications.

**Q:** What are the first-line statin alternatives for LDL lowering and when is each used?

**A:** Ezetimibe: add to statin when LDL not at target. Anti-PCSK9 antibodies (evolocumab, alirocumab): add to maximally tolerated statin + ezetimibe when still not at target, especially in familial hypercholesterolemia or ASCVD.

Statins inhibit HMG-CoA reductase, the rate-limiting step in cholesterol biosynthesis. Major side effects include elevated liver enzymes, myositis, and rhabdomyolysis (increased risk with fibrate combination). IMPROVE-IT trial showed ezetimibe added to statins reduces cardiovascular events post-ACS.

**Q:** Differentiate primary (hypergonadotropic) from secondary (hypogonadotropic) hypogonadism.

**A:** Primary: testicular failure, ↑LH/FSH, ↓testosterone. Causes: Klinefelter syndrome, cryptorchidism, orchitis. Secondary: hypothalamic-pituitary failure, ↓LH/FSH, ↓testosterone. Causes: Kallmann syndrome, prolactinoma, hemochromatosis.

Testosterone deficiency syndrome requires clinical manifestations AND documented low total testosterone on 2 separate fasting morning samples. Testosterone replacement improves libido, muscle mass, strength, and bone mass but is contraindicated in prostate/breast cancer.

**Q:** What are the causes of gynecomastia (DOC TECH mnemonic)?

**A:** Drugs (spironolactone, cimetidine, digoxin, ketoconazole), Obesity, Congenital (Klinefelter), Tumour (germ cell tumours), Excess estrogen (liver disease), Chronic disease (cirrhosis, CKD), Hyperthyroidism.

Physiologic gynecomastia has a trimodal distribution: neonatal, pubertal (46%, regresses in 90%), and ages 50-80 (>65%). It results from hormonal imbalance with increased estrogen or decreased androgen activity. Red flags suggesting breast cancer: unilateral, eccentric, hard/fixed mass.

**Q:** What paraneoplastic syndrome is associated with small cell lung cancer producing ectopic ACTH?

**A:** Cushing's syndrome — presenting with weight gain in trunk/face, stretch marks, easy bruising, slow wound healing, acne, hirsutism.

Paraneoplastic syndromes are non-metastatic systemic effects triggered by antibodies cross-reacting with normal tissue or by production of physiologically active substances. SIADH is another common paraneoplastic syndrome of small cell lung cancer.

**Q:** What is Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS) and what malignancy is it associated with?

**A:** LEMS causes proximal limb muscle weakness due to antibodies interfering with acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction. It is associated with small cell lung cancer.

LEMS differs from myasthenia gravis in that weakness improves with repeated use (vs. worsens in MG). Myasthenia gravis is associated with thymoma. Both are paraneoplastic autoimmune conditions affecting neuromuscular transmission.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of renal osteodystrophy and what are the four types of bone disease?

**A:** Features: soft tissue calcifications, bone pain, fractures, pruritus, neuromuscular irritability. Four types: osteomalacia (low turnover + increased osteoid), adynamic bone disease (low turnover), osteitis fibrosa cystica (high turnover from secondary hyperPTH), mixed uremic osteodystrophy.

Renal osteodystrophy is caused by hyperphosphatemia (inhibits 1,25(OH)2 vitamin D synthesis) and loss of renal mass (reduced 1-alpha-hydroxylase). Treatment includes phosphate restriction, calcium supplements, phosphate binders, and calcitriol with monitoring to avoid hypercalcemia.

**Q:** What are the key findings differentiating vitamin D deficiency osteomalacia from other causes on labs?

**A:** Vitamin D deficiency: ↓phosphate, ↓to normal calcium, ↑ALP, ↓calcitriol. Hypophosphatemia: ↓phosphate, normal calcium, ↑ALP. Proximal RTA: ↓phosphate, normal calcium, normal ALP with hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis.

Osteomalacia is osteopenia with disordered calcification (rickets in children). Radiologic findings include Looser zones (pseudofractures), loss of vertebral body trabeculae, and changes from secondary hyperparathyroidism. Bone biopsy is gold standard but usually unnecessary.

**Q:** What is the corrected sodium formula in DKA?

**A:** Corrected sodium = current sodium + [0.3 × (current glucose - 5)]. For every increase in blood glucose by 10 mmol/L, sodium decreases by 3 mmol/L (pseudohyponatremia).

In DKA, hyperglycemia causes water shift to ECF, producing pseudohyponatremia. Total body potassium is depleted but serum K may be normal or elevated due to shift from ICF to ECF (lack of insulin, increased osmolality). Monitor K closely as insulin drives it intracellularly.

**Q:** What GLP-1 receptor agonists provide cardiovascular outcomes benefit, and what are their key contraindications?

**A:** Liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide provide CV mortality and outcomes benefit. Contraindications: T1DM, DKA, acute pancreatitis history, MEN2 syndrome, medullary thyroid carcinoma (personal/family history).

GLP-1 agonists promote insulin release, suppress glucagon, slow gastric emptying, and promote weight loss. A1c reduction is high (~1.0%). Side effects include GI symptoms (nausea, diarrhea), increased HR, and rare pancreatitis. Negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy.

**Q:** What is the SMART mnemonic for diabetes management goals?

**A:** A1C <7%, BP <130/80, Cholesterol LDL-C <2 mmol/L if treating, Drugs (consider ACEI/ARB, statin, ASA), Exercise/Eating, Smoking cessation.

The SMART framework provides a comprehensive approach to diabetes management beyond glycemic control. Cardiovascular risk reduction is critical since DM patients are twice as likely to die prematurely.

**Q:** When should T2DM screening begin and at what interval?

**A:** FBG or HbA1C in everyone age ≥40 every 3 years; more frequent/earlier if ≥1 risk factor present.

Risk factors for earlier screening include first-degree relative with DM, obesity, HTN, dyslipidemia, membership in high-risk populations (Indigenous, Hispanic, Asian, African descent), and prior gestational diabetes.

**Q:** What is the first-line drug for T2DM, and when should insulin be started at diagnosis?

**A:** Metformin is first-line. Start insulin + metformin at diagnosis if symptomatic hyperglycemia and/or metabolic decompensation is present.

If A1C is >1.5% above target, start metformin plus a second agent. For patients with ASCVD, HF with reduced EF, or CKD, agents with cardiorenal benefits (GLP-1 RA, SGLT2i) should be considered.

**Q:** Which diabetes medications have demonstrated cardiorenal benefits?

**A:** SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin) and GLP-1 receptor agonists (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide).

These agents reduce major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) and/or hospitalization for heart failure. They are preferred second-line agents in patients with ASCVD, CKD, or HF with reduced ejection fraction.

**Q:** What causes should be suspected when HTN is refractory to ≥3 drugs?

**A:** Primary hyperaldosteronism (suspect with spontaneous hypokalemia or profound diuretic-induced hypokalemia <3.0 mmol/L). Also consider renovascular HTN, pheochromocytoma, and Cushing's syndrome.

Screening for hyperaldosteronism uses morning plasma aldosterone and renin levels (aldosterone-to-renin ratio). Discontinue aldosterone antagonists, ARBs, beta-blockers, and clonidine prior to testing.

**Q:** When should pharmacotherapy for osteoporosis be initiated?

**A:** When 10-year fracture risk is >20% (using FRAX or CAROC), OR T-score <-2.5 and age >70, OR after a fragility fracture.

All adults should receive balance/muscle-strengthening exercises twice weekly, calcium-rich foods, and minimum vitamin D 400 IU daily. Recommended calcium: 1000 mg/day (19-50 yr), 1200 mg/day (>50 yr).

**Q:** What is Whipple's triad for insulinoma?

**A:** Symptomatic fasting hypoglycemia, serum glucose <2.8 mmol/L, relief of symptoms when glucose is administered.

Insulinoma is the most common pancreatic endocrine neoplasm; 10% associated with MEN1 syndrome. Only 10% are malignant. Investigations show decreased glucose with elevated insulin and C-peptide. Tumours are evenly distributed throughout the pancreas.

**Q:** What characterizes Zollinger-Ellison syndrome?

**A:** Gastrin-secreting tumour (gastrinoma) causing massive gastric acid hypersecretion with multiple peptic ulcers in atypical locations refractory to antacid therapy, diarrhea, and abdominal pain. Serum gastrin usually >1000 pg/mL.

50% of gastrinomas are malignant. Associated with MEN1. 70-90% found in Passaro's triangle (head of pancreas, 2nd portion of duodenum, confluence of cystic/CBD). Treatment: surgical resection + high-dose PPI + octreotide.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic workup for an adrenal incidentaloma?

**A:** CT (size >4-6cm suggests carcinoma). Functional studies: plasma metanephrines (pheochromocytoma), 24h urine cortisol or 1mg dexamethasone suppression test (Cushing's), AM aldosterone/renin ratio (aldosteronoma). FNA biopsy is NOT appropriate in most cases.

70-80% are benign adenomas. Functional tumours require resection. Non-functional tumours >4cm should be considered for resection; <4cm can be followed with imaging at 6-12 months. >30% of pheochromocytomas are related to genetic syndromes.

**Q:** What is the starting dose and titration strategy for basal insulin added to anti-hyperglycemic agents in T2DM (Diabetes Canada 2018)?

**A:** Start at 10 U at bedtime (lower for lean patients <50 kg). Increase by 1 U every night until fasting BG reaches target of 4–7 mmol/L. Most patients need 40–50 units/day; there is no maximum dose.

Basal insulin titration targets fasting BG of 4–7 mmol/L. If fasting hypoglycemia occurs, reduce basal dose; if daytime hypoglycemia occurs, reduce oral anti-hyperglycemic agents (especially secretagogues).

**Q:** In a 100 kg patient starting a basal-bolus insulin regimen, calculate the total daily insulin (TDI), basal dose, and each mealtime bolus dose.

**A:** TDI = 0.5 U/kg × 100 kg = 50 U. Basal = 40% of TDI = 20 U at bedtime. Bolus = 60% of TDI = 30 U total → 10 U with each of 3 meals.

The basal-bolus formula is TDI = 0.3–0.5 U/kg; 40% basal, 60% prandial. Insulin secretagogues are stopped when bolus insulin is added; metformin is continued.

**Q:** What is the Correction Factor (CF) for insulin, and how is it applied when BG is between 8 and (8 + CF)?

**A:** CF = 100 ÷ Total Daily Dose (TDD). It represents the change in BG per unit of insulin. If BG is between 8 and (8 + CF), give one additional unit of insulin.

The CF-based sliding scale guides correction boluses. BG <4 mmol/L: give 15 g rapid-acting carbs and recheck in 15 min. BG 4–8: no additional insulin. Each CF above 8 mmol/L adds one correction unit.

**Q:** What are the key risks of Continuous Subcutaneous Insulin Infusion (CSII) pump therapy?

**A:** Risk of DKA if the pump is inadvertently disconnected or malfunctions. There is no subcutaneous depot of long-acting insulin, so interruption quickly leads to insulin deficiency.

CSII delivers a continuous basal dose of rapid-acting insulin analogue (aspart, glulisine, or lispro). Improved quality of life and flexibility are benefits, but pump failure can precipitate DKA rapidly. Insulin pump coverage for T1DM varies by province in Canada.

**Q:** What is the key pathophysiologic difference between DKA and HHS regarding ketosis?

**A:** In DKA: complete insulin deficiency allows uninhibited lipolysis → FFA → ketoacids → metabolic acidosis. In HHS: a small amount of residual insulin inhibits lipolysis, preventing significant ketosis despite severe hyperglycemia.

HHS is characterized by hyperglycemia (typically 44.4–133.2 mmol/L), hyperosmolality, and profound dehydration WITHOUT ketosis. DKA has anion gap metabolic acidosis; HHS does not unless a comorbid precipitant (e.g., lactic acidosis from MI) causes acidosis.

**Q:** What is pseudohyponatremia in DKA, and how is the corrected sodium calculated?

**A:** Hyperglycemia shifts water from ICF to ECF, diluting serum sodium. For every 10 mmol/L rise in BG above normal, serum Na⁺ falls by approximately 3 mmol/L. Corrected Na⁺ = current Na⁺ + [0.3 × (current glucose − 5)].

Accurate sodium assessment is critical in DKA management to guide choice of IV fluid (NS vs. 0.45% NaCl). Potassium balance is also important: there is total body K⁺ depletion despite potentially normal or elevated serum K⁺ due to extracellular shift.

**Q:** What are the clinical features that distinguish DKA from HHS on presentation?

**A:** DKA: Kussmaul respirations, acetone breath, nausea/vomiting, abdominal pain, rapid onset. BG typically 14–55 mmol/L. HHS: insidious onset, no Kussmaul respirations (unless co-precipitant causes acidosis), decreased LOC/coma, severe dehydration. BG typically 44.4–133.2 mmol/L.

HHS most commonly occurs in T2DM patients who are bedridden or have impaired fluid intake. The more gradual onset and prolonged duration lead to more severe dehydration than DKA. Mental status changes in HHS are driven by hyperosmolality.

**Q:** What is the most important endpoint used to monitor resolution of metabolic acidosis in DKA?

**A:** The anion gap (AG) is the most important endpoint to monitor resolution of metabolic acidosis in DKA — NOT blood glucose or serum ketone levels.

Serum or urinary ketone tests (nitroprusside) detect acetone and acetoacetate but NOT β-hydroxybutyrate (BHB), the predominant ketone in DKA. As DKA improves, BHB converts to acetoacetate, causing apparent rise in ketones even as the patient improves. Therefore, the AG is the primary monitoring tool.

**Q:** Describe the insulin and potassium management protocol in DKA treatment.

**A:** Do NOT start insulin if serum K⁺ <3.3 mmol/L — first give 40 mEq/L K⁺ replacement until K⁺ ≥3.3 mmol/L. Then maintain 0.1 U/kg/h regular insulin infusion. When K⁺ is 3.3–5.0 mmol/L, add KCl 10–40 mEq/L to keep K⁺ at 3.5–5 mEq/L.

Insulin drives K⁺ into cells, worsening hypokalemia. Despite total body K⁺ depletion, serum K⁺ may appear normal or elevated initially. Once BG reaches 14 mmol/L, add D5W or D10W to prevent hypoglycemia while continuing insulin to close the anion gap.

**Q:** When is sodium bicarbonate indicated in DKA management?

**A:** HCO₃⁻ is indicated if pH <7.0 OR if there is hypotension, arrhythmia, or coma. Give 1 ampule (50 mmol) in 200 mL D5W over 1 hour, repeated q1–2h until pH ≥7.0. Do NOT give if pH >7.1 (risk of metabolic alkalosis).

Bicarbonate can also be given in life-threatening hyperkalemia. Routine bicarbonate use in DKA is not recommended because it may worsen intracellular acidosis, impair oxygen delivery, and cause paradoxical CSF acidosis.

**Q:** What IV fluid strategy is used in HHS, and when is the switch from NS to 0.45% NaCl made?

**A:** Start with 1–2 L/h NS if severe deficit (shock). Evaluate corrected serum Na⁺: if high or normal → switch to 0.45% NaCl (4–14 mL/kg/h); if low → maintain NS. Switch to D5W when BG reaches 13.9 mmol/L (250 mg/dL).

HHS has less severe K⁺ depletion than DKA. Urinary electrolyte losses from osmotic diuresis are usually hypotonic, so switching to 0.45% NaCl once euvolemic is appropriate. Rate of fall of plasma osmolality should not exceed 3 mOsm/kg/h to prevent cerebral edema.

**Q:** What is euglycemic DKA and in which populations should it be suspected?

**A:** Euglycemic DKA is DKA with near-normal blood glucose levels. It should be suspected in: (1) pregnancy, and (2) patients using SGLT2 inhibitors.

SGLT2 inhibitors promote glucosuria, which lowers blood glucose even in the presence of significant ketoacidosis. The normal BG may mask DKA, so clinicians must check for anion gap acidosis and ketones in any patient on SGLT2 inhibitors who presents unwell.

**Q:** How does the nitroprusside test for ketones fail during DKA treatment, and what is the clinical implication?

**A:** The nitroprusside test detects acetone and acetoacetate but NOT β-hydroxybutyrate (BHB). As DKA is treated, BHB is converted to acetoacetate, causing serum/urine ketone levels to appear to RISE even though the patient is improving.

This can falsely suggest worsening DKA when the patient is actually responding to treatment. Clinicians should monitor the anion gap as the primary endpoint of DKA resolution rather than relying on ketone measurements.

**Q:** What are the macrovascular complications of diabetes mellitus and their relative risks?

**A:** CAD (risk of MI is 3–5× higher), ischemic stroke (2–3× higher in men, 2–5× higher in women), and peripheral arterial disease (risk of foot gangrene 30× higher, risk of lower extremity amputation 15× higher).

CAD is the leading cause of death in T2DM. Diabetes accelerates atherosclerosis. Most patients with DM are classified as high-risk for CAD. HbA1c is an independent predictor of stroke risk. Peripheral arterial disease manifests as intermittent claudication, intestinal angina, and foot ulceration.

**Q:** What BP target and lipid targets are recommended for macrovascular risk reduction in diabetes mellitus?

**A:** BP target: <130/80 mmHg. Lipid target: LDL ≤2.0 mmol/L or >50% LDL reduction from baseline. Statins are indicated for patients with clinical CVD, age ≥40, or diabetes duration >15 years with age >30, or microvascular complications.

Treatment also includes ACEI or ARB in high-risk patients, anti-hyperglycemic agents with cardiovascular benefit (SGLT2i/GLP-1 RAs), smoking cessation, healthy diet, and physical activity. Tight glycemic control is particularly beneficial in early DM (per ACCORD, UKPDS, DCCT studies).

**Q:** What is the most common cause of incident blindness in people of working age, and what are the two forms of diabetic retinopathy?

**A:** Diabetic retinopathy is the most common cause of new-onset blindness in working-age people. Two forms: (1) Non-proliferative: microaneurysms, intraretinal hemorrhage, vascular tortuosity, vascular malformation; (2) Proliferative: abnormal new vessel growth.

Macular edema (diffuse or focal vascular leakage at the macula) can occur in either form. Retinal capillary closure is a key pathologic feature. Glycemic control, lipid control, BP management, and smoking cessation delay onset and progression.

**Q:** What are the ophthalmological screening intervals for diabetic retinopathy in T1DM vs T2DM?

**A:** T2DM: annual eye exam beginning at diagnosis. T1DM: begin annual screening 5 years after diagnosis (for patients ≥15 years old). Follow-up interval should be tailored to severity of retinopathy.

Examination must be performed through dilated pupils by an optometrist or ophthalmologist, regardless of whether the patient is symptomatic. Immediate referral after T2DM diagnosis; 5-year delay in T1DM reflects the rarity of retinopathy in the early years of disease.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of renal failure in North America, and what is the earliest clinical sign of diabetic nephropathy?

**A:** Diabetes mellitus-induced renal failure is the most common cause of renal failure in North America. Microalbuminuria (persistently elevated urinary albumin) is the earliest clinical sign.

ACR (albumin-to-creatinine ratio) is used to detect microalbuminuria. Diagnosis requires persistent elevated urinary albumin in 2 out of 3 urine samples over 3 months. 24-hour urine collection is the gold standard but difficult; random ACR is the practical screening tool.

**Q:** What is the preferred treatment approach for diabetic nephropathy in T2DM according to current Canadian guidelines?

**A:** For T2DM: use SGLT2 inhibitor first, followed by GLP-1 receptor agonist. Also: BP control (<130/80 mmHg), ACEI or ARB (to delay CKD progression), nonsteroidal mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist (finerenone), and limit nephrotoxic drugs.

SGLT2 inhibitors provide both kidney and cardiovascular risk reduction. Finerenone (a nonsteroidal MRA) reduces both kidney and cardiovascular risk in diabetic nephropathy. ACEI/ARB are often used first-line for CVD protection and to slow CKD progression.

**Q:** What screening tools are used for diabetic neuropathy, and what are the three types of diabetic neuropathy?

**A:** Screening tools: 128 Hz tuning fork or 10 g monofilament. Three types: (1) Peripheral sensory neuropathy, (2) Motor neuropathy, (3) Autonomic neuropathy.

Screening begins annually at diagnosis for T2DM and >5 years after diagnosis of T1DM (postpubertal patients). Approximately 50% of patients develop neuropathy within 10 years of T1DM and T2DM onset.

**Q:** Describe the clinical features of peripheral sensory neuropathy in diabetes mellitus.

**A:** Bilateral and symmetric; paresthesias (tingling, itching), neuropathic pain, radicular pain, numbness; decreased perception of vibration and pain/temperature especially in lower extremities. Classic stocking-glove distribution. Distal-predominant (longest nerves affected first). Decreased ankle reflex. May cause neuropathic foot ulceration.

Peripheral sensory neuropathy is the most common form. The distal-predominant pattern reflects metabolic damage to the longest peripheral nerves first. Foot care education is essential to prevent ulceration and amputation.

**Q:** What are the features of autonomic neuropathy in diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Postural hypotension, tachycardia, decreased cardiovascular response to Valsalva maneuver, gastroparesis, alternating diarrhea and constipation, urinary retention, and erectile dysfunction.

Gastroparesis is treated with dietary modification, domperidone or metoclopramide (dopamine antagonists), and erythromycin (motilin receptor agonist). Orthostatic hypotension may be managed with Jobst stockings and tilting the head of bed.

**Q:** What are the reversible cranial nerve palsies seen in diabetic motor neuropathy?

**A:** CN III (ptosis/ophthalmoplegia with PUPIL SPARING), CN VI (inability to laterally deviate eye), and CN VII (Bell's palsy). These are thought to be due to ischemic infarction of peripheral nerves and often spontaneously resolve after 6–8 weeks.

Pupil sparing in diabetic CN III palsy distinguishes it from CN III palsy due to posterior communicating artery aneurysm (which causes pupil involvement). Diabetic amyotrophy (Bruns-Garland Syndrome) refers to pain, weakness, and wasting of hip flexors or extensors.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are recommended for painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy?

**A:** First-line options include: tricyclic antidepressants (e.g., amitriptyline), SNRIs (e.g., duloxetine), pregabalin, anticonvulsants (e.g., gabapentin, carbamazepine), and topical capsaicin. All have demonstrated superiority over placebo.

Per the 2014 Ann Intern Med meta-analysis (65 RCTs, 12,632 patients): carbamazepine (SMD −1.57), venlafaxine (SMD −1.53), duloxetine (SMD −1.33), and amitriptyline (SMD −0.72) were effective. Relative efficacy between agents is not well established.

**Q:** What are the dermatologic complications of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Diabetic dermopathy (atrophic brown pretibial spots), eruptive xanthomas (due to hypertriglyceridemia), and necrobiosis lipoidica diabeticorum (rare; thinning shin skin revealing subcutaneous vessels).

Diabetic dermopathy ('shin spots') is the most common skin manifestation and is thought to result from increased glycosylation of tissue proteins or vasculopathy. Eruptive xanthomas indicate severe hypertriglyceridemia, which often accompanies poorly controlled diabetes.

**Q:** What is Whipple's triad and when is it used?

**A:** Whipple's triad diagnoses clinically significant hypoglycemia: (1) Blood glucose <4.0 mmol/L, (2) Neuroglycopenic symptoms (dizziness, headache, visual clouding, confusion, seizure, coma), and (3) Rapid relief with glucose administration.

Autonomic symptoms (palpitations, sweating, anxiety, tremor, tachycardia, hunger) typically occur first, followed by neuroglycopenic symptoms as BG falls further. Pseudohypoglycemia is defined as symptoms with BG >3.9 mmol/L or BG <3.9 mmol/L without symptoms.

**Q:** What is hypoglycemia unawareness and why does it occur?

**A:** Hypoglycemia unawareness is when patients remain asymptomatic until severe hypoglycemia is reached. It occurs most commonly in T1DM after repeated hypoglycemic episodes, which blunt/minimize the autonomic response (decreased glucagon and epinephrine response).

History of repeated hypoglycemia and low HbA1c are risk factors. Patients with unawareness may not be safe to drive and should consider a Medic-Alert bracelet and advanced glucose monitoring (e.g., continuous glucose monitor). Refer to Diabetes Canada 2018 guidelines regarding driving.

**Q:** What is the treatment for hypoglycemia in the unconscious patient or patient who is NPO?

**A:** D50W 50 mL (1 ampule) IV over 1–3 minutes, OR 1 mg glucagon SC or IM if no IV access. May need ongoing glucose infusion once BG >5 mmol/L.

For the awake, self-treating patient: give 15 g of rapid-acting carbohydrates (e.g., glucose tablets, juice), wait 15 minutes, recheck BG, repeat until BG >5 mmol/L, then eat a snack with 15 g carbohydrate and protein if the next meal is >1 hour away.

**Q:** What biochemical markers should be measured during a hypoglycemic episode when the cause is unknown?

**A:** During spontaneous hypoglycemia or supervised fast (up to 72 h): plasma glucose, insulin, proinsulin, C-peptide, β-hydroxybutyrate, insulin antibodies, and screen for oral hypoglycemic agents (all available sulfonylureas and glinides).

C-peptide interpretation: elevated = endogenous hyperinsulinemia (insulinoma, sulfonylurea use); low/normal = exogenous insulin. A glucagon stimulation test (1 mg IV) distinguishes endogenous from exogenous hyperinsulinism by measuring the plasma glucose response.

**Q:** How does C-peptide level distinguish exogenous from endogenous hyperinsulinemia?

**A:** Increased C-peptide = endogenous hyperinsulinemia (e.g., insulinoma, sulfonylurea use). Decreased or normal C-peptide = exogenous insulin administration (factitious hypoglycemia).

C-peptide is co-secreted with endogenous insulin in equal molar amounts but is absent from exogenous insulin preparations. Therefore, high insulin + low C-peptide = exogenous source. Insulin antibodies may also be present in factitious hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What is the treatment for insulinoma-related hypoglycemia when surgical resection is not possible?

**A:** Diazoxide can be used for patients with insulinoma who cannot undergo resection. For non-insulinoma pancreatogenous hypoglycemia and post-bariatric bypass hypoglycemia: dietary changes (reduced carbs, small frequent meals); if severe, acarbose.

Diazoxide inhibits insulin secretion from beta cells. Post-gastric bypass hypoglycemia results from exaggerated incretin response and rapid gastric emptying causing postprandial hyperinsulinism. Acarbose slows carbohydrate absorption, blunting postprandial insulin surges.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for metabolic syndrome (≥3 required)?

**A:** ≥3 of: (1) Central obesity (waist circumference: Europid men >94 cm, women >80 cm; Asian men >90 cm, women >80 cm); (2) Triglycerides ≥1.7 mmol/L; (3) HDL-C <1.0 mmol/L (men) or <1.3 mmol/L (women); (4) BP ≥130/85 mmHg; (5) Fasting glucose ≥5.6 mmol/L. Drug treatment for any elevated marker is an alternate indicator.

Metabolic syndrome is associated with insulin resistance, hyperglycemia, hyperinsulinemia, HTN, central obesity, and dyslipidemia. Complications include DM, atherosclerosis, CAD, MI, and stroke. Women with PCOS are at increased risk.

**Q:** What is the order of anterior pituitary hormone loss with progressive mass effect on the pituitary?

**A:** Sequential loss order (mnemonic 'Go Look For The Adenoma Please'): GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH, PRL.

GH is the most sensitive to pituitary compression and is lost first, followed by gonadotropins (LH, FSH), then TSH, then ACTH. PRL is typically elevated (not lost) with mass effect because the tumour compresses the pituitary stalk, reducing dopamine inhibition of prolactin secretion.

**Q:** What happens to pituitary hormones after transection of the pituitary stalk?

**A:** Stalk transection leads to hypersecretion of PRL (loss of dopamine inhibition) and hyposecretion of all remaining anterior pituitary hormones (GH, LH, FSH, TSH, ACTH).

Most pituitary hormones are under net stimulatory control from the hypothalamus. Prolactin is unique in being primarily under tonic inhibitory control by dopamine. Disruption of hypothalamic input removes dopamine inhibition → PRL rises; all other hormones fall.

**Q:** What are the secretory stimuli and inhibitory stimuli for ACTH?

**A:** Secretory stimuli: CRH, metyrapone, hypoglycemia, vasopressin, fever, pain, and stress. Circadian rhythm (highest in morning, lowest at midnight). Inhibitory stimuli: dexamethasone, cortisol, and other glucocorticoids.

The dexamethasone suppression test exploits the negative feedback of glucocorticoids on ACTH release to evaluate for Cushing's syndrome. ACTH stimulates growth of the adrenal cortex and secretion of cortisol via cAMP.

**Q:** What drugs commonly cause hyperprolactinemia and through what mechanism?

**A:** Drugs with anti-dopaminergic properties: antipsychotics (most common), tricyclic antidepressants, antihypertensives (verapamil, methyldopa), bowel motility agents (metoclopramide, domperidone), H2-blockers, opiates (morphine), and estrogens (oral contraceptives). Mechanism: block dopamine-mediated inhibition of prolactin.

Primary hypothyroidism also causes hyperprolactinemia via elevated TRH stimulating prolactin. TSH should be measured in all patients with hyperprolactinemia. Macroprolactinemia (high molecular weight PRL) can cause falsely elevated serum PRL without clinical effects.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for prolactinoma, and which dopamine agonist is preferred?

**A:** First-line: dopamine agonists (bromocriptine, cabergoline, quinagolide). Cabergoline is preferred due to higher efficacy in normalizing PRL levels and shrinking pituitary tumours.

PRL-secreting tumours are often slow-growing. Treatment is indicated for symptomatic patients (galactorrhea, hypogonadism-amenorrhea, low libido, infertility). Women with prolactinomas should discontinue dopamine agonist therapy immediately upon becoming pregnant (except those with large invasive tumours).

**Q:** What are the clinical features of hyperprolactinemia?

**A:** Galactorrhea (breast milk secretion, can occur in men), infertility, hypogonadism, amenorrhea, oligomenorrhea, and erectile dysfunction.

Prolactin inhibits gonadotropin secretion, leading to low LH/FSH and resulting hypogonadism. In approaching nipple discharge, differentiate galactorrhea (milky, bilateral, non-bloody from multiple ducts) from pathologic discharge (unilateral, may be bloody/clear — must rule out breast malignancy).

**Q:** What is the gold standard test for GH deficiency in adults?

**A:** The insulin tolerance test (inducing hypoglycemia) is the gold standard dynamic test for GH deficiency. A diagnosis can also be made with low serum IGF-1 in a patient with deficiencies in three or more pituitary axes.

GH deficiency in adults causes increased adipose tissue, decreased lean body mass, decreased bone mineral density, and fatigue. GH replacement is considered for adult-onset GH deficiency without active malignancy if the patient prefers treatment after discussing benefits, adverse effects, and cost.

**Q:** What is the first-line investigation for acromegaly, and what is the confirmatory test?

**A:** First-line: serum IGF-1 (expected to be elevated). Confirmatory/most specific: oral glucose suppression test (75 g glucose PO — suppresses GH in healthy individuals but NOT in acromegaly). MRI of sella turcica to identify tumour.

Acromegaly results from a GH-secreting pituitary adenoma in adults (after epiphyseal fusion), leading to gigantism in children (before fusion). GH excess causes insulin resistance, IGT, cardiomyopathy, HTN, sleep apnea, and increased colon cancer risk.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for acromegaly?

**A:** Surgery (transsphenoidal) is recommended initial therapy for most patients. Second-line: somatostatin analogue (octreotide), dopamine agonist (cabergoline), GH receptor antagonist (pegvisomant), or radiation (for disease not controlled by surgery or medical therapy).

GH excess can also rarely result from neuroendocrine tumours secreting ectopic GH or GHRH. Risks of GH excess include cardiac disease (in 1/3 of patients), OSA, HTN (in 1/3 of patients), DM, and increased colon cancer risk.

**Q:** What is Kallmann syndrome?

**A:** Kallmann syndrome is a congenital cause of hypogonadotropic hypogonadism characterized by deficient GnRH secretion combined with anosmia (absent sense of smell) due to failure of olfactory and GnRH neurons to migrate to the hypothalamus.

It causes low FSH, low LH, and low sex hormones. Patients present with absent or incomplete puberty. Treatment is gonadotropin replacement or pulsatile GnRH for fertility, and sex hormone replacement for symptomatic treatment.

**Q:** What is the difference between hypogonadotropic and hypergonadotropic hypogonadism in terms of FSH/LH levels?

**A:** Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism: LOW FSH, LOW LH, and low sex hormones — problem is at the hypothalamus/pituitary level. Hypergonadotropic hypogonadism: HIGH FSH, HIGH LH, and low sex hormones — problem is at the gonad level (gonadal failure).

Hypogonadotropic causes include Kallmann syndrome, pituitary tumours, hyperprolactinemia, eating disorders. Hypergonadotropic causes include Turner syndrome, Klinefelter syndrome, gonadal toxins (chemotherapy, radiation), premature ovarian insufficiency, and STIs.

**Q:** What are the common causes of SIADH?

**A:** Malignancy (ectopic ADH): small cell lung carcinoma, extrapulmonary small cell carcinomas, squamous cell carcinoma of head and neck. CNS disease (inflammatory, hemorrhage, tumour, Guillain-Barré). Respiratory disease (TB, pneumonia, empyema). Drugs: SSRIs, vincristine, chlorpropamide, cyclophosphamide, carbamazepine, nicotine, morphine, DDAVP, oxytocin. Post-surgical stress.

Small cell lung carcinoma is the classic malignant cause. SIADH from CNS disease results from direct hypothalamic stimulation of ADH. Carbamazepine both causes SIADH and paradoxically is used to treat central DI by enhancing ADH action at the kidney.

**Q:** What is the treatment for SIADH?

**A:** Treat underlying cause, fluid restriction (800–1000 mL/day), vasopressin receptor antagonists (tolvaptan, conivaptan), and furosemide.

Fluid restriction is the cornerstone of SIADH treatment. Vasopressin V2 receptor antagonists ('vaptans') promote aquaresis (free water excretion). Correction of hyponatremia must not be too rapid — risk of osmotic demyelination syndrome (central pontine myelinolysis) if Na⁺ corrected >8–10 mEq/L per 24 hours.

**Q:** What is Diabetes Insipidus (DI) and how are central DI and nephrogenic DI differentiated?

**A:** DI is a disorder of ineffective ADH (decreased production or peripheral resistance) causing large volumes of dilute urine (>3 L/day). Differentiation: fluid deprivation → both forms show persistent high urine output and urine osmolality < plasma osmolality. Administration of exogenous ADH (DDAVP): urine concentrates in central DI but NOT in nephrogenic DI.

Central DI causes: pituitary surgery, tumours, idiopathic/autoimmune, stalk infiltration, trauma, hydrocephalus, Langerhans cell histiocytosis. Nephrogenic DI causes: lithium, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, CKD, hereditary.

**Q:** What is the treatment for central DI vs. nephrogenic DI?

**A:** Central DI: first-line = desmopressin (DDAVP); second-line = chlorpropamide, thiazides, NSAIDs, carbamazepine. Nephrogenic DI: solute restriction and thiazide diuretics.

Thiazides paradoxically reduce urine output in nephrogenic DI by causing mild volume depletion → increased proximal tubular sodium and water reabsorption → less fluid reaches the collecting duct. NSAIDs and chlorpropamide enhance residual ADH action.

**Q:** What is Cerebral Salt Wasting (CSW) and how does it differ from SIADH?

**A:** CSW occurs in subarachnoid hemorrhage. Malfunctioning renal tubules excrete sodium, mimicking SIADH biochemically. The key hallmark distinguishing CSW from SIADH is HYPOVOLEMIA in CSW (SIADH is euvolemic).

Both CSW and SIADH present with hyponatremia and high urine sodium, making differentiation difficult. Volume status is the critical distinguishing feature. CSW is treated with sodium replacement and volume repletion; SIADH is treated with fluid restriction.

**Q:** What physiologic effects does GLP-1 receptor agonist therapy have on cardiovascular and renal outcomes in T2DM?

**A:** Per 2021 Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol meta-analysis (8 trials, >60,000 patients): GLP-1 RAs reduce MACE by 14% (HR 0.86), all-cause mortality by 12% (HR 0.88), hospitalization for heart failure by 11% (HR 0.89), and composite kidney outcome by 21% (HR 0.79). No increase in severe hypoglycemia, retinopathy, or pancreatic adverse effects.

GLP-1 RAs with proven cardiovascular benefit include dulaglutide, exenatide ER, liraglutide, and semaglutide. These agents are preferred in T2DM patients with established ASCVD or high cardiovascular risk, per Diabetes Canada guidelines.

**Q:** What are the effects of SGLT2 inhibitors on kidney outcomes regardless of diabetes status?

**A:** Per 2022 Lancet meta-analysis (13 trials): SGLT2 inhibitors reduce kidney disease progression by 37% (RR 0.63), AKI by 23% (RR 0.77), and cardiovascular death or hospitalization for heart failure by 23% (RR 0.77). Benefits are seen irrespective of diabetes status, primary kidney disease, or kidney function.

SGLT2 inhibitors are now recommended for kidney protection beyond diabetes management. They reduce cardiovascular death by 14% but do not significantly reduce non-cardiovascular death. SGLT2i should only be initiated if eGFR >30 mL/min/1.73 m².

**Q:** What is the pulsatile GnRH frequency effect on FSH vs. LH release?

**A:** Low-frequency pulsatile GnRH stimulation → FSH release. High-frequency pulsatile GnRH stimulation → LH release. Continuous (non-pulsatile) GnRH infusion → suppresses both FSH and LH (downregulation of GnRH receptors).

This principle is clinically exploited: GnRH analogues (agonists) given continuously cause initial flare then suppression (used in prostate cancer, endometriosis, precocious puberty). Pulsatile GnRH can be used to treat hypogonadotropic hypogonadism when fertility is desired.

**Q:** What are the bone and joint complications of diabetes mellitus?

**A:** Juvenile cheiroarthropathy (chronic hand stiffness from glycosylated collagen causing skin contracture over joints), Dupuytren's contracture, increased fracture risk (T1DM and T2DM, due to decreased bone quality), and adhesive capsulitis ('frozen shoulder').

Diabetic bone disease is due to glycosylation of collagen and other connective tissue proteins as well as vasculopathy. Fracture risk is elevated despite often normal bone mineral density by DXA, reflecting impaired bone quality rather than quantity.

**Q:** What are the cataracts associated with diabetes mellitus and their mechanism?

**A:** Diabetes causes subcapsular and senile cataracts due to glycosylation of lens proteins and increased sorbitol accumulation (polyol pathway), causing osmotic change and fibrosis in the lens.

The polyol pathway converts glucose to sorbitol via aldose reductase. Sorbitol accumulates intracellularly (because it is poorly permeable across cell membranes), causing osmotic swelling and lens opacification. This mechanism also contributes to diabetic neuropathy.

**Q:** What is the role of ADH at the collecting duct and what stimulates vs. inhibits its secretion?

**A:** ADH acts on V2 receptors in renal collecting duct tubules → insertion of aquaporin channels → water reabsorption → concentrated urine. Stimulated by: increased serum osmolality, hypovolemia/decreased effective circulating volume, stress, pain, fever, CNS disorders. Inhibited by: decreased serum osmolality, ethanol.

Contracted plasma volume detected by baroreceptors is a more potent stimulus for ADH secretion than increased osmolality. Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect serum osmolality. ADH is an octapeptide synthesized in the supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei and stored in the posterior pituitary.

**Q:** What are the local mass effects of a pituitary adenoma on vision?

**A:** Bitemporal hemianopsia (compression of optic chiasm), diplopia (oculomotor nerve palsies), loss of colour vision, headaches. Increased ICP is rare.

The optic chiasm lies directly above the pituitary fossa. As a pituitary adenoma grows superiorly, it compresses the decussating nasal fibres of the optic chiasm, producing the classic bitemporal (outer field) visual loss. Recognizing this pattern is high-yield for MCCQE1.

**Q:** Which hormones does a pituitary adenoma most commonly secrete in excess, and what are the resulting syndromes?

**A:** PRL → galactorrhea and hypogonadism; GH → acromegaly (adults) or gigantism (children); ACTH → Cushing's disease (Cushing's syndrome caused by a pituitary tumour). TSH-secreting tumours are rare.

Prolactinomas are the most common secretory pituitary adenoma. GH excess before epiphyseal fusion causes gigantism; after fusion it causes acromegaly. ACTH excess drives bilateral adrenal cortisol overproduction (Cushing's disease). TSH-secreting adenomas account for <1% of pituitary tumours.

**Q:** What is the imaging modality of choice for evaluating a suspected pituitary adenoma?

**A:** MRI of the sella turcica.

MRI with gadolinium provides superior soft-tissue contrast compared to CT, allowing visualization of microadenomas (<10 mm) and their relationship to the optic chiasm, cavernous sinuses, and surrounding structures. CT is an alternative when MRI is contraindicated.

**Q:** List the eight etiologic categories of hypopituitarism using the mnemonic 'The Eight I's'.

**A:** Invasive (tumours, craniopharyngioma, metastases), Infarction/hemorrhage (Sheehan's syndrome, pituitary apoplexy), Infiltrative/inflammatory (sarcoidosis, hemochromatosis, histiocytosis), Infectious (syphilis, TB, fungal, parasitic), Injury (severe head trauma), Immunologic (autoimmune hypophysitis), Iatrogenic (surgery, radiation, immunotherapy), Idiopathic (familial, congenital midline defects).

A structured differential is essential for MCCQE1. Sheehan's syndrome (postpartum pituitary infarction due to hypovolemic shock) and pituitary apoplexy (acute hemorrhage/infarction of a pituitary tumour presenting with sudden headache and altered LOC) are classic high-yield scenarios.

**Q:** In what order are pituitary hormones typically lost in progressive hypopituitarism?

**A:** GH → LH → FSH → TSH → ACTH → PRL → then posterior pituitary hormones (ADH and oxytocin). Mnemonic: 'Go Look For The Adenoma Please'.

GH-secreting somatotrophs and LH/FSH-secreting gonadotrophs are most sensitive to compression, so their deficiency appears first. ACTH deficiency is particularly dangerous (central adrenal insufficiency). Posterior pituitary deficiency (diabetes insipidus) occurs late or with specific lesions.

**Q:** What is Sheehan's syndrome and how does it present?

**A:** Pituitary infarction due to excessive postpartum blood loss and hypovolemic shock. Presents with failure to lactate (PRL deficiency), amenorrhea, and features of other pituitary hormone deficiencies.

The pituitary gland enlarges during pregnancy, making it vulnerable to ischemia if blood pressure drops precipitously during postpartum hemorrhage. Failure to lactate after delivery is the classic first clue. Hormonal replacement is required for life.

**Q:** What is pituitary apoplexy and how is it recognized?

**A:** Acute hemorrhage or infarction of a pituitary tumour. Presents with sudden severe headache, visual field defects, oculomotor palsies, altered LOC, and loss of pituitary hormones. Can be fatal if not recognized and treated promptly.

Pituitary apoplexy is a neurosurgical emergency. The sudden expansion of blood within the sella compresses the optic chiasm and cavernous sinuses. Urgent neurosurgical decompression and corticosteroid replacement (for adrenal insufficiency) are required. It may masquerade as subarachnoid hemorrhage.

**Q:** What clinical syndrome results from isolated ACTH deficiency in hypopituitarism, and how does it differ from primary adrenal insufficiency?

**A:** Central adrenal insufficiency: fatigue, weight loss, hypoglycemia, anemia, hyponatremia. Unlike primary (Addison's), there is NO hyperpigmentation (ACTH is low, not high) and NO hyperkalemia (aldosterone remains intact via the renin-angiotensin axis).

ACTH deficiency from a pituitary source leads to cortisol deficiency but spares mineralocorticoid function (aldosterone is primarily regulated by angiotensin II and potassium). The absence of hyperpigmentation and hyperkalemia distinguishes central from primary adrenal insufficiency.

**Q:** When investigating suspected hypopituitarism, concurrent adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism are found. Which should be treated first and why?

**A:** Treat adrenal insufficiency (with glucocorticoids) FIRST, then treat hypothyroidism. Starting thyroid hormone replacement first can precipitate an adrenal crisis by increasing cortisol metabolism.

Thyroid hormone increases hepatic clearance of cortisol. If thyroid hormone is replaced before glucocorticoids in a patient with combined deficiency, the remaining adrenal reserve may be overwhelmed, precipitating life-threatening adrenal crisis. This is a classic MCCQE1 management principle.

**Q:** What initial investigation is used to evaluate suspected ACTH deficiency, and what test follows if results are equivocal?

**A:** Initial: 8 AM serum cortisol. If low or borderline: cosyntropin stimulation test (250 mcg ACTH analogue). If equivocal: insulin tolerance test (hypoglycemia should normally raise cortisol and GH).

The cosyntropin (Synacthen) stimulation test is the standard confirmatory test for adrenal insufficiency in Canada. A normal cortisol rise (≥500 nmol/L) rules out primary and most cases of secondary adrenal insufficiency. The insulin tolerance test is the gold standard but carries risks (hypoglycemia) and requires close supervision.

**Q:** More than 90% of thyroid hormone secreted by the thyroid gland is which hormone, and which is more biologically active?

**A:** More than 90% secreted is T4. T3 is approximately 4× more biologically active than T4. Most plasma T3 is derived from peripheral conversion of T4 by deiodinase enzymes.

T4 serves as a prohormone. Peripheral 5'-deiodinase converts T4 to the active T3. Approximately 85% of T4 is converted peripherally to either T3 (active) or reverse T3 (inactive). Reverse T3 is produced during illness to reduce metabolic activity.

**Q:** What is the best single test for assessing thyroid function, and when is this insufficient?

**A:** TSH is the best test for primary thyroid disorders. If secondary (pituitary/hypothalamic) thyroid disease is suspected, both TSH AND free T4 must be measured, as TSH may be low or inappropriately normal despite low T4.

In primary thyroid dysfunction, TSH reflects the pituitary's response to circulating thyroid hormones via negative feedback and is therefore exquisitely sensitive to small changes. In secondary/tertiary hypothyroidism, the feedback axis itself is broken, making TSH unreliable as a standalone screening test.

**Q:** What do thyroid autoantibodies (TRAb, TPOAb, TgAb) indicate in clinical practice?

**A:** TPOAb and TgAb (blocking variety of TRAb) are elevated in Hashimoto's thyroiditis. TRAb of the stimulating variety (also called TSI) cause Graves' disease. Both blocking and stimulating TRAb can coexist in Graves' disease.

Third-generation TRAb assays have >98% sensitivity and specificity for Graves' disease, making them useful to determine the etiology of hyperthyroidism. TPOAb are the most sensitive marker for Hashimoto's thyroiditis. TgAb can interfere with thyroglobulin assays used to monitor thyroid cancer recurrence.

**Q:** When is plasma thyroglobulin measured and what limits its interpretation?

**A:** Used to monitor for residual or recurrent thyroid cancer after thyroidectomy. Elevated or detectable levels suggest persistent, recurrent, or metastatic disease. TgAb interferes with the assay, so both TgAb and thyroglobulin must be measured simultaneously.

After total thyroidectomy for thyroid cancer, thyroglobulin should be undetectable. Any detectable level raises concern for residual or metastatic disease. Because TgAb can falsely lower measured thyroglobulin (immunometric assay interference), its concurrent measurement is mandatory for accurate interpretation.

**Q:** When is a radioisotope thyroid scan (Technetium-99) indicated, and what is the clinical significance of a 'hot' vs. 'cold' nodule?

**A:** Indicated when: (1) one or more thyroid nodule(s) AND (2) the patient is hyperthyroid. Hot nodule = hyperfunctioning → very low malignancy risk; treat hyperthyroidism. Cold nodule = non-functioning → further workup required (U/S, then FNAB if concerning sonographic features).

In a euthyroid patient with a nodule, radioisotope scanning is not the first-line test — ultrasound is. Scanning is reserved for hyperthyroid patients with nodules to identify whether the nodule is the source of excess hormone. Hyperfunctioning nodules are rarely malignant because autonomous growth implies differentiated function.

**Q:** What does RAIU (radioactive iodine uptake) measure, and how does it distinguish causes of thyrotoxicosis?

**A:** RAIU measures in vivo iodine turnover by the thyroid. Increased uptake = overproduction (Graves', toxic nodular goitre, toxic adenoma). Decreased uptake = leaking thyroid hormone (thyroiditis), exogenous thyroid hormone use, or excess iodine load (amiodarone, contrast dye).

RAIU differentiates destructive thyroiditis (low uptake, as hormone leaks from damaged follicles) from true hyperthyroidism (high uptake, as the gland is actively over-producing). This distinction is critical because thyroiditis is self-limited and should NOT be treated with antithyroid drugs or RAI ablation.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and most common cause of hyperthyroidism?

**A:** Hyperthyroidism affects approximately 1% of the general population. Graves' disease is the most common cause, occurring at any age with a peak in the 3rd–4th decades; M:F ratio approximately 1:7.

Graves' disease accounts for roughly 60-80% of all hyperthyroidism cases. Its autoimmune pathogenesis involves TSI (thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins) that bind and activate the TSH receptor. It has familial predisposition and is associated with HLA-B8 and DR3.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of Graves' ophthalmopathy?

**A:** TSI stimulates fibroblasts in orbital connective tissue and extraocular muscles, leading to inflammation and accumulation of glycosaminoglycans. This increases osmotic pressure, causes fluid accumulation, and results in forward displacement (proptosis) of the eyeball.

Graves' ophthalmopathy (thyroid-associated orbitopathy) is distinct from the lid lag seen in any thyrotoxicosis (adrenergic effect). True orbitopathy is immune-mediated and may worsen independently of thyroid status. Smoking is a major risk factor; RAI ablation may worsen it and should be covered with concurrent prednisone if ophthalmopathy is present.

**Q:** List the classic clinical features of Graves' disease beyond general thyrotoxicosis symptoms.

**A:** 1. Diffuse goitre with thyroid bruit. 2. Ophthalmopathy: proptosis, diplopia, conjunctival injection, corneal abrasions, periorbital puffiness, lid lag, decreased visual acuity. 3. Dermopathy (rare): pretibial myxedema (non-pitting edema from glycosaminoglycan deposition). 4. Acropachy: clubbing and thickening of distal phalanges.

The triad of diffuse goitre + exophthalmos + pretibial myxedema is pathognomonic of Graves' disease. Lid lag is non-specific (any thyrotoxicosis), whereas true proptosis and periorbital changes are specific to Graves'. Acropachy is a rare but specific sign.

**Q:** What are the investigations that confirm a diagnosis of Graves' disease?

**A:** Low TSH, elevated free T4 (and/or T3), positive TRAb (>98% sensitivity and specificity), elevated RAIU, homogeneous diffuse uptake on thyroid scan.

Third-generation TRAb assays are sufficiently accurate to diagnose Graves' disease without requiring a thyroid scan in most cases. The combination of suppressed TSH + elevated free T4 + positive TRAb is diagnostic. Thyroid scan confirms homogeneous diffuse uptake and excludes toxic nodular disease.

**Q:** How do methimazole (MMI) and propylthiouracil (PTU) work, and what is the key difference between them?

**A:** Both inhibit thyroid hormone synthesis by blocking peroxidase-catalyzed reactions (organification and coupling of iodotyrosines). PTU also inhibits peripheral conversion of T4 to T3. MMI is preferred due to once-daily dosing, faster resolution of hyperthyroidism, and lower hepatotoxicity. PTU is preferred in the first 16 weeks of pregnancy (MMI is teratogenic in T1 — aplasia cutis).

The dual mechanism of PTU (synthesis inhibition + peripheral conversion blockade) makes it useful in thyroid storm where rapid lowering of T3 is critical. However, PTU's risk of fulminant hepatic necrosis limits its routine use. In pregnancy, the teratogenicity profile switches the preference: PTU for T1, MMI for T2/T3.

**Q:** What are the major and minor side effects of thionamides (MMI and PTU)?

**A:** Major: hepatotoxicity (cholestasis or hepatitis), agranulocytosis, vasculitis. Minor: mild rash, pruritus.

Agranulocytosis (rare, ~0.3%) is the most feared acute side effect — patients must be counselled to present immediately for a CBC if they develop fever, sore throat, or mouth ulcers. PTU carries a higher risk of fulminant hepatic necrosis than MMI, which is one reason MMI is now preferred for most patients.

**Q:** What are the remission rates after a course of thionamides for Graves' disease, and what predicts long-term remission?

**A:** Remission (normal thyroid indices 1 year after stopping PTU or MMI) occurs in 20-50% after 12-18 months of antithyroid therapy. Predictors of remission: small goitre, mild hyperthyroidism, low TRAb titres at end of treatment.

The high recurrence rate (~50%) of medical therapy is why many patients ultimately require definitive treatment with RAI or surgery. Monitoring TRAb levels helps guide the decision to stop antithyroid drugs — a negative or very low TRAb suggests a better chance of remission.

**Q:** What are the indications for total thyroidectomy in Graves' disease?

**A:** Large goitre, suspicious nodule for cancer, intolerance to thionamides + declines/not candidate for RAI, women wishing to conceive in the near future, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism not responding to anti-thyroid drugs in pregnancy (surgery safest in second trimester), or patient preference.

Thyroidectomy offers rapid and definitive control but carries surgical risks including hypoparathyroidism and recurrent laryngeal nerve damage (vocal cord palsy). Patients become permanently hypothyroid and require lifelong L-thyroxine replacement. Recurrence risk after total thyroidectomy is very low (~2%).

**Q:** What is subacute (de Quervain's) thyroiditis, and how does it present?

**A:** Presumed viral or postviral inflammatory thyroiditis (associated with HLA-DR5). Presents with painful thyroid swelling (may radiate to jaw/ears), malaise, fever, often preceded by URTI. Transient thyrotoxicosis followed by hypothyroidism then recovery. Near-absent RAIU; elevated ESR and CRP.

The triphasic course (hyperthyroid → hypothyroid → recovery) over weeks to months, combined with a painful tender thyroid and low RAIU, is the classic presentation. Treatment is symptomatic: NSAIDs or prednisone for pain, beta-blockers for adrenergic symptoms. Antithyroid drugs are NOT indicated (low uptake, no overproduction). 90% recover fully; 10% develop permanent hypothyroidism.

**Q:** How does painless (silent) thyroiditis differ from de Quervain's thyroiditis?

**A:** Painless thyroiditis: variant of Hashimoto's (associated with HLA-DR3), thyroid enlargement WITHOUT discomfort, affects women more. Same triphasic course and near-absent RAIU. Postpartum subtype common. 10% risk of permanent hypothyroidism; at risk for recurrent episodes.

The key distinguishing features are absence of pain/tenderness and absence of elevated ESR/CRP in painless thyroiditis. Postpartum thyroiditis (occurring within 1 year after delivery) is the most common form. It can be mistaken for postpartum depression when the hypothyroid phase predominates.

**Q:** What is toxic multinodular goitre (Plummer's disease) and how does its demographic differ from Graves' disease?

**A:** Autonomous thyroid hormone production from multiple hyperfunctioning nodules. More common in elderly individuals (versus Graves' which peaks in 3rd–4th decades). Thyroid scan shows heterogeneous uptake with suppression of normal tissue. Treat with RAI (I-131) or surgical excision.

In elderly patients presenting with atrial fibrillation, heart failure, or weight loss without the classic signs of Graves' (no ophthalmopathy), toxic multinodular goitre should be high on the differential. RAI ablation is preferred first-line; pretreatment with MMI/PTU may be needed to prevent worsening hyperthyroidism post-RAI in elderly patients with cardiovascular disease.

**Q:** What is thyroid storm (thyrotoxic crisis) and what are its hallmark clinical features?

**A:** Medical emergency: acute exacerbation of thyrotoxicosis in a life-threatening state (mortality 10-30%). Features: extreme hyperthermia (≥40°C), severe tachycardia, vomiting, diarrhea, hepatic failure with jaundice, atrial fibrillation, CHF, CNS manifestations (agitation, delirium, psychosis, seizures, coma). Often precipitated by infection, trauma, or surgery in a hyperthyroid patient.

Thyroid storm is a clinical diagnosis — there is no specific lab threshold. Management is aggressive and multi-pronged (PTU → block synthesis; iodide 1h later → block release; beta-blockers → control adrenergic symptoms; hydrocortisone → block peripheral T4→T3 conversion; supportive care). The 1-hour gap before giving iodide prevents using iodide as substrate for new hormone synthesis.

**Q:** In thyroid storm, why is PTU given before iodide, and what are the specific treatment steps?

**A:** PTU is given first to block synthesis; iodide is given 1 hour AFTER PTU to prevent iodide from being used as substrate for new hormone synthesis. Steps: (1) High-dose PTU or MMI; (2) Iodide 1h later (sodium iodide IV, or Lugol's solution, or SSKI); (3) Beta-blockers (propranolol preferred — also blocks T4→T3 conversion); (4) Hydrocortisone or dexamethasone (blocks peripheral T4→T3 conversion); (5) Supportive care (cooling, fluids, acetaminophen, ICU).

The Wolff-Chaikoff effect explains iodide's acute ability to suppress thyroid hormone release. However, if iodide is given before the synthesis is blocked by PTU/MMI, the excess iodide may be used to produce even more thyroid hormone (especially relevant in iodine-deficient glands). Corticosteroids have the added benefit of treating potential relative adrenal insufficiency.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of hypothyroidism in North America, and what is the most common cause worldwide?

**A:** North America: Hashimoto's thyroiditis (autoimmune). Worldwide: iodine deficiency.

Iodine fortification of table salt has essentially eliminated dietary iodine deficiency as a cause of hypothyroidism in Canada. In areas without iodine supplementation, endemic goitre with resultant hypothyroidism remains the leading cause globally. Post-ablative hypothyroidism (after RAI or thyroidectomy) is the most common iatrogenic cause.

**Q:** Describe the TSH and free T4 pattern in primary hypothyroidism vs. secondary hypothyroidism vs. subclinical primary hypothyroidism.

**A:** Primary hypothyroidism: TSH elevated + free T4 decreased. Subclinical primary hypothyroidism: TSH elevated + free T4 normal. Secondary (pituitary) hypothyroidism: TSH decreased or inappropriately normal + free T4 decreased.

Understanding the feedback axis explains each pattern. In primary disease, the thyroid fails → less T4 → less negative feedback → pituitary secretes more TSH. In secondary disease, the pituitary fails → less TSH → thyroid produces less T4. Both TSH and free T4 are needed to identify secondary hypothyroidism. Monitoring secondary hypothyroidism uses free T4 (not TSH) as the target.

**Q:** What are the classic cardiovascular manifestations of hypothyroidism?

**A:** Bradycardia, hypertension (diastolic), pericardial effusion, worsening CHF and angina, hypercholesterolemia, hyperhomocysteinemia, myxedema heart.

Hypothyroidism reduces cardiac contractility and heart rate (opposite of hyperthyroidism). Lipid abnormalities (elevated LDL, total cholesterol) increase cardiovascular risk. Pericardial effusion is common but rarely causes tamponade. 'Myxedema heart' refers to cardiomegaly from a combination of effusion and dilated myopathy.

**Q:** What is the classic neurological sign of hypothyroidism on physical examination?

**A:** Delayed relaxation phase of deep tendon reflexes ('hung reflexes' or 'slow relaxing reflexes'). Also: slow speech, muscle cramps, carpal tunnel syndrome, paresthesias.

The delayed relaxation is due to slowed muscle metabolism from thyroid hormone deficiency. It is detectable at the ankle jerk. Other neurological features include proximal myopathy, cerebellar ataxia (rare), and peripheral neuropathy. These are reversible with thyroid hormone replacement.

**Q:** How is L-thyroxine dosed in hypothyroidism, and what special considerations apply to the elderly and those with CAD?

**A:** Standard starting dose: 1.6 mcg/kg/day (range 0.05-0.2 mg PO once daily). Elderly patients and those with CAD: start at 0.025 mg/day and increase gradually every 6 weeks ('start low, go slow') to avoid precipitating angina or arrhythmia. Recheck TSH after 6 weeks of any dose change.

Rapid normalization of thyroid hormone in elderly or cardiac patients can increase myocardial oxygen demand before coronary flow can adapt, triggering ischemia. Once a maintenance dose is achieved, annual TSH monitoring suffices. L-thyroxine should be taken on an empty stomach (at least 1h before breakfast or at bedtime) for consistent absorption.

**Q:** What drugs and substances decrease levothyroxine absorption or increase its clearance?

**A:** Decreased absorption: calcium carbonate, sucralfate, aluminum hydroxide, iron (citrate), proton pump inhibitors/antacids (reduced stomach acidity), soy, coffee. Increased clearance: rifampin, antiepileptics (e.g., phenytoin, carbamazepine), tricyclic antidepressants, SSRIs. Also: lithium, metformin.

Patients on levothyroxine must be counselled about these interactions. Iron and calcium should be taken at least 4 hours apart from levothyroxine. GI conditions impairing absorption (celiac disease, atrophic gastritis, H. pylori, lactose intolerance) may also reduce drug efficacy and require higher doses.

**Q:** What is Hashimoto's thyroiditis and what are its two major morphological forms?

**A:** Most common form of primary hypothyroidism in North America. Chronic autoimmune thyroiditis. Two forms: (1) Goitrous variant — small rubbery goitre, initially euthyroid then hypothyroid, associated with fibrosis. (2) Atrophic variant — hypothyroid from onset, no goitre. Both share same autoimmune pathophysiology.

Hashimoto's is a risk factor for rare primary thyroid lymphoma. The pathogenesis involves T-cell mediated destruction of follicles and B-lymphocyte production of antibodies against thyroid peroxidase (TPOAb), thyroglobulin (TgAb), TSH receptor, and Na+/I- symporter. Associated with Down syndrome, Turner syndrome, and other autoimmune conditions.

**Q:** What are the key investigations for Hashimoto's thyroiditis?

**A:** High TSH, low T4 (T3 measurement not necessary). Presence of TPOAb and TgAb in serum. Treatment: L-thyroxine replacement if hypothyroid.

TPOAb is the most sensitive and specific antibody for Hashimoto's thyroiditis. TgAb is also elevated but less specific. Approximately 10% of the population have positive TPOAb without overt hypothyroidism (subclinical or 'euthyroid' Hashimoto's), and these individuals are at increased risk of progressing to overt hypothyroidism.

**Q:** What is myxedema coma, and what are its hallmark features and treatment?

**A:** Medical emergency: severe hypothyroidism decompensated by precipitants (infection, trauma, cold exposure, MI, narcotics/hypnotics). Mortality up to 40%. Hallmarks: decreased consciousness + hypothermia + hyponatremia + hypotension + hypoglycemia + bradycardia + hypoventilation + non-pitting edema. Treatment: ICU, hydrocortisone 100 mg q8h (rule out adrenal insufficiency first), IV L-thyroxine loading dose (0.2-0.5 mg) then 0.1 mg IV daily, passive rewarming, mechanical ventilation, IV dextrose.

Hydrocortisone must be given before or simultaneously with thyroid hormone replacement in case of concurrent adrenal insufficiency — as in hypopituitarism, replacing thyroid hormone alone can precipitate adrenal crisis. Passive rewarming is preferred over active rewarming (which causes peripheral vasodilation and can worsen hypotension). Monitor for arrhythmia during treatment.

**Q:** What is sick euthyroid syndrome (non-thyroidal illness), and how should it be managed?

**A:** Transient alterations in thyroid hormone levels in severely ill, traumatized, or starving patients WITHOUT intrinsic thyroid disease. Pattern: initially low free T3 → then low TSH → then low free T4 (if severe). TSH may be transiently elevated during recovery. Management: treat the underlying illness; thyroid hormone replacement is NOT beneficial and not indicated.

This is a common source of confusion in hospitalized patients with abnormal TFTs. The low T3 is thought to be a protective adaptation (reducing tissue catabolism during stress). The key distinction from true hypothyroidism is clinical context — sick euthyroid occurs in the setting of acute severe illness, and TFTs normalize once the patient recovers.

**Q:** What is a non-toxic goitre and what are its causes?

**A:** Generalized thyroid gland enlargement in a euthyroid individual NOT from inflammatory or neoplastic processes. Causes: iodine deficiency or excess, goitrogens (brassica vegetables — turnip, cassava; drugs — lithium, iodine, para-aminosalicylic acid), any disorder of hormone synthesis causing compensatory TSH-driven growth, peripheral resistance to thyroid hormone.

Non-toxic goitre is most common during periods of increased thyroid hormone demand (adolescence, pregnancy, lactation). Early: diffuse goitre. Late: multinodular goitre with cysts, fibrosis, and ischemic areas. Complications include compression (stridor, dysphagia, hoarseness) and eventual autonomous function → toxic multinodular goitre.

**Q:** What is the role of calcitonin in thyroid physiology, and when is serum calcitonin measured clinically?

**A:** Calcitonin is a peptide hormone secreted by parafollicular C-cells. It inhibits osteoclast activity and increases renal calcium excretion (lowers serum calcium). Clinically: serum calcitonin is measured when medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC) is suspected (e.g., thyroid nodule + MEN 2A/2B or RET gene mutation) and to monitor for residual or recurrent MTC post-thyroidectomy.

Calcitonin is NOT routinely measured for thyroid nodule workup. Its main clinical utility is as a tumour marker for MTC (derived from C-cells). MEN 2A and 2B syndromes carry a near-100% lifetime risk of MTC, so calcitonin and RET genetic testing are essential in this context.

**Q:** What are the steps in thyroid hormone synthesis?

**A:** 1. Iodide trapping by sodium-iodide cotransporter (NIC). 2. Oxidation of iodide to iodine by thyroid peroxidase (TP). 3. Organification: iodination of thyroglobulin tyrosine residues to MIT and DIT. 4. Coupling: MIT + DIT → T3; DIT + DIT → T4. 5. Proteolysis of thyroglobulin → release of T4 and T3 into blood.

Thionamides (MMI, PTU) block steps 2 and 3 (peroxidase-mediated reactions). Iodide excess (Wolff-Chaikoff) blocks step 3. Lithium blocks proteolysis and release (step 5). Understanding these steps explains the mechanisms of antithyroid drugs and iodine effects, which is essential for pharmacology questions on MCCQE1.

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman presents at 8 weeks of pregnancy with a new diagnosis of Graves' disease. Which antithyroid drug should be used?

**A:** PTU (propylthiouracil) during the first 16 weeks of pregnancy. MMI is contraindicated in the first trimester due to risk of aplasia cutis (and other embryopathy). Switch to MMI after 16 weeks due to risk of PTU-associated hepatotoxicity in the second and third trimesters.

Aplasia cutis congenita (scalp skin defect) and choanal/esophageal atresia are associated with first-trimester MMI exposure. PTU's hepatotoxicity risk makes it less desirable in later pregnancy. This trimester-specific switching strategy is a high-yield MCCQE1 topic. Both drugs cross the placenta and can cause fetal hypothyroidism — use the lowest effective dose.

**Q:** A patient with hyperthyroidism is found to have low TSH, elevated free T4, and markedly decreased RAIU. What is the most likely diagnosis and how does management differ from Graves' disease?

**A:** Likely thyroiditis (subacute painful or painless) or exogenous thyroid hormone ingestion. Management: symptomatic treatment only (beta-blockers for adrenergic symptoms; NSAIDs/steroids for pain in de Quervain's). Antithyroid drugs and RAI are NOT indicated because the thyroid is not actively over-producing hormone.

The low RAIU is the critical differentiating feature from Graves' disease or toxic nodular disease. In thyroiditis, preformed hormone leaks from inflamed follicles — there is no new synthesis to block. Using thionamides in this setting would be ineffective and exposes the patient to unnecessary side effects.

**Q:** What clinical signs are specific to Graves' disease versus any form of thyrotoxicosis?

**A:** Specific to Graves' disease: proptosis (exophthalmos), periorbital puffiness, conjunctival injection, chemosis, corneal abrasions (ophthalmopathy/TAO), pretibial myxedema (dermopathy), acropachy, thyroid bruit, leukopenia, lymphocytosis, splenomegaly. Non-specific (any thyrotoxicosis): lid lag (adrenergic), tachycardia, tremor, heat intolerance, weight loss.

Lid lag is frequently misattributed exclusively to Graves' disease, but it results from sympathetic overstimulation of the superior tarsal muscle (Müller's muscle) and can occur in any thyrotoxic state. True ophthalmopathy (proptosis, chemosis) is immune-mediated and specific to Graves'.

**Q:** What is the significance of subclinical hypothyroidism, and which patients should be treated?
