**A:** Defined as elevated TSH with normal free T4. Affects up to 10% of adults; 10-20% of women >50. Associated with increased risk of CHF and CAD events, and risk of progression to overt hypothyroidism. Treatment with levothyroxine is recommended for patients with TSH ≥10 mU/L, and for young/middle-aged patients with symptoms of mild hypothyroidism. Others can be observed.

The decision to treat subclinical hypothyroidism is controversial, particularly in elderly patients where the evidence for cardiovascular benefit is less clear. TSH ≥10 mU/L is a widely accepted threshold for treatment in Canada. TPOAb positivity increases the likelihood of progression to overt hypothyroidism.

**Q:** What are the features of hypokalemic periodic paralysis in thyrotoxicosis?

**A:** Episodic muscle weakness or paralysis with low serum potassium, more common in Asian individuals. Triggered by carbohydrate intake, exercise, or rest after exertion. Treat the underlying thyrotoxicosis; acute attacks managed with potassium replacement and non-selective beta-blockers.

Thyrotoxicosis increases Na+/K+-ATPase activity, driving potassium intracellularly and causing hypokalemia. The condition predominantly affects Asian males. It can mimic familial hypokalemic periodic paralysis but resolves with treatment of the underlying hyperthyroid state. Propranolol (non-selective beta-blocker) is particularly useful for acute attacks.

**Q:** What is the prognosis and lifetime management for Graves' disease treated with radioactive iodine (I-131)?

**A:** RAI is less invasive than surgery but results in permanent hypothyroidism in the majority, requiring lifelong L-thyroxine replacement. It may worsen Graves' ophthalmopathy (concurrent prednisone should be used if ophthalmopathy is present or patient is high risk). Contraindicated in pregnancy. Precautions with close contacts are required for several days post-treatment.

I-131 is preferentially taken up by the thyroid and emits beta radiation that destroys thyroid tissue. The risk of post-RAI hypothyroidism increases over time (cumulative risk approaches 80-90% by 10 years). Follow-up TSH every 6-12 months after RAI is required to detect the onset of hypothyroidism and initiate replacement.

**Q:** What is the role of fine needle aspiration biopsy (FNAB) in evaluating thyroid nodules?

**A:** FNAB is the primary cytological tool to differentiate benign from malignant thyroid nodules. Best performed under ultrasound guidance. Accuracy is decreased if the nodule is >50% cystic or posteriorly located. In hyperthyroid patients with nodules, a radioisotope scan is done first — hot nodules generally do not require FNAB.

Ultrasound is the first-line imaging for thyroid nodules in euthyroid patients, and FNAB is guided by sonographic risk stratification. Nodules with suspicious features (microcalcifications, irregular margins, taller-than-wide shape, hypoechogenicity) warrant FNAB regardless of size thresholds. FNAB avoids unnecessary surgery for the majority of thyroid nodules, which are benign.

**Q:** How is total calcium corrected for low albumin levels?

**A:** Corrected Ca²⁺ (mmol/L) = measured Ca²⁺ + 0.02 × (40 − albumin [g/L]). For every decrease in albumin by 10 g/L, calcium increases by 0.2 mmol/L.

Total serum calcium does not reflect ionized calcium when albumin is abnormal. Since calcium is partially protein-bound, low albumin falsely lowers measured total calcium. The correction formula adjusts for this discrepancy. If ionized calcium is available, it should be measured directly.

**Q:** When should ionized calcium be measured instead of relying on total calcium?

**A:** Ionized calcium should be assessed (or total calcium corrected) in: abnormal albumin levels, critical illness, and chronic hepatic or renal failure.

These conditions alter protein binding of calcium, making total calcium unreliable. The albumin correction formula can be used when ionized calcium measurement is unavailable.

**Q:** What is the classic mnemonic for symptoms of hypercalcemia and what does each component refer to?

**A:** 'Bones, stones, groans, and psychiatric overtones': Bones = bone pain/osteoclastic activity; Stones = nephrolithiasis; Groans = GI symptoms (nausea, vomiting, constipation, pancreatitis, PUD); Psychiatric overtones = cognitive dysfunction, anxiety, depression, psychosis.

This mnemonic encompasses the four major symptom clusters in hypercalcemia. Cardiac effects (shortened QT, arrhythmia) and neuromuscular (weakness, hypotonia) symptoms are also important. Hypercalcemic crisis (usually >4 mmol/L) presents with oliguria/anuria and altered mental status.

**Q:** What are the two initial steps in the differential diagnosis of hypercalcemia?

**A:** 1. Measure PTH: high/normal PTH vs. low PTH. 2. If PTH is low, check phosphate: high/normal phosphate suggests vitamin D–related causes; low phosphate suggests humoral hypercalcemia of malignancy (PTHrP) or other PTH-independent causes.

A high or normal PTH with hypercalcemia indicates PTH-dependent causes (primary or tertiary hyperparathyroidism, lithium, familial hypocalciuric hypercalcemia). A suppressed PTH indicates PTH-independent causes (malignancy, vitamin D toxicity, granulomatous disease, immobilization, etc.).

**Q:** What are the causes of primary hyperparathyroidism?

**A:** Solitary parathyroid adenoma (81%), parathyroid hyperplasia (15%), parathyroid carcinoma (4%). Associated with MEN 1 and MEN 2a.

Primary hyperparathyroidism is the most common cause of outpatient hypercalcemia. It is characterized by high PTH and often low phosphate. Tertiary hyperparathyroidism occurs after prolonged secondary hyperparathyroidism due to renal failure (autonomous PTH secretion).

**Q:** What are the mechanisms by which malignancy causes hypercalcemia?

**A:** 1. Secretion of PTHrP (mimics PTH — prevents renal calcium excretion). 2. Cytokines in multiple myeloma. 3. Extra-renal calcitriol production by lymphoma. 4. Osteolytic bone metastases (direct effect). 5. Excess PTH in parathyroid cancer.

Malignancy is the most common cause of hypercalcemia in hospitalized patients and usually occurs in advanced disease. Common tumours: lung, renal, breast, ovarian, squamous cell carcinomas, lymphoma, and multiple myeloma. PTHrP-mediated humoral hypercalcemia causes low PTH and low/normal phosphate.

**Q:** What is familial hypocalciuric hypercalcemia (FHH) and how does it cause hypercalcemia?

**A:** FHH is caused by a loss-of-function mutation in the calcium-sensing receptor (CaSR) gene. The defective receptor fails to suppress PTH appropriately in response to elevated calcium, resulting in hypercalcemia with relative hypocalciuria and normal/high PTH.

FHH is benign and does not require parathyroid surgery. It can be distinguished from primary hyperparathyroidism by a low calcium-to-creatinine clearance ratio (<0.01). Family members may also be hypercalcemic.

**Q:** How do granulomatous diseases (e.g., sarcoidosis, tuberculosis) cause hypercalcemia?

**A:** Activated macrophages within granulomas produce extra-renal 1α-hydroxylase, leading to unregulated conversion of 25-OH vitamin D to 1,25-(OH)₂ vitamin D (calcitriol), increasing GI calcium absorption.

This mechanism is PTH-independent; PTH will be suppressed. Calcitriol levels are elevated. Treatment: corticosteroids, which inhibit 1α-hydroxylase in activated mononuclear cells and reduce calcitriol production.

**Q:** At what serum calcium level is hypercalcemia considered a medical emergency requiring urgent correction?

**A:** >3.5 mmol/L is severe hypercalcemia requiring urgent correction due to risk of dysrhythmia and coma. Hypercalcemic crisis (usually >4 mmol/L or 16 mg/dL) presents with oliguria/anuria and mental status changes including somnolence, eventually coma.

Treatment thresholds: <3.0 mmol/L often asymptomatic; 3.0–3.5 mmol/L may require treatment if symptomatic; >3.5 mmol/L requires urgent treatment. Hypercalcemic crisis is a medical emergency.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for acute hypercalcemia/hypercalcemic crisis?

**A:** Aggressive IV isotonic saline (4–6 L over 24 h) — volume expansion is the initial therapy. Loop diuretics (e.g., furosemide) only if hypervolemic (urine output >200 mL/h). Followed by bisphosphonates (treatment of choice for reducing bone resorption) — zoledronic acid 4 mg IV over 15 min or pamidronate 60–90 mg IV over 2 h.

Saline expands intravascular volume and promotes calciuresis. Bisphosphonates inhibit osteoclastic bone resorption; calcium-lowering effects are seen 24–48 h after administration. Calcitonin (4 IU/kg IM/SC q12h) has rapid onset (4–6 h) but only works for 48 h due to tachyphylaxis — ask about fish allergy before prescribing.

**Q:** Why is calcitonin used in conjunction with bisphosphonates for acute hypercalcemia?

**A:** Calcitonin has a rapid onset of action (4–6 h) while bisphosphonates take 24–48 h to lower calcium. Calcitonin provides bridging therapy until bisphosphonates take effect. However, calcitonin develops tachyphylaxis within 48 h, limiting its ongoing use.

This combination exploits the complementary pharmacokinetics of each drug. Always screen for fish allergy before prescribing calcitonin. If bisphosphonates are contraindicated (severe renal impairment), denosumab can be used with calcitonin.

**Q:** How do corticosteroids treat hypercalcemia, and in which conditions are they most useful?

**A:** Corticosteroids inhibit 1α-hydroxylase in activated mononuclear cells (macrophages), reducing calcitriol production. Most useful in: vitamin D toxicity (hypervitaminosis D), granulomatous diseases (sarcoidosis, tuberculosis), and some malignancies (lymphoma). Effects are seen within 2–5 days.

Corticosteroids are not effective for PTH-mediated hypercalcemia (e.g., primary hyperparathyroidism). Their mechanism targets extra-renal calcitriol production rather than PTH or bone resorption pathways.

**Q:** What drugs are known to cause hypercalcemia?

**A:** Lithium, thiazide diuretics, theophylline, estrogen/tamoxifen, vitamin D analogues (hypervitaminosis D), calcium carbonate (milk-alkali syndrome), vitamin A excess.

Thiazides decrease urinary calcium excretion. Lithium raises the set-point for PTH suppression. Milk-alkali syndrome (excessive calcium carbonate intake) causes hypercalcemia, metabolic alkalosis, and renal insufficiency.

**Q:** What are Chvostek's and Trousseau's signs and what do they indicate?

**A:** Chvostek's sign: tapping cranial nerve VII anterior to the ear causes ipsilateral facial (orbicularis oris) muscle twitching — indicates latent tetany in hypocalcemia. Trousseau's sign: inflation of a blood pressure cuff above systolic pressure for 3 minutes causes carpal spasm — indicates latent tetany in hypocalcemia.

These are classic bedside signs of hypocalcemia-induced neuromuscular irritability. Trousseau's sign is more specific. Both reflect increased neuromuscular excitability caused by low calcium.

**Q:** What are the acute vs. chronic clinical features of hypocalcemia?

**A:** Acute: paresthesias, laryngospasm (with stridor), hyperreflexia, tetany, Chvostek's/Trousseau's signs, ECG changes (prolonged QT), psychiatric symptoms, seizures. Chronic: CNS (lethargy, seizures, psychosis, basal ganglia calcification, Parkinsonism, papilledema), CVS (prolonged QT → Torsades de pointes), GI (steatorrhea), endocrine (impaired insulin release), skin (dry, alopecia, brittle nails, candidiasis), MSK (generalized muscle weakness).

Tetany is the hallmark of hypocalcemia. Mild tetany: perioral numbness, paresthesias of hands/feet, muscle spasm. Severe tetany: carpopedal spasm, laryngospasm, focal/generalized seizures. Chronic hypocalcemia leads to basal ganglia calcification and Parkinson-like features.

**Q:** What is the ECG finding associated with hypocalcemia?

**A:** Prolonged QT interval, which can lead to Torsades de pointes (a form of ventricular tachycardia).

In contrast, hypercalcemia shortens the QT interval. Prolonged QT from hypocalcemia is due to prolonged phase 2 of the cardiac action potential. This is a life-threatening arrhythmia requiring urgent calcium replacement.

**Q:** What is the treatment for severe symptomatic hypocalcemia?

**A:** IV calcium gluconate 1–2 g over 10–20 min followed by slow infusion (1–2 mg/kg/h). If positive Chvostek's/Trousseau's or seizures, give IV calcium bolus (1 amp IV push) first. Must also treat concurrent hypomagnesemia and provide vitamin D replacement (use 1,25-OH vitamin D if hypoparathyroid).

Hypomagnesemia must be corrected concurrently — low magnesium impairs PTH secretion and end-organ PTH responsiveness, so calcium will not normalize if hypomagnesemia is untreated. Severe hypocalcemia is a medical emergency.

**Q:** Why must hypomagnesemia be corrected in hypocalcemia?

**A:** Hypomagnesemia impairs PTH secretion and reduces end-organ responsiveness to PTH, preventing calcium normalization. Calcium levels will not correct until magnesium is replenished.

This is a critical pearl: always check and correct Mg²⁺ when treating hypocalcemia. Hypomagnesemia is common in alcoholism, malabsorption, and diuretic use.

**Q:** What is the approach to diagnosing the cause of hypocalcemia using PTH and phosphate levels?

**A:** 1. High PTH + low phosphate: vitamin D deficiency, pseudohypoparathyroidism (if phosphate is high/normal), malabsorption. 2. Low/normal PTH + high phosphate: hypoparathyroidism (post-thyroidectomy, autoimmune, infiltrative, post-surgical). 3. Low Mg²⁺: hypomagnesemia-induced PTH resistance. 4. Check liver/kidney function for vitamin D metabolism defects.

Post-thyroidectomy hypoparathyroidism is the most common cause of permanent hypoparathyroidism (<3% of surgeries). Pseudohypoparathyroidism is characterized by end-organ PTH resistance (high PTH, high phosphate, low calcium).

**Q:** What is the target serum calcium level when treating hypoparathyroidism?

**A:** The goal is to raise Ca²⁺ to the low normal range (2.0–2.1 mmol/L) — enough to prevent symptoms but allowing maximum stimulation of PTH secretion.

Treating to high normal calcium in hypoparathyroidism risks hypercalciuria and nephrolithiasis since PTH is absent/low and cannot enhance renal calcium reabsorption. Use 1,25-(OH)₂ vitamin D (calcitriol) since PTH-dependent 1α-hydroxylation is impaired.

**Q:** What is osteoporosis and how is it diagnosed using bone mineral density (BMD)?

**A:** Osteoporosis is a condition characterized by decreased bone mass and microarchitectural deterioration with increased fracture susceptibility. Diagnosed by BMD T-score ≤ −2.5 at the hip or lumbar spine by DEXA. Osteopenia: T-score between −1.0 and −2.5.

BMD is measured using dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry (DEXA). The T-score compares patient BMD to the mean peak bone mass of young healthy adults. A T-score ≤ −2.5 meets WHO diagnostic criteria for osteoporosis.

**Q:** What are the indications for BMD testing in Canada?

**A:** BMD testing is suggested in males and postmenopausal females who are: (a) aged 50–64 yr with a previous osteoporosis-related fracture OR ≥2 clinical risk factors; (b) aged 65–69 yr with ≥1 clinical risk factor; (c) aged ≥70 yr (regardless of risk factors).

Key clinical risk factors include: prior fracture after age 40, glucocorticoid use >3 months, ≥2 falls in the past year, parent with hip fracture, BMI <20 kg/m², current smoking, alcohol >3 drinks/day, secondary osteoporosis.

**Q:** What are the two fracture risk assessment tools used in Canada for osteoporosis management?

**A:** 1. WHO Fracture Risk Assessment Tool (FRAX) — estimates 10-year probability of major osteoporotic fracture. 2. Canadian Association of Radiologists and Osteoporosis Canada Risk Assessment Tool (CAROC).

Both tools stratify patients into low (<15%), intermediate (15–19.9%), or high (≥20%) 10-year fracture risk. Risk stratification guides pharmacotherapy decisions. Patients with T-score ≤ −2.5 and age >70 are automatically high risk.

**Q:** What are the Health Canada–recommended dietary calcium and vitamin D intakes for adults with osteoporosis?

**A:** Calcium: 1000 mg/day (males 51–70 yr); 1200 mg/day (females >50 yr and males >70 yr). No supplements needed if adequate dietary intake. Vitamin D: 600 IU/day (age 51–70 yr) and 800 IU/day (age >70 yr). Adults >50 yr should take a supplement of 400 IU/day in addition to vitamin D–rich foods.

Combined calcium and vitamin D supplementation reduces total fracture risk by ~6% and hip fracture risk by ~16% (JAMA Netw Open 2019). Vitamin D alone does not significantly reduce fracture risk. High-dose vitamin D (≥4000 IU/day) may paradoxically decrease BMD.

**Q:** What are the first-line pharmacological treatments for osteoporosis and their fracture prevention evidence?

**A:** First-line treatments (Grade A): 1. Bisphosphonates (alendronate PO, risedronate PO, zoledronic acid IV) — inhibit osteoclasts; prevent hip, nonvertebral, and vertebral fractures. 2. Denosumab (RANKL inhibitor) — prevents hip, nonvertebral, and vertebral fractures. Note: Denosumab must NOT be abruptly stopped (risk of multiple vertebral fractures from rebound bone turnover).

Bisphosphonates are first-line. Denosumab is an alternative first-line option, particularly in postmenopausal women at high fracture risk. Teriparatide (PTH analogue, 18–24 months) and romosozumab (sclerostin inhibitor, 12 months) are anabolic agents for high-risk patients.

**Q:** Why must denosumab not be abruptly stopped?

**A:** Abrupt discontinuation of denosumab leads to a rebound increase in bone turnover (osteoclast activity surges), causing rapid bone loss and an increased risk of multiple vertebral fractures.

Unlike bisphosphonates (which remain in bone), denosumab is a monoclonal antibody with a finite half-life. After stopping, RANKL-mediated osteoclast activity rebounds dramatically. Patients must transition to a bisphosphonate when discontinuing denosumab.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and use of teriparatide in osteoporosis?

**A:** Teriparatide is a recombinant PTH analogue (PTH 1-34). Given as intermittent SC injection, it has an anabolic effect on bone (stimulates osteoblasts more than osteoclasts), significantly reducing vertebral (RR 0.27) and nonvertebral (RR 0.62) fractures. Duration is 18–24 months, followed by long-term antiresorptive therapy.

The paradox: continuous PTH (as in hyperparathyroidism) causes bone resorption, but intermittent PTH (teriparatide) stimulates bone formation. It is used for high-risk patients who fail or cannot take bisphosphonates. Must be followed by bisphosphonate or RANKL inhibitor to maintain gains.

**Q:** What is the role of raloxifene (SERM) in osteoporosis management?

**A:** Raloxifene is a selective estrogen-receptor modulator with agonistic effects on bone and antagonistic effects on uterus and breast. It prevents vertebral fractures (Grade A evidence). Advantages: reduces fracture risk, improves lipid profile, reduces breast cancer risk by 24%. Disadvantages: increased DVT/PE, stroke mortality, hot flashes, leg cramps.

Raloxifene does not prevent hip or nonvertebral fractures, distinguishing it from bisphosphonates and denosumab. It is an option for postmenopausal women with osteoporosis who cannot tolerate bisphosphonates, particularly those at high breast cancer risk.

**Q:** What is a fragility fracture and what are the most common sites?

**A:** A fragility fracture is a fracture from a fall from standing height or less (excluding fractures of the hand, face, cervical spine, fingers, and toes). Most common sites: hip, vertebrae, humerus, and wrist.

Fragility fractures are pathognomonic of osteoporosis in the absence of other metabolic bone disease. Vertebral compression fractures may appear as wedge fractures on x-ray (minimum 20% height loss). A prior fragility fracture is a strong predictor of future fracture risk.

**Q:** What is the effect of glucocorticoid therapy on bone metabolism?

**A:** Glucocorticoids cause bone loss through: increased bone resorption, decreased bone formation, increased urinary calcium loss, decreased intestinal calcium absorption, and impaired sex hormone and growth hormone production. Patients on ≥7.5 mg prednisone daily for >3 months should be assessed for fracture risk.

Glucocorticoid-induced osteoporosis (GIOP) is the most common form of secondary osteoporosis. Risk of fracture is disproportionate to BMD loss. Bisphosphonates are the first-line treatment/prevention for GIOP.

**Q:** What are the physical examination findings that suggest vertebral fracture or osteoporosis?

**A:** Height loss >3 cm (Sn 92%), weight <51 kg (Sp 97%), kyphosis (Sp 92%), tooth count <20 (Sp 92%), reduced grip strength, occiput-to-wall distance >0 cm, rib-to-pelvis distance <2 fingerbreadths (Sn 88%).

The wall-occiput test and rib-pelvis distance test are simple bedside screens for thoracic and lumbar vertebral fractures, respectively. Kyphosis results from anterior wedge compression fractures of thoracic vertebral bodies.

**Q:** What is osteomalacia/rickets and what conditions predispose to it?

**A:** Osteomalacia (adults)/rickets (children): osteopenia with disordered mineralization leading to increased unmineralized osteoid. Caused by: vitamin D deficiency (nutritional, malabsorption, liver/kidney disease, anticonvulsants, nephrotic syndrome), calcium deficiency, hypophosphatemia (Fanconi syndrome, X-linked hypophosphatemic rickets, tumour-induced), enzyme deficiency (hypophosphatasia), and calcification inhibitors (aluminum, fluoride).

The hallmark is excess osteoid (unmineralized matrix). Rickets occurs before epiphyseal closure; osteomalacia occurs after. Anticonvulsants (phenytoin, carbamazepine, phenobarbital) impair vitamin D hydroxylation in the liver.

**Q:** What are the clinical features distinguishing rickets from osteomalacia?

**A:** Rickets (children): skeletal deformities, bow-legs, fracture susceptibility, weakness, hypotonia, disturbed growth, rachitic rosary (prominent costochondral junctions), Harrison's groove (indentation of lower ribs), hypocalcemia. Osteomalacia (adults): less severe deformities, diffuse skeletal pain, bone tenderness, fractures, waddling gait, proximal muscle weakness, hypotonia.

Rickets is more clinically dramatic due to active growth plates. Osteomalacia presents subtly with vague bone pain and weakness. Both share biochemical features of impaired mineralization.

**Q:** What are the laboratory findings in vitamin D deficiency osteomalacia?

**A:** Decreased serum phosphate, decreased to normal serum calcium, increased ALP, may have decreased calcitriol (1,25-(OH)₂ vitamin D). Secondary hyperparathyroidism (high PTH) is typical.

Increased ALP reflects increased osteoblastic activity trying to compensate for poor mineralization. Compare to hypophosphatemia-related osteomalacia: phosphate is low, calcium is normal, ALP is elevated. Proximal renal tubular acidosis-related disease: phosphate low, calcium normal, ALP normal, with hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis.

**Q:** What radiographic findings are seen in osteomalacia?

**A:** Pseudofractures (Looser zones) — wide transverse lucencies traversing a bone, thought to be healed stress fractures; loss of distinctness of vertebral body trabeculae; concavity of vertebral bodies; subperiosteal resorption of phalanges (from secondary hyperparathyroidism); bowing of tibia.

Looser zones (pseudofractures) are pathognomonic of osteomalacia. They represent fissures filled with unmineralized osteoid. Bone biopsy (showing excess unmineralized osteoid) is the gold standard but rarely needed.

**Q:** What is renal osteodystrophy and what are its four bone disease subtypes?

**A:** Renal osteodystrophy refers to changes in mineral metabolism and bone structure secondary to CKD. Four subtypes: 1. Osteomalacia (low turnover, excess osteoid). 2. Adynamic bone disease (low turnover from excessive PTH suppression). 3. Osteitis fibrosa cystica (high turnover from secondary hyperparathyroidism). 4. Mixed uremic osteodystrophy (both high and low turnover, marrow fibrosis, excess osteoids).

The pathophysiology involves hyperphosphatemia (inhibits 1,25(OH)₂ vitamin D synthesis) and loss of renal mass (reduced 1α-hydroxylase). This leads to low calcitriol, hypocalcemia, and secondary hyperparathyroidism. Metastatic calcification from hyperphosphatemia is a complication.

**Q:** Why are bisphosphonates and denosumab generally not used in renal osteodystrophy?

**A:** Bisphosphonates and denosumab can worsen the adynamic component of renal osteodystrophy and are contraindicated at low eGFRs. Bone biopsy may be needed to identify if increased bone turnover is present before considering bisphosphonates.

In adynamic bone disease, bone turnover is already suppressed; further suppression with antiresorptive agents would worsen the condition. Activated vitamin D (calcitriol) is preferred with close monitoring to prevent hypercalcemia and metastatic calcification.

**Q:** What is Paget's disease of bone and what is the proposed etiology?

**A:** Paget's disease is a metabolic bone disease characterized by excessive bone destruction followed by structurally abnormal repair. Proposed etiology: slowly progressive viral infection of osteoclasts (possibly paramyxovirus) in genetically predisposed individuals. Strong familial incidence.

Prevalence: ~3% of population; up to 10% in those >80 years. The cycle begins with increased osteoclastic resorption, followed by compensatory osteoblastic activity producing woven (disorganized, fragile) bone. Elevated ALP with normal GGT in an older adult should prompt consideration of Paget's.

**Q:** What are the three characteristic radiographic findings of Paget's disease of bone?

**A:** 1. Osteolytic lesions (lytic phase — 'blade of grass' leading edge in long bones). 2. Cortical thickening ('cotton wool' or 'jigsaw/mosaic pattern'). 3. Pseudofractures (small fissures on the convex surface of deformed long bones — 'banana fractures' are horizontal pathological fractures).

Other named signs: 'osteoporosis circumscripta' (radiolucent skull regions), 'tam o'Shanter sign' (skull enlargement/expansion), 'picture frame vertebra' (thickened vertebral cortex), 'ivory vertebra' (diffuse vertebral sclerosis), 'Looser zones' (wide transverse lucencies). Plain x-ray of skull, abdomen, and tibiae are initial screening.

**Q:** What are the characteristic laboratory findings in Paget's disease of bone?

**A:** High serum ALP (markedly elevated, reflects increased bone turnover), normal or high Ca²⁺, normal PO₄³⁻, normal LFTs (normal PT/INR, aPTT, albumin, bilirubin, GGT), elevated PINP (procollagen type I N-terminal propeptide — bone formation marker).

The key distinguishing feature is high ALP with normal GGT — ruling out hepatic cause. ALP elevation reflects osteoblastic activity during the repair phase. Normal phosphate helps distinguish from osteomalacia.

**Q:** What are the most common bones affected by Paget's disease?

**A:** Most commonly: skull, thoracolumbar spine, pelvis, and long bones of the lower extremities (particularly femur and tibia).

Clinical manifestations at these sites include: deafness (cranial nerve VIII compression from skull thickening), spinal stenosis, bowed tibias, kyphosis, and pelvic deformities. Paget's disease is usually asymptomatic and found incidentally on x-ray or elevated ALP.

**Q:** What are the local and systemic complications of Paget's disease of bone?

**A:** Local: fractures, osteoarthritis, cranial nerve compression/palsies (e.g., deafness CN VIII), spinal cord compression, osteosarcoma/sarcomatous transformation (1–3%) — indicated by marked bone pain, new lytic lesions, suddenly increased ALP. Systemic: hypercalcemia and nephrolithiasis (with immobilization), high-output CHF (from increased vascularity of pagetic bone).

Osteosarcoma transformation is rare but carries very poor prognosis. High-output CHF occurs because extensive pagetic bone acts as an arteriovenous shunt. Sarcomatous change is suggested by sudden marked increase in ALP and severe pain.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Paget's disease of bone?

**A:** Goals: decrease pain and rate of remodelling. Treat medically if symptomatic OR asymptomatic with ALP >3× normal OR planned surgery. First-line: bisphosphonates — zoledronic acid 5 mg IV/year (preferred), or alendronate 40 mg PO daily × 6 months, or risedronate 30 mg PO daily × 3 months. If bisphosphonate-intolerant: calcitonin 50–100 units/day SC. Surgery for fractures, deformity, or resistant osteoarthritis.

Bisphosphonates suppress osteoclast activity, reducing bone turnover. Adequate calcium and vitamin D intake is important to prevent secondary hyperparathyroidism. Weight-bearing exercise is encouraged.

**Q:** What is the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis in males?

**A:** GnRH (from hypothalamus) → stimulates LH and FSH from anterior pituitary → LH stimulates Leydig cells (testosterone production); FSH stimulates Sertoli cells (spermatogenesis, inhibin production). Testosterone provides negative feedback on LH/GnRH; inhibin suppresses FSH.

Testosterone is primarily involved in negative feedback on LH and GnRH. Inhibin B from Sertoli cells specifically suppresses FSH. This axis regulation is the basis for understanding hypogonadism patterns (primary vs. secondary).

**Q:** What tests are used to evaluate testicular function?

**A:** 1. Testicular size (lower limit: 4 cm × 2.5 cm or 12–25 mL volume by orchidometer). 2. LH, FSH, total, bioavailable, and/or free testosterone. 3. Semen analysis (volume, sperm concentration, morphology, motility). 4. Testicular biopsy (indicated with normal FSH and azoospermia/oligospermia).

Morning fasting testosterone (8–9 AM) is the preferred initial test, as testosterone peaks in the morning. Testosterone testing should be confirmed on 2 separate analyses if low. Free testosterone is important if SHBG is altered (e.g., obesity, liver disease).

**Q:** How is testosterone deficiency syndrome (adult-onset primary hypogonadism) diagnosed?

**A:** Requires BOTH: 1. Clinical manifestations of testosterone deficiency (symptoms/signs). 2. Documented total testosterone below the laboratory reference range, confirmed on 2 separate fasting morning (8–9 AM) analyses.

Testosterone is measured fasting in the morning (8–9 AM) when levels are at their peak. Secondary causes must be ruled out. Free testosterone is measured if SHBG is altered or total T is borderline.

**Q:** What distinguishes primary (hypergonadotropic) from secondary (hypogonadotropic) hypogonadism?

**A:** Primary (hypergonadotropic) hypogonadism: primary testicular failure → high LH and FSH, low testosterone and sperm count. Secondary (hypogonadotropic) hypogonadism: hypothalamic-pituitary axis failure → low/normal LH and FSH (inappropriately normal), low testosterone and sperm count.

In primary hypogonadism, the pituitary tries to compensate with elevated gonadotropins. In secondary hypogonadism, the pituitary fails to mount this response. A key clinical pearl: in primary hypogonadism, sperm count is affected to a greater extent than serum testosterone.

**Q:** What are the congenital causes of primary (hypergonadotropic) hypogonadism?

**A:** Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY), Noonan syndrome, cryptorchidism, disorders of sexual development (DSD), bilateral anorchia (vanishing testis syndrome), myotonic dystrophy, mutations of FSH or LH receptor gene, disorders of androgen synthesis, germ cell defects, Sertoli cell–only syndrome, Leydig cell aplasia/failure.

Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY) is the most common chromosomal cause of male hypogonadism, presenting with small firm testes, azoospermia, gynecomastia, tall stature, and cognitive issues. Karyotype is essential for diagnosis.

**Q:** What are the causes of secondary (hypogonadotropic) hypogonadism?

**A:** Congenital: Kallmann syndrome (GnRH deficiency + anosmia), Prader-Willi syndrome, abnormal LH/FSH subunit. Acquired: hyperprolactinemia, pituitary/hypothalamic tumours, hypopituitarism, drugs (alcohol, cannabis, spironolactone, ketoconazole, GnRH agonists, anabolic steroids, opioids), chronic illness (cirrhosis, renal failure, AIDS, sarcoidosis, hemochromatosis), obesity, critical illness.

Kallmann syndrome: GnRH deficiency + anosmia (absent smell due to failure of GnRH neuron migration). Hemochromatosis deposits iron in the pituitary, causing secondary hypogonadism. Hyperprolactinemia suppresses GnRH pulsatility.

**Q:** What is the definition of male infertility and what is the timeline for investigation?

**A:** Infertility: failure of a couple to conceive after 12 months of regular intercourse without contraception (in women <35 yr); after 6 months if the woman is ≥35 yr. Male factor contributes to ~50% of cases. Initial workup: semen analysis ×2.

Semen analysis should assess volume, sperm concentration, morphology, and motility. If semen analysis is abnormal, workup includes LH, FSH, total testosterone, prolactin, thyroid function, DNA fragmentation, karyotype, and Y chromosome deletion analysis. Scrotal ultrasound looks for varicocele.

**Q:** What additional investigations are indicated in secondary hypogonadism to identify the underlying cause?

**A:** Prolactin levels, iron saturation (hemochromatosis), pituitary MRI (if hyperprolactinemia, pituitary tumour, or unexplained secondary hypogonadism), free T4 and 8 AM cortisol (pituitary panel), karyotype (if Klinefelter suspected), Y chromosome deletion analysis.

Secondary hypogonadism has a lower LH/FSH with low testosterone. The pituitary MRI is critical to exclude prolactinoma or other hypothalamic-pituitary lesions. Iron overload in hemochromatosis preferentially deposits in the pituitary gonadotrophs, causing selective LH/FSH deficiency.

**Q:** What are the named radiographic signs in Paget's disease of bone and what do they describe?

**A:** Tam o'Shanter: overall enlargement of cranium with skull falling over facial bones (advanced skull Paget's). Blade of grass: lucent leading edge in long bone lytic phase. Osteoporosis circumscripta: radiolucent skull regions. Jigsaw/mosaic pattern: thickened disorganized trabeculae with areas of sclerosis. Picture frame vertebra: thickened vertebral body cortex. Cotton wool: thickened sclerotic trabeculae. Banana fracture: horizontal pathological fracture in deformed long bone. Ivory vertebra: diffuse homogeneous vertebral sclerosis.

These eponymous radiographic findings are classic MCCQE1 exam material. Confirmation of diagnosis requires x-ray findings. Bone scan evaluates extent of metabolically active disease. MRI/CT are not recommended for diagnosis but may assess complications.

**Q:** Which vitamin D level thresholds define sufficiency, insufficiency, and deficiency in Canada?

**A:** <30 nmol/L: high risk of vitamin D deficiency. 30–50 nmol/L: potential risk of inadequacy for bone health. >50 nmol/L: adequate for bone and overall health. ≥125 nmol/L: potential adverse effects. Measured as 25-OH vitamin D (calcidiol).

25-OH vitamin D is the best marker of vitamin D status. Vitamin D deficiency leads to impaired calcium absorption, secondary hyperparathyroidism, and osteomalacia/rickets. Risk factors for deficiency: malabsorption, reduced skin synthesis, liver/renal failure, nephrotic syndrome, anticonvulsants, parathyroid disorders.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to testosterone replacement therapy?

**A:** Prostate or breast cancer, palpable prostate nodule, PSA >4 ng/mL, elevated hematocrit, untreated severe OSA, severe LUTS, uncontrolled CHF, MI or stroke within last 6 months, thrombophilia.

Testosterone therapy stimulates prostate tissue and can worsen erythrocytosis, OSA, and cardiovascular/thromboembolic conditions. Canadian guidelines emphasize ruling out prostate cancer prior to initiation and monitoring hematocrit regularly during therapy.

**Q:** A 28-year-old man with hypogonadal infertility due to a pituitary adenoma is started on fertility-directed therapy. What is the preferred treatment?

**A:** hCG plus recombinant FSH (rFSH). GnRH agonist in pulsatile fashion is used for hypothalamic dysfunction with intact pituitary; hCG + rFSH is used for both hypothalamic and pituitary lesions.

Pituitary lesions prevent endogenous gonadotropin secretion, so exogenous gonadotropins (hCG mimics LH, rFSH provides FSH) are needed to stimulate spermatogenesis. Pulsatile GnRH only works when the pituitary is intact.

**Q:** Why does testosterone replacement therapy cause infertility?

**A:** Exogenous testosterone suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis, reducing GnRH, LH, and FSH secretion, which decreases spermatogenesis and reduces sperm counts.

This is a key clinical pitfall: men seeking both symptom relief and fertility should not receive testosterone therapy. Instead, gonadotropins or pulsatile GnRH should be used to preserve fertility.

**Q:** Which hereditary disorders are associated with male infertility and what is their mechanism?

**A:** Kartagener syndrome (primary ciliary dyskinesia — immotile sperm) and cystic fibrosis (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens — obstructive azoospermia).

Kartagener syndrome causes dynein arm defects impairing ciliary/flagellar motility, rendering sperm non-motile. CF mutations (particularly CFTR) lead to aplasia of the vas deferens, causing obstructive azoospermia without systemic CF symptoms in some carriers.

**Q:** What are the effects of testosterone deficiency in the first trimester in utero?

**A:** Incomplete virilization of external genitalia (ambiguous genitalia) and incomplete development of Wolffian ducts, causing male pseudohermaphroditism.

Testosterone and DHT are essential for male sexual differentiation during the first trimester. The Wolffian ducts give rise to epididymis, vas deferens, and seminal vesicles; their failure to develop results in lack of internal male genitalia.

**Q:** What are the effects of testosterone deficiency occurring in the third trimester in utero?

**A:** Micropenis and cryptorchidism (failure of normal testicular descent).

Testicular descent and penile growth in the third trimester require adequate androgen stimulation. Deficiency at this stage does not affect internal genitalia (already developed) but impairs growth and descent.

**Q:** What is eunuchoidal body habitus and what causes it?

**A:** Greater growth of extremity long bones relative to axial bones (arm span > height, pubic-to-floor > crown-to-pubis), caused by prepubertal testosterone deficiency, which delays epiphyseal closure.

Testosterone normally closes the epiphyses at puberty. In its absence, long bones continue to grow, producing a distinctive skeletal proportions. This is a classic finding in Klinefelter syndrome and other prepubertal hypogonadism.

**Q:** What are the postpubertal clinical features of testosterone deficiency?

**A:** Decreased energy, mood, and libido; fine wrinkles at corners of mouth and eyes; decreased pubic/axillary hair, hematocrit, muscle mass, strength, and bone mineral density (BMD).

Postpubertal androgen deficiency does not reverse established secondary sex characteristics but leads to gradual regression. BMD loss predisposes to osteoporosis, and decreased erythropoiesis causes a mild normochromic normocytic anemia.

**Q:** What is true gynecomastia and how does it differ from pseudogynecomastia?

**A:** True gynecomastia is benign proliferation of the glandular component of the male breast, forming a concentric, rubbery, firm mass around the nipple. Pseudogynecomastia (lipomastia) is enlargement of soft adipose tissue without glandular proliferation, common in obese men.

Distinguishing these on exam is critical: gynecomastia is concentric, rubbery, and not fixed, while lipomastia is soft and diffuse. True gynecomastia warrants investigation for hormonal causes; pseudogynecomastia generally does not.

**Q:** What is the trimodal distribution of physiologic gynecomastia?

**A:** Neonatal, pubertal, and older males (ages 50–80). It peaks between ages 13–44 and spontaneously regresses in 90% of cases.

In neonates, maternal estrogens cause transient gynecomastia. In puberty, the estrogen-to-androgen ratio is transiently elevated. In older men, decreased testosterone production with peripheral aromatization of androgens to estrogen causes the imbalance.

**Q:** List six drug classes that commonly cause gynecomastia.

**A:** Spironolactone, cimetidine, digoxin, ketoconazole, antiandrogens/GnRH agonists/5-alpha-reductase inhibitors (e.g., androgen deprivation therapy), and anabolic steroids/doping agents.

The mnemonic DOC TECH covers common culprits: Digoxin, hCG/recombinant GH, Isoniazid, Spironolactone, Cimetidine/Ketoconazole, Estrogen/anti-testosterone agents, Testicular damage. All cause gynecomastia by either increasing estrogen activity or decreasing androgen activity.

**Q:** What pathophysiologic mechanism underlies gynecomastia?

**A:** Hormonal imbalance: increased estrogen activity (increased production/precursors or decreased metabolism) OR decreased androgen activity (decreased production, increased SHBG binding, or androgen receptor blockade).

The net effect is a shift in the estrogen-to-androgen ratio in favour of estrogen. Any condition or drug that tips this balance causes glandular breast proliferation. This is why cirrhosis (impaired estrogen catabolism) and Klinefelter syndrome (primary hypogonadism) both cause gynecomastia.

**Q:** What red flags on breast exam suggest male breast cancer rather than benign gynecomastia?

**A:** Unilateral mass, eccentric (not concentric) location, hard or fixed mass, skin dimpling or retraction, and nipple discharge (especially bloody) or crusting.

Gynecomastia is concentric around the nipple, rubbery, and not fixed to underlying tissue. Any deviation from this pattern warrants further investigation with breast ultrasound and diagnostic mammography (90% sensitivity and specificity for distinguishing malignant from benign).

**Q:** What investigations are indicated in the workup of gynecomastia?

**A:** Serum TSH, PRL, LH, FSH, total testosterone, estradiol, LFTs, creatinine, hCG (if elevated, locate primary tumour); ± breast U/S and mammography if malignancy cannot be excluded clinically; CXR and CT chest/abdomen/pelvis if neoplasm suspected.

The investigation is guided by clinical findings: hCG elevation points to a germ cell or hCG-secreting tumour, elevated estradiol to an adrenal or testicular tumour, low testosterone/high LH to primary hypogonadism, and elevated TSH/LFTs to systemic disease.

**Q:** What is the first-line medical treatment for gynecomastia and when is surgery indicated?

**A:** First line: correct the underlying disorder and discontinue the responsible drug. Medical: tamoxifen (anti-estrogen, most evidence for benefit). Surgical: for longstanding gynecomastia (>12 months, fibrotic), significant discomfort, or psychological distress.

Most gynecomastia resolves with removal of the offending cause. Tamoxifen blocks estrogen receptors in breast tissue. Surgery is reserved for fibrotic/longstanding cases where medical therapy is unlikely to reverse glandular changes.

**Q:** What is a paraneoplastic syndrome and what is its general mechanism?

**A:** Clinical syndromes involving non-metastatic systemic effects accompanying malignant disease, triggered by: (1) antibodies against the neoplasm cross-reacting with normal tissue, or (2) production of a physiologically active substance by the neoplasm.

Paraneoplastic syndromes can affect any organ system. They are important clinically because they may be the presenting feature of an occult malignancy and can cause significant morbidity independent of tumour burden.

**Q:** Which malignancy most commonly causes ectopic ACTH secretion and what syndrome does it produce?

**A:** Small cell lung cancer (also pancreatic carcinoma, neuroendocrine tumours, thymoma). It produces Cushing's syndrome via ectopic ACTH or ACTH-mimicking substance secretion.

Ectopic Cushing's from small cell lung cancer often presents with rapid onset, severe hypokalemia, and less prominent adiposity compared to pituitary-driven Cushing's. Cortisol levels are very high and often do not suppress with high-dose dexamethasone.

**Q:** What malignancies are associated with SIADH as a paraneoplastic syndrome?

**A:** Small cell lung cancer and CNS malignancies.

SIADH causes euvolemic hyponatremia with inappropriately concentrated urine. Symptoms include oliguria, nausea/vomiting, headache, confusion, fatigue, muscle weakness/cramps, and seizures. Treatment involves fluid restriction and treating the underlying tumour.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of paraneoplastic hypercalcemia and which cancers are most commonly responsible?

**A:** Secretion of PTH-related protein (PTHrP), TGF-alpha, or TNF by the tumour. Associated cancers: lung cancer, breast carcinoma, renal cell carcinoma, multiple myeloma, ovarian carcinoma.

PTHrP mimics PTH action on bone and kidney, increasing calcium reabsorption and bone resorption. This is distinct from osteolytic hypercalcemia of myeloma (local osteoclast activation) and from primary hyperparathyroidism.

**Q:** What is Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS), which cancer causes it, and what is its mechanism?

**A:** LEMS is a paraneoplastic neuromuscular disorder associated with small cell lung cancer. Autoantibodies interfere with presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels, reducing acetylcholine (ACh) release, causing proximal limb muscle weakness.

Unlike myasthenia gravis (postsynaptic ACh receptor antibodies, worse with repetition), LEMS typically improves with repetitive muscle use (post-tetanic facilitation). It is also associated with autonomic dysfunction.

**Q:** What paraneoplastic endocrine syndrome is associated with hepatocellular carcinoma and fibrosarcoma, and what is the mechanism?

**A:** Paraneoplastic hypoglycemia, caused by secretion of insulin or insulin-like substances (e.g., IGF-2) by the tumour.

IGF-2 from large mesenchymal tumours (fibrosarcoma, GIST) or hepatocellular carcinomas binds insulin receptors causing hypoglycemia. This is distinct from insulinoma, which secretes actual insulin. Clinically presents with tachycardia, tremor, diaphoresis, and dizziness.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of carcinoid syndrome and what mediates them?

**A:** Flushing, diarrhea, shortness of breath, tachycardia. Mediated by serotonin and bradykinin secretion from gastrointestinal neuroendocrine tumours.

Carcinoid syndrome typically occurs when metastatic disease allows mediators to bypass first-pass hepatic inactivation. Serotonin drives diarrhea and cardiac fibrosis; bradykinin causes flushing. Diagnosis is confirmed with 24-hour urine 5-HIAA.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of metformin, and what are its absolute contraindications?

**A:** Sensitizes peripheral tissues to insulin, increases glucose uptake, and decreases hepatic glucose production via stimulation of hepatic AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK). Absolute contraindications: moderate-to-severe liver dysfunction, renal dysfunction (GFR <15 mL/min), cardiac dysfunction.

Metformin reduces hepatic gluconeogenesis and improves both fasting and postprandial hyperglycemia. It is weight neutral with negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy. Lactic acidosis risk is the concern in renal/hepatic failure. HbA1c reduction ~1.5%.

**Q:** What are the key side effects of metformin?

**A:** GI upset (abdominal discomfort, bloating, diarrhea) and vitamin B12 deficiency. It is weight neutral with negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy.

GI side effects are minimized by dose titration and taking with food. Vitamin B12 deficiency occurs in up to 30% of long-term users due to reduced ileal absorption; periodic B12 monitoring is recommended by Canadian Diabetes Association guidelines.

**Q:** How do sulfonylureas work, what is their HbA1c reduction, and which has the lowest hypoglycemia risk?

**A:** Stimulate insulin release from beta cells by closing K+ channels → depolarization → Ca2+-mediated insulin release. HbA1c reduction ~1%. Gliclazide has the lowest incidence of hypoglycemia among sulfonylureas.

Sulfonylureas are effective but cause weight gain and hypoglycemia. Glyburide should be avoided in the elderly (prolonged action, increased hypoglycemia risk). Dose adjustment needed in renal dysfunction for glyburide and glimepiride; avoid in severe renal dysfunction.

**Q:** What are meglitinides (non-sulfonylurea secretagogues), and what is their clinical advantage?

**A:** Short-acting insulin secretagogues (repaglinide, nateglinide) that close K+ channels. Short half-life (~1 hour) causes brief, rapid insulin release, making them effective for postprandial glucose control. Must be dosed with meals.

Meglitinides carry less hypoglycemia risk than sulfonylureas due to their brief action. They are not to be combined with sulfonylureas or preprandial insulin. More costly than sulfonylureas; HbA1c reduction 0.5–1%.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of thiazolidinediones (TZDs), and why are they rarely used in Canada now?

**A:** Bind PPAR-gamma nuclear receptors, sensitizing peripheral tissues to insulin and reducing free fatty acid release from adipose. Rarely used due to side effects: peripheral edema, fluid retention, CHF exacerbation, increased cardiac events with rosiglitazone, increased bladder cancer with pioglitazone, and fractures.

Rosiglitazone requires written informed consent regarding cardiovascular risks. The maximum efficacy of TZDs takes 6–12 weeks. They are absolutely contraindicated in NYHA class II+ CHF and bladder cancer. HbA1c reduction ~0.8%.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of DPP-4 inhibitors (gliptins), and what are their notable side effects?

**A:** Inhibit degradation of endogenous incretin hormones (GLP-1, GIP), which stimulate insulin secretion, inhibit glucagon release, and delay gastric emptying. Side effects: nasopharyngitis, URTI, headache, pancreatitis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, bullous pemphigoid.

DPP-4 inhibitors are weight neutral with negligible hypoglycemia risk as monotherapy. Saxagliptin is associated with CHF risk. Dose reduction needed in renal dysfunction (except linagliptin). HbA1c reduction ~0.7%.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of GLP-1 receptor agonists, and what are their key clinical benefits beyond glycemic control?

**A:** Bind GLP-1 receptors to promote glucose-dependent insulin release, suppress glucagon, slow gastric emptying. Key benefits: weight loss, cardiovascular mortality reduction in patients with known CVD (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide).

GLP-1 analogues are insulinotropic only when glucose >4 mmol/L, minimizing hypoglycemia risk. They also cause beta-cell regeneration in vitro. Contraindicated in personal/family history of MTC or MEN2 (C-cell stimulation in animals). Common side effect: nausea/diarrhea.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of SGLT2 inhibitors, and what are their key clinical benefits and risks?

**A:** Inhibit renal proximal tubule glucose reabsorption, enhancing urinary glucose excretion. Benefits: weight loss, cardiovascular mortality reduction (empagliflozin), renal protection, HbA1c reduction 0.7–1%. Risks: UTI, genital infections, hypotension, rare euglycemic DKA, hyperlipidemia, decreased BMD.

SGLT2 inhibitors are contraindicated in eGFR <15 or dialysis. Dapagliflozin is contraindicated in active/prior bladder cancer. Euglycemic DKA is a rare but important complication — blood glucose may not be markedly elevated, delaying diagnosis.

**Q:** A patient with T2DM and known atherosclerotic CVD needs to start a second agent. Which drug classes have proven cardiovascular mortality benefits?

**A:** GLP-1 receptor agonists (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide) and SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, canagliflozin, dapagliflozin) have demonstrated added cardiovascular mortality and outcomes benefit in patients with known CVD.

This is a key Canadian Diabetes Association guideline recommendation. SGLT2 inhibitors also offer renal protection. Both classes should be preferred add-on agents in T2DM with established ASCVD or high-risk features, independent of glycemic control needs.

**Q:** What are the first-line agents for dyslipidemia and how do statins work?

**A:** Statins (HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors) are first-line. Mechanism: inhibit cholesterol biosynthesis → upregulate hepatic LDL receptors → increased LDL clearance. Also modestly increase HDL and decrease VLDL. Effect: primarily LDL reduction.

Statins are the most evidence-based lipid-lowering therapy for cardiovascular risk reduction. Adverse effects include elevated liver enzymes, myositis, and rarely rhabdomyolysis (risk increased when combined with fibrates). Liver disease is a contraindication.

**Q:** When are fibrates indicated and what is their major drug interaction risk?

**A:** Fibrates (bezafibrate, fenofibrate, gemfibrozil) are used for elevated triglycerides and hyperchylomicronemia. Activate PPAR-alpha. Major risk: rhabdomyolysis when combined with statins.

Fibrates upregulate lipoprotein lipase and apo A1, primarily lowering triglycerides and modestly raising HDL. The statin-fibrate combination carries a significant rhabdomyolysis risk, especially gemfibrozil + statin. Contraindicated in hepatic and renal disease.

**Q:** What are PCSK9 inhibitors, when are they used, and what is their mechanism?

**A:** Monoclonal antibodies (evolocumab, alirocumab) that inhibit PCSK9, preventing LDL receptor degradation and thus increasing LDL clearance. Used as add-on to maximally tolerated statin therapy in heterozygous and homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia, and in patients with atherosclerotic CVD not at LDL target despite maximal statin + ezetimibe.

PCSK9 normally degrades LDL receptors on hepatocytes. By blocking PCSK9, these agents dramatically lower LDL-C. Administered subcutaneously every 2–4 weeks. Side effects: nasopharyngitis, URTI, injection site soreness, myalgia.

**Q:** What are the two thionamides used for hyperthyroidism in Canada, and how do they differ in safety profile during pregnancy?

**A:** Propylthiouracil (PTU) and methimazole (MMI). PTU is recommended in the first trimester; MMI is preferred in the second and third trimesters. Both are safe with breastfeeding at recommended doses (PTU all doses; MMI <20–30 mg/day).

PTU is preferred in the first trimester because MMI carries a risk of embryopathy (aplasia cutis, choanal atresia). However, PTU carries a higher risk of drug-induced hepatitis, so MMI is preferred in non-pregnant patients and after the first trimester.

**Q:** What are the side effects of antithyroid drugs (thionamides)?

**A:** Both: rash, agranulocytosis, vasculitis, hypoglycemia. PTU-specific: drug-induced hepatitis. MMI-specific: cholestasis.

Agranulocytosis is the most dangerous complication (~0.3–0.5%); patients should be warned to seek immediate assessment for fever or sore throat. LFTs and CBC should be monitored. Agranulocytosis onset is typically within the first 3 months of therapy.

**Q:** What are the indications and contraindications for radioactive iodine (RAI, I-131) therapy?

**A:** Indications: hyperthyroidism, thyroid malignancy. Contraindications: hypersensitivity, concurrent antithyroid medication, pregnancy, lactation. Doses: ~12 mCi for hyperthyroidism; 50–150 mCi for thyroid cancer.

RAI is incorporated into thyroid follicular cells and destroys them via beta radiation. It is absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy (causes fetal hypothyroidism). Antithyroid drugs must be stopped before RAI to allow iodine uptake. Common side effects include sialadenitis and thyroiditis.

**Q:** What are the key contraindications to oral bisphosphonates (alendronate, risedronate) for osteoporosis?

**A:** Esophageal stricture or achalasia, inability to remain upright for >30 minutes after ingestion, hypocalcemia, renal insufficiency (CrCl <35 mL/min for alendronate, <30 for risedronate), hypersensitivity, history of atypical femoral fracture or osteonecrosis of the jaw.

Oral bisphosphonates must be taken with a full glass of water and the patient must remain upright for 30 minutes to prevent esophageal irritation/ulceration. IV zoledronate is an alternative when oral administration is not feasible.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of denosumab for osteoporosis and what are its unique adverse effects?

**A:** Monoclonal antibody that inhibits RANKL (osteoclast-differentiating factor), reducing osteoclast formation and bone resorption. Given as 60 mg SC every 6 months. Adverse effects: hypocalcemia, atypical femur fractures, osteonecrosis of the jaw, fatigue/GI.

Denosumab is approved for postmenopausal women at high fracture risk, glucocorticoid-induced osteoporosis, and prevention of skeletal-related events from solid tumour bone metastases. Unlike bisphosphonates, it has no renal contraindication but hypocalcemia must be corrected before initiation.

**Q:** What is teriparatide and how does it differ mechanistically from antiresorptive agents?

**A:** Teriparatide is a PTH analog that stimulates new bone formation by preferential stimulation of osteoblastic over osteoclastic activity. It is an anabolic agent (builds bone), unlike bisphosphonates and denosumab which are antiresorptive (prevent bone loss).

Teriparatide (20 mcg SC once daily) is used for high-risk osteoporosis for 18–24 months. Contraindicated in Paget's disease, prior radiation to skeleton, bone metastases, and other metabolic bone diseases. Side effects: orthostatic hypotension, hypercalcemia, dizziness.

**Q:** What is romosozumab and what is its key cardiovascular warning?

**A:** Romosozumab is an anti-sclerostin monoclonal antibody that inhibits the Wnt/beta-catenin pathway inhibitor sclerostin, increasing bone formation AND reducing bone resorption (dual mechanism). Given 210 mg SC monthly for 12 months. Warning: may increase risk of MI, stroke, and cardiovascular death.

Romosozumab is indicated for postmenopausal women at high fracture risk (history of osteoporotic fracture or multiple risk factors). Its dual anabolic and antiresorptive action makes it highly effective. Contraindicated in hypocalcemia; caution in patients with prior cardiovascular events.

**Q:** What is the role of raloxifene in osteoporosis management and what are its contraindications?

**A:** Selective estrogen receptor modulator (SERM) used as second-line treatment/prevention of postmenopausal osteoporosis. Decreases bone resorption. Contraindicated in: pregnancy, lactation, active or prior DVT/PE/retinal vein thrombosis. Side effects: hot flashes, leg cramps, increased risk of fatal stroke and venous thromboembolism.

Raloxifene acts as an estrogen agonist in bone (decreasing resorption) and an antagonist in breast tissue (potential breast cancer risk reduction). Unlike estrogen, it does not stimulate endometrial tissue. It is second-line due to limited anti-fracture efficacy at non-vertebral sites.

**Q:** What are the glucocorticoid equivalents and how does mineralocorticoid activity differ among steroids?

**A:** Hydrocortisone (potency 1x, dose 20 mg), cortisone acetate (0.8x, dose 25 mg), prednisone (4x, dose 5 mg), dexamethasone (30x, dose 0.75 mg). Hydrocortisone, cortisone, and prednisone have mineralocorticoid activity; dexamethasone has NO mineralocorticoid activity.

Understanding steroid equivalents is essential for managing adrenal insufficiency and stress dosing. Dexamethasone is useful when pure anti-inflammatory effect is needed without salt retention. Fludrocortisone replaces aldosterone in primary adrenal insufficiency.

**Q:** What is the standard treatment protocol for adrenal crisis?

**A:** Hydrocortisone 50–100 mg IV bolus, then 50–100 mg IV q8h (or continuous infusion) for 24–48 hours. Once stable, switch to oral hydrocortisone (50 mg q8h x 48h) then taper over 14 days.

Adrenal crisis is a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate IV glucocorticoids, IV saline (for volume depletion), and glucose. Precipitants include infection, surgery, or failure to increase steroid doses during illness ('sick day rules'). Fludrocortisone is added for primary adrenal insufficiency once oral therapy resumes.

**Q:** What did the DPP (Diabetes Prevention Program) trial demonstrate?

**A:** Lifestyle interventions (≥7% weight loss, ≥150 min/week physical activity) reduced T2DM incidence by 58%; metformin (850 mg BID) reduced it by 31% vs. placebo. Lifestyle was significantly more effective than metformin.

This landmark NEJM 2002 trial enrolled 3234 non-diabetic patients with impaired glucose tolerance. It established lifestyle modification as the most effective intervention for T2DM prevention, with metformin as an evidence-based pharmacologic option in high-risk individuals.

**Q:** What did the Look AHEAD trial demonstrate regarding intensive lifestyle intervention in T2DM?

**A:** Intensive lifestyle intervention for weight loss in overweight/obese T2DM patients improved weight, HbA1c, and cardiovascular risk factors but did NOT reduce cardiovascular morbidity or mortality. The trial was stopped early for futility.

Look AHEAD (NEJM 2013) challenged the assumption that weight loss alone would translate to cardiovascular event reduction in T2DM. This highlights the importance of addressing glycemia, BP, lipids, and antiplatelet therapy comprehensively rather than focusing on weight alone.

**Q:** What were the key findings of the ACCORD trial and why was it stopped early?

**A:** Intensive glucose lowering (target HbA1c <6.0%) in T2DM with CVD or CVD risk factors was stopped early due to increased mortality. No difference in cardiovascular events, but increased hypoglycemia, fluid retention, and weight gain in the intensive group.

ACCORD (NEJM 2008) was a landmark trial demonstrating that overly aggressive glucose lowering is harmful in high-risk T2DM patients. This led to individualized HbA1c targets in Canadian guidelines, with less stringent targets for patients with established CVD, hypoglycemia risk, or frailty.

**Q:** What did the ADVANCE trial show about intensive glucose control targeting HbA1c <6.5%?

**A:** Intensive glucose control (HbA1c <6.5%) significantly reduced nephropathy (4.1% vs 5.2%) but did NOT significantly reduce major macrovascular events or all-cause mortality. Severe hypoglycemia was more common in the intensive group (2.7% vs 1.5%).

ADVANCE (NEJM 2008) enrolled 11,140 T2DM patients. The primary benefit was microvascular (renal). This supports intensive control for preventing diabetic nephropathy but cautions against it for macrovascular risk reduction, particularly in patients where hypoglycemia is dangerous.

**Q:** What was the conclusion of the VADT trial regarding intensive glucose control?

**A:** In veterans with long-standing, poorly controlled T2DM, intensive glucose control (HbA1c 6.9% vs 8.4%) did NOT significantly reduce major cardiovascular events, microvascular complications, or death from any cause, but increased hypoglycemia.

VADT (NEJM 2009) reinforced findings from ACCORD and ADVANCE: intensive glucose control in established, long-duration T2DM does not reduce hard cardiovascular outcomes and increases adverse events. Early tight control ('legacy effect') may be more beneficial.

**Q:** A 70-year-old man is started on spironolactone for heart failure and develops bilateral breast enlargement. What is the mechanism?

**A:** Spironolactone is an aldosterone antagonist that also blocks androgen receptors and inhibits testosterone synthesis, decreasing androgen activity and shifting the estrogen-to-androgen ratio in favour of estrogen, causing gynecomastia.

Drug-induced gynecomastia is common and should always be considered in new cases. The preferred management is discontinuing or switching the offending agent when clinically feasible. Tamoxifen is an option if the drug cannot be stopped.

**Q:** What is the 5-alpha-reductase deficiency and how does it present?

**A:** A defect in androgen action where 5-alpha-reductase fails to convert testosterone to dihydrotestosterone (DHT). Presents as ambiguous genitalia at birth (DHT needed for external genital masculinization) with virilization at puberty (testosterone-mediated).

This X-linked recessive condition highlights the distinct roles of testosterone (Wolffian duct and pubertal virilization) vs. DHT (external genitalia masculinization). At puberty, rising testosterone causes virilization, voice deepening, and muscle growth despite DHT deficiency.

**Q:** What is complete androgen insensitivity syndrome (CAIS) and what is its karyotype and presentation?

**A:** CAIS is an X-linked defect in the androgen receptor causing complete resistance to testosterone and DHT. Karyotype 46,XY. Presents as phenotypically female with absent uterus, absent/sparse pubic and axillary hair, blind-ending vagina, and intra-abdominal or inguinal testes.

Despite high testosterone levels, there is no androgen effect. Estrogen (from peripheral conversion of testosterone) causes female breast development. Risk of gonadal malignancy (gonadoblastoma) mandates gonadectomy after puberty (after allowing estrogen-driven feminization).

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for paraneoplastic syndromes?

**A:** Primary: treat the underlying tumour (surgery, radiation, chemotherapy). Secondary (immune-mediated disorders): IVIG, steroids, immunosuppressive drugs, plasmapheresis (reserved for patients with identifiable serum antibodies).

The immune-mediated paraneoplastic syndromes (neurological, rheumatological) often improve with tumour treatment but may require immunotherapy. Plasmapheresis removes circulating antibodies but is reserved for antibody-confirmed cases. Symptomatic management is important in parallel.

**Q:** A patient with T2DM has an eGFR of 12 mL/min. Which diabetes medications are contraindicated?

**A:** Metformin (GFR <15), SGLT2 inhibitors (eGFR <15/ESRD/dialysis), sulfonylureas (glyburide and glimepiride in severe renal dysfunction), DPP-4 inhibitors require dose reduction (except linagliptin), GLP-1 agonists are relatively contraindicated in ESRD.

Renal dosing of diabetes medications is a high-yield topic. Linagliptin is unique among DPP-4 inhibitors in not requiring dose adjustment in renal impairment (eliminated hepatically). Insulin remains the safest option in severe CKD but requires careful dose titration.

**Q:** What is the Occurrence Rate of Gynecomastia at infancy, puberty, and ages 50-80?

**A:** Infancy: 60–90% affected; Puberty: 46–9% (peaks ages 13–14); Ages 50–80: 26–5% affected.

Physiologic gynecomastia is common at these three stages due to transient hormonal fluctuations. Pubertal gynecomastia spontaneously regresses in 90% within 1–2 years. Persistent gynecomastia beyond 2 years of puberty or onset in adults warrants investigation.

**Q:** What is the role of fludrocortisone and when is it used?

**A:** Fludrocortisone (Florinef) is a mineralocorticoid used to replace aldosterone in primary adrenal insufficiency. Dose: 0.1 mg/day chronically. It has 100% mineralocorticoid activity and replaces the salt-retaining function of aldosterone.

In primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease), both glucocorticoids AND mineralocorticoids are deficient. Glucocorticoid replacement alone (hydrocortisone) is sufficient for secondary/tertiary adrenal insufficiency (ACTH-deficient states) where the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis remains intact.
